# Greg Girard and Ian Lambot, *City of Darkness Revisited* (2014): the secondary parts

This file holds the secondary parts of *City of Darkness Revisited*, the 2014 edition of the
book about Kowloon Walled City (九龍城寨). They are the 2014 essays, the captions and standfirsts,
the short features with no speaker, the fold-out panorama, the popular-culture pages, the later
studies, the jacket and the end pages. Their writers wrote them in 1993 or later, or did not see
the City. The primary parts of the book are the photos of Girard and Lambot, the interviews, the
1992 map and the reproduced documents. They are in the twin file
[`../../primary-sources/city-of-darkness-revisited/SOURCE.md`](../../primary-sources/city-of-darkness-revisited/SOURCE.md).
In the data, mark each fact from this file as secondary (see [`../README.md`](../README.md)).

- **Citation:** Greg Girard and Ian Lambot, *City of Darkness Revisited*. Watermark
  Publications, 2014. ISBN 978-1-873200-88-9. 356 pages and a fold-out.
- **Link:** https://search.worldcat.org/search?q=bn:9781873200889 (library record; the book is
  not online).
- **Copy used:** John's own copy. The imprint (p4) gives "First Published October 2014" and
  lists reprints up to January 2025, thus the copy is a reprint. John photographed each spread
  on 2026-10-30 (190 photos). An agent flattened and cropped each page and corrected the colour.
  The photos were then deleted, as John asked; he keeps them in his Photos library.
- **Copyright:** © Greg Girard and Ian Lambot. The other images belong to the owners on p356.
  The fold-out is © 2014 Hitomi Terasawa (寺澤一美); the book says that it "must not be copied or
  reproduced in any form".
- **Stored in git:** no copy of the book. Only this file is in git. This folder holds no files.
- **Files:** the scans and the OCR text are in the primary folder's `local/`:
  `final/pages/` (one image for each page) and `ocr/ocr_book.txt` (the OCR text of each page).
  They are on John's Mac only. To find a printed page N in `final/pages/`: for N ≤ 209 the file
  is `NNN = N + 7`; for 210 ≤ N ≤ 356 it is `N + 13` (since pages 84–85 were added on 2026-11-16).
- **Notes:** agents read the 13 parts of the book on the page images and the OCR text. For
  each part, a second agent then checked each row against the page images (2026-11-10 for
  part 3, 2026-11-16 for the others; the note of part 2 gives no date). Their 13 note
  files are in `research/sources-2026-11/codr/` (`01-popham.md` to `13-captions-3.md`). Git
  ignores `research/`, thus the notes are on John's Mac only. This file holds all their checked
  rows. I built it on 2026-11-16 with the script
  `research/sources-2026-11/codr/tmp/secondary_build/build.py`.

## What is secondary in this book

The credits (p4) give the "captions and commentary" to Charles Goddard, Ian Lambot and Greg
Girard. In this file, "the editors" means these three. The book does not say who wrote each
caption, or when. Some captions are from the 1993 edition ("In 1992, he was married", p172).
Others are from 2014 ("while researching this new edition", p145; Kai Tak (啓德) "was finally
closed in 1998", p316). The twin file explains why each part is secondary ("Which parts are
primary").

The authors' own memories inside the essays are retrospective, not secondary. They are the rows
with "own visit" in "Rests on": Peter Popham (pp6–19), Jon Resnick (p217, from 1983), Lui Tai
Lok (p222, no year) and Ian Lambot (pp270–283, from the early 1980s). If you use one of them,
tag it "retrospective (2014)", as the twin file says.

The essays and captions also quote the words of other witnesses. These words are first-hand
testimony, thus they are not secondary facts. Tag each one "retrospective (year)" if the person
spoke years later, and treat it as a lead to a primary record:
- Richard Margolis, in an interview with McHugh "26 years later" (about 2012) (p337).
- Sir John Boyd (spring 2014), Gordon Jones ("26 years later") and Qiao Zonghuai in a Chinese
  television documentary of 2011 (pp336–339).
- Duncan Pescod, "in 2013" (pp344–345).
- The former residents whom Lambot interviewed for the 2014 edition (pp273–281). Lambot gives
  their memories in his own words.
- Chan Shing, a water supplier, in the words of a caption (p112).
- Yu Wing Leung, a manga artist, on his childhood near the City (pp202–204).
The words of the 32 interviews of the book are primary. They are in the twin file, not in this
file. A row of this file gives only the words of the editors about an interview (a standfirst, a
caption or a gloss).

## What it rests on

No essay of the book has notes or a list of sources. The writers name their sources only in the
text, and seldom with a file number. The "Rests on" column of each table in "Facts and details"
gives the source of each row. "Leads to primary sources" lists the records to find.

**Peter Popham (pp6–19).**
- His own tour of the City with a guide, "not long before its demolition" (p6), "around the same
  time" as the photos of Girard and Lambot (p15). He wrote the Introduction of the 1993 edition
  (p15), and he quotes its verdict (p16).
- Records with no file number, some with no year: a secret memo of a Governor to the Secretary
  of State, an Attorney General's opinion, and a confidential memo on the electricity (p10); the
  *Hong Kong Standard* and the New China News Agency (p11); a police survey of 1952 and a police
  report of March 1953 (p12); a Government statement of 1973 (p12).
- The writings of the architects Suenn Ho (何巽) and Aaron Tan (p19), with no title or date.
- The film *Long Arm of the Law* (省港旗兵) and the work of Jackie Pullinger (p15).

**Julia Wilkinson (pp62–75).** She gives no list of sources, but she quotes many records in the
text:
- J.H. Stewart Lockhart's report of 1898 on the wall (p62).
- The *Hongkong Weekly Press* (1899 and 1904), *The Hongkong Telegraph* (1890), *The Daily
  Press* (1899) and the *Hongkong Guide* (1893) (pp62–72).
- Reverend Krone's paper of 1858 and the statements of the Hong Kong General Chamber of Commerce
  of 1898 (p65).
- The letters and telegrams of MacDonald, Balfour and Salisbury; the Peking Convention for the
  Extension of Hong Kong of 9 June 1898; the Order in Council of 27 December 1899 (pp65–71).
- George Kennedy-Skipton's account of 1970 (p72).
- The captions rest on old maps and photos of 1845–1924 (credits on p356).

**James Saywell (pp116–143).** He "was too young to visit the Walled City" (p116). Thus no row is
"own visit".
- First half: one attribution only, "a Government survey" for the building count of 1954 (p129).
  Its numbers are those of the police count of November 1954 in the memo of 4 March 1955 (p274).
- Second half: one source only, Suenn Ho, who "argued years ago" that the City was an ideal form
  of town (p140). He does not say where she argued it.
- The captions rest on the photos of Mak Fung (1950), Ko Tim Keung (高添強) (1962–1972) and the
  Government Information Services (政府新聞處) (1970–1976); the Survey and Mapping Office (測繪處)
  map of 1968 and its aerial photos; the *Hong Kong Standard* of 11 January 1963 (p130); the
  footprint plans of William McElhinney (1962 and 1987, p134); Suenn Ho's drawings
  (pp136–139); and the study of the University of Waterloo (pp121, 140).

**Jon Resnick (pp192–217), and the fold-out.**
- His own visits: he came to Hong Kong in 1982, first went into the City in 1983 with an
  Associated Press reporter, and went back several times alone (p217).
- The films, games, novels, manga and replicas themselves. The websites of the Hong Kong SAR
  Government, the Hong Kong Tourism Board, Untapped Cities and Hong Wrong. The radio show *99%
  Invisible* (2012) and *The New York Times* of 1 March 2012 (pp213–215).
- Quotes of Christopher Nolan, Scott Evans, Yu Wing Leung, Douglas Young, Stefan Herda, Miru Kim,
  Roger Ming Jun Hom, Taishiro Hoshino and the cameraman Hamdani Milas, with no place or date.
- The captions of pp208–209 tell how Takayuki Suzuki's team surveyed the empty City in 1993, and
  how Hitomi Terasawa drew the cross-section. Suzuki asked her to fill the surveyed section "with
  people and activity" (p208). Thus the people and the uses in the fold-out are the artist's
  additions, not survey records. The book says that the drawing "is far from accurate" (p208).
- Rows marked "fiction" come from a novel, a game, a manga or the plot of a film. Use them only
  as INFERRED (see `../README.md`, rule 5).

**Lui Tai Lok (pp220–225).** No source and no notes. The essay rests on his knowledge of Hong
Kong and on his own "limited experience" of the City (p222). He gives no year, number or purpose
of his visits, and he tells them in the second person ("you").

**Ian Lambot (pp270–285).**
- Colonial Office and Foreign Office files at The National Archives, Kew: CO 537/4807 (1948),
  CO 1030/394 (March 1953), CO 1030/934 (11 March 1955) and CO 1030/810 (1958).
- The memorandum of the Commissioner of Police of 4 March 1955. The book reproduces two of its
  pages (pp274–275, primary; see the twin).
- A Hong Kong Police report of 2000 on Triad societies (p277), with no title or number.
- Interviews with former residents and with one police officer ("we spoke to", pp273, 277,
  281). The book does not name them.
- His own visits from the early 1980s ("when Greg and I first visited the City").
- Picture credits (p356): Lambot (pp271, 273, 276, 284–285), Getty Images/Hulton Archive (p272),
  the Public Records Office (歷史檔案館), Hong Kong (pp274–275), the Hong Kong Police Museum
  (警隊博物館) (p277), Ko Tim Keung (pp278–279), Nicholas Millman (pp280–281), Greg Girard
  (pp282–283).

**Fionnuala McHugh (pp328–348).** She gives no notes and no file numbers, and she tells no visit
of her own. She came to Hong Kong in 1993, thus after the residents had left (INFERRED).
- 1899–1975: Government statements; the *China Mail* (front page of 13 January 1948, p330); a
  telegram of Sir Ralph Stevenson to the Foreign Office (27 August 1949); the report on the
  riots of 1956; the ruling of Chief Justice Sir Michael Hogan (the Wong Hon case, 1959–1960);
  the *Hong Kong Standard* of 19 January 1963 (p334); a secret Colonial Office briefing of 1963;
  a Government list of services of 1973.
- 1985–1987: later testimony of Richard Margolis (about 2012), Sir John Boyd (spring 2014) and
  Gordon Jones ("26 years later"), and Qiao Zonghuai in a Chinese television documentary of
  2011.
- 1987–1995: words of Duncan Pescod "in 2013" (she does not say to whom); the Special Committee
  of the Housing Authority (香港房屋委員會); statements to the Executive Council in 1993; the
  Director of Audit (核數署署長); *South China Morning Post* reports and a column of Kevin
  Sinclair (1992); a letter of January 1993 to a newspaper; a statement of the Chinese Foreign
  Ministry; two court cases.
- The images are by Girard, Lambot, *Sing Pao Daily News* (成報), Isaac Chu and Simon Lam, Suenn
  Ho (the plans of p340) and Anna Dalgliesh (the map) (p356).

**The captions, standfirsts and glosses (parts 1–4, 7–13).**
- The editors' own visits and photos.
- The original interviews by Emmy Lung (about 1989–1992), and the new interview of Lam Po Chun
  (林寶鎮) for the 2014 edition.
- Counts with no source: "more than 150" dentists at the peak and 86 by 1987 (p36); "70 or so"
  water suppliers (p112); "350 or so buildings" (p164); Chiu Chow (潮州) people "more than 70 per
  cent" (p183); "700 or so industrial premises" (p256).
- Hearsay: "it was said" (pp290, 294).
- Quotations of Jan Morris (1990, p191), Lui Tai Lok (p234) and Martin Booth (2004, p303).
- The police standfirst (p251) says that the officer spoke without the authorisation of his
  department.

**The features with no speaker (part 10).** The photos of Girard and Lambot (primary) and short
texts by the editors. The Water Supply (pp106–111) is the only text that cites Government
records: a Government statement of 1963, a complaint of the Kai Fong Association
(九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會) of 1964 and the Government count of
wells of 1987. The other texts rest on folklore (p32), a survey of 1975 (p246), unnamed former
residents (pp107, 324) and the visits of the authors.

**The Map (p353).** The caption tells that Anna Dalgliesh drew the map in 1992 for the 1993
edition. It says that the map "cannot be considered entirely accurate".

**The words of the columns.** In "Rests on":
- "not given": the book gives no source for the fact.
- "caption", "standfirst": the words of the editors.
- "own visit", "own photo": the writer's own visit or photo (retrospective; see above).
- "photo", "photo (my reading)", "drawing (my reading)": the agent describes what a picture
  shows. The picture of the time is primary; the words of the row are not in the book.
- "fiction": use only as INFERRED.
- "INFERRED" in a row marks the agent's own reasoning or arithmetic, not the book's words.

## Limits

### The book's own warnings

- **The Map** (p353): "this map cannot be considered entirely accurate".
- **The fold-out** (p208): the drawing "is far from accurate", "but it captures the full majesty
  of life in the City", and Terasawa worked "Without accurate survey drawings". But p204 calls it "Based on actual measurements and with meticulous
  detail". The book disagrees with itself. Suzuki asked her to fill the section "with people and
  activity" (p208). Thus every person and every use in the fold-out is a guess of the artist.
- **The captions** have no dates. The editors wrote them in 1993 or in 2014, and some tell of
  events after the clearance. The book does not say which caption is from which year.
- **The police standfirst** (p251): the officer spoke without the authorisation of his
  department.

### Pages 84–85 (added 2026-11-16)

The first scan did not have pages 84–85, because two leaves were turned together. John photographed
them on 16 Nov 2026. Their secondary part is one caption on p84, about Chan Pui Yin's (陳沛然) shop.
Its rows are in Part 11. The main agent read them on the page image, and no second agent checked them.

### Text cut at the binding

Near the spine, the binding hides the ends of some lines. The agents did not quote a lost part.
Their completions in "Doubts" are guesses from the sense.
- p22 (the word before "storeys", read as "four"); p32 (12 lines of the Fuk Tak Shrine text);
  p44; p62 (the right column of Wilkinson's essay); p68 and p70 (captions); p82; p98 (the count
  "400 to 5…" meters); p100; p108 (most lines of the caption).
- p204 (the right column of Resnick's essay; the handover year "199[7]"); p224 (the right column
  of Lui's essay); p236 ("announced in 198?", probably 1987); p260 (every line of the caption
  box; the end is lost); p272 (the right column of Lambot's essay).
- p353 (map pin 33 is half hidden); p356 (the right edge of the credits: Lambot's list, the list
  of the Public Records Office after "329", the names of the University of Waterloo, the
  footnote). Thus the credits of pp104/105, p312, p330 and p334 are not legible.

### The book against itself

The essays and the captions disagree in many places. The note files list each conflict (see
"Doubts of each part" below). The main ones:
- **Population.** 35,000 (jacket; pp6, 9, 107, 249); "at least 33,000" (p213); 33,000 (p110;
  p339 with the day workers; p342); "28,200 occupants" in 8800 flats (p340); 30,000 (p345); an
  estimated 40,000 in 1980 (p129). For about 1960: around 10,000 (pp129, 281, 334), but the
  police memo of 1955 gives about 13,000 and 5,000 floating (p274), and the clipping of January
  1963 gives 20,000–30,000 (p334).
- **Buildings.** About 800 in 1954 (p129); "well over 650" in 1962 (p134); "around 300" (p134)
  and "some 300" (p340) in 1987; "350 or so" (p164).
- **Businesses.** 700 (p110); "700 or so industrial premises" (p256); "some 900 factories,
  restaurants and shops" (p249).
- **Heights.** About 1960: two or three storeys "as late as 1962" (p116), four or five in 1960
  (p118), one to three "until the 1960s" (p118). Six or seven storeys (p130) against nine from
  1963 (p130 clipping, p132). The maximum: "12 storeys and more" (p132), 14 (p135), 12, 13 and
  14 in 1973 (p334), 15 (pp314, 340). The 14-storey limit: "by 1972" (p118), "by the end of the
  1970s" (p118), 1975 (pp334, 336).
- **Water.** Stand-pipes from 1950 (p121), "a few" in the early phases (p130), the first in 1963
  (p107); five in 1964 and eight in 1987 (p107). Wells: 77 (p14), "50 or so" (p121), "up to
  300" sunk and 67 working in 1987 (p107), "70 or so private water suppliers" (p112).
- **Electricity.** "Only in the 1960s" (p14) and an agreement "by 1962" (p127). The China Light
  and Power (中華電力) engineer Mok Chung Yuk (pp98–99, primary) gives a supply "in a major way
  in 1977".
- **Density.** "about 30 square feet per person" (p9) against "seven square feet per person"
  (p135).
- **Dates.** The fort: 1841 (p9) against 1847 (p62). The seizure of 1899: "two years after" the
  lease (p10) against "less than a year after the June 1898 Convention" (p328). *Long Arm of
  the Law*: 1982 (p15) against 1984 (pp198–199). The Kai Fong Association: 1962 (pp281, 332)
  against "established on 1 May 1963" (p178, primary). The failed clearance: 1947 (p129)
  against the *China Mail* of 13 January 1948 (p330). The survey of the empty City: February
  1993 (p204) against January 1993 (p209). The first residents left in "the summer of 1991"
  (p338), but some left in 1990 (pp23–24, p169). The clearance ran "July 1991 to August 1992"
  (p344), but the essay ends it on 2 July 1992 (p346), and p121 says "late 1992".
- **Vice.** Lui (p221) limits the vice to "a short period in the 1950s"; p252 says that it ended
  by the early 1960s. But Wong Wai Chung (pp258–259, retrospective) and Isaac Liu (劉知三) (p305) tell of
  drugs in the 1970s.
- **Captions against interviews.** Ho Chi Kam (何智錦) (p28); Wong Yu Ming (黃如明) and his wife (pp35–36);
  Cheung Mei (p36); Hui Kwong (許光), 1961 or 1965 (p40); store hours, 6am or 8am (pp91, 93); the
  postman's training (pp146, 149); Chung Yuk Yee's flat (pp159, 161); the factory on p236; Yim
  Kwok Yuen's (嚴國源) shop (p286); Kwok Lau Hing (郭柳馨) and Junk Bay (p308); "Hongkong Telecom" (p318); Law Yu
  Yi, "Formerly a housewife" (p80) or "retired maid" (p353). The interviews are primary: they
  win.
- **Wrong cross-references.** p43 and p49 (each two pages too low); p205 ("see page 206"); p208
  ("page 214" for Hashima, on p216); p210 ("see page 140"); p234 ("see page 218"; the essay
  starts on p220); p299 ("see page 302"; the interview starts on p304).
- **Names.** "Hiromi Terasawa" (p216, p356) against "Hitomi Terasawa"; "Isaac Lui" (map key)
  against "Liu" (p304); "Peter Poham" and "John Resnick" (p355).

### Errors that outside knowledge shows

The agents found these with knowledge from outside the book. The doubts of each part tell more.
- The Sung Dynasty "(960-1297)" (p62): it ended in 1279.
- The Governor in March 1955 was Sir Alexander Grantham, not "Sir Robert Black" (p273). Black
  became Governor in 1958.
- The police protests against the ICAC were in 1977, not "1978" (p282).
- Khrushchev withdrew from Cuba after the Cuban missile crisis, not "the Bay of Pigs crisis"
  (p332).
- "His Majesty's Colony" (p328) in 1899: Queen Victoria was on the throne.
- "40 catties [60 pounds]" (p93): 40 catties are about 53 pounds.
- "Act" (p12): Hong Kong laws were ordinances. "Explosive Ordinance Disposal Unit" (p345) is
  probably a misprint of "Ordnance".
- "1997 days" (p346) counts the first and the last day; the difference of the dates is 1996
  days.
- Lambot's "15 dog-meat shops" (p273): the memo prints 13 (p275). Its typewriter "3" looks like
  a "5". Use 13.

### Readings that are not sure

- Small print and signs in the photos: the clinic signs of pp8–9; the mark "W5168" (p10); the
  sale contract (p116: the names 黃錫炎 and 羅煥章 and the Lung Chun Road (龍津道) number have no second
  reader); the advert 中華戲院 (p122); the year on p125; "$600" in the clipping (p130); 方濟成
  (p131); the room and dot counts (pp137, 139); the names on p140; the "NO TRESPASSING" notice
  (p144); the painted marks of pp144, 148, 164 and 168; the card on p186; the Japanese text of
  pp208–209; the banners of pp276–277; the marks of p283; the sign of p285; the small print of
  the *China Mail* (p330); 屆 or 届 (p332); the second sign of p336; the handwritten figures of
  the plans (p340); the chalk marks and the calendar of p341; the labels of the 1847 plan and
  the 1845 map (pp62, 64).
- The fold-out has no labels. Each fold-out row is an agent's reading of a drawing.
- The photo dates from calendars are INFERRED: p34 (21 July 1989), p56 (10 March 1989), p80
  (31 March 1989), p89 (27 October 1990). The calendar on p244 prints March 1989.
- The map pins are an agent's reading of a map that is not "entirely accurate".

### Chinese names from outside the book

The English pages of the book print few Chinese characters. Some names of people in the rows
(for example 羅婉儀, 余慶雲, 陳偉瑞 and 王海明) come from the repo's `data/people.json` and
`docs/people.md`. These cite the table of contents of the Chinese edition (中華書局, 2015). For
the Kai Fong Association, this file uses 九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會, as the twin does;
`docs/people.md` gives 九龍城寨街坊福利會.

### Parts with no notes

- The 2014 introduction to Cathie Breslin's article (p264) has no rows here. Her article of 1963
  is primary (see the twin).
- Pages 218–219 (Lambot) and 284–285 (Lambot) are photos with no text.
- The note of part 13 also lists 13 photos with no caption (pp232–309). They are primary, thus
  they are not in this file.

### Doubts of each part

The "Doubts" lists of the 13 note files, as the checkers left them.

#### Part 1. The jacket, the front matter and Peter Popham, "Kowloon Walled City – the Reality"

- p10: Popham dates the British seizure "In 1899, two years after" the 99-year lease. McHugh (p328) says that 1899 was "less than a year after the June 1898 Convention". The book disagrees with itself. I know that the lease was signed in 1898.
- p9: Popham says that the new fort was built "In 1841". Wilkinson (p62) says that the wall was finished "By 1847", and that 1841 is the year when Britain occupied Hong Kong Island. Popham's 1841 is probably the year of the threat, not of the building.
- p15: Popham dates *Long Arm of the Law* "As early as 1982". Resnick (pp198-199) gives 1984 twice.
- p12: Popham writes "Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) Act". McHugh (p334) writes "ordinances". Hong Kong laws were ordinances.
- p11: Popham says that China's Foreign Minister called in the British chargé d'affaires. McHugh (p332) says that the West European Affairs Department of the Foreign Ministry did it, on 1 January 1963.
- p12: Popham joins two police statements. The memo (p275, para 10) and Lambot (p273) use "each more daring than the other" and "all playing to capacity crowds" for three theatres open by mid-February 1953. The total of 16 is from 17.3.53 (p275, para 12).
- Dog-meat shops in 1952: Lambot's text on p273 prints "15 dog-meat shops". I checked the page image: this is not an OCR error. Popham (p12) and the typed memo (p275) give 13. The typewriter of the memo prints its 3 with a flat top, like a 5. Thus Lambot probably misread the memo. Use 13.
- p14: Popham says that the electricity company came in with meters "Only in the 1960s, after a serious fire". The China Light and Power engineer Mok Chung Yuk (pp98-99) says that the supply came "in a major way in 1977". He also says that about 20 per cent of households had a supply before the master plan. The two do not conflict directly, but the weight is different.
- p14: Popham gives "a total of 77 wells". The Water Supply feature (p107) gives "67 working wells owned by some 40 suppliers" in 1987. The two counts can measure different things (all wells sunk vs working wells in 1987). p107 also says that "up to 300 'scientific wells'" were said to have been sunk over the years.
- p9 and p12 captions: the p9 caption calls Tung Tau Tsuen Road "by far the most affluent side of the City". The p12 caption calls the west side "the wealthiest part of the City". The captions disagree.
- p9: "about 30 square feet per person". The book does not say how it got this figure. The City had 6.5 acres (p10) and blocks of 10-14 storeys. Thus each of 35,000 people has more than 30 sq ft, even after factories and stairs (my own arithmetic).
- Population: the jacket flap, the p6 caption and p9 give 35,000. Saywell (p129) gives an estimated 40,000 in 1980. The registration of January 1987 gives just over 33,000 with day workers (p339).
- Jacket flap: "25 years since its demolition" and "Twenty-five years on" do not fit a 2014 text. Probably the flap was revised for a later reprint.
- p5: the contents list calls Chu Yiu Shan (朱耀山) a "private water supplier". The primary SOURCE.md calls him a property developer and estate agent (interview p113), and names Chan Shing as the private water supplier (photo and caption p112).
- p7: the book prints "it did not makes its final descent" (a typo in the book).
- p12: "the 19th-century yamen, now an almshouse and old people's home": "now" means the City's last years, not 2014. The text does not give a date.
- p6, p12: near the outer edge, the scan bends the caption and the right column. All words are readable on the image.
- Jacket back: a vertical Chinese sign is partly under the coloured bars. I cannot read all its characters, thus I do not give them.
- p8-9: I read the sign names from a small, busy photo. Some characters can be wrong. Check them against `docs/room_places.md`, which used the same photo. The check zoomed each sign and confirmed the names in the table. The first clinic sign prints 衆, not 眾. The strip under the Coca-Cola sign ends in "冰室". I cannot read the characters before it with certainty; `docs/room_places.md` reads "□□飲冰室".
- p16 speaks of "two Hong Kong architects". p19 then names Suenn Ho and Aaron Tan, and calls Ho a "Hong Kong-born American architect" in New York.
- p10 photo: the painted marks "W5168" and "W6…" have no explanation in the book. They can be clearance survey numbers (INFERRED; not checked).
- Jacket back flap: the address prints "Somerset TW13 5JX". TW13 is not a Somerset postcode area (outside knowledge). Not a City fact.

#### Part 2. Julia Wilkinson, "A Chinese Magistrate's Fort"

- The book prints the Sung Dynasty as "(960-1297)" (p62). The Song dynasty ended in 1279; "1297" is probably a misprint (my outside knowledge).
- Text cut at the binding on p62, right column: "just a quarte[r of]", "was soon to b[e]...", "administratively respon[sible]", "the chief mi[litary]", "then occupied as fa[mily]", "and doze[ns of]" cannons, "paper-bur[ning]", "barbarian influences an[d]". My completions are guesses from the sense.
- Captions cut at the binding: p68 ("Kowloon City ta[ken]", "at the far e[nd of Kau] Lung Gai, Kowloon City's main tho[roughfare]", "in the d[istance]") and p70 ("Kowlo[on]", "ma[r]ket", "rathe[r]", "reputat[ion]", "when it was b...", "gambling hou[ses]").
- The 1847 plan (p62) prints Chinese labels, but they are too small and blurred to read with certainty, thus I give none. A centre label is under glare (it starts "M" and ends "-en"). A bottom-centre label is under glare (it starts "Ci"). One temple label reads "... Di Temple"; the first word is not clear. On the 1845 map (p64), a small label under "KOWLOON" is possibly "Stone Fort", but it is too blurred to read with certainty.
- The paper-burning pavilion: the essay puts it "near the east gate" (p62); the caption puts it on the east side "near the south-east gate" (p68).
- The two cannon: the essay puts them "near the south gate" (p72); the caption puts them "by the entrance to the yamen on Lung Chun Back Road" for 45 years after the War (p70). The caption does not say when they moved. "45 years" after 1945 gives about 1990 (my arithmetic).
- The yamen: the essay calls it the office of the deputy magistrate (p62); the caption calls it "the Assistant Magistrate's Yamen" and "the only building to survive" to 1993 (p62). The essay says that the Lung Chun school and one private house also stood in 1940 (p72), but it does not say when they went.
- The *Hongkong Weekly Press* of 1904 gives both "of a very superior class" (p65) and "nothing but desolation" (p72). Thus the two quotes possibly come from different articles, or the first tells of an earlier time.
- Troops of 1899: the Viceroy sent 300; on 3 April "a further 600" (the sender is not named); and "the day before the flag-raising ceremony" "a further 600" (p69-70). The book does not say if the two groups of 600 are the same. The book prints no date for the second 600: the flag went up on 16 April, one day before the planned ceremony. Thus "the day before" is 15 April or 16 April.
- The date 16 May 1899 for the British landing is my arithmetic: the book says "Two days later" after "by 14 May" (p70).
- The 1865 photo (pp66-67): I cannot tell if the smoke and the tiled houses at the top left are inside the City or beyond its far wall.
- Pp74-75 hold one photo, not two: the credit is "74/75" (p356), and the spread image shows one continuous scene. The book gives no date for it.
- The essay gives the Anglican chapel in the former "San Sheng (Three Saints) Temple" (p72); the 1847 plan labels an "Er Sheng Temple" (p62). They are possibly different temples.
- The MacDonald quote prints "allowing it do to so" (p66), a misprint in the book.
- The date of the essay is not printed. "Probably 1993" is my inference from the acknowledgements (p355).
- OCR errors in ocr_book.txt that the page images correct: "Kowioon" (Kowloon), "ceremany" (ceremony), "feel" (feet), "Ithe" ([the). Pages 67, 74 and 75 have no OCR text: they are images only.

#### Part 3. James Saywell, "The Architecture of a Mini City", first half, with its captions

- p125 caption: the scan is out of focus here. The year of the aerial view reads "1961" or "1962". The p129 caption says "taken just 12 years later" than 1949, which gives 1961. I read "1963" for the end of the demolition plan with fair confidence only. I give no exact quote from this caption. (Check: an OCR at scale 3 reads "1962" for the photo and "1963" for the plan, but the digits are too blurred to confirm by eye.)
- Building heights about 1960 disagree inside this part. The p116 caption says "two or three storeys as late as 1962". The p118 caption says an average of "four or five storeys in 1960". The essay (p118) says one to three storeys "until the 1960s".
- The date of the 14-storey limit disagrees. The p118 caption says "14 storeys and more by 1972". The essay (p118) says "by the end of the 1970s". The p336 caption, in another part, says that a 14-storey building "appeared on Tung Tau Tsuen Road in 1975".
- Wells: the p121 caption gives "Some 50 or so" private wells for 1950–1990. The feature on p107, in another part, says that surveyors in 1987 "identified 67 working wells owned by some 40 suppliers".
- Stand-pipes: the p121 caption dates Government stand-pipes for squatter areas from 1950. The feature on p107 says "The first stand-pipe was installed in 1963", and that only one of eight stand-pipes was inside the City in 1987.
- Electricity: the p127 caption gives an agreement with China Light and Power "by 1962", with no source. The China Light and Power engineer Mok Chung Yuk (pp98–99, primary) dates the supply "in a major way" to 1977.
- Population: the essay's figures (2000 in 1949, 10,000 by 1960, 27,000 by 1970, 40,000 in 1980) have no source. The police memorandum of 4 March 1955 gives about 13,000 residents and 5,000 floating (p274). The registration of January 1987 gives just over 33,000 (p339).
- "the last person moved out in late 1992" (p121 caption) disagrees with McHugh (p346), who ends the clearance on 2 July 1992.
- "only one weaving factory was still operating" by 1987 (p127 caption): the book's interview with Lau Yeung Yin tells of a weaving business on Sai Shing Road (西城路) from 1982 (pp48–50). Fact C78 names Wing Fai Weaving Co. at 35 Kwong Ming Street (光明街) in 1990–92. These are two places.
- "the schools had closed" by 1987 (p127 caption): the book also has an interview about the Salvation Army (救世軍) kindergarten (p300). The caption is probably about the schools that the 1968 map marks.
- The essay dates the failed clearance to 1947 (p129). The book's *China Mail* front page on the "Kowloon Hut City Incident" has the date 13 January 1948 (p330). Check the date against McHugh's essay.
- The "Government survey" of 1954 (p129) has no reference. Its numbers are the numbers of the police reconnaissance of November 1954 (p274).
- The contract on p116: the points from the body of the contract are my reading of small handwriting, and no second reader checked them. I read the seller as 黃錫炎 and the buyer as 羅煥章; do not use these characters before a check. The contract also names a number on "龍津路" that I cannot read (fact C51 says the same). (Check: the number has the form "?拾五號", a two-digit number that ends in 5. Its first mark looks like an "X". I cannot read it as a sure digit.)
- p129: "with the same number again arriving over the next 10 years" does not say which number (one million or two million).
- The p127 caption has two misprints as printed: "boundares" and "buidings". The p356 credit for the map prints "126/167" for pages 126/127. The University of Waterloo credit is cut at the binding after "Christopher". The p140 caption gives the full name, "Christopher Glebe".
- The map labels round the City (Quoted records) are my reading of a small copy with glare. (Check: on larger crops, "TUNG WOR R…" and "SAI TAU TSUN CENTRE ST" are legible. The map prints "TSUN", not "TSUEN". The east edge of p126 is blurred at the binding, and I do not read labels there.)
- The p120 caption starts in the middle of a sentence: "1950 and a woman draws water ...". The page prints no words before "1950". Thus the first words of the caption are missing in the book itself, not only in the scan.
- The p122 photo row: I read the painted advert as 中華戲院 on a crop of a small, grainy sign. Another painted sign beside it ends in two characters of the same shape, but I cannot read it. Do not use these characters before a second check.
- The essay stops in the middle of a sentence at the end of p129 and continues on p130, in the next part.

#### Part 4. James Saywell, "The Architecture of a Mini City", second half, with its captions and the later studies

- The clipping on p130 is small and blurred in the scan (about 5 px for each letter). I read it from enlarged crops. On 3x crops, "$4,000", "$3,000" and "$50" are clear. "$600" is not sure: the 6 can be an 8. The sums agree: $10,000 for 200 square feet is $50 for each square foot.
- In the check, I compared each quote of the clipping with a 3x crop. They agree letter for letter. The small print is still blurred, thus a full-size copy is better.
- The caption on p130 does not quote the clipping letter for letter. The clipping prints "single-storeyed buildings" and "nine-storeys high". The caption prints "single-storey buildings" and "nine storeys high".
- The essay and the captions disagree about the 1960s. The essay (p130) gives a change to "six- to seven-storey" buildings. The clipping and the p132 caption give nine storeys from 1963.
- The heights disagree. The p132 caption gives "12 storeys and more" as the maximum on the north side. The essay (p135) gives "up to 14 storeys". The caption on p314 gives "150 feet or a maximum of 15 storeys along the southern boundary". The essay on p340 gives "up to a possible 15 storeys".
- The stand-pipes disagree. The essay (p130) gives "a few stand-pipes" in the early phases. The Water Supply feature (p107) says that the first stand-pipe came in 1963.
- The populations disagree. The essay gives more than 15,000 at the time of the boom after 1963 (p135). The numbers on p129 give about 10,000 in 1960. The police memo of 1955 gives about 13,000 (p274). The p135 caption gives 35,000; the essay on p129 gives a peak of 40,000 in 1980.
- The density disagrees. Saywell gives "seven square feet per person" (p135). Popham gives "about 30 square feet per person" (p9). Seven square feet agrees with ground area only: six acres are about 261,000 square feet, for 35,000 to 40,000 people.
- The "small temple near Lo Yan Street" (p135) has no name in the essay. The Tin Hau Temple feature (p241) gives a temple "Built in 1951 on an alley off Lo Yan Street" below a metal grill that caught the refuse. The essay says that rubbish buried the temple; it does not mention the grill.
- The lease of the yamen in 1949 (p135) has no source. Reverend Isaac Liu gives 1979 for the start of the Old People's Centre (老人中心) (p305). The p305 text does not say that his centre was in the yamen. I did not find which society the essay means.
- "none ever did collapse" (p136) and "the Kai Tak Airport height limit that existed for the entire area of Kowloon" (p130) are general claims with no source.
- "its 45-year life" (p134 caption): the editors do not give the two years. The late 1940s to 1993 give about 45 years.
- On p140 the name "Haneen Dalla-Ali" and the name "Christopher Glebe" are not sure: the letters l and i look the same in this type. The credit on p356 prints "Christopher B" and then letters that the binding hides.
- On p140 the line breaks at "semi-" and "prison". I did not quote this phrase.
- The room labels in Suenn Ho's level plans (p137) are too small to read. The street label below "Level 0" is also not readable. My room counts and my dot counts (p139) come from a small print and are approximate.
- In the 1963 photo (p131), the middle character of the doctor's name 方濟成 is in a free hand. The repo reads 濟 (fact C59); on the crop it can also be 濤. Other signs are too small to read.
- The photo on p131 has no caption of its own. The photo on pp142–143 has no caption at all, and the book gives no date for it.
- The OCR file has no usable text for the body of the clipping. The OCR text of the essay and of the captions agrees with the page images.

#### Part 5. Jon Resnick, "Popular Culture and the Walled City", and the fold-out by Hitomi Terasawa (寺澤一美)

- p204 dates the Suzuki survey "February 1993"; the p209 caption says "January 1993". The primary SOURCE.md already notes this.
- p204 says that Terasawa's section is "Based on actual measurements and with meticulous detail". The p208 caption says that she worked "Without accurate survey drawings" and that the drawing "is far from accurate". The two pages disagree on the accuracy.
- p204 gives the full drawing as "six-metre-wide"; p208 gives "some 18 feet wide" (about 5.5 m). These roughly agree.
- p216 caption spells the artist "Hiromi Terasawa"; every other page says "Hitomi Terasawa".
- p205 caption says "(see page 206)" for 『大図解 九龍城』, but the book shows it on pp208-209.
- p210 caption says that Herda saw "a photograph of the City (see page 140)"; p211 says "one of Ian Lambot's photographs". The primary SOURCE.md lists p140 under University of Waterloo. Check p140.
- p216 Hashima: the caption says "for 87 years between 1897 and 1974"; the text says "from 1887 to 1974". Only 1887-1974 gives 87 years.
- p213: *99% Invisible* says "By its peak in the 1990s". The clearance ran from 1987 to 1992, and other pages put the peak earlier (p129: 1980). Its "at least 33,000" matches the January 1987 registration (p339).
- p215: the *NYT* graphic prints no unit for 603, 25, 70 and the other numbers.
- p197: Pullinger's "two toilets for all thirty thousand people" comes from a 1980 book. The book does not say which year she saw it.
- p208 (Daizukai text): rooms lit by many fluorescent lamps. The repo (docs/furnishings.md F18, HK01 2024) says that tungsten bulbs lit the flats and tubes lit the corridors. The two disagree for the empty rooms of 1993.
- p199: the text puts *Bloodsport* in the list of films "actually filmed inside its walls" but says only that the City "is the setting". It is not clear that the crew filmed there.
- p204: the right column is cut at the page edge: "When the actio[n]", "gamers wh[o]", "delicat[e]", "who trie[s]", "only i[n]", "in 199[7]". The year of the handover in the *Kowloon's Gate* line loses its last digit; the context gives 1997.
- p208-209: the Japanese text is small and blurred. I read the p208 lines with confidence; the p209 floor notes and the second p208 note are partly guesses. The repo already has the p209 notes as M89-M93.
- p217 prints "poular culture" (a typo); p216 caption prints "the the military compound".
- Fold-out: the book does not say which end is east. It has no labels. The stairwell marks and the shop signs are too blurred to read. All my fold-out rows are my reading of a drawing that the book calls "far from accurate".
- p196: the caption about M+ sits on p196, but the exhibition photo is on p197. I matched them from the layout.
- "Hak Nam": the book prints no Chinese characters, and the repo gives none. I wrote none.
- Many quotes (Nolan, Evans, Yu, Young, Herda, Hoshino, Milas) have no date or place. They can come from the author's own interviews or from the press.
- p208 caption sends the reader to "page 214" for Hashima Island. Hashima is on p216; p214 shows the Kawasaki replica.
- *Long Arm of the Law*: Resnick gives 1984 twice (pp198-199). Popham's essay gives "As early as 1982" (p15, OCR text; see `01-popham.md`). The two essays disagree.
- p200 says that the manga issues were "each of around 40 pages"; the p202 caption says "32 issues of 40 pages each". The difference is small.
- p208: the Daizukai word for the lamps is blurred. Its first character can be 蛍 or 螢, thus I removed the earlier quote "蛍光灯".
- p202: the tails of the two right-hand speech bubbles do not show clearly which man speaks.
- p214: the small letters under the street plate 光明街 are blurred. I do not write them.
- p205: the three credits on the 『大図解 九龍城』 cover are blurred. They match the repo's `../../primary-sources/daizukai-kowloon-1997/SOURCE.md`, but I cannot read them letter by letter.
- Fold-out p1: the "chair with a slanted back" can also be a bed or a work-table. The dentist or barber reading is a guess.

#### Part 6. Lui Tai Lok (呂大樂), "A Triply Neglected City"

- The essay names no source for any fact. Use each row as the view of a Hong Kong sociologist in about 2014, not as a record.
- The writer does not give the date, the number or the purpose of his visits to the City. He writes them in the second person ("you"). He says only "In my limited experience" (p222). The "own visit" rows are thus retrospective testimony of an unknown year. The primary `SOURCE.md` does not list them with the authors' own memories.
- "Except for a short period in the 1950s" (p221) has no data behind it. The 1955 police memo (pp274-275) gives counts of vice for the 1950s. But Wong Wai Chung (p258, retrospective) says that drug booths stood "at the ends of all of the streets" of the City in the 1970s. He also names "three main haunts, in Sham Shui Po, the Walled City and in the hills above Tin Hau" (p259). Thus he agrees that the City was not alone, but he does not limit its drug trade to the 1950s.
- The essay gives no year for the film Long Arm of the Law. The year 1984 comes from p198 and p199.
- The Chinese characters are very small in the scan. I read them at eight times the size: 紅番, 踢人入會, 箍頸黨, 黃賭毒, 省港旗兵, 三不管, 臥虎藏龍. The first character of the last term can be the variant 卧, and the first of 黃賭毒 can be the variant 黄. The OCR text has wrong signs in each of these places.
- The romanized terms (hongfan, tiren ruhui, gujing dang, huangdudu, sanbuguan) look like Mandarin pinyin, not Cantonese. Page 4 credits "New translations by Ian Channing". The book does not say if this essay is a translation from Chinese.
- "Tsz Wan Shan (Temple Hill)" (p220): the English name in brackets is from the book.
- The essay sets Temple Street and Jaffe Road (p222) against the City as other places. Thus this Temple Street is not the Tin Hau Temple Street of the model. The book does not say where the two streets are.
- The photo on p221 has no caption. The book does not give its date, its side of the City, or the job of the two men in uniform.
- On p224 the ends of the lines in the right column are blurred at the binding. I read the words from the letters that show and from the sense. No fact, name or number is in the blurred part.
- pp224-225 print "it fits in well with those have been looking to the past". A word (probably "who") is missing in the book.
- A pull quote of this essay is on p234, with the caption about Sai Shing Road (西城路). Its words differ from p223: "PRC or British Governments" (p223: "PRC and British Governments"), "a truly unique place", "a mass of people". It says "see page 218", but the essay starts on p220. Pages 218-219 are one photo spread by Ian Lambot (p356), with no text of this essay.
- The three governments of 'triply neglected' agree with two other pages. The jacket front flap gives "the British, Chinese and Hong Kong Governments". The postman on p146 gives "[by Great Britain, Hong Kong and China]".
- The postman Lam Po Chun (p146, retrospective) agrees with the labyrinth rows of p222. He says that "the numbering was all over the place" and that the alleys "were never straight".
- The caption on p222 says "12-storey and more". This agrees with the caption on p334 (12, 13 and 14 storeys) and with p340 (up to a possible 15 storeys).
- The statement about dentists with no licence (p223) gives no count. The caption on p36 gives the counts (more than 150 at the peak, 86 by 1987).
- The essay does not print the Chinese characters of Lui Tai Lok (呂大樂), The University of Hong Kong (香港大學) or Tsim Sha Tsui (尖沙咀). The repo gives them: `primary-sources/city-of-darkness-revisited/SOURCE.md`, `primary-sources/hku-newspaper-clippings/SOURCE.md` and `data/weather.json`. The repo gives no characters for Tsz Wan Shan, Lam Tin, Chai Wan, Chungking Mansions, Nathan Road, Temple Street or Jaffe Road. Thus these names stay in English only.
- The photo readings (p221, p222, p225) are mine. In the p222 photo, the item at the end of the second pole looks like a mop, but it is dark and small. In the p225 photo, the round object on the turquoise wall can be a clock, a mirror or an ornament.
- p4 says "First Published October 2014" and lists reprints up to January 2025. The scan is of a reprint. The book does not say if the essay changed in a reprint.

#### Part 7. Ian Lambot, "Myth and Reality", with its captions

- Dog-meat shops in 1952: the essay prints "15 dog-meat shops" (p273). The memo prints "13 Dog Meat Shops" (p275, para 9). The memo's typewriter "3" has a flat top and looks like a "5". The essay writer probably misread it. Use 13.
- File numbers: the essay gives "CO 1030/394" for the March 1953 report (p272) and "CO 1030/934" for the Savingram of 11 March 1955 (p273). The two numbers have the same digits. One can be a misprint.
- The Governor in March 1955: the essay says that the Savingram of 11 March 1955 came "from the Governor, Sir Robert Black" (p273). I know from outside the book that Sir Alexander Grantham was Governor in 1955, and that Black became Governor in 1958. The essay is probably wrong on this name.
- Two dates for the memo: the p274 caption says that the memo went to London on 4 March 1955. The essay quotes the same counts from a Savingram of 11 March 1955. The Savingram can carry the memo; the book does not say.
- The Triad passage (p277) comes from "The same police report quoted above". The essay does not say if this is the 1953 report or the 1955 memo. The passage is not on the two reproduced pages.
- ICAC rebellion: the essay says "a rebellion in 1978" (p282). I know from outside the book that the police protests against the ICAC and the partial amnesty were in October-November 1977.
- Kai Fong start date: Lambot says that the Kai Fong Residents' Association was "set up in 1962" (p281). The Kai Fong vice-president's interview (p178) says "established on 1 May 1963".
- Population: the 1955 memo gives about 13,000 plus 5,000 floating (p274). The essay gives "around 10,000 in 1960" (p281). The figures do not agree well.
- Memo digits (pp274-275), typewriter "3" and "5" look alike: I read opium pipes 1953 as 1,133 (same glyph as the "3" of "1953" and "43"); raw opium 1953 as 15 taels (the glyph has the left stem of a "5", but can be 13). The area reads "9¼" acres: the figure looks typed, and a pen ring surrounds it. The bottom figure of the fraction is small; I read "4".
- Binding: on p272 the ends of the lines in the right column are blurred at the spine (for example "Incon-", "Order in", "shall", "formed", "been", "entitled", "Nego-", "agreement", "In short,", "entire-"). I read them from the context. I quote none of the blurred words.
- p276 photo: I read the large altar cloth as 秉正除奸. The small writing on the cloth and the top line (紅牡丹... ) are not clear to me.
- p283 photo: the painted marks on the walls are hard to read. I read "45" and a longer mark that ends in "46".
- pp284-285 have no caption. The place (Tung Tau Tsuen Road) and the house numbers come from the repo's room research, not from these pages.
- The p272 opium photo (Getty/Hulton) shows Hong Kong in the early 1950s. The caption does not say that the place is in the City.
- Sir Robert Black: the book prints no Chinese name. The first notes took 柏立基 from the repo's "Robert Black Health Centre" (柏立基健康院, `data/context.json`). That file is not one of the sources that the rules permit for a person's name, thus the check removed the characters.

#### Part 8. Fionnuala McHugh, "The Walled City Clearances", first half

- p332: the sentence "it announced that it had plans to demolish Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme" has lost some words. The sense is probably a demolition in the City under the Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme. I checked the page image: the book prints the sentence this way.
- p332 and p334: the caption says that the Government planned to demolish "the north-west corner of the City". The *Hong Kong Standard* clipping says that the City (about 70 mow) "was to be cleared under Stage II" of the scheme. The two do not agree on the size of the plan.
- p332: the caption dates the Kai Fong Association to 1962. The Kai Fong interview on p178 says "established on 1 May 1963" (see the primary `SOURCE.md`). The clipping dates the Anti-Demolition Committee to 24 March 1962.
- p332: the caption says "second annual dinner in 1964". The banner in the photo says only that this is the group photo of the second-term standing committee, 7 June 1964.
- p332 and p334: the essay names the "Kowloon Walled City Anti-Demolition and Anti-Removal Committee". The clipping names the "Kowloon Walled City Residents' Anti-Demolition Committee".
- p334: the caption gives a population of "around 10,000 in 1960". The clipping of January 1963 gives "the estimated 20,000-30,000 residents". The 1955 police memo (p274) gives about 13,000 and a floating 5,000. The caption figure for 1960 looks too low.
- p334 and p336: the caption for 1973 says that buildings then reached "the maximum height of 12, 13 and 14 storeys". But the limit came from the 1975 case, when a 14-storey building lost two storeys. The essay tells of two buildings in 1975; the p336 caption tells of one.
- p338: the caption says that the first residents started to move out "in the summer of 1991". Other pages of the book give earlier dates: Yau Lap Cheong "moved out in 1990" (pp23-24), and Cheng San worked on Tai Chang Street until 1990 (p169). Do not use the caption date for the first departures.
- p337: Qiao dates the banquet to 1985. The British side dates the first order for a clearance paper to 14 April 1986. The essay does not explain the order of the two events.
- p332: the book says that Khrushchev "had just withdrawn from Cuba after the Bay of Pigs crisis". This is wrong. The Bay of Pigs was in April 1961; the withdrawal of late 1962 came after the Cuban missile crisis.
- p328: the book prints "His Majesty's Colony" for an Order in Council of 1899. Queen Victoria was then on the throne, thus the original words are probably "Her Majesty's". Check the Order.
- p328 and p334: McHugh gives 6.5 acres for the City. The 1963 clipping gives "about 70 mow". I cannot tell if the two figures are for the same land.
- p329 and p330: the casualty counts differ. The Government statements give five minor injuries and one later case. The *China Mail* gives six people in hospital.
- p330: the small print of the *China Mail* page is blurred in the scan (the page image is 1758 px wide). I read with certainty only the headings, the lead, the second paragraph, the bold lead of the pact report and most of the photo caption. The check deleted the row of uncertain small-print details.
- p330, leads only: on an enlarged crop, the "Eyewitness" column seems to tell of a battle of about 30 minutes with "some 100" occupants of illegal sheds. It seems to name a Sub-Inspector Channing of Hung Hom Police Station, hurt in the nose by a brick. It also seems to name a Land Bailiff who asked the police for help, and an "East Gate". Do not use these details before you see a clear copy.
- p330: the issue number on the masthead reads "No. 33869", but the digits are small and blurred.
- p332: the banner character after 第二 can be 屆 or its variant 届; the scan is too blurred to tell. The notes write 屆.
- p336: the primary `SOURCE.md` reads a second sign, "郭衛人醫師" (D30), in this photo. On this scan I can read only the character 人 of that sign, thus the notes do not use it.
- p356: the line of the Public Records Office, HK is cut at the binding after "329". Thus the credits for the *China Mail* page (p330) and the clipping (p334) are not legible.
- p334, clipping: the petition date reads "Dec. 29", but the digit 9 can also be 0. "Three days later" and "New Year's Day" fit 29. The deadline "19" prints like "10"; McHugh gives 19. The name of the Chinese inspector reads "Mr. Seto Chijan", but the letters are not clear. The clipping prints "principles" for "principals". The quote of Dr Lam has no closing quote mark, thus the clipping is possibly cut.
- p337: "an interview 26 years later" gives no year. 1986 plus 26 gives 2012. The essay does not say how McHugh got Gordon Jones's account.
- p338: the handwriting of the posters in the photo is too small to read. The signboard shows "洪耀譚" and a fourth character that the photo edge cuts. I did not interpret the sign.
- p339: the OCR text gives the two columns in the wrong order. The correct order is: the visits of Gregory Chan Tak-ping, then "Early on the fine, sunny morning", then "believed was some sort of drill". The essay continues on p340.
- No text of these twelve pages is hidden in the binding. Pages 333 and 335 are full-page photos; their captions are on p333 and p334.

#### Part 9. Fionnuala McHugh, "The Walled City Clearances", second half; the park today; the caption and key of The Map; Acknowledgements; Credits

- Clearance dates disagree. The p 344 caption gives "July 1991 to August 1992". The essay gives these ends: phase 1, 28 Nov 1991 to 8 Jan 1992; phase 2, 3 Mar 1992; phase 3, 7 Apr 1992. The last area went on 1 July 1992, and the camp on 2 July 1992. The p 338 caption (not this part) says that the first residents began to move out in the summer of 1991.
- Evictions disagree: the p 342 caption says that "a few dozen" objectors were forcibly evicted; the essay (p 346) gives a total of 16 evictions.
- Population figures differ: "the 33,000 residents" (p 342 caption; p 339 says that 33,000 includes the day workers), "28,200 occupants" in 8800 flats (p 340), "30,000 people" (Chok, p 345).
- The essay spells the department "Buildings and Lands Department" (p 343) and "Building and Lands Department" (p 346).
- The review board has three names: "Assessment of Review Board" and "ARB" (p 346), "Walled City Clearance Assessment Review Board" (p 348).
- The book prints "Explosive Ordinance Disposal Unit" (p 345). The usual name has "Ordnance"; probably a misprint.
- "1997 days" (p 346) counts both the first and the last day; the difference of the dates is 1996 days.
- The text says "7 April" for phase 3 with no year; the context gives 1992.
- The OCR read the p 340 plan date as "9 NOV 1997"; the image shows "9 NOV 1987" (the plan's typed 8 looks like 9 at low zoom). The stamp under "Plan prepared by" reads like "WONG Chak-shem SO/SD (Hg.)"; I cannot confirm this reading.
- Photo readings are uncertain: the chalk marks "C3?/14/1-4" (p 341), "B22"/"81" (p 342) and "B3?/73 (1-4)" (p 354); the second banner word on p 342 (like "B?'GYNG", perhaps "BY GIVING"); the year on the p 341 calendar (the weekdays fit both 1985 and 1991); the bank name on the calendar is too small to read with confidence; the number of batons on p 341 (four or five).
- The p 340 plan figures are handwritten. I read the clear ones only; the figure near room (A) is "(4.65)" or "(9.65)". That the bracketed figures are areas in m² is my inference.
- The sheet 2 street name "Cheung On Lane" and the building name "Shing Hing Bldg" are handwritten and hard to read. "T.T.T. Rd" as Tung Tau Tsuen Road is my inference.
- The essay gives Pescod's words as said "in 2013" but does not say to whom. Thus it is not certain that McHugh interviewed him.
- Map: the map prints no north arrow. The directions in the map rows take the top (Tung Tau Tsuen Road) as north. The pin places are my readings of the drawing, and the caption says that the map "cannot be considered entirely accurate".
- Map: the street label prints "ALMSHOUSE BACK ST" as one word "ALMSHOUSE" (the p 348 photo also shows "ALMSHOUSE" on the yamen entrance).
- Map key 11: data/room_research.json gives "松發冰室" as "probably the Chung Fat Café"; the match is not certain.
- Map key: "Fuk Tak Temple ... 12", but the Fuk Tak Shrine feature is on p 32 (Contents). The key also spells "Reverend Isaac Lui"; the interview (p 304) spells "Liu". The caption prints "represenation".
- Map: pin 33 is half hidden in the gutter; I read the number from the key order and the visible half. Pins 14 and 21 are printed faint (grey); the book does not say why.
- Map against the interviews (to check): primary-sources SOURCE.md puts Lee Pui Yuen's store at 12 Tai Chang Street, next to Ho Chi Kam's shop at 10 Tai Chang Street. But pin 24 is east of Sai Shing Road, far from pin 13. It puts Lau Yeung Yin's weaving business on Sai Shing Road, but pin 21 is near the south end of Kwong Ming Street.
- Credits: the right edge of p 356 is cut (Lambot's list, the Public Records Office list, the University of Waterloo names and the footnote). "126/167" is probably a misprint of 126/127; "Hulton Archivee" is a misprint. The credits spell "Hiromi Terasawa"; primary-sources SOURCE.md spells "Hitomi Terasawa (寺澤一美)".
- Acknowledgements misprint two names: "Peter Poham" (the essay writer is Peter Popham) and "John Resnick" (the essay writer is Jon Resnick).
- Text at the binding: no essay text is lost on pp 340-348; the gutter hides only part of the map (pin 33) and the right edge of the credits.

#### Part 10. The short features with no speaker

- **p32, text cut at the binding.** The ends of 12 lines are lost. My reading of the lost letters: "had be[en]", "W[hen]", "eas[t]" or "eas[tern]", "restaura[nts]", "o[utside]", "go[od]", "pr[ay]", "shrine [was]", "to be [the]", "north-south a..." (the word is lost), "Walled C[ity]", "packa[ge]" or "packa[ges]". No quote above uses a lost part.
- **p108, caption cut at the binding.** The ends of most lines are lost: "Stree[t]", "comme[rcial]", "was [the]", "people –" (the next word is lost), "paid wa[ter]", "to f[ill]", "had [to]", "sta[irs]", "sunligh[t]", "besi[de]", "basket o[f]", "fishballs [in]".
- **Law Yu Yi's work.** p80 says "aged 90" and "Formerly a housewife". The map key (p353, no. 23) says "Law Yu Yi retired maid 80"; the checker read it on the page image. The "80" is the page number, not an age. Thus the key calls her a retired maid, but the p80 text calls her a former housewife.
- **Chinese names from outside this book.** The book prints no Chinese for Law Yu Yi or Yu Hing Wan. The names 羅婉儀 and 余慶雲 come from the repo's `data/people.json`, which cites the table of contents of the Chinese edition (中華書局, 2015). They are not in `data/room_research.json` or in a `SOURCE.md`.
- **Population and businesses.** p107 and p249 give 35,000 people; p110 gives 33,000 people. p110 gives 700 businesses; p249 gives "some 900 factories, restaurants and shops"; p107 gives "hundreds of factories".
- **Height limit.** p314 gives 150 feet or "a maximum of 15 storeys". The main SOURCE.md quotes p334 ("12, 13 and 14 storeys") and p340 ("up to a possible 15 storeys").
- **Stand-pipes.** p107 gives five stand-pipes in 1964 and eight in 1987. The book does not say when the other three came.
- **Fuk Tak shrine location.** The book puts the shrine in the low row on the east side. This row is in front of the buildings on the east side of Lung Shing Road (龍城路). The repo's clearance files give a Fuk Tak Temple (福德土地廟) at G/F, 5 Tai Chang Street, 5th Lane. These can be two different shrines. The map key (p353) lists "12 Fuk Tak Temple neighbourhood shrine 12", and pin 12 stands in the low row east of Lung Shing Road. The map thus agrees with p32.
- **St Stephen's Society date.** p263 calls 'The Well' (1973) a mission of the St Stephen's Society. The repo's `primary-sources/retrospective-testimony-web/SOURCE.md` says that she founded the Society in 1981. Both can be true: the book does not say when the Society began.
- **Calendar on p80.** The month on the day calendar is torn. The date 31 March 1989 is my own calculation (INFERRED), not the book's.
- **Unreadable items.** At this scan resolution I cannot read these items: most of the tinsel banner and the whiteboard (p262); the red signboard of the shrine and some small characters on the Sang Kee sign (p33); the poster on p311; the vertical sign by the barber's pole (p108); the chalk marks and notice on p105.
- **Photo details that the checker changed.** On p310, a speckled vertical band crosses the machine. The checker reads it as glare on the glossy page, not as dust in a shaft of light, and removed that detail. On p316, the line of lights can be the airport, but the caption names only the light trail of a landing aircraft. On p323, the jet's name cannot be read.
- **Photo credits.** The p356 credits for pp104/105 and p312 are cut at the binding of the scan. The note "probably Lambot" rests on the visible start of the cut numbers.
- **OCR errors found.** p249 "four" for "flour"; p107 "mainiy" for "mainly"; p104 "consclences" for "consciences"; p316 "Kowioon" and "Kal Tak"; p324 "Its" for "its"; p104 "w33'" is the painted door mark "W33". The OCR does not read the text in the photos (signs, calendar, notices); I read those on the page images.
- **Photo identity.** The book does not say that the p55 children are Mrs Law's grandchildren (the text speaks of two; the photo shows three). The book names nobody in the p262-263 photos.

#### Part 11. Standfirsts, captions and glosses on the interview pages, part 1

- In the quotes I removed the hyphens that only break a word at the end of a printed line (for example "Associa-tion", "exter-nal"). All other letters are as printed.
- p22: the word before "storeys" is blurred at the binding. It reads "fo?r"; I read it as "four". The OCR gives "forst".
- p44: the word after "cake shop," is cut at the binding (probably "on"). The word after "A sign" is also cut (probably "in"). I quoted only the next line.
- p45: the last letter of the label "FOR CHINESE WORSHIP IDOL…" is unclear. It looks like "S" (the OCR reads "IDOLE"). The board "VERY NICE TO EAT" / "AND GOOD LOOK." is a separate board above the label.
- p82: the caption is cut at the binding: "into tw[o]" and "on the oth[er]".
- p98: the caption is cut at the binding on every line: "the [elec]tricity", "a certain or[der]", "the meter[ read]ers", "torches [and]", "400 to 5[??] meters". The second number starts with "5"; it is probably 500, but I cannot read it.
- p100: the caption is cut at the binding: "spreads out fro[m]" and "individual apart[ments]".
- p91 against p93 and p94: the p91 caption gives store hours "from 6am to midnight". Lee Pui Yuen says "We're open from 8am to midnight" (p93), and the p94 caption gives "a 16-hour day", which fits 8am. The p91 caption does not name the store, but it stands in Lee's pages and fits 12 Tai Chang Street (docs/utilities.md also takes the two as one store).
- p112 against p107: the caption gives "70 or so private water suppliers". The Water Supply feature (p107, in SOURCE.md) gives "67 working wells owned by some 40 suppliers" in 1987. "70" can be the number of wells, not of suppliers.
- p40: the standfirst says that Hui Kwong lived in the City "since 1961". In his interview he says that he brought his family to Hong Kong in 1965, and then found a place in the City.
- p35: the standfirst says that Wong Yu Ming fled "with his wife and children". In his interview he says "I came by myself", and that his wife and children came later.
- p36: the caption says that many City dentists sent tooth extractions to other dentists. Wong Yu Ming (p35) says that he did extractions himself. The caption is general; this is not a direct conflict.
- p40: the gloss dates the Government's demolition attempt "[in 1962]". The Kai Fong Association was "established on 1 May 1963" (p178, in SOURCE.md), and the press reports in the book are of January 1963. The attempt can span late 1962 and early 1963.
- p93: the gloss "40 catties [60 pounds]" is wrong. I know from outside the book that 1 catty is about 0.6 kg, thus 40 catties are about 53 pounds.
- p39: "For the last five years of its life", after 1985, is approximate: the City stood until the demolition of 1993-1994.
- p34: I computed the date myself. 21 July 1989 was a Friday, and lunar 6th month, 19th day, as the calendar shows. 21 July 1990 was a Saturday. A calendar can be out of date, thus this photo date is INFERRED.
- p56 and p89: I computed the weekdays myself. 10 March 1989 was a Friday, and lunar 2nd month, 3rd day. On p89, Saturday 27 with lunar 初十日 fits 27 October 1990 (the repo's checkNote in data/room_research.json gives the same date). A calendar can be out of date, thus these photo dates are INFERRED.
- p43 and p49: the three cross-references are each two pages too low. I checked the OCR of the pages. The Salvation Army kindergarten interview starts on p300, not "page 298". The yamen is on pp298-299; p296 is the interview of Lam Tseng Yat. Yu Hing Wan is on pp310-311, not "page 308". The p300 standfirst also puts the kindergarten on Lung Chun Back Road, as p43 does.
- p36 against p35: the caption says that Cheung Mei, the dental assistant, is Wong Yu Ming's wife. In his interview, Wong says that his wife lives with the children elsewhere and only visits, and that his assistants have left. Thus the caption and the interview do not agree, or they tell of different times.
- p28: the standfirst says that Ho Chi Kam lived "in the City" long before 1985. In his interview, his flat (bought in 1974) is on Tung Tau Tsuen Road, the edge road, and he "didn't really have to come inside the City" until he ran the shop.
- p89: the name on the CLP envelope starts "NG"; the rest is not legible. The account number is not clear either.
- p60-61 and p79: I read the Chinese characters on the signs from the photos. The characters on the p79 wall are large; their order (樓 and 上) and the painted telephone numbers are not clear.
- The Chinese names of the people come from docs/people.md, which cites the table of contents of the Chinese edition. The book's English pages do not print them. I found no Chinese name in the repo for these names: Chan Kwong, Mok Chung Yuk, Chan Shing, Cheung Mei, the Chung Fat café, Carpenter Road Park, Junk Bay.

#### Part 12. Standfirsts, captions and glosses on the interview pages, part 2

- Pages with no caption of their own: pp147, 148, 151, 165, 166, 168, 171, 174, 190. The caption of a photo is often on the facing page: p152 (caption p153), p155 (p154), p156 (standfirst p157), p162 (p163), p177 (p176), p181 (p180), p182 (p183), p186 (p187), p189 (p188). The words of the speakers are primary (retrospective for Lam Po Chun), and these notes do not record them.
- Buildings: the p164 caption gives "350 or so buildings". Other pages give about 300 in 1987: "around 300" (p134) and "some 300 individual buildings" (p340).
- Time to learn the round: the p149 caption gives "more like three weeks" for a trainee. Lam Po Chun (p146) says that he learned it in two weeks and that other postmen took two months.
- Building boom: the p144 caption puts it in "the late 1970s"; the p164 caption puts the growth of the linked stairways in "the 1960s and early '70s". The two can refer to different booms.
- The p145 standfirst says that the photos of Mr Lui are on "this spread and the next" (pp144-147), but p148 also shows him. Lam Po Chun (p146) says that Mr Lui moved to a different job at the end of the 1980s. INFERRED: thus the photos of Mr Lui date from before about 1989-1990. The clearance marks beside him (p144, p148) and the "NO TRESPASSING" notice (p144) suggest a date after the clearance started in 1987. INFERRED: thus about 1987-1989.
- The small print of the "NO TRESPASSING" notice (p144) is blurred. I read "Premises under Management of SPECIAL DUTIES OFFICE", then perhaps "BLOCK ..., LOK FU". The words after "SPECIAL DUTIES" are not sure.
- Chung Yuk Yee's flat: p159 puts it "in the bowels of the City", but p161 puts it "in the same building as her mother", whose flat was at 82 Lung Chun Road, on the south edge. The two agree only if her flat was at the back (north side) of that building. The book does not give her floor or number.
- The Chiu Chow share of "more than 70 per cent" (p183) has no source and no year. No other page in this part gives a number.
- Glosses: only the square brackets on p146 (and on p191) are clearly the editors'. The parentheses in the interview texts (p145 sifu, p153 "the Chinese court", p172 "Sun Hui (Guangdong)", p173 Sai Tau village "pulled down in 1985") can be glosses of the editors or the translator, or the speakers' own words. The book does not say. The p145 gloss speaks as "I". Thus I deleted the four rows on these glosses: the notes on the interviews can record them as interview text.
- Misprints in the book, kept in the quotes: p145 "were able to speak Mr Lui's former colleague" (no "to" after "speak"); p176 "familar" for "familiar".
- p185: the book gives no date for the appointment or for the framed photo with Sir David Wilson.
- p186: the card in the frame is turned on its side and small. On a zoomed crop I read "HONG KONG CHINESE HERBALISTS ASSOCIATION MEMBERSHIP CARD" (an earlier reading gave "DENTALISTS"). The captions do not say whose clinic this photo shows. It is in the pages of Tsin Mu Lam.
- Signs in the photos: 鄭光中醫師 (p174) can be a name and a title in more than one way, and I do not give a romanization. The house number and the shop name in the p165 advertisement are only partly legible. The chalk marks on the wall on p173 and the painted characters on p190 are too blurred to read.
- Scan artefacts: on p155, a smeared band crosses the door and the posters (from the flattening of the page photo). On p172 the text lines under the photo are wavy. Neither changes the words that I quote. The centre character of the diamond poster on the p155 door is not clear, and I do not record it.
- OCR errors that I corrected from the page images: p146 "thuangdudu" and "siots"; p153 "whichhe"; p164 "045/3/1-5uр"; p191 "Walled Cityl" and "slightiy".
- More OCR errors, found in the check: p144 "B22/28" (the wall reads "B22/128") and "UNBAN SERVICES DEPANTMENY"; p150 "Invarlably"; p159 "In area" (scale 3 gives "in area"); p164 "→Tog" (the door reads "TO9"); p172 "in1965"; p175 "ВС 15" (the pillar reads "BC座" and "123室").

#### Part 13. Standfirsts, captions and glosses on the interview pages, part 3

- p236: the right edge of the caption is lost in the binding ("simila[r]", "t[he]", "tha[n]"). The words are clear from the sense, but I quote only the visible part.
- p260: the caption box is cut at the binding on every line. The last line ends "the most extraordinary depravati..." and the end of the caption is lost. The page also prints "notorious. was confined" with a full stop where a comma belongs.
- p234: the quote names Lui Tai Lok's essay "(see page 218)". The primary SOURCE.md contents table puts the essay at p220.
- p299: the caption sends the reader to Reverend Isaac Liu "(see page 302)". His interview starts on p304; p302 is an uncaptioned alley photo.
- p229 and p289 disagree. p229 says health and hygiene officers did visit the City's factories; p289 says the cooked-food factories had no health inspections. In the primary interviews, Lam Leung Po (p292) and Yim Kwok Yuen (p289) say that no inspector ever came.
- p252 says the City stopped being a den of vice by the early 1960s. p254 says Triad activity grew again for a time after 1974. Wong Wai Chung (pp258-259, retrospective) tells of drug booths at the ends of all streets in the 1970s, and Isaac Liu (p305) says the vice "disappeared slowly during the '70s". The early-1960s date of p252 is thus doubtful.
- p236: the caption "A factory on Lo Yan Street" is on the To Gui Bon page, but the p237 standfirst puts his factory on Lung Chun Back Road. The p236 photo is thus probably not his factory; the caption does not say whose it is.
- p237: the standfirst says "For 25 years"; in his interview To Gui Bon says "more than 20 years" in this spot. No real conflict.
- p286: the standfirst dates Yim's factory on Lo Yan Street to 1981. In the interview he says that he started his own roasting business when his brother came, three years after 1978, but that he rented "this processing shop three years ago". The 1981 date thus fits the business, not this shop.
- p308: the standfirst says that Kwok Lau Hing moved to "distant Junk Bay". Her interview names the Lok Wah estate for the 1985 resettlement and a hostel for the elderly later. The book does not say when she moved to Junk Bay.
- p256: "700 or so industrial premises" is not the same count as "some 900 factories, restaurants and shops" (p249, from the primary SOURCE.md). The two counts can fit together, but neither page names its source.
- p259: the book does not give the Chinese term behind "'Electric Station' Street". I did not guess it.
- p233: the standfirst spells "Guandong Province", as printed.
- p293: the caption prints "one of City's cleaner" without "the".
- The Mr Shum caption (p228) quotes his own words at length. Those words are primary, thus I did not record them here.
- p236: the binding hides the end of "198?" in "announced in 198?". The OCR and the sense give 1987, the year of the clearance announcement.
- p299: the caption prints "The oldest part the Walled City", without "of".
- p303: the Booth quotation does not name the Walled City. Only its place in the book links it to the City.
- p229 and the interviews: To Gui Bon (p237, primary) says that the Labour Department "turns up occasionally for safety inspections". This fits p229 better than p289.
- p318: the standfirst names his employer "Hongkong Telecom"; in the interview (p318) he names "the Hong Kong Telephone Company". From outside the book: the name Hongkong Telecom dates from about 1988, after his move in 1981. The standfirst thus uses the later name.
- Chinese characters: the page prints only 聯英社 (p258) and, in photos, 中華傳道會 and the signs in the photo section below. The person names (陳偉瑞, 王海明, 杜古邦, 鄭冠耀, 嚴國源, 吳錦妹, 林良寶, 林俊逸, 劉知三, 王國清, 郭柳馨, 陳昆亮), 松發冰室 and 龍津後街 come from the repo's people data (`data/people.json`, `docs/people.md`), which cites the table of contents of the Chinese edition of the book (中華書局, 2015). They are not in `data/room_research.json`, `docs/room_places.md` or a SOURCE.md. The other place names come from SOURCE.md or `data/room_research.json`.

## Facts and details

One sub-section for each part, in the order of the note files. Each table keeps every checked
row of the note file. The script removed only exact duplicates. The words in quotes are exact,
as printed. The column "Rests on" gives the source of the row (see "What it rests on").

The primary twin had a table "Captions and essays (secondary: use as leads)", checked on
2026-10-30. Its 13 rows moved here on 2026-11-16. Each of its facts is also in a row below (parts
3, 4, 8, 9, 10 and 11). Where that row did not quote the same words, the twin's exact words are
now added to its column "Words in the book" (pp107, 249, 336, 346, 347, 353).

These rows are secondary. Mark each fact from them as secondary in the data. Where a row is a
photo, the photo is primary, but the words of the row are an agent's reading.

### Part 1. The jacket, the front matter and Peter Popham, "Kowloon Walled City – the Reality" (jacket and flaps, pp1–19)

Writer: the publisher (jacket, front matter); Peter Popham (essay, 2014); the editors (eight captions). Checked on 2026-11-16: 169 rows checked, 18 fixed, 1 deleted (a duplicate), 14 added. 168 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| jacket front flap | The blurb says that the City was a dark presence in the heart of Hong Kong (香港) for nearly 50 years. It gives no start year. | "For nearly 50 years, the extraordinary community of Kowloon Walled City cut a dark presence in the heart of Hong Kong." | not given (publisher's blurb) |
| jacket front flap | The City had no legislation and little regard for basic services, planning rules or building standards, but it "positively thrived". | "Yet without legislation and with little regard for basic services, planning regulations or building standards, the City not only survived, it positively thrived." | not given (blurb) |
| jacket front flap | The blurb calls the City 'triply neglected', a Hong Kong term: neglected by the British, the Chinese and the Hong Kong Governments. (The term is the title of Lui Tai Lok's (呂大樂) essay, p220.) | "'triply neglected' – to use a Hong Kong term – by the British, Chinese and Hong Kong Governments" | not given (blurb) |
| jacket front flap | Many photos and interviews of the original book, *City of Darkness: Life in Kowloon Walled City*, come back in this book. The new material came to light over the past 20 years: new photos, drawings, maps, documents and essays, many not published before. | "new photographs, drawings, maps, documents and essays, many previously unpublished" | publisher's statement |
| jacket front flap | The book covers the City's growth "between 1945 and 1990", its darker past and its myths. It explains the clearance and why earlier attempts failed. | "stymied over the matter of its dual jurisdiction" | publisher's statement |
| jacket front flap | The book also traces how the City and its myths entered architectural debate and popular culture. | "film, literature, Japanese manga, video games, art and design" | publisher's statement |
| jacket front flap | Most Hong Kong people shunned the City while it stood. Now people see it as part of the city's cultural and architectural heritage. | "shunned by most Hong Kong residents during its lifetime" | not given (blurb) |
| jacket front flap | Peak population: about 35,000. The blurb calls it the most densely populated neighbourhood that the world has known. | "home to some 35,000 people at its peak and by far the most densely populated neighbourhood the world has ever known" | not given (blurb) |
