| jacket front flap | The blurb counts "25 years" since the demolition, twice. That fits about 2018-2019, not October 2014. Thus the flap text of this copy (January 2025 reprint, p4) is probably a later revision (my inference). | "Twenty-five years on from the Walled City's demolition" | my inference from p4 |
| jacket back | Full-bleed photo of a City facade: caged balconies with iron grilles, laundry, window air-conditioners, awnings, a lit ground-floor window and a vertical Chinese sign. Coloured horizontal bars (the book's graphic motif) cover part of it. p356 credits "Susan Scott Jacket 1 2/3" (the designer, p4). | (no text) | photo; p356 credits the jacket images to Susan Scott and names no other photographer for them |
| jacket back flap | Greg Girard is a Canadian photographer. He has worked on Asia's largest cities for more than three decades. His books include *Phantom Shanghai* (2007), *Hanoi Calling* (2010) and *In the Near Distance* (2010), early photos made in Asia and North America in 1973-1986. | "a book of early photographs made in Asia and North America between 1973 and 1986" | publisher's bio |
| jacket back flap | The novelist William Gibson wrote the foreword to *Phantom Shanghai*. Three Canadian public galleries hold Girard's work. | "the National Gallery of Canada, The Art Gallery of Ontario, the Vancouver Art Gallery" | publisher's bio |
| jacket back flap | Ian Lambot trained as an architect and worked briefly for the Richard Rogers Partnership. He arrived in Hong Kong in February 1979 and lived there for 18 years (thus to about 1997). | "before arriving in Hong Kong in February 1979, where he lived for the next 18 years" | publisher's bio |
| jacket back flap | Lambot ran an architectural model-making studio and worked with Foster and Partners on the early stages of the Hongkong Bank project. Then he set up Watermark Publications. He now lives in the UK. | "he set up Watermark Publications" | publisher's bio |
| jacket back flap | The publisher's address: Watermark Publications (UK) Limited, The Bailiff's Cottage, Church Street, Lopen, Somerset; websites www.watermarkpublications.com and www.cityofdarkness.co.uk. | "www.cityofdarkness.co.uk" | publisher |
| p1, p2 | p1 is the half title ("city of darkness revisited"). p2 has only the coloured-bar graphic. | "city of darkness revisited" | - |
| p3 | Title page: Greg Girard and Ian Lambot, with contributions by Charles Goddard, Fionnuala McHugh, Lui Tai Lok, Peter Popham, Jon Resnick, James Saywell and Julia Wilkinson. Publisher: Watermark. | "with contributions by" | title page |
| p4 | All prices in the book are in Hong Kong dollars. When the book was first edited, in 1992, about HK$13.60 bought £1.00. | "at the time the book was originally edited, in 1992, the exchange rate was approximately HK$13.60 to £1.00 sterling" | publisher's note |
| p4 | First published October 2014. Reprinted February 2018, March 2021, March 2023 and January 2025. John's copy is thus the January 2025 reprint or a later one. | "Reprinted February 2018 March 2021 March 2023 January 2025" | imprint |
| p4 | Credits: conceived and edited by Lambot and Girard; designed by Susan Scott and Lambot; captions and commentary by Charles Goddard, Lambot and Girard. | "Captions and commentary by Charles Goddard, Ian Lambot and Greg Girard" | imprint |
| p4 | Emmy Lung arranged and did the original interviews. Eunice Chu did the new interviews. The English words of the interviews are translations: the original ones by Wong & Hoffman, the new ones by Ian Channing. | "Original translations by Wong & Hoffman" ; "New translations by Ian Channing" | imprint |
| p4 | Copy editors: Julia Dawson, Charmaine Chan and Stephen McCarty. Colour separations by Evergreen Colour Management Ltd, Hong Kong. Printed in China by Everbest Printing Co Ltd. ISBN 978-1-873200-88-9. | "Printed in China by Everbest Printing Co Ltd" | imprint |
| p4 | The photos are by Girard and Lambot unless the book says otherwise. | "Photographs copyright Greg Girard and Ian Lambot unless otherwise indicated" | imprint |
| p5 | The contents list gives a trade or role for each person. The mix of trades: retired storekeeper, hairdresser, two dentists, Chiu Chow (潮州) Music Club, Chiu Chow cake maker, weaving factory, child-minding grandmother, café, candy factory, retired maid, herbal doctor and pharmacist, golf-ball maker, two shopkeepers, private water supplier, postman, Kai Fong officer and vice-president, two "family resident" entries, wooden ruler maker, two noodle makers, doctor, second herbal doctor, rubber factory owner, reformed heroin addict, Jackie Pullinger (潘靈卓) of St Stephen's Society (p262), cooked meat factory, fishball factory owner, Salvation Army (救世軍) kindergarten, missionary, retired office cleaner, muslin maker, pigeon breeder. | "Mrs Law" ... "child-minding grandmother"; "Chu Yiu Shan" ... "private water supplier" | contents page |
| p5 | Features with no speaker in the contents: Fuk Tak Shrine (福德古廟) p32, The Electricity Supply p98, Refuse Collection p104, The Water Supply p106, Tin Hau Temple (天后廟) p240, Life in the Alleys p246, A Policeman's Story p250, Life on the Roof p322, The Map p352. | "Life in the Alleys" | contents page |
| p5 | The essays and their pages: Popham p6; Julia Wilkinson, "A Chinese Magistrate's Fort" p62; James Saywell, "The Architecture of a Mini City" p116; Jon Resnick, "Popular Culture and the Walled City" p192; Lui Tai Lok (呂大樂), "A Triply Neglected City" p220; Cathie Breslin, "The World's Wickedest City" p264; Ian Lambot, "Myth and Reality" p270; Fionnuala McHugh, "The Walled City Clearances" p328. Acknowledgements p355, Credits p356. | "A Triply Neglected City" | contents page |
| p6 | Bio: Popham toured the City not long before its demolition. He is a foreign correspondent with *The Independent*, wrote *The Lady and the Peacock* (Aung San Suu Kyi) and *The City at the End of the World* (1985, about Tokyo). He lives near Regent's Park, London. | "Peter Popham toured the Walled City not long before its demolition." | editors' bio |
| p6 | No photo can show the full experience of a visit. | "no photographs can do full justice to what it was like to visit Kowloon Walled City" | own visit |
| p6 | The City had no thoroughfares and no vehicles, except a bicycle now and then. It had dozens of alleys. | "There were no thoroughfares in the City – and no vehicles except the odd bicycle – only dozens of alleys." | own visit (text of about 1992-93) |
| p6 | An alley entrance looked harmless from outside. The alley was at most four feet wide. At once it dipped and twisted, and the outside world was gone. | "The space was no more than four feet wide." | own visit |
| p6 | Inside it was dark and very damp. Nobody was able to stand upright, because plastic water pipes, many of them dripping, lined the roof of the alley. | "It was impossible to stand upright because the roof of the alley was lined with a confusion of plastic pipes carrying water" | own visit |
| p6 | The smells, in the order that he met them: damp under everything; incense burned outside homes; charcoal; rotting pigs' guts; sweet-and-sour cooking; raw and probably rotting fish; burning plastic from a factory; polish; incense again; mildew. | "smells of incense – burned outside homes – or charcoal, of putrefying pigs' guts, of sweet-and-sour cooking" | own visit |
| p6 | He calls it "the symphony of stinks". | "the symphony of stinks commenced" | own visit |
| p6 | The light was dim and deep green. Water dripped all the time on to the stone. | "The light was dingy at best, deep green: there was the endless spatter of water leaking on to stone." | own visit |
| p6 | One very bad small alley, wet and spongy underfoot, led to the gate of the Tin Hau Temple. A big rat hopped away. | "spongily wet underfoot, a big rat hopping off" | own visit |
| p6 | Wire netting covered the temple courtyard against the rubbish that people threw from upstairs windows. Old filth lay on the netting, and daylight came through it in spots. | "shielded from the rubbish routinely heaved out of upstairs' windows by wire netting" | own visit |
| p6 | He compares the City to a tropical rainforest: rich, varied and sensual, but toxic. | "The only drawback was that it was obviously toxic." | own visit (opinion) |
| p6 | He went with a guide. They climbed the stairs of an apartment block. | "My guide and I climbed and climbed the steps of an apartment block." | own visit |
| p6 | There was a postal service. The alleys and blocks had few numbers, so the postmen made their own system: rough, complex signs painted on each door. (Compare the postman Lam Po Chun (林寶鎮), pp144-149.) | "the postmen had devised their own system, roughly daubing complicated signs on each door as a reference" | own visit |
| p6 | Higher up the stairs, the smells were less, the air better and the light brighter. The roof was the only part of the City with space to spare. | "the only part of the Walled City where there was any space to spare" | own visit |
| p6 | From the roof the City looked like one lump of building. | "essentially a single lump of building" | own visit |
| p6 | Each block had its own history. The best blocks were copies of Hong Kong municipal blocks. Some had home-made rooftop annexes of brick, iron or plastic. | "Some had home-made annexes of brick or iron or plastic on the roofs" | own visit |
| p6 | The roofs touched so closely that a cat was able to go round the whole City at roof level. | "an agile cat could circle the City at that level without difficulty" | own visit |
| p6 | Refuse: the City never solved rubbish collection. Organic waste went somewhere. People carried the inorganic waste up to the roof and left it there: old TV sets, broken furniture, worn-out clothes, bedsprings. | "old television sets, broken furniture, worn-out clothes, bedsprings and the like – they lugged up to the roof and abandoned" | own visit |
| p6 | Among the heaps of junk on the roof, people hung washing between the many TV aerials. | "Washing was strung up between the thousand television aerials." | own visit |
| p6 | On the roof, small children played a game like hopscotch while old women watched them. Pigeons cooed. | "Small children played something like hopscotch under the eyes of elderly women." | own visit |
| p6-7 | About every 10 minutes a jumbo jet came down towards Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場), straight over the City and very low. | "every 10 minutes or so another jumbo jet descended on Kai Tak Airport – heading straight for the Walled City" | own visit |
| p6 (caption to the p7 photo) | Food factories, such as this barbecued-meat factory, were common in the City. | "Food factories, like this one preparing barbecued meat, were a dramatic yet utterly commonplace example of the Walled City's unregulated reality." | not given (caption) |
| p6 (caption) | The walls came down in the Second World War. The City's bad name hid a community of 35,000 residents from the rest of Hong Kong. | "this extraordinary community of 35,000 residents" | not given (caption) |
| p7 (photo) | The barbecued-meat factory: dressed whole pigs hang from the ceiling; large black drum ovens; pork strips hang on hooks; a red plastic basin of marinating meat on the floor; a tub of sliced meat; gas cylinders; red plastic buckets on hooks. One worker is bare-chested in rubber boots, one squats in flip-flops; both smoke. The floor is wet and dirty. | (no text) | photo (Girard, p356); my description |
| p7 | The pp6-7 description is Popham's earlier text: the City came down in 1993, "not long after I wrote those words". The City's name in Cantonese was Hak Nam, 'the City of Darkness'. | "In 1993, not long after I wrote those words, Hak Nam, 'the City of Darkness', was torn down." | own text |
| p7 | The colonial authorities wanted to remove the City for at least 50 years before they did it. | "what they had been itching to do for at least the previous 50" | not given |
| p7 | The Japanese removed the walls in the Second World War to expand Kai Tak Airport. | "the Japanese removed them to expand Kai Tak Airport during the Second World War" | not given |
| p7 | Its status as a Chinese enclave inside British Kowloon protected it. It had a name for squalor, crime and danger. Crime fell steadily over the years. | "though the quotient of crime diminished steadily over the years" | not given |
| p7 | Homeless refugees made the City, and it was never subject to an outside authority (his view). | "Nowhere else in the world have homeless refugees created something like this" | not given (opinion) |
| p7 | Popham says that the City is now more famous, discussed and mysterious than in its lifetime. While it stood, it was only a stain on the colony's name. | "merely the colony's most notorious blot" | not given (opinion) |
| p8-9 (caption on p9) | The spread photo shows Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道) in late afternoon sun. The caption calls it the richest side of the City. Outsiders reached it easily, thus many 'illegal' doctors and dentists set up there. | "This was by far the most affluent side of the City" | not given (caption) |
| p9 (caption) | Nearly all the signs in this photo are for dental clinics. | "the profusion of signs attest, nearly all of which are for dental clinics" | not given (caption) |
| p8-9 (photo) | My reading of some of the signs (small print; some characters can be wrong): 大衆診所 (the sign prints 衆); 西醫陳杰生; 譚樹輝牙科; 美斯鑲牙室 and 美斯鑲牙; 孫仰光牙科 and 孫仰光鑲牙; 黃景志 牙醫; 王偉權鑲牙室; 許天勳; 馬慶輝; 慈安醫所; 國際診所; 郭衛人醫師; 平民醫療所; 張廷華皮膚專科; 光明診所; 康寧診所; 富士牙科; 胡湘婦科; 莊天麗牙科. Each name except 大衆診所 is already in `docs/room_places.md`, some in a shorter form (for example 醫師郭衛人, 黃景志). | (signs in the photo) | photo (Girard, p356 "8/9") |
| p8-9 (photo) | Some signs give floors and numbers: a clinic sign "…西診所" with "32號三樓"; a dental sign "…源牙科" with "32號1樓"; 張廷華皮膚專科 with "35號二樓". The skin clinic sign also lists 全科 (general practice), 針灸 (acupuncture), 電療 (electrotherapy) and 驗孕即知 (pregnancy tests with a quick result). I cannot read its two right-hand columns with certainty (my reading). | "35號二樓" | photo (Girard); my reading |
| p8 (photo) | Other things in the street photo: a Coca-Cola sign over an ice-café (冰室) sign; an English sign "SHING TAK TRADING CO."; window air-conditioners and caged balconies with pot plants above; laundry; street lamps; rubbish bags on the pavement; red plastic buckets; a wide road with a car; people walking; a bare-chested man at a handcart. | "SHING TAK TRADING CO." | photo (Girard); my reading |
| p9 | In its best years the City had all the parts of a town: homes, schools, shops, workplaces, a temple, a shrine, a church, medical and dental clinics, pharmacies, places for rest, culture, sex and drugs, and help to quit drugs. | "temple, shrine and church; medical and dental clinics and pharmacies" | not given |
| p9 | About 35,000 residents had on average about 30 sq ft each. | "The average space occupied by its 35,000 or so residents was only about 30 square feet per person – barely room to lie down" | not given |
| p9 | The City's sport was pigeon racing. | "if you can count pigeon racing" | not given |
| p9 | The City had water, drains and electricity. For daylight you climbed 14 floors to the roof. | "there was even daylight if you were willing to climb 14 floors to the roof" | not given |
| p9 | Popham says that the City never had law or authority, up to the last day. Then the last resident left by force. | "the last resident was hauled kicking and screaming from his beloved home" | not given |
| p9 | The City had no law, authority, taxation or regulation; no courts or bailiffs. At one time it had many Triad thugs. | "no courts or bailiffs (though, at one time, more than enough Triad thugs)" | not given (opinion) |
| p9 | It was a Cantonese squatter colony like dozens of others in Kowloon in the colony's first 60 or 70 years. | "This was a Cantonese squatter colony, like dozens of others" | not given |
| p9 | Many residents had their roots in the Chiu Chow community of the Chinese coast. | "Many of the residents had their roots in China's coastal Chiu Chow community" | not given |
| p9 | In its people and culture the City was like the rest of Hong Kong. Only history and diplomacy made its growth unique. | "genetically and culturally it was of a piece with the rest of Hong Kong" | not given |
| p9 | Kowloon means 'Nine Dragons'. Imperial Chinese troops held the place as early as the 11th century, near the sea, against pirates. | "garrisoned by imperial Chinese troops as far back as the 11th century" | not given |
| p9-10 | In 1841, with the British threat, a new fort was built for the troops, with the yamen (衙門) at its heart: a simple complex of traditional buildings for the military bureaucracy. (See Doubts for the date.) | "In 1841, when the menace from Britain and other Western powers had become alarming, a new fort was built" | not given |
| p10 (caption) | A typical alley: above it, a tangle of water pipes and electricity and telephone cables. Additions to the buildings on each side project across the alley and shut out the daylight. | "an impossible tangle of water pipes and electricity and telephone cables" | not given (caption) |
| p10 (photo) | The alley photo: louvred wooden doors; cables and pipes in a mass overhead; litter on the floor. Painted on a door is "W5168"; another mark "W6…" is on the wall to the left. The book does not explain the marks. | (painted "W5168") | photo (Girard, p356); my reading at high zoom: "W5168" is on a panel above a louvred shutter |
| p10 | In 1899 British troops took the City, sent away the Chinese soldiers and officials, and occupied it. Queen Victoria claimed jurisdiction by a Royal Order in Council. Nobody asked China. | "Queen Victoria asserted jurisdiction over the Walled City by a Royal Order in Council" | Order in Council (not quoted) |
| p10 | A secret memo of the Governor to the Secretary of State in London called the claim safe for domestic purposes. | "for domestic purposes ... unchallengeable" | secret memo, Governor to Secretary of State (no date or reference) |
| p10 | The Attorney General, "five decades later", said that the claim was weak abroad. In his view, a trial in a Hong Kong court of an offence in the City invited a Chinese Communist challenge. | "would invite challenge by Chinese Communist interests" | Attorney General's opinion (no date or reference) |
| p10 | The City covered 6.5 acres. | "on this 6.5 acre plot" | not given |
| p10 | The authorities were not able to enforce the law in the City, for fear of a claim by Beijing. They also did not improve it to the standard of the colony. | "the authorities found their hands tied" | not given |
| p10 | Electricity: people stole part of the supply from the mains. It was a constant fire risk and cost the electric company money. | "the Walled City's supply was in part stolen from the mains and was a constant fire risk" | not given |
| p10 | A confidential memo said that big improvements (for example to the electricity) implied that the British accepted the situation for ever. | "that we accepted the present situation for ever" | confidential memo (no date or reference; "a few years after" the post-war attempt) |
| p10 | Britain's strongest attempt to control the City came at once after the War and ended in humiliation. The essay gives no date or detail. | "Britain's most determined attempt to get to grips with the City immediately after the War ended in humiliation" | not given |
| p10 | After a big fire near Shek Kip Mei in 1953, the Government built public housing for refugees. The Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme had big oblong blocks in rows, almost against the City's eastern edge. | "its uniformly big, oblong blocks, laid out like dominoes, almost rubbed up against the eastern edge of the Walled City" | not given |
| p11 | In the early 1960s the Government planned to expand the Tung Tau scheme westwards and demolish part of the City. | "a plan to expand the project westwards, demolishing part of the Walled City" | not given |
| p11 | The *Hong Kong Standard* reported that most residents agreed to resettlement and gave the population as 20,000-30,000. | "The majority of the 20,000 to 30,000 residents are willing to be resettled on government's terms" | *Hong Kong Standard* (no date) |
| p11 | The businesses opposed the plan: brothel owners, opium divans and heroin factories, and also legal trades. | "the weavers and noodle makers and plastic extruders whose profit margins risked being wiped out" | not given |
| p11 | Dentists, doctors and pharmacists from the mainland had no certificates to work in the rest of Hong Kong. Thus they opposed the demolition. | "the dentists and doctors and pharmacists from the mainland who lacked the certificates necessary to work in the rest of Hong Kong" | not given |
| p11 | In March 1962 they set up an anti-demolition committee and sent delegations to Beijing and Taipei. | "So in March 1962 they set up an anti-demolition committee" | not given |
| p11 | Taipei showed no interest. China's Foreign Minister called in the British chargé d'affaires. | "Communist China's Foreign Minister summoned the British chargé d'affaires" | not given |
| p11 | The Government issued eviction notices with deadlines. The New China News Agency in Hong Kong attacked the plan. | "issuing time-bound eviction notices" | New China News Agency articles (no date) |
| p11 | The dispute grew and made news round the world. Britain chose discretion: the Cold War was on and Mao's regime was unknown. | "The issue escalated, making headlines around the world." | not given |
| p11 | The Government shelved the plan. Nobody raised it again until 1986, two years after Deng Xiaoping and Margaret Thatcher met about Hong Kong's future. | "It was not to be tabled again until 1986" | not given |
| p11 | Popham calls the years after this the City's "Golden Age". | "the Walled City's Golden Age" | not given (opinion) |
| p11 | The committee's success led to the Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會) for residents. It became the real authority in the City, with the last word on property rights. | "having the last word on property rights and other issues arising within the community" | not given |
| p11 | The developers then built boldly. Apartment blocks of 10 to 14 storeys took the place of one- and two-storey shacks like those of the squatter colonies outside. | "apartment blocks of 10 or even 14 storeys" | not given |
| p11 | Some blocks were designed by Hong Kong municipal architects who worked on them in their own time. The blocks were like their day-job designs but had almost no foundations. | "some designed by moonlighting Hong Kong municipal architects" | not given |
| p11 | The blocks stood so close that at roof level a person was able to step from one to the next. | "at roof level you could step from one to the next" | not given |
| p11 | Nobody set standards for daylight, water or sewage. The lower floors were always dark. The City became almost one solid block. | "consigning their lower floors to permanent gloom" | not given |
| p11 (caption) | Lam Mei Kwong's dental clinic was large for the City. It was at 4A Lung Shing Road (龍城路). He had been at this address almost all the time since he came to the City in 1949. | "at 4A Lung Shing Road, an address he had occupied almost continuously since arriving in the City in 1949" | not given (caption) |
| p11 (caption) | The caption says that his dental equipment was possibly imported second-hand from Japan. It appears to date from the same period (about 1949). | "possibly imported second-hand from Japan, appears to date from that period as well" | not given (caption) |
| p11 (photo) | The photo: Lam stands in a flat cap, glasses, jacket and tie. Beside him is an old cream-coloured dental unit with a spittoon bowl, a lamp on an arm and a tray. A wall clock, a carved wooden chair, framed pictures, a tiled floor and a boarded ceiling with round panels. (Already in `data/furnishings.json`, F116.) | (no text) | photo (Girard, p356); my description |
| p12 | Two places stayed out of the solid block: the 19th-century yamen, "now" an almshouse and old people's home, and a temple near it. | "the 19th-century yamen, now an almshouse and old people's home" | not given |
| p12 | Popham's reasons: the yamen's official Chinese origins kept developers away from it. Superstition kept them away from the temple. | "likewise preserved by superstitious instinct" | not given (opinion) |
| p12 | Life on the lower, darker floors got worse and less hygienic. But civic life became more orderly. | "especially for those stuck on the lower, darker floors" | not given |
| p12 | A police survey of 1952 found 154 opium dens ('narcotic divans'), 11 brothels, seven gambling houses and 13 dog-meat shops. | "154 'narcotic divans' (opium dens), 11 brothels, seven gambling houses and 13 dog-meat shops" | police survey, 1952 |
| p12 | By March 1953 there were also 16 strip-tease theatres. The police called them more and more daring, and full to capacity. The authorities closed all of them in the end, and also the dog-meat shops (Popham gives no date; see the records table). | "each more daring than the other" ; "the police reported in disgust" | police report (quoted); the closures: not given |
| p12 | In the 1960s small retail drug sales stayed common. The opium dens closed one by one. The heroin refineries moved to places with fewer people, where the smell of the work drew less notice. | "the unmistakable stench of manufacture would attract less attention" | not given |
| p12 | In the 1960s the Hong Kong police patrolled the City every day. After the anti-corruption campaign in the police in the mid-1970s, they became effective. | "the Hong Kong police were patrolling on a daily basis" | not given |
| p12 | With safety from the police and the residents' association, village values took over: hard work, the extended family, mahjong and cards, festivals such as the Mid-Autumn (moon cake) Festival, respect for learning and religion. | "the love of mahjong and cards, the devotion to festivals such as the Mid-Autumn (moon cake) Festival" | not given |
| p12 | In 1975 developers built higher than before. Government officers ordered the top floors demolished under the Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) Act. The rest stayed. | "ordered the demolition of the topmost floors, citing the Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) Act" | not given |
| p12 | In 1973 the Government said that the Resettlement Department did not do clearance in the City. | "The Resettlement Department does not conduct clearance activities within the Walled City." | Government statement, 1973 |
| p12 | After an agreement with China that followed the Thatcher-Deng accords, the authorities first found out who really lived there and had a claim to rehousing. Then they tore the City down. | "after carefully establishing the identities of the people who actually lived there" | not given |
| p12 | It was the largest demolition in Hong Kong's history. | "It was the largest demolition in Hong Kong's history." | not given |
| p12 | Many people in Hong Kong thought that the City was gone years before. | "they thought the City had gone years before" | not given |
| p12 | Squatter shacks spread out from the City for many years. Before the demolition they were cleared. On two of the four sides a park took their place, with footpaths, cycle tracks and artificial grassy mounds. The City then rose straight from bare ground. | "footpaths and cycling tracks interspersed with artificial grassy hillocks" | own visit (in part) |
| p12 | In the final years, with strong policing, a visitor with a proper introduction was able to go in and look round. | "as long as you had a proper introduction" | own visit |
| p12 (caption to the p13 photo) | The west side was the richest part of the City: cleaner streets and fewer factories. | "This was considered the wealthiest part of the City, with cleaner streets and fewer factories than other areas." | not given (caption) |
| p12 (caption) | Folklore: two wells, one on the east side and one on the west, were the eyes of the City's guardian dragon. People filled in the east well. The dragon was blind on that side, thus good luck moved west. | "Folklore had it that this came about when a well on the east side of the City was filled in." | folklore (no teller named) |
| p13 (photo) | The west side in low sun: blocks of about 10-14 storeys with caged balconies, laundry and TV aerials; at the foot, a paved path with boulders, lamp posts and trees; two people walk on the path. | (no text) | photo (Lambot, p356) |
| p14 | The City was a self-contained, extra-legal community of tens of thousands. Each person thought of survival. Their needs: water, light, food and space. | "water, light, food and space" | not given |
| p14 | Water: the people sank 77 wells round the City, as in their home villages, to at most about 300 feet. | "sinking a total of 77 wells around the City, to a maximum depth of some 300 feet" | not given |
| p14 | Electric pumps lifted the well water to rooftop tanks. From there it went down through leaky pipes to the homes of subscribers. | "Electric pumps shot water up to tanks on the rooftops" | not given |
| p14 | Effluent from the factories spoiled the wells. The water came out in different colours. People used it only for bathing and laundry. | "residents never knew what colour it was going to be" | not given |
| p14 | Drinking water came from stand-pipes inside and outside the City. People carried it up the stairs in buckets; carriers did this for a fee. | "had to be lugged up the stairs in buckets, a service provided at a price" | not given |
| p14 | Electricity ran the water pumps and the lights of homes and alleys. People stole it from the mains. | "it was stolen from the mains" | not given |
| p14 | Fire was the worst hazard. After a serious fire in the 1960s, the electricity authorities came in with their meters. | "Only in the 1960s, after a serious fire (much the most terrifying hazard in the City), were the electricity authorities allowed in with their meters." | not given |
| p14 (photo) | A roof: a bare-chested man lies on a folding deck chair; washing on lines; a pot plant; a corrugated-iron hut with a barred door; on a lower roof, something laid out to dry on a tray; tangles of cables; TV aerials; blocks of 4-6 storeys round the roof. | (no text) | photo (Girard, p356); my description |
| p15 (caption to the p14 photo) | For upper-floor residents the roof was a quiet refuge with fresh air. The flats below had no windows. Children played and adults relaxed on the roof. The roof was angular, harsh and full of waste. | "escape the claustrophobia of their windowless apartments below" | not given (caption) |
| p14-15 | The City had industry of every kind. | "there was industry of every description" | not given |
| p15 | The doctors and dentists were competent but had no local certificates. Their small, neat clinics were along the north face, at street level and facing out, so that outside patients did not go into the alleys. | "They set up their neat little clinics along the north face of the City, at street level and facing out" | not given |
| p15 | Many outside patients came because the rates were low. | "their rates being very competitive" | not given |
| p15 | The best sites for the City's many restaurants were also on the outside, for the same reason. | "The sought-after sites for the City's numerous restaurants were also on the outside" | not given |
| p15 | Deep in the City were the religious places: the temple, spattered with rubbish, where gamblers asked and bribed the gods for luck; and the church for the Christian minority. | "the rubbish-spattered temple where gamblers came to implore and bribe the gods to improve their luck" | not given |
| p15 | The Salvation Army ran a school and a kindergarten in the City. | "the Salvation Army, for example, which ran a school and kindergarten" | not given |
| p15 | Jackie Pullinger (潘靈卓), an Englishwoman, came to the City as a teacher in the mid-1960s. She was a born-again Christian who believed that prayer cured heroin addiction. | "who came to the City as a teacher in the mid 1960s" | not given |
| p15 | Popham describes Pullinger as young and beautiful when she came. He says that she is still at work in Hong Kong. | "young and beautiful, with blazing eyes and a powerful jaw" | not given (his description) |
| p15 | She set up a youth club and worked with addicts and street sleepers until the demolition. She says that she helped more than 500 addicts. | "she claims to have helped more than 500 addicts to turn their lives around" | her own claim |
| p15 | Mahjong tiles clacked in the alleys. | "The alleys were alive with the clacking of mahjong tiles." | not given |
| p15 | Hundreds of racing pigeons lived on the roof in cages almost as big as some homes. | "in cages not much smaller than some of the City's homes, hundreds of racing pigeons cooed" | not given |
| p15 | An amateur Chinese orchestra played twice a week. Its music sounded through the alleys. (The contents list a Chiu Chow Music Club, p40.) | "There was an amateur Chinese orchestra, and twice a week their plangent, melancholy music echoed through the rancid alleys." | not given |
| p15 | In 1987 most residents accepted the clearance calmly, because the People's Republic agreed with it. | "in 1987 the great majority of residents accepted their fate philosophically" | not given |
| p15 | A few residents barricaded themselves into their homes. Most left quietly for municipal flats. | "A few residents put up resistance, barricading themselves into their homes." | not given |
| p15 | The authorities, and the mainland Chinese who were soon to get the colony back, were glad of the demolition. | "the City's demolition was a great relief" | not given |
| p15 | The crime film *Long Arm of the Law* used the City "As early as 1982". Its final 20-minute shootout was filmed inside the City. | "the final 20-minute shootout of which was shot inside the City" | not given |
| p15 | After the City became a traditional Chinese park, artists and designers in Asia and the West used its photos for ideas. | "started plundering the photographic record for ideas" | not given |
| p15 | The City inspired video games and Japanese and Chinese manga. Douglas Young used its look in his Goods of Desire stores and in installations. In 2005 the den called the Narrows in the first of the new *Batman* films copied its look. | "the villain's den, called the Narrows, was explicitly modelled on the look of the Walled City" | not given |
| p15 | Some of these copies were cheap and some very costly. The Japanese copies were the most careful. All of them saw the City as a picture of hell on earth. | "the Japanese versions were extraordinarily painstaking in their attention to detail" | not given (opinion) |
| p15 | Girard and Lambot began their photos "more than 20 years ago" (before about 1994). Popham visited at about the same time and wrote the Introduction to the first edition. | "when I visited it around the same time and set my own impressions down on paper in the Introduction to the first edition" | own visit |
| p16 (caption) | The photo of the south-west corner is of early evening in 1987. In front was empty ground. The Sai Tau Tsuen (西頭村) squatter settlement had stood there until a few years before. | "taken in 1987" ... "the sprawling Sai Tau Tsuen squatter settlement had stood until just a few years earlier" | not given (caption) |
| p16 (caption) | By 1993 the ground was a landscaped park. Many former residents of the City and other local people used it. | "frequented by many of the City's former residents as well as others living in the area" | not given (caption) |
| p16-17 (photo) | Dusk, 1987: most windows are lit with a cold white light, some warm yellow, one red. Caged balconies on every floor. TV aerials on the roofs. Lit ground-floor fronts. In front: bare earth, rubble and a row of fence posts; a small hut and a parked car. The white light suggests fluorescent tubes (INFERRED). | (no text) | photo (Girard, p356 "16/17"); my description |
| p16 | All three (Popham, Girard, Lambot) were most struck by the paradox: the City was built outside the law, but gave its residents a life worth living. | "it had somehow succeeded in giving its residents a life worth living" | own view |
| p16 | His 1993 verdict: the builders and residents made a city that changed with the needs of its users. It met every need from water to religion, and it felt like one large household. | "the city as 'organic megastructure'" ; "the warmth and intimacy of a single huge household" | Popham's Introduction, 1993 (quoted) |
| p16 | In the months before the demolition, two Hong Kong architects also saw the City as a good place to live for many thousands of residents. | "a highly satisfactory place to live" | not given |
| p19 | Suenn Ho, a Hong Kong-born American architect in New York, heard of the coming demolition. She got a Fulbright research grant and mapped the inside of the City floor by floor, block by block. | "Through floor-by-floor plans of contiguous blocks" | not given (see `primary-sources/mplus-suenn-ho-ca17/`) |
| p19 | Ho wrote that builders used every chance: they built on terraces and roofs, or into gaps between buildings, usually without the neighbours' permission. | "squeezing into the narrow spaces left over by neighbouring buildings, usually without permission from the neighbouring owners" | Suenn Ho's study (quoted) |
| p19 | Ho wrote that the City showed the power and efficiency of communal systems in high-density housing. | "demonstrates the potential power and efficiency of communal systems within the context of high-density housing" | Suenn Ho's study (quoted) |
| p19 | Ho wrote that communal living gave the residents a strong sense of bond and of need for each other. | "Communal living has created for the inhabitants an enormous sense of bonding and interdependency" | Suenn Ho's study (quoted) |
| p19 | Aaron Tan, an architect and colleague of Rem Koolhaas, explored the City before it was cleared. He called it poor but lively. | "as destitute as it is vivacious" | Aaron Tan's writing (quoted; no title) |
| p19 | Tan called the City a misfit that made itself without history, law or order to approve it. | "a misfit in the urban arena ... a site that generated itself in the absence of a validating history, law and order" | Aaron Tan's writing (quoted) |
| p19 | Tan explained the City's success with the 'rhizome' idea of Deleuze and Guattari (*A Thousand Plateaus*). | "the more flexible and ill-defined the spatial, temporal, material and social structures, the greater the stability of the structure as a whole" | Aaron Tan's writing (quoted) |
| p19 | Tan described the built structures as locked into each other and living off each other, with no order that a person can read. | "interlocking, parasitic, colliding, incoherent in the absence of structuration, impossible to decipher" | Aaron Tan's writing (quoted) |
| p19 | The buildings had very small foundations. They stood because their neighbours boxed them in. The City never had a serious collapse. | "buildings that stayed standing by the simple stratagem of being boxed in by their neighbours" ; "never suffered any serious collapse" | not given |
| p19 | Popham says that the City broke all the usual rules of architecture and engineering, with no hierarchy or zoning. But it worked and kept growing. Avant-garde architects (Archigram, Kisho Kurokawa, Richard Rogers, Rem Koolhaas) had wanted this for decades. | "a home-made megastructure that did all those things" | not given (opinion) |
| p19 | Tan called the British "nihilistic". Popham notes the irony that Tan saw: only the demolition made the outside world see the City's glory. | "as he called them" ; "in all its improbable glory" | Aaron Tan's writing; Popham's view |
| p19 (caption to the p18 photo) | People used the caged balconies to air bedding, dry washing, grow plants, keep birdcages and sit to look at the view. People called these cages 'buildings on buildings'. | "'buildings on buildings' – as the caged balconies were known" | not given (caption) |
| p19 (caption) | The next spread (pp20-21) shows the City from the south-west across the start of what became the Carpenter Road Park, with Lion Rock on the horizon. | "the beginnings of what was to become the Carpenter Road Park" | not given (caption) |
| p18 (photo) | Caged balconies on four floors: iron grilles with scroll patterns; corrugated sheets over each cage; laundry and bedding; a striped sheet; many pot plants on shelves; round woven trays; boxes. The lowest floor is painted pale blue, with a hanging plant. | (no text) | photo (Lambot, p356); my description |

### Part 2. Julia Wilkinson, "A Chinese Magistrate's Fort" (pp62–75)

Writer: Julia Wilkinson (essay; not dated, probably the 1993 edition, INFERRED); the editors (captions, later than 1993). Checked by a second agent (no date in the note): 135 rows checked, 20 fixed, 0 deleted, 5 added. 113 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 62 | Standfirst: the City was a slum of 6.5 acres. China and Britain both claimed it, but neither governed it properly. | "this 6.5-acre slum"; "a legal no-man's land" | not given |
| 62 | Salt, not vice, started the place. In the Sung Dynasty, north-east coastal Kowloon was a salt-field, one of several in the district of San On. | "It wasn't sin, but salt, which first gave the Walled City life." | not given |
| 62 | The salt-field's old name was Kuan-fu Ch'ang. The name Kowloon became official only in 1840. | "the vernacular name, was only officially adopted in 1840" | not given |
| 62 | Early in the Sung Dynasty, a small fort held imperial soldiers who controlled the salt trade. | "to house imperial soldiers who controlled the salt trade" | not given |
| 62 | In 1277 the fort probably held, for a short time, the court of the young Sung emperor in flight from the Mongols. | "'travelling palace'" | not given (the writer says "probably") |
| 62 | The locals called the fort Kau Lung Shing (Kowloon City (九龍城)). It stood just north-west of the settlement of Kau Lung Gai (Kowloon Street). | "called Kau Lung Shing (Kowloon City) by the locals" | not given |
| 62 | Kau Lung Gai became notorious for its gambling dens in the 1890s. | "notorious in the 1890s for its gambling dens" | not given |
| 62 | 1668: a watchpost with 30 guards. Later the garrison fell to 10. | "a watchpost was established on the site with a small garrison of 30 guards" | not given |
| 62 | 1810: a second fort near the coast. The site was a quarter of a mile from the sea, across the harbour from Hong Kong (香港). | "additional fort was built near the coast" | not given |
| 62 | 1841: Britain occupied Hong Kong Island. In 1843 China moved a deputy magistrate to Kowloon City, with the county's chief military officer and a garrison of 150. The magistrate was responsible for 492 villages. | "for 492 neighbouring villages"; "an increased garrison of 150 soldiers" | not given |
| 62 | The Viceroy of Canton proposed offices, barracks, training grounds and a stone wall. The wall was complete by 1847. | "proposal was to build a solid stone wall. By 1847 it was finished" | not given |
| 62 | The wall made the plain fort into the Kowloon Walled City. It was a visible sign of imperial power, aimed at the British in Hong Kong. | "symbol of imperial control to the barbarians in Hong Kong" | not given |
| 62 | The wall in 1898: granite ashlar facing, 15 feet wide at the top, 13 feet high on average. | "The wall is built of granite ashlar facing"; "averages in height 13 feet." | J.H. Stewart Lockhart, Colonial Secretary, survey of the New Territories, 1898 (quoted) |
| 62 | Plan: a rough parallelogram, 700 by 400 feet, 6.5 acres inside. | "A rough parallelogram"; "measured 700 feet by 400 feet and enclosed an area of 6.5 acres" | Lockhart 1898 (the writer's paraphrase) |
| 62 | An older arm of the wall ran from the northern corner up the rocky hill behind. By 1898 it was out of use. | "extension which ran from the northern corner up the rocky hill behind" | Lockhart 1898 (paraphrase) |
| 62 | The wall had six watch-towers, then used as family homes (the word is cut at the binding), four gateways, a granite parapet with 119 embrasures, and dozens of cannons (cut). | "It featured six watch-towers"; "four gateways, a granite parapet with 119 embrasures" | Lockhart 1898 (paraphrase) |
| 62 | In 1904 a newspaper called the place the City of Cannons: old broken guns lay everywhere. | "Kowloon might ... be styled the City of Cannons,"; "ancient dismantled guns." | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, 1904 (quoted) |
| 62 | Soon after 1847 the City got a yamen (衙門), the deputy magistrate's office. | "a yamen (office for the deputy magistrate)" | not given |
| 62 | A traditional paper-burning pavilion stood near the east gate. | "pavilion near the east gate" (the word before it is cut at the binding: "paper-bur") | not given |
| 62-63 | The Lung Chun Yee Hok (龍津義學), a communal school, opened in 1847. It was a moral defence against foreign influence and a meeting place for officials. | "founded in 1847 to act as a moral defence against barbarian influences" | not given |
| 62 | Caption: the plan is the Viceroy's proposal of 1847. Only a few of its military buildings were built. The Assistant Magistrate's Yamen was the only building that stood until the demolition of 1993. | "Only a few of the new military premises were built"; "the only building to survive through to the Walled City's demolition in 1993" | caption; the plan is a modern drawing, credit "Internet" (p356) |
| 62 | The plan's English labels: Barracks, Armoury, Parade Pavilion, Er Sheng Temple, "... Di Temple", Warehouse, Parade Ground, Major-General's Mansion, a gunpowder building, Military Officer's Yamen, a school ("Longj..."), Reflecting Wall & Kuixing Pavilion, Paper Burning Pavilion, and Civilian residence (two clear labels, along the south wall). A label in the centre is under glare (it starts "M" and ends "-en"). A third label at the bottom centre is under glare too; it starts "Ci", possibly a third "Civilian residence". | "Reflecting Wall & Kuixing Pavilion"; "Major-General's Mansion" | modern drawing (credit "Internet") |
| 63 | Caption: the 1898 view from Lung Chun Pier (龍津石橋) looks to Kowloon City and a pai lau (ceremonial arch) at the start of the main street. The walled building on the left is the Chinese Maritime Customs Station. | "pai lau, or ceremonial arch, that marked the beginning of the main street"; "The walled structure on the left is the Chinese Maritime Customs Station." | caption; photo credit The National Archives, UK (p356) |
| 63 | Photo detail: a stone pier runs to a gate building with a two-tier tiled roof. Three tall masts stand by it. Thatched sheds stand on the right; people stand at the gate. | (none) | my reading of the 1898 photo |
| 63 | By 1880 the Lok Sin Tong (樂善堂) (Hall of Willing Charity), a charity, gave free schooling and free medicine to the villages around. | "to distribute free education and free medicine to the surrounding villages" | not given |
| 63 | The Walled City had no market and no shops. It was a garrison town for officials and soldiers. | "The one thing the Walled City did not have was a market or, indeed, shops of any kind." | not given |
| 63 | 1898: a garrison of 500 and 200 civilians, mostly soldiers' dependants, reportedly under the military commander. | "In 1898 the garrison numbered 500 and the civilian population 200" | not given ("reportedly") |
| 63 | The area from the east gate to the shore, a quarter of a mile away, was the original Kau Lung Gai. By 1898 it was a busy market town. Villagers came from Shatin and Sai Kung. | "attracting villagers from as far as Shatin and Sai Kung" | not given |
| 63 | 1871: a provincial customs station against smuggling from Hong Kong, mainly of opium. 15 years later (thus about 1886, my arithmetic) the more important Chinese Maritime Customs Station replaced it. | "to prevent smuggling – especially opium – from Hong Kong" | not given |
| 63 | Lung Chun Pier: a stone jetty on 21 pillars, complete in 1875, 700 feet long. It let the mandarin reach his boat in style. In 1892 a 260-foot wooden extension was added. | "an imposing stone jetty supported by 21 pillars"; "In 1892 a 260-foot wooden extension was added." | not given |
| 63 | The two-storey Lung Chun (Mandarin Greeting) Pavilion stood at the head of the pier. It was a reception hall and a rest-house for travellers. A pai lau completed the formal entrance. | "an official reception area" | not given |
| 63 | 1854: Taiping rebels took the City, robbed its houses and took the livestock. The officials fled to Hong Kong Island and came back a week later. | "ransacking its houses and seizing livestock" | not given |
| 63-65 | The rebels left a garrison of 300. It quarrelled and broke up; within two weeks most had left. | "had already become quarrelsome and restless" | not given |
| 64-65 | Caption (p65) of the map (p64): the 1845 survey map marks a stone fort in the north-east of the Kowloon Peninsula. The British settled on Hong Kong Island and the tip of the peninsula in 1841. In answer, the Chinese made the fort the seat of a county magistracy and completed a new wall. | "Remarkably accurate for 1845"; "upgrading the fort to the seat of a county magistracy" | caption; map credit The National Archives, UK (p356) |
| 65 | The Triads were secret societies, first made to oppose Manchu imperial rule. 1884: the mandarin of the Walled City warned the British of a possible Triad rising and told them to be on guard. Nothing happened. The event showed the fear in Hong Kong of trouble in Kowloon. | "the republican secret societies which had originally been formed to oppose imperial Manchu rule"; "warned British authorities of a possible Triad uprising" | not given |
| 65 | Before 1898, the two sides worked together on law and order. An ordinance of 1850 sent Chinese fugitives in Hong Kong to the Kowloon officials. China was under no duty to return the favour, but it punished criminals harshly. | "required that Chinese fugitives in Hong Kong were handed over to Kowloon officials" | ordinance of 1850 (cited) |
| 65 | 1890: Hong Kong freed two pirates for lack of evidence. Kowloon arrested their associates, convicted them and beheaded them on the beach in front of the fort. British officials were invited to watch. | "beheaded on the beach in front of the fort"; "British officials were politely invited to witness the event." | not given |
| 65 | By then, people called both the Walled City and the town beside it "Kowloon City". The fort was a tourist sight for Hong Kong's Europeans from the 1850s. | "now confusingly known as Kowloon City"; "something of a tourist attraction" | not given |
| 65 | 1858: Reverend Krone, on San On district, had little to say about the fort. | "low walls and miserable forts of Kowloong [sic]" | Rev. Krone, 1858 (quoted) |
| 65 | 1893: a guide book sends picnic walkers to the City. A walk round the walls takes five minutes. One or two petty officers live there. The main trade is the gambling houses near the beach. | "curious and particularly dirty little Kowloon City"; "can be made in five minutes"; "You come, you see, you walk round." | *Hongkong Guide*, 1893 (quoted) |
| 65 | The guide says that the City does not keep the usual formalities of a garrison town. | "The formalities usually insisted upon in garrison towns ... are dispensed with here." | *Hongkong Guide*, 1893 (quoted) |
| 65 | The guide jokes that the wall cannons are dangerous to fire. | "The cannon on the walls suggest the idea that you would rather be a good way off when any attempt is made to fire them." | *Hongkong Guide*, 1893 (quoted) |
| 65 | After Britain took the Kowloon peninsula in 1860, the population of Kowloon City grew fast and its state declined. | "ceded to Britain in 1860" | not given |
| 65 | The walled fort kept its dignity as a centre of civil and military government. Inside the walls: good houses and wide streets with trees. | "an important centre of civil and military administration"; "of a very superior class"; "wide and commodious" | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, 1904 (quoted) |
| 65 | Outside the walls, the suburb was a slum with plague and narrow dirty streets. | "wretched agglomeration of Chinese hovels" | *The Hongkong Telegraph*, 1890 report (quoted) |
| 65 | The suburb held brothels, opium parlours and gambling dens. Many British and foreign people of Hong Kong gambled there. | "festering sore"; "favourite [gambling] rendezvous of a considerable number of the British and foreign community" | "the Hong Kong press" (no title or date) |
| 65 | Gambling was illegal in Hong Kong. The gambling houses gave free steam-launch rides from Hong Kong, with free coffee and cigars. | "with complimentary coffee and cigars on board" | not given |
| 65 | 1898: the Hong Kong General Chamber of Commerce wanted the City inside British borders to fight crime. | "The inclusion of this small city within British boundaries would greatly assist in the detection and repression of crime." | Hong Kong General Chamber of Commerce, 1898 (quoted) |
| 65 | Early 1898: Sir Claude MacDonald, British Minister in Peking, started talks to lease more land. Kowloon City was the main problem at once. | "Great difficulty is Kowloon City"; "necessity of withdrawing permanent officials being much resented." | MacDonald to the Foreign Office, 1898 (quoted) |
| 66 | Caption: the 1865 photo was taken from the small hill behind the fort and looks south. It shows the wall, public and private houses and the magistrate's compound. | "Taken from the small hill behind the fort, the view looks south" | caption; photo credit Arthur Hacker (p356) |
| 66 | Caption: the Kowloon peninsula was much more hilly then. Quarrying had started by 1865. Later it levelled the stone ridge in the distance; little sign of the ridge stays today. | "Quarrying, already visible in this image, has seen the jagged stone ridge in the distance levelled" | caption |
| 66-67 | Photo detail: two crenellated walls cross the photo, one near the camera and one further away. The far wall has an arched gate. Trees and a large tiled building stand between the two walls. Beyond the far wall are more tiled houses, trees, wide paddy fields, a small village and hills. A thin plume of smoke rises among the trees near the far wall. | (none) | my reading of the 1865 photo |
| 66 | Arthur Balfour of the Foreign Office asked if China wanted dignity or money. He offered money, or to leave the Chinese officials in the town under paramount British authority. | "Is it a question of dignity or money? Would a lease get over it?" | Balfour, telegram, 1898 (quoted) |
| 66 | MacDonald replied that the issue was face. The Tsungli Yamen (Chinese Foreign Office) said that the change of power was a cause of instability. | "saving 'face', a very important consideration with Chinese" | MacDonald, 1898 (quoted) |
| 66 | A belief: an emperor who lost a city was unable to enter the royal temple in heaven and face his ancestors' spirits. The writer gives this as a possible reason only. | "any Emperor who lost a city during his reign would be unable to enter the royal temple in heaven" | not given ("There may also have been some concern") |
| 66 | China refused to give up jurisdiction in "Kowloon City". In the talks this meant the Walled City only, not the suburbs. Britain limited it with a phrase about the defence of Hong Kong. | "by which they meant the Walled City, not the suburbs"; "except so far as may be inconsistent with the military requirements for the defence of Hong Kong" | not given; the phrase is quoted |
| 66 | MacDonald said that the Tsungli Yamen fought hardest on this point. He expected the City to come under British rule soon. | "showed the greatest determination"; "It is not to be supposed that the City of Kowloon will long remain outside British jurisdiction" | MacDonald, after the final draft of the lease, 1898 (quoted) |
| 68 | Caption: the paper-burning pavilion stood on the east side of the City, near the south-east gate. People burned written paper there with respect. | "The written word was greatly revered in China and its disposal demanded due respect." | caption; photo credit Hong Kong Museum of History (香港歷史博物館) (p356) |
| 68 | Photo detail: a pavilion with curved ridge roofs, balustrades and a central stone piece; two men stand in front; a wall on the right; a boulder hill behind. | (none) | my reading of the photo (undated) |
| 66-68 | The Peking Convention for the Extension of Hong Kong was signed on 9 June 1898. Its Kowloon City clause let the Chinese officials stay, and kept the landing-place for Chinese ships and officials. In the rest of the leased land, Britain alone had jurisdiction. The writer says that the vague words made later problems worse. | "the existing landing-place near Kowloon City shall be reserved for the convenience of Chinese men-of-war, merchant and passenger vessels"; "Great Britain shall have sole jurisdiction" | Peking Convention for the Extension of Hong Kong, 9 June 1898 (quoted) |
| 68 | The Hong Kong press and business were angry about the clause. The Chamber of Commerce said that Chinese rule kept alive the "moral danger" of the notorious suburbs. | "moral danger"; "could not fail to exercise a malign influence on the minds of the natives" | Hong Kong General Chamber of Commerce (quoted) |
| 68 | A British journal wanted the officials out for order and sanitation. | "The Chinese officials must go, and the sooner they go, the better for all concerned." | "A British journal" (no title or date) |
| 68 | The convention started on 1 July 1898. Britain took formal possession only in April 1899. | "The convention was to take effect on 1 July 1898." | not given |
| 68 | Caption: a second view from further back along Lung Chun Pier. The Walled City was a quarter of a mile inland, at the far end of Kau Lung Gai, at the foot of the small hill (the words are cut at the binding). | "A second view of Kowloon City"; "Kowloon City's main" | caption; photo credit The National Archives, UK (p356) |
| 68-69 | Photo detail: the pier is of long stone slabs. Boats lie on the mud. A row of houses lines the shore, with a two-storey building with verandas. A line like a wall runs up the hill behind. | (none) | my reading of the photo (undated) |
| 69 | 1899: villagers and clan leaders resisted the takeover. Governor Sir Henry Blake asked the Viceroy of Canton for protection. The old Viceroy disliked the British, but he sent 300 troops at once. | "ample protection"; "immediately complied and sent down 300 troops" | Blake's request (quoted) |
| 69 | 3 April 1899: a police party that guarded workers on the mat-sheds for the ceremony was attacked. A further 600 troops came. | "erecting 'mat-sheds' for the official possession ceremony"; "a further 600 troops were sent" | not given |
| 69 | Fighting went on, and some Chinese died. The Union Jack went up on 16 April, one day early. | "the Union Jack was hurriedly raised on 16 April, one day earlier than planned" | not given |
| 69 | British residents were sorry to miss the ceremony. | "a pleasant little outing and an opportunity for indulging in an outburst of patriotic fervour" | *The Daily Press*, 1899 (quoted) |
| 69 | The public demanded that the Chinese officials leave the Walled City. | "demanded the expulsion of Chinese officials from the Walled City as reparation" | not given |
| 69-70 | The demand suited the Colonial Office. It wanted to end the City's awkward status, which it had always hoped was temporary. It needed an excuse, and the Viceroy gave it one. | "it was the Viceroy himself who provided it" | not given |
| 70 | The day before the flag-raising ceremony, the Viceroy sent 600 more troops: half to the Walled City, half to the border town of Shum Chun. | "half had gone to the Walled City, and half to the border town of Shum Chun" | not given |
| 70 | The Governor suspected that the Viceroy had a part in the well-organised disturbances. He asked for all Chinese troops and officials to leave, except customs officials. | "removal of all Chinese troops and officials from leased territory, except Customs officials" | the Governor's request (quoted) |
| 70 | The Tsungli Yamen promised to instruct the Walled City. The Colonial Office proposed to blockade the garrison and starve it out. The British Government hesitated. By 14 May 1899, with 200 soldiers still inside, it agreed to take the City. | "by 14 May, with 200 soldiers still holding out" | not given |
| 70 | Caption: in the early 1920s the City was a small rural place of a few hundred people. Most buildings were in decay. | "a small rural community with a static population of only a few hundred" | caption |
| 70 | Caption: the cannon were already a tourist sight in the early 1920s. The two last cannon lay by the entrance of the yamen on Lung Chun Back Road (龍津後街) for 45 years after the War. In 2014 they are a tourist sight again in Kowloon Walled City Park. | "the two remaining cannon survived the War and 45 years lying by the entrance to the yamen on Lung Chun Back Road" | caption (2014) |
| 70 | Photo detail: four boys in pith helmets stand and sit on two long cannon. A man crouches beside them with a helmet in his hands. Behind is a plastered wall with a decorated roof. | (none) | my reading of the photo, early 1920s; credit Hong Kong Museum of History (p356) |
| 70 | Caption: a small alley off Kau Lung Gai, about 1910. Kowloon City was a market place but run down, and known for nightlife and gambling houses in the late 1800s (the words are cut at the binding). | "City, circa 1910"; "run down and had a disreputable" | caption; credit Hong Kong Museum of History (p356) |
| 70 | Photo detail: men in tunics, one with a carrying pole; a balustraded upper floor; cloth awnings over the shops. | (none) | my reading of the photo |
| 71 | Caption: a draft map of 1923 shows the City and the suburb from the south-east gate to Kowloon Bay. Private houses lined the south wall. The military buildings were in decay. | "There were still a number of private residences in the City at this time, lined along the south wall" | caption; map credit Survey and Mapping Office (測繪處) (p356) |
| 71 | Map detail: inside the wall, a group of large compound buildings stands in the middle. Rows of small house plots line the inside of the lower wall. Outside, dense rows of narrow house plots spread to the right and below. A dark, irregular band runs from the lower wall to the bottom edge. The map prints no legible labels. | (none) | my reading of the 1923 draft map |
| 70-71 | Two days after 14 May (thus 16 May 1899, my arithmetic), British troops attacked. The operation was supposed to be secret, but a large crowd watched on the beach. 100 Hong Kong volunteers and 200 Royal Welch Fusiliers landed at Lung Chun Pier with Maxim-guns. | "despite the supposed secrecy of the operation"; "100 Hong Kong volunteers and 200 Royal Welch Fusiliers disembarked at Lung Chun Pier" | not given |
| 71 | The troops went through the suburb's dark streets and the open south gate. The Chinese soldiers had gone. | "evil-smelling streets"; "what did they find? Nothing. The Viceroy's soldiers had gone." | the street words are a quote; source not given |
| 71 | Only the mandarin and about 150 people were inside. The British took some arms, raised the flag, fired a 21-gun salute and left a small force. | "Only the mandarin, protesting loudly, was there to greet them, and about 150 inhabitants." | not given |
| 71 | A newspaper said the soldiers enjoyed the trip. | "was immensely enjoyed by all who took part in it." | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, 1899 (quoted) |
| 71 | The inhabitants left by junk a few days later. The garrison's officers refused to leave without orders from the Viceroy. | "the Walled City's inhabitants left by junk a few days later" | not given |
| 71 | The Viceroy and the Tsungli Yamen protested. Lord Salisbury refused to give the City back to Chinese rule. He said that the Chinese garrison gave no protection and added danger. | "the worthlessness of the protection"; "it is impossible for Her Majesty's Government to allow the resumption of Chinese authority within the walls of that City" | Salisbury to the Chinese Minister in London, 1899 (quoted) |
| 71-72 | The Order in Council of 27 December 1899 made the City part of the Colony. Its reason: the Chinese jurisdiction did not fit the military needs of the defence of Hong Kong. | "part and parcel of Her Majesty's Colony of Hong Kong ... as if it had originally formed part of the said Colony" | Order in Council, 27 December 1899 (quoted) |
| 72 | 1900: the British put the New Territories' first Land Court in the Lung Chun school building. Otherwise they left the City alone. China did not set up a new administration. | "establishing the New Territories' first Land Court in the City's Lung Chun school building in 1900" | not given |
| 72 | 1904: the City was empty and sad inside. | "nothing but desolation" | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, 1904 (quoted) |
| 72 | The Government did not want more Chinese criticism. Thus it gave only short leases inside the City, for public uses, and many church charities came. | "only prepared to grant short-term leases inside the City for public purposes" | not given |
| 72 | The Protestant church ran schools and charities. The Anglican Holy Trinity church made the old San Sheng (Three Saints) Temple into a chapel, with services twice a week. | "converted the former San Sheng (Three Saints) Temple into a chapel and held twice-weekly services" | not given |
| 72 | The church made former offices into an old people's home, a school and an almshouse. | "An old people's home, school and almshouse – all run by the church – were converted from former offices" | not given |
| 72 | 1905: the Lands Office left the Lung Chun school. It became a free secondary school and a public dispensary. | "it became a free secondary school and public dispensary" | not given |
| 72 | Tourists came again to see and photograph the City. | "a little bit of Old China" | quote, source not given |
| 72 | Two huge cannon near the south gate were the only cannon not broken up or sold to metal dealers after 1899. | "two huge cannon near the south gate" | not given ("apparently") |
| 72 | One inhabitant sold copies of the City's old stone inscriptions to visitors. | "One inhabitant capitalised on this tourist trade by selling copies of the Walled City's historical stone inscriptions to visitors." | not given |
| 72 | The reclamation of Kowloon Bay put the City further inland. New roads, private housing and Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場) closed in on it. | "steadily encroached upon by new roads, private housing developments and Kai Tak Airport" | not given |
| 72 | 1920s: the south wall had fallen and Lung Chun Pier was out of use. The mandarin's office and many of the 60 houses were nearly ruins. | "by the 1920s its south wall had crumbled and Lung Chun Pier had fallen into disuse"; "many of the 60 domestic buildings were practically in ruins" | not given |
| 72 | Thousands of Chiu Chow (潮州) squatters kept pigs and poultry on the hills around. Many moved into the City. | "thousands of Chiu Chow squatters raised pigs and poultry; many of them had even filtered into the City itself" | not given |
| 72 | The Hong Kong authorities wanted to be rid of the awkward and dirty place. 1933: the Government planned to pull down the houses, pay the 436 squatter-residents and make a park of historic interest. | "this awkward and insanitary place once and for all"; "compensate the 436 squatter-residents"; "a place of popular resort and antiquarian interest" | 1933 Government plan (quoted) |
| 72 | The residents protested and asked China for help. The Government then also offered a new house for each family. | "a new house for each family was offered" | not given |
| 72 | George Kennedy-Skipton, District Officer South, ran the move. In 1970 he said the offers made people move of their own will, against the efforts of the Nationalist Foreign Minister, who came from Canton. | "despite resistance from the Nationalist Chinese Minister for Foreign Affairs who made a trip from Canton to dissuade them from moving" | Kennedy-Skipton, memory recorded in 1970 (quoted) |
| 72 | One resident kept making trouble and China kept protesting, but the move went on. | "With one recalcitrant stirring up trouble" | not given |
| 72 | By 1940 only three buildings stood: the Lung Chun school, the yamen (later an old people's home) and one private house. | "the yamen – later to be used as an old people's home – and one private house" | not given |
| 72 | The writer says that the clearance was the Government's first taste since 1899 of China's claim to jurisdiction. It was also its last success there without much conflict. | "its last taste of success in achieving its aims relatively peacefully" | not given |
| 72 | The park was never made. | "much to Kennedy-Skipton's dismay" | not given |
| 72 | In the Second World War the Japanese pulled down the wall and used its stone to extend Kai Tak Airport. | "the stone being used, ignominiously, to extend Kai Tak Airport" | not given |
| 72 | The line of the old wall stayed in people's memory. | "where they had once stood was still ingrained in people's memories" | not given |
| 73 | Caption: in 1924 the City and its land were a rural backwater with paddy fields. That year part of Kowloon Bay next to Kowloon City was reclaimed; Kai Tak Airport opened there three years later. | "still considered a rural backwater at this time"; "which three years later became home to Kai Tak Airport" | caption; photo credit Public Records Office (歷史檔案館), HK (p356) |
| 72-73 | Photo detail: a line like a wall runs up the rocky hill behind the City. A row of houses stands in front of a crenellated wall. Poles stand in the field. | (none) | my reading of the 1924 photo |
| 74 | No caption. The left half of one colour photo across pp74-75: a City facade close up, with rows of metal window cages with patterned bars, pot plants, laundry, wires and poles, corrugated awnings, open casement windows and an aircon box. | (none) | photo, Ian Lambot (credit "74/75", p356); the book gives no date |
| 75 | No caption. The right half of the same photo: the tall facades of the City against the sky, with cages and TV aerials, and a plain block with a grid of windows at the right. Trees and a pole stand below. At the bottom right is a billboard for "金裝良友" cigarettes with a pack marked "Good Companion". A vertical strip at its left reads "清記士" (the rest is cut). | "金裝良友"; "Good Companion" | photo, Ian Lambot (credit "74/75", p356); the book gives no date |

### Part 3. James Saywell, "The Architecture of a Mini City", first half, with its captions (pp116–129)

Writer: James Saywell (essay, 2014); the editors (captions). Checked on 2026-11-10: 112 rows checked, 9 fixed, 0 deleted, 13 added. 112 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 116 | The writer, James Saywell, is an architect and writer. In 2014 he had lived and worked in Hong Kong for 15 years. He edits the design magazine *Hinge*. | "has lived and worked in Hong Kong for 15 years" | standfirst (the editors) |
| 116 | He did not see the City. His family lived in Hong Kong for one year when he was a boy. | "but he was too young to visit the Walled City" | standfirst (the editors) |
| 116 | The Chinese Government first kept the Walled City as a small military post inside the colony. | "a minor military outpost within Hong Kong" | not given |
| 116 | The City lay inside the British colony, but it was kept apart from the colony on purpose, in politics and in law. The essay does not say who kept it apart. | "it was deliberately kept apart from it politically and jurisdictionally" | not given |
| 116 | State of the City at the end of the Second World War: the wall was gone. The yamen (衙門), the old magistrate's quarters, stood. Most other buildings were derelict or demolished. | "its wall gone and, apart from the original Chinese magistrate's quarters, the yamen, with most of its other buildings derelict or demolished" | not given |
| 116 | Two groups filled the City after the war: refugees from China, and Hong Kong people who looked for a low cost of living. Immigration rules were hard to apply there. | "immigration regulations couldn't easily be enforced" | not given |
| 116 | Reputation: crime and "licentiousness" made the City look like an island in the colony. Outside law had little force, and the people made their own rules. | "external laws and regulations could largely be ignored and rules were self-imposed" | not given |
| 116 | Outsiders used the City often. They kept flats there, used its businesses, and went to drug dens, prostitutes and pornographic cinemas. Then they went home. | "keeping flats there, patronising businesses, using drug dens or prostitutes or pornographic cinemas" | not given |
| 116 | Other people did everything inside the City: home, work, school and old age. They had little contact with the rest of the colony. | "lived and worked (and studied and retired) entirely within the Walled City environs" | not given |
| 116 | Caption: the pressure to grow started in 1947, but early growth was small. In 1950 large areas of waste land remained. | "large areas of waste land still in existence in 1950" | caption; photo by Mak Fung, 1950 (credit p356) |
| 116 | The 1950 photo shows boys at play on bare ground. Behind them stand low buildings with painted Chinese signs and a hill. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 116–117 | Caption: as late as 1962 the buildings had only two or three storeys. | "buildings restricted to two or three storeys as late as 1962" | caption; photo by Ko Tim Keung (高添強), 1962 (credit p356) |
| 117 | The 1962 photo shows a two-storey and a three-storey building on a dirt road. Things hang to dry on a frame on the roof. A sign says "DANGER 危險". Cars, a van and a bicycle use the road. The repo reads a school sign, 龍城學校, on the near building (fact C52). | — | photo (my reading); `data/room_research.json` C52 |
| 116 | Caption: a handwritten contract of 14 December 1960. Wong Shek Yin sold two buildings in the City to Law Wun Cheung for $2200. The caption calls the contract "typical". | "A typical handwritten contract, dated 14 December 1960" | caption; the contract (Kowloon Walled City Residents' Association, credit p356) |
| 117 | For the people who stayed inside, the City gave all that they thought they needed. | "a self-contained 'city' which provided all that they perceived they needed" | not given |
| 117 | The main argument of the essay: the City was a double of Hong Kong. Hong Kong, too, was an anomaly: it stood in large part on leased land with a fixed end date. Each had a fixed area and grew upward. | "a close parallel to or doppelgänger of Hong Kong's own status and development" | the writer's argument |
| 117 | Housing supply: residents took the flats that developers supplied, of any quality. The City had almost no building rules. | "in the Walled City almost none" | not given |
| 117 | In Hong Kong, developers had more control than they had a right to, the writer says. Government policy in Hong Kong favoured developers strongly. | "exercised more control than they were probably entitled to (a fact true to the present day)" | not given |
| 117 | Property prices in the City rose because the prices round it rose. While the City stayed cheaper than the Hong Kong market, it was able to take the rising costs. | "As long as it could lag below the general Hong Kong property market as a cheaper alternative, it could absorb rising costs." | not given |
| 117 | As people left their first poverty, the City grew for them. Its area was fixed; thus the only direction left was up. | "it grew like a balloon being squeezed, wherever it could" | not given |
| 118 | Taller buildings started to give a profit in the late 1950s and the early 1960s. From then they were the usual type. | "once profit margins could be achieved on taller structures during the latter part of the 1950s and early '60s, they predominated" | not given |
| 118 | How the money worked: the owner of a small building sold the right to develop the site. Early owners got a profit from the growth of Hong Kong. | "the chance to benefit from selling one's development rights proved to be a potent incentive" | not given |
| 118 | Before this change the City was an ordinary squatter settlement, the writer says. | "a more or less standard squatter settlement" | not given |
| 118 | Hong Kong building heights by period, for comparison: walk-ups of five or six storeys until the 1960s; lift towers of 20 to 30 storeys until the 1980s; then 40 and 60 storeys from the 1990s. | "five- or six-storey walk-ups until the 1960s, 20- to 30-storey towers with lifts until the 1980s" | not given |
| 118 | City building heights by period: one to three storeys until the 1960s; then six to nine storeys; then up to 14 storeys by the end of the 1970s. | "from six to nine storeys initially, then to as many as 14 storeys by the end of the 1970s" | not given |
| 118 | The early low buildings were handmade. | "handmade one- to three-storey buildings" | not given |
| 118 | The flight paths of Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場) set the height limit. The writer calls this possibly the only real limit on building in the City. | "possibly the only meaningful hindrance to the city's renegade development experience" | not given |
| 118 | The writer calls the City a smaller and rougher twin of Hong Kong, with the same urban and architectural "DNA". | "its smaller, scruffier twin that shared virtually identical urbanistic and architectural DNA" | the writer's argument |
| 118 | Caption: the building boom ran through the 1960s. The average height rose from four or five storeys in 1960 to 14 storeys and more by 1972. | "from four or five storeys in 1960 to the final limit of 14 storeys and more by 1972" | caption; photo by Government Information Services (政府新聞處), 1972 (credit p356) |
| 118 | The 1972 photo shows two towers in scaffolding, higher than the finished blocks beside them. Sheds and overgrown open ground lie in front. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 118–119 | Caption: by 1976 a building stood on each free plot. | "with every available plot of land built upon" | caption; aerial photo by Government Information Services, 1976 (credit p356) |
| 119 | The 1976 aerial photo shows the City as one solid block of towers. Low squatter huts fill the ground on one side. Long public housing slabs stand on two other sides. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 120 | Caption: in 1950 a woman draws water from a well in old Kowloon City (九龍城), just south-east of the Walled City. The caption starts in the middle of a sentence, "1950 and a woman ..."; its first words are not on the page. | "a well located in the area of old Kowloon City, just to the south-east of the Walled City" | caption; photo by Mak Fung, 1950 (credit p356) |
| 120 | The 1950 photo shows a woman who draws water from a wide round well with a stone rim. Buckets and a wooden tub stand near brick houses. A man wrings a cloth. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 120 | Caption: by the late 1980s the City had one natural ground well left. It dated from the nineteenth century. It was in an alley off Tai Chang Street (大井街).
