The caption translates the street name as "Big Well". | "the only remaining natural ground well in the Walled City by the late 1980s" | caption; photo by Ian Lambot (credit p356) |
| 120 | The colour photo of this well shows a low round concrete ring in a paved yard. Red plastic buckets stand beside it. A window with a grille looks on the yard. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 120 | The City kept an unclear political status almost until the return of Hong Kong to China. The Hong Kong Government did not take full control, because its few attempts failed. | "never felt confident exercising full supervision there because the rare attempts to do so had not met with success" | not given |
| 120 | The City's own borders kept the British administrators back. | "The British custodians of Hong Kong were kept at bay, so to speak, by the enclave's own borders." | not given |
| 120 | What the residents wanted: better services, most of all fresh water, electricity and rubbish removal. | "particularly fresh water, electricity and rubbish removal" | not given |
| 120 | What the residents did not want: taxes, and a larger reform of the City. | "such as property and income taxes" | not given |
| 120 | When the Government planned even a partial demolition, the residents appealed to China's legal authority over the City. The writer says that this turned the British efforts aside with good effect. | "appealed to that authority quite effectively in order to deflect the British efforts" | not given |
| 120 | An irony that the writer notes: many residents had fled the mainland government, and then used it against the colony. | "when presented with a method to offset 'colonialist oppression'" | not given |
| 120 | The Government stayed nervous about China's position until 1987, the year of the decision to demolish. | "right up until the decision to demolish it was conclusively taken in 1987" | not given |
| 120 | Nobody stated what China accepted. People in the City used this doubt. | "one Government could be played against the other" | not given |
| 120–121 | British fears: a rising in the City that forced Beijing to support it; and a lasting Chinese presence in the City or in Hong Kong. | "anything from a physical military or police presence to some form of jurisdictional body to arbitrate disputes" | not given |
| 121 | Thus the British avoided all action that drew China's attention. They feared a loss of British influence. | "lest it yield less British influence instead of more" | not given |
| 121 | Thus the Government looked away while the problems were bearable. Criminals and developers saw this and used it. | "various criminal elements and developers" | not given |
| 121 | The writer calls the City's place outside the rules the base on which the City was built. | "This extra-regulatory status had been the foundation stone upon which the City was built" | not given |
| 121 | The Government's worries: safety, health, hygiene and crime, and a disaster that put its agencies in a bad light. | "the growing risk of major fire, building collapse, epidemic outbreak and so on" | not given |
| 121 | A trap for the Government: action looked like colonial force, and no action looked cruel after a disaster. The writer calls it "a Catch-22". | "colonialist overreach, authoritarian and provocative" | not given |
| 121 | The writer says that the later, higher blocks gave the Government good reason to worry. | "the new blocks represented a disaster waiting to happen" | not given |
| 121 | Caption: from 1950 the Government gave water to all squatter settlements through stand-pipes. In most areas the stand-pipes went by the late 1970s. | "water was supplied to all squatter settlements via Government stand-pipes" | caption; photo by Mak Fung, 1950 (credit p356) |
| 121 | The 1950 photo shows girls who fill containers at a street tap. Water runs over the ground. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 121 | Caption: in the City the stand-pipes stayed the only source of clean drinking water until the last person left in late 1992. | "the sole source of clean drinking water in the City until the last person moved out in late 1992" | caption (no source given) |
| 121 | Caption: about 50 private artesian wells went down below the City between 1950 and 1990. | "Some 50 or so privately owned artesian wells were sunk beneath the City between 1950 and 1990" | caption (no source given) |
| 121 | Caption: how well water reached a flat. Most wells filled tanks on the roof. Pipes ran down from the tanks to many flats. | "most filling tanks on the rooftops from where pipes would lead down to any number of apartments" | caption (no source given) |
| 121 | Caption: the diagram on p121 gives a deepest well of 300 metres. The caption says that this is too deep. Few wells reached 150 metres, and most were less deep. | "the maximum well depth of 300 metres is almost certainly an exaggeration" | caption; diagram credited to University of Waterloo and four names (p356); the p140 caption calls them "architectural students and tutor" |
| 121 | The diagram shows a section through the City with four well shafts of unequal depth. Only the deepest shaft has a label. | "300m" | diagram, University of Waterloo (a later study, not a record of the time) |
| 122 | A second legal trap: an order to owners to make a building safe was an acceptance of their ownership. That ownership had almost no legal basis. | "an acceptance of ownership that had little or no legal basis" | not given |
| 122 | The writer says that such action also sets a legal precedent. It shows that the Government accepts building that was illegal from the start. | "that the Government approved of that which was not legal to begin with" | not given |
| 122 | The writer says that the unregulated building went on, and that the people with the least to gain from reform led it. | "led by the very people with the least to gain from reforming their ways" | not given |
| 122 | Builders and owners knew this. The Government did nothing about the first tall buildings of the 1960s. After that, people built more and higher. | "ambitious inhabitants felt freed to ramp up their activities and build with impunity, denser and higher" | not given |
| 122 | How the builders built: fast, cheap and large, with as little money as possible for extras. | "quickly, cheaply, larger, and with the least expenditure on 'amenities' that cost extra" | not given |

| 122–123 | The 1968 photo shows low shops of one to three storeys with signs at the corner. Cars park on open ground in front. A street with stalls and awnings runs along one side. Behind are parked cars, a lamp post, a bus, low shops and blocks, and a hill. | — | photo (my reading); Ko Tim Keung, credit "122/123" (p356) |
| 122 | Caption: large fires were rare in the later years, although the risk was plain. The caption shows a fire of 1970. | "major fires in the later years, such as the one shown here in 1970 (below), were surprisingly rare" | caption; photo by Government Information Services, 1970 (credit p356) |

| 125 | Caption: the street grid of Kowloon City lay further south. The new Tung Tau public housing estate (東頭邨) stood to the north-east, just completed. | — (no exact quote) | caption |
| 125 | Caption: the Government planned to extend the estate over the north-east corner of the City, and to demolish that corner. It dropped the plan quietly in 1963, after China objected to interference in land that China held to be Chinese. | — (no exact quote) | caption (no source given) |
| 127 | Caption: the official policy was no interference at all, but the authorities watched the City closely. | "one of total non-interference" | caption (no source given) |
| 127 | Caption: the police patrolled the City from the early 1950s and made arrests. Few cases went to court. | "and even making arrests – although few cases were taken to court" | caption (no source given) |
| 127 | Caption: by 1962 an agreement let China Light and Power (中華電力) do and check all electrical work in the City. | "allowing China Light and Power to carry out and monitor all electrical installations within the City" | caption (no source given) |
| 127 | Caption: the reason for the agreement was the fire risk. The City was crowded, had little water, and gave little access to fire-fighting equipment. | "with little access to water or, indeed, for fire fighting equipment" | caption (no source given) |

| 127 | Caption: the Sai Tau Tsuen settlement lay round the City on three sides through the 1960s. On the west side it stayed well into the 1970s. | "on the western side (as seen here), well into the 1970s" | caption |
| 127 | Caption: how the tall blocks came. Developers bought the many small shacks and buildings, or made a deal with the owners. Then they built larger and often taller buildings. | 127 | Caption: in 1968 many small plots remained, most of all on the east side of the City. | "particularly on the eastern side of the City, still persisted" | caption; the 1968 map |

| 129 | Hong Kong population, for context: about 600,000 at the end of the war; about one million at the start of 1946; about two million four years later. | "Within four years, the population doubled to around two million, the vast majority refugees from China" | not given |
| 129 | Squatter settlements grew all over the colony. | "across the slopes backing Kowloon, and on parts of Hong Kong Island" | not given |
| 129 | Why squatters chose the Walled City area: it was near the old Chinese community of Kowloon City, and it had some electricity and water. | "already had access to some electricity and water" | not given |
| 129 | The post-war City started as one part of the larger Sai Tau Tsuen squatter settlement. | "it started life as just an extension of the larger Sai Tau Tsuen squatter settlement" | not given |
| 129 | In 1947 the Government tried to clear the Walled City area and failed. This fixed the special status of the City. | "when the Government tried to clear the Walled City area, without success, in 1947" | not given |
| 129 | Through the 1950s the City still looked like a joined part of Sai Tau Tsuen. | "a contiguous part of Sai Tau Tsuen" | not given |
| 129 | How the land filled: the first or the strongest person took each free space, and put up a building fast to hold the claim. | "available space was claimed by the earliest or strongest force around" | not given |
| 129 | The writer calls a building the strongest form of claim to land. | "the most durable statements of claim" | not given |
| 129 | The legal status kept the outline of the City after the stone walls went. | "its legal status protected its boundaries after physical walls were not there to do so" | not given |

| 129 | Population in 1949: about 2000 residents. | "in 1949 the City held only some 2000 residents" | not given |
| 129 | Population growth: 8000 more people in the 1950s, about 17,000 more in the 1960s. The sums give about 10,000 in 1960 and about 27,000 in 1970. | "The 1950s added 8000 people, the 1960s some 17,000 more" | not given |
| 129 | Population peak: an estimate of 40,000 in 1980. | "by 1980, at its peak, the City housed an estimated total of 40,000" | not given ("estimated") |
| 129 | The writer calls the years after the war and after 1949 a turning point for the City. In those years the new Communist rulers of the mainland and the colonial Government set their relations. | "when the relationship between the newly installed Communists on the mainland and the colonial Government of Hong Kong was being defined" | not given |
| 129 | The City did well because the standard of living rose outside it. | "the Walled City prospered in reaction to the rising standards of living outside it" | not given |
| 129 | Money, and the lack of it, drove building in the City, the writer says. | "Money – and the lack of it – fed development." | not given |
| 129 | Most residents did not think of the City as a slum. | "most of its residents did not consider it a slum" | not given |
| 129 | The writer compares the City with the slums of Mumbai and the favelas of Rio de Janeiro: it lived from the larger city. | "functioned as a parasitic organism dependent on the larger body of Hong Kong" | the writer's argument |
| 129 | The City was always cheaper for a home and for a business. It took in people who did not keep up with the Hong Kong economy. | "Always a less expensive place to live and do business in" | not given |
| 129 | At its end the City was among the densest settlements in the world. | "it was one of the densest urban settlements on the planet" | not given |
| 129 | The early phases of the City had building types that the writer calls predictable. | "predictable building typologies" | not given |
| 129 | The first buildings went up on the principle of squatters' rights, on spots that nobody planned. The sentence continues on p130. | "constructed on the principle of squatters' rights, with random construction on spots" | not given |
| 128–129 | Caption: an aerial view of 1949 (p128). The area had far fewer buildings than in the view on pp124–125, which the caption dates 12 years later. | "taken just 12 years later" | caption; aerial photo by Survey and Mapping Office, 1949 (credit p356) |
| 128 | The 1949 aerial photo shows a loose group of low roofs with open ground and paths. Farm plots lie on the south-west side. The street grid of Kowloon City is at the bottom. | — | photo (my reading) |
| 129 | Caption: in 1962 the land south-east of the City still had few buildings. One building there was a remnant of old Kowloon City from the nineteenth century. | "a remnant of old Kowloon City, dating back to the nineteenth century" | caption; photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1962 (credit p356) |
| 129 | The 1962 photo shows an old house with a tiled roof and sheds on rough ground. Behind them are low buildings, one block in construction and a bare hill. | — | photo (my reading) |

### Part 4. James Saywell, "The Architecture of a Mini City", second half, with its captions and the later studies (pp130–143)

Writer: James Saywell (essay, 2014); the editors (captions); Urbanus (都市實踐), the University of Waterloo, Adolfo Arranz (later studies). Checked on 2026-11-16: 189 rows checked, 20 fixed, 0 deleted, 12 added. 178 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 130 essay | In the early phases, people built at random on each free spot. The person who came first took the land. The sentence starts on p129. | "of available land by whoever got there first" | not given |
| 130 essay | The alleys and passages grew with no plan. They became the fixed layout of the City. This layout stayed until the demolition. | "Alleyways and passages evolved – unplanned – into the established 'map' of the City" | not given |
| 130 essay | In the early phases the City had a basic electricity supply. Illegal connections often overloaded it. | "A basic electricity supply existed, increasingly burdened by illegal connections" | not given |
| 130 essay | In the early phases, a few stand-pipes gave all the water. | "a few stand-pipes supplied the only water" | not given |
| 130 essay | In the 1960s, the usual house changed from two or three storeys to six or seven storeys. The cause was the growth of the residents and of the businesses. | "made the leap from two- to three-storey residential structures to taller, six- to seven-storey ones" | not given |
| 130 essay | The taller buildings were more complex. They used more labour, reinforced concrete and more money. | "required greater labour to realise, reinforced concrete, more investment and so on" | not given |
| 130 essay | The taller buildings needed a different way of life. People carried the water up to the higher floors by hand. | "Water had to be carried up to the higher floors by hand." | not given |
| 130 essay | People also carried propane gas canisters up by hand. The gas was the fuel to cook and to heat water. | "Likewise the propane gas canisters that furnished fuel to cook or heat water." | not given |
| 130 essay | No architect and no engineer took part in the new buildings. The buildings had no proper foundations and no piles. | "Erected without architectural or engineering participation, proper foundations or piling" | not given |
| 130 essay | The builders used materials of doubtful quality. They did not follow the usual mechanical and electrical standards. | "they used materials of dubious quality, ignored conventional mechanical and electrical standards" | not given |
| 130 essay | The new buildings had no proper routes, no fire escape, little daylight and little fresh air. They had no proper water supply and no waste disposal. | "lacked proper circulation and fire egress, access to daylight or fresh air, water supply or waste disposal" | not given |
| 130 essay | Nobody did sufficient maintenance on the buildings after the construction. | "certainly didn't enjoy adequate maintenance once constructed" | not given |
| 130 essay | The builders used each free space. Title deeds, plot limits and regulations did not stop them. | "free from the normal constraints of title deeds, property limits and regulations" | not given |
| 130 essay | Each new building adapted to the conditions of its own site. Saywell calls the new buildings inventive outlaws of architecture. | "They were inventive, renegade architectural specimens." | the writer's opinion |
| 130 essay | The builders obeyed one rule each time: the height limit for Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場). The essay says that this limit applied to all of Kowloon. | "The only constraint observed with consistency was the Kai Tak Airport height limit that existed for the entire area of Kowloon." | not given |
| 130 essay | Saywell guesses the reason. Each developer knew that a building above the limit forces a negative response from the Government. | "Presumably even the most unscrupulous developer realised that to challenge this would be counterproductive" | the writer's guess ("Presumably") |
| 130 essay | Saywell calls the airport rule the "golden height limit". Builders tried all other things. | "Aside from this one 'golden height limit', almost anything was worth a try." | not given |
| 130 essay | In the 1950s the City still had small gardens and animal pens at ground level. Concrete structures and alleys soon replaced them. | "the small gardens and animal pens still present in the 1950s" | not given |
| 130 essay | The daylight at ground level became less each month while the buildings grew. | "At ground level, daylight shrank month by month." | not given |
| 130 essay | A "developer" was any person with nerve, money and some knowledge of simple building work. | "anyone with gumption, funds, and access to knowledge about rudimentary building construction" | not given |
| 130 essay | A developer made an offer to the existing "owner" of a house. The owner gave the old two-storey house. In return, the owner got larger units or more units in the new building on that site. | "they might trade their current two-storey structure for larger or more units in new, more modern buildings" | not given |
| 130 essay | If possible, a developer joined the plots of neighbours. The larger footprint gave a larger tower and more profit. | "grouped sellers' adjacent properties together to produce a larger footprint" | not given |
| 130 essay | Thus the buildings of the City became larger in height and also in width. | "a step up in scale not only in height but also in breadth" | not given |
| 130 caption | The page shows a newspaper clipping of 1963 about the sudden tall buildings. See "Quoted records" for its contents. | "A clipping from the Hong Kong Standard of 11 January 1963, noting the sudden appearance of tall buildings in the Walled City." | *Hong Kong Standard*, 11 January 1963 |
| 130 caption | The editors correct the newspaper. By 1963, buildings of four or five storeys had replaced most of the first houses of one and two storeys. This change came in the 1950s. | "had long since been replaced by four- or five-storey structures during the 1950s" | the 1963 photo of Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道) on p131 |
| 131 photo | My reading of the 1963 photo of Tung Tau Tsuen Road. It shows buildings of three to five storeys with shops at street level. One shop has a Coca-Cola sign. Laundry hangs from the upper floors. A small car stands at the kerb. | (no caption on p131; the p130 caption names the photo) | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1963 (credit, p356); primary, see the primary `SOURCE.md` (C52–C61) |
| 131 photo | My reading of the signs in the 1963 photo. The repo gives these names: 楊永光牙科 and 楊永光鑲牙 (a dentist), 濟寧堂 (a medicine shop), 魏漢文牙科 and 魏漢文鑲牙 (a dentist), 醫師李志青 (a doctor; the sign says 二樓, an upper floor), 醫師方濟成 (a doctor) and 宏圖電器工程 (an electrical engineering firm; its name is painted high on a building). | (signs in the photo) | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1963; `data/room_research.json` (C56–C61) |
| 131 essay | The City had no master plan and no regulations. Thus unusual routes went through the City. They went up and down through adjacent structures. | "unconventional circulation routes started to weave through the City, rising and descending through adjacent structures" | not given |
| 131 essay | New work took over the staircases of older buildings. | "Existing staircases were co-opted" | not given |
| 131 essay | A new building often closed the windows of the building beside it with a wall. | "windows in adjacent buildings were ignored and walled over" | not given |
| 131 essay | Upper floors stuck out over the alleys. Some floors touched the floors on the other side of the alley. | "floors were cantilevered over alleys, sometimes until they touched those across the way" | not given |
| 131 essay | The roofs became a public place. They had potted plants, children at play and adults at rest. | "The roofscape of the Walled City became its own public realm, with potted plants, playing children, reposing adults" | not given |
| 131 essay | People moved across the roofs from building to building with ease. The postman also used the roofs. | "with easy lateral circulation for all – even the postman" | not given |
| 131 essay | The builders did not always demolish a low building. They put more storeys on top of it. Sometimes the taller building next door built these storeys. | "simply had additional storeys built atop them, occasionally even by taller buildings next door" | not given |
| 131 essay | On the upper levels, the corridors of different buildings joined. People went from building to building and did not go down to the ground. | "corridors were connected to allow people to traverse different buildings without returning to ground level" | not given |
| 131 essay | A person on the move in the City did not know where one building stopped and the next started. | "it became difficult to know when you had crossed a 'property line' between one building and the next" | not given |
| 131 essay | The residents who knew the routes moved through the joined buildings with ease. | "who navigated the interwoven circulation system of the City fluidly" | not given |
| 131 essay | Saywell's view: this "organic" growth became one of the most distinctive features of the City for architects and urbanists. | "This extraordinary 'organic' growth became one of the Walled City's most distinctive and interesting characteristics" | the writer's opinion |
| 131 essay | No authority objected to the mix of public and private property. The residents had no reason to object, because the system was efficient. | "residents had no motive to do so, because it was actually a very efficient system" | not given |
| 131 essay | Saywell's view: only need and opportunity made the City grow. | "it grew by necessity and opportunity alone" | the writer's opinion |
| 132 caption | The building boom went on through all the 1960s. | "The building boom continued throughout the 1960s" | not given |
| 132 caption | By 1972, buildings of nine storeys filled almost all of the City. The editors date the start of these buildings to 1963. | "the nine-storey revolution begun in 1963 had all but taken over the whole of the Walled City" | the 1972 photo (pp132–133); the 1963 clipping (p130) |
| 132 caption | In 1972 a few of the older buildings of four and five storeys still stood on Tung Tau Tsuen Road. | "A few of the older four- and five-storey buildings remained along Tung Tau Tsuen Road" | the 1972 photo (pp132–133) |
| 132 caption | In 1972 the newest buildings on the north side had 12 storeys and more. The editors call this the maximum height for the north side. | "rising 12 storeys and more – the maximum height possible for buildings on this, the northern side of the City" | the 1972 photo (pp132–133) |
| 132–133 photo | My reading of the 1972 photo of the north side. One tall tower on the right stands in scaffolding. The photo does not show the material of the scaffolding. | 132–133 photo | My reading: one low building among the taller blocks has a pointed roof with stripes. The book does not say what it is. | (no words) | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1972; primary |
| 132 essay | Saywell compares the City with Hong Kong. Routes in Central (中環) also pass through public and private ground. Hong Kong towers also put services, shops, recreation and flats in one block. In this sentence, "the City's" probably means Hong Kong (my reading). | "Hong Kong found a way to 'civilise' a version of the same urbanistic phenomenon" | the writer's opinion |
| 132 essay | Saywell's view: the buildings of the Walled City showed the same things as the buildings of Hong Kong, but in a messier way with no plan. | "concentrated the same phenomena in a messier, unplanned but otherwise quite similar manner" | the writer's opinion |
| 132 essay | In 1963 the local authorities tried to clear a part of the north-east corner of the City and to evict its people. | "An attempt in 1963 by local authorities to clear and evict a section of the north-east corner of the Walled City was halted" | not given |
| 132 essay | The Chinese Government objected, and the authorities stopped the 1963 clearance. | "after objections from the Chinese Government" | not given |
| 132 essay | The stop showed that Britain had no power in the City. Fast building followed. | "this explicit denial of British power within the Walled City opened the floodgates to rampant development" | not given |
| 132 essay | In 1963 the residents made the Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會). | "That same year, the local Kai Fong Association was formed by the residents" | not given |
| 132 essay | The Kai Fong Association had two first aims: to answer the British eviction, and to judge property claims. | "in part to help face the challenge of the British eviction effort and in part to arbitrate property claims" | not given |
| 132, 135 essay | The building boom made the property claims more frequent and more urgent. | "undoubtedly increased in number and urgency thanks to the building boom" | the writer's inference ("undoubtedly") |
| 134 plans | The page shows two plans of the building footprints, for 1962 (top) and for 1987 (bottom). The credit on p356 gives William McElhinney. The book does not give the date of the drawings or their base maps. | (caption below) | William McElhinney (credit, p356) |
| 134 caption | The editors give the City a life of 45 years. They do not give the first year and the last year. | "The grain of the Walled City changed dramatically during its 45-year life." | not given |
| 134 caption | In the late 1940s people built their own shacks of one or two storeys. | "self-built one- or two-storey shacks that began to appear in the late 1940s" | not given |
| 134 caption | In the 1950s these shacks were rebuilt as structures of four or five storeys. The caption does not say who rebuilt them. These structures still made up most of the City in 1962. | "subsequently rebuilt as four- or five-storey structures during the 1950s" | McElhinney plan of 1962 |
| 134 caption | In 1962 a few taller buildings stood, most of them in the western half of the City. | "A few taller buildings were beginning to appear at this time, particularly in the western half of the City" | McElhinney plan of 1962 |
| 134 caption | Each of these taller buildings of 1962 stood on the site of two, three or four old huts. | "their footprints occupying sites where two, three or four huts had once stood" | McElhinney plan of 1962 |
| 134 caption | In 1962 the City had much more than 650 separate buildings. | "the City still comprised well over 650 individual buildings" | McElhinney plan of 1962 |
| 134 caption | In the 1970s and the 1980s, builders tore down groups of small buildings. One larger building replaced each group. | "groups of smaller buildings torn down and replaced by single larger ones" | not given |
| 134 caption | In 1987 the City had about 300 buildings, less than half of the 1962 number. | "that number had more than halved to around 300" | McElhinney plan of 1987 |
| 134 plans | My reading of the two plans. The yamen (衙門) compound is the large block in the centre of each plan. The 1962 plan shows many small plots and open yards.
