The book does not explain the shades. | (drawing) | William McElhinney (credit, p356) |
| 135 essay | By the time of the building boom of the 1960s, the population of the City was more than 15,000. The essay says "by this point", after a passage about 1963; it gives no exact year. | "The population by this point had surpassed 15,000 people." | not given |
| 135 essay | Saywell's view: by 1963 the City had too many people for a life with no rules at all. | "Complete anarchy was no longer sustainable." | the writer's opinion |
| 135 essay | The Kai Fong Association became the working authority for property disputes and for property sales in the City. | "the de facto authority for property disputes and transactions in the City" | not given |
| 135 essay | The stamp of the Kai Fong Association on a property record was the only accepted proof of ownership. | "established its stamp as the sole recognisable proof of ownership on property records" | not given |
| 135 essay | Soon the Kai Fong Association also had the oversight of other services. | "it also assumed responsibility for oversight of other services as well" | not given |
| 135 essay | The Kai Fong Association spoke with the Hong Kong Government for the residents. | "for negotiations with the Hong Kong Government on the residents' behalf" | not given |
| 135 essay | The Kai Fong Association stayed important until the demolition. | "It proved a vital organisational body right up to the demolition of the Walled City." | not given |
| 135 essay | More building and more density made the living conditions worse. The weak utilities got more load. | "put yet more pressure on inadequate infrastructure" | not given |
| 135 essay | The water supply and the water quality became worse. The drains were too small and were often blocked. Power cuts became regular. | "Water supply and quality deteriorated, drainage was insufficient and often blocked, electrical outages became regular" | not given |
| 135 essay | The Government and the utility companies made real efforts to correct these problems. | "despite genuine outside efforts by the Government and the utility companies to address the problems" | not given |
| 135 essay | Two things stopped the authorities: new pipes and cables were almost impossible to fit, and people made illegal connections all the time. | "frustrated by conditions that made retrofitting near impossible and by the constant illegal connections made to the systems" | not given |
| 135 essay | People dug private wells below the City and sold the water for profit. | "Private water wells were also dug under the City (and the water sold for profit) with no thought of the consequences." | not given |
| 135 essay | The authorities added one more water stand-pipe. The supply stayed too small for the demand. | "The authorities did add an additional water stand-pipe, but the supply couldn't keep pace with the growth in demand" | not given |
| 135 essay | The higher and denser City made each change to the utilities more difficult. | "it became ever more difficult to rationalise and modernise the utilities anyway" | not given |
| 135 essay | The Government had a problem with no answer. Better conditions bring more growth, and the Government did not want more growth. | "it wanted to improve conditions without encouraging further growth" | not given |
| 135 essay | The maze of the City also made improvements difficult for the Government. | "because of the puzzling maze the Walled City had become" | not given |
| 135 essay | By the 1970s the City had its full form, with buildings of up to 14 storeys. | "By the 1970s, the City had filled out to its maximised form, with buildings of up to 14 storeys high" | not given |
| 135 essay | By the 1970s almost no daylight came to ground level. The centre of the City was the exception. | "virtually no ground level daylight penetration save at its centre" | not given |
| 135 essay | An estimate gives seven square feet for each person. The essay does not name the estimate. | "Its density was estimated to have reached a mere seven square feet per person." | an estimate that the writer does not name |
| 135 essay | The yamen area stayed low while all other parts grew tall. | "The yamen area had somehow remained an exception to the vertical development" | not given |
| 135 essay | In 1949 a missionary society leased the yamen. It used the yamen as an almshouse and as a home for old people. The essay does not name the society. | "leased to a missionary society in 1949 for use as an almshouse and old people's home" | not given |
| 135 essay | The yamen was the only large open space in the City where a person saw the sky. | "the sole substantial void within the Walled City, with visible sky above it" | not given |
| 135 essay | A small temple near Lo Yan Street (老人街) also stayed. The essay does not name it. On p241 the book names the Tin Hau Temple (天后廟), on an alley off Lo Yan Street. | "A small temple near Lo Yan Street also survived" | not given |
| 135 essay | People in the towers dropped rubbish on the temple. The rubbish covered it, and almost no sky showed above it. | "all but buried under the rubbish dropped from surrounding towers to the point where skylight was virtually invisible above it" | not given |
| 135 essay | In the 1980s builders filled the last empty plots. | "the last remaining 'missing teeth' in the urban block of the Walled City were filled in" | not given |
| 135 essay | From outside and from above, the City of the 1980s looked like one mass. | "it had coalesced into an almost continuous mass, especially when seen from without or above" | not given |
| 135 caption | The editors compare the City with Shibam Hadhramaut in Yemen, an oasis town of the sixteenth century. About 500 tower houses of mud brick stand inside its walls. They have up to 11 storeys. | "around 500 mud-brick tower houses – rising up to 11 storeys high – crammed into the town's defensive walls" | not given; photo by George Steinmetz/Corbis (p356) |
| 135 caption | The editors give the City a population of 35,000. | "the Walled City's population of 35,000 pales into insignificance" | not given |
| 135 caption | The slums of Mumbai and the favelas of Rio de Janeiro hold more people than the City. But they stay low and wide, and they do not get the density of the City. | "these settlements tend to stay low and spread sideways, never achieving anything like the density found in the Walled City" | not given; photo of Rio from Corbis (p356) |
| 135–136 essay | Saywell describes the City of the 1980s as a living body. Routes of different sizes, water pipes and electric cables ran through it. | "circulation arteries of different sizes, plumbing and electricity veins streaming through it" | the writer's image |
| 136 essay | The outer walls had windows of irregular sizes and places. The stucco and the concrete had bad maintenance and stains. | "irregular fenestration, badly maintained stucco and concrete walls" | not given |
| 136 essay | Many things hung on the outer walls: balconies, cages, extensions, racks and air-conditioner units. | "all manner of balconies, cages, extensions, racks and air-conditioner units clinging to it like moles and other growths" | not given |
| 136 essay | Some buildings looked as if they leaned on the buildings beside them. Some were very narrow. Many had no clear party walls. | "Some buildings seemed to lean against their neighbours, some were impossibly narrow, many were undefined as to their party walls." | not given |
| 136 essay | The last towers were the least safe of all the buildings in the history of the City. | "These final-stage towers were undoubtedly the most precarious, if inventive, of all in the Walled City's history." | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | The builders said that the towers were safe. They said that they followed the same building regulations as the builders outside the City. | "Their builders claimed they were safe, that they mimicked all the same building regulations of structures outside the City in Hong Kong." | the builders' claim; the essay gives no source (compare the 1963 clipping, p130) |
| 136 essay | Saywell thinks that the claim is mostly true. His reason: freelance workers from outside "designed" or built many of these buildings, or contractors did. These contractors had experience of legal projects elsewhere. | "'designed' and/or erected by people freelancing from outside, or by contractors with experience of legitimate projects elsewhere" | the writer's inference ("probably mostly true") |
| 136 essay | No building in the City fell down. | "And after all, none ever did collapse." | not given |
| 136 essay | The builders worked at the lowest standard that still held. | "they were built at the very knife-edge of minimum standards" | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | Profit was the only aim of the last towers. | "They were constructed purely as profit machines." | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | Saywell thinks that the buildings held each other up when the City became one mass. | "buildings held each other up, or at least stabilised the entity as a whole" | the writer's inference ("probably true") |
| 136 essay | His comparison: in a full subway car at rush hour, it is hard to fall down, because there is little space to fall. | "Rather like a full subway car at rush hour, with bodies packed skin to skin" | the writer's image |
| 136 essay | He adds a limit to the comparison: a tower can still fall down into its own footprint. | "a tower can collapse vertically into itself" | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | Saywell's view: the buildings depended on each other for space and also for stability, up to a limit. | "its structures became parasitic not only in terms of space, but of stability" | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | Saywell asks if the City was a good test of a city that grows by itself with no rules and no plan. He also asks if it was a "Mad Max" world. | "a good laboratory test-case for urban self-growth, absent regulation or planning" | the writer's question |
| 136 essay | Saywell's view: the City made an early form of government. It used rules that nobody wrote down. | "forming a proto-government, using unwritten laws instead of written ones" | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | Saywell's view: the City worked as a democracy and as a pure market economy. | "functioning as a true democracy (certainly a true market economy)" | the writer's opinion |
| 136 essay | An outsider did not see the order of the City. The residents understood it. | "Its coherence, which may not have been obvious but was understood by its inhabitants" | not given |
| 136 essay | Saywell's view: the City worked better in practice when its parts joined together. | "It grew more workable in a practical sense as it became internally integrated." | the writer's opinion |
| 136 caption | In recent years, architects studied the City to find lessons for usual projects. | "seeking lessons that might be applied to more mainstream architectural projects" | not given |
| 136 caption | The Chinese firm Urbanus studied bridges between buildings. The bridges make informal routes at many levels. This is a design study, not a record of the City. | "how buildings might be linked by bridges to create informal routes at any number of levels" | Urbanus (image credit, p356) |
| 136 image | My reading of the Urbanus image: a colour model of a dense block. Red, yellow and blue bridge strips and green roof gardens cross above grey blocks. | (caption above) | Urbanus (credit, p356: 136, top left) |
| 136 image | The lower right image on p136 is a white model of a tower by Suenn Ho (credit, p356). A cut in the model shows the floor slabs. A box wing sticks out on one side near the top. My inference: it is the model of the building on p137, because the p137 caption speaks of "plans and a model". | (no caption on p136; see p137) | Suenn Ho (credit, p356: 136, bottom right); the link to p137 is my inference |
| 137 essay | Saywell's view: with time the City became a community that worked rather well. | "it matured into a fairly functional community machine" | the writer's opinion |
| 137 essay | Saywell's view: the City was a truly local growth with no plan. Thus architects and urbanists, who learn the benefits of planning, find it fascinating. | "It was by then a truly indigenous physical entity, which is why it has fascinated architects and urbanists" | the writer's opinion |
| 137 essay | Saywell's view: the City looked so much like the buildings of Hong Kong that it was possibly an uncomfortable mirror for Hong Kong. | "Its very similarity to the outside architectural development may have been an uncomfortable mirror on Hong Kong" | the writer's opinion |
| 137 essay | The building materials of the City changed from granite in the first buildings to plain concrete in the later ones. Hong Kong outside changed in the same way. | "passing from early granite buildings to later structures of banal concrete" | not given |
| 137 essay | Saywell's view: this way to build had no future. A collapse, a fire or a different disaster was sure to come. | "collapse or fire or some other form of disaster was inevitable" | the writer's opinion |
| 137 essay | The authorities decided that they had no more time to wait. | "The authorities felt they could wait no longer" | not given |
| 137 essay | The Government announced the clearance on 14 January 1987. | "on 14 January 1987, the clearance of Kowloon Walled City was announced" | not given |
| 137 caption | The architect Suenn Ho made an architectural study of the City in 1992. | "who undertook an architectural study of the City in 1992" | Suenn Ho's study |
| 137 caption | Suenn Ho found one building that grew over the roofs of its lower neighbours while it went up. | "one particular building that had gradually colonised the rooftops of its lower neighbours as it rose" | Suenn Ho's study, 1992 |
| 137 caption | She showed this building with floor plans and a model. | "which she explored through plans and a model" | Suenn Ho's study, 1992 |
| 137 drawing | My reading of the plans. They have the labels "Level 0", "Levels 1 - 5", "Levels 6 - 7", "Level 8", "Levels 9 - 10" and "Level 11". All levels share one stair core with two stairs side by side. | (labels on the drawing) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992 (credit, p356); primary |
| 137 drawing | My reading, continued. Levels 0 to 5 have two rooms beside the stairs. Levels 6 and 7 have three rooms, with one long new wing. Levels 8 to 10 have four rooms. Level 11 has about eight rooms on passages. | (drawing) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 137 drawing | My reading, continued. At Level 8 a short second stair joins the new part to the stair core. Thus the floors of the two parts are probably not at the same height. | (drawing) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary. The height is my inference. |
| 137 caption | A second study of Suenn Ho is about one alley. Many shops and factories opened directly onto the alley, and most kept their doors open. | "because they opened directly onto the alley and, for the most part, kept their doors open" | Suenn Ho's study, 1992 |
| 137 caption | The open doors made the narrow alley feel much more open. | "significantly increased the feeling of openness in what might otherwise have been a very confined, even claustrophobic space" | Suenn Ho's study, 1992 |
| 137 drawing | My reading of the alley study: three plans of one long alley. The alley runs from one end of the drawn strip to the other and bends at the bottom. Plan 1 shows the rooms on the two sides. Plan 2 shows the alley alone as a thin black band. Plan 3 adds the open rooms to the band, which becomes much wider in many places. | (drawing; the alley has no name) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 138 caption | Suenn Ho grew up in Hong Kong. She is the daughter of the architect Tao Ho (何弢). | "the daughter of celebrated architect Tao Ho" | not given |
| 138 caption | She studied architecture at Columbia University in New York. | "studying architecture at Columbia University in New York" | not given |
| 138 caption | In late 1991, in New York, she first heard of the coming clearance and demolition. | "in late 1991, she first heard that Kowloon Walled City was due to be cleared and demolished" | not given |
| 138 caption | A telling detail: people in Hong Kong did not talk about the City, but the City was news on the other side of the world. | "somewhere no one in Hong Kong would talk about was suddenly considered newsworthy on the other side of the world" | not given |
| 138 caption | The City matched her old interest in housing of high density. | "her long-standing interest in high-density housing" | not given |
| 138 caption | A Fulbright Scholarship paid for 10 months in Hong Kong, in which she studied the City. | "Suenn was able to secure a Fulbright Scholarship that allowed her to spend 10 months in Hong Kong studying the City in detail" | not given |
| 138 caption | She put her results in a report of 32 pages, which was also a publication. The caption says that drawings from it are on p138 and on p137. The credits (p356) also give Suenn Ho for p136 (bottom right) and p139. | "distilled into a 32-page report and publication" | not given |
| 138 drawing | My reading of the alley map (top left). The buildings are one black mass, and the alleys are white lines. The yamen is a white court in the centre. A row of small blocks in outline stands along one short side. | "a diagrammatic map of the City's alleys" (p139 caption) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 138 drawing | My reading of the staircase sheet on p138. It has six types. Each type has one diagram, two real examples and a small map that shows the place of each example. | (drawing; no words) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 138 drawing | My reading of the six types on p138: a stair in the centre of the building; a stair in the middle of one side; a stair in one corner; a stair with a corridor along two sides; a stair outside the building on a short link; a stair and corridor in a strip along one side. | (drawing; no words) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 139 caption | Students of the University of Hong Kong (香港大學) helped Suenn Ho. She was a part-time lecturer there in her 10 months. | "With the help of students at the University of Hong Kong, where she also worked as a part-time lecturer" | not given |
| 139 caption | One set of her diagrams shows where the factories and the other non-residential units were on each level, and how dense they were. | "the distribution and density of factories and other non-residential units on the City's different levels" | Suenn Ho's study, 1992 |
| 139 caption | Her studies of staircases and corridors show two cases. In the first, one structure holds the stair. In the second, the stair or corridor joins two or more buildings. | "accommodated into single structural units or were used to form links between two or more different buildings" | Suenn Ho's study, 1992 |
| 139 drawing | My reading of the six types on p139: a corridor between two stairs at the ends of a slab; a stair in the inner corner of an L shape; a stair at the foot of an L shape; a stair at the joint of a T shape; one stair between two buildings that touch at a corner; one stair between three buildings. | (drawing; no words) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 139 drawing | My reading of the factory diagrams. Six small plans of the City have the labels "Basement", "Ground Floor", "Mezzanine", "1st Floor", "2nd Floor" and "3rd Floor". Each dot is probably one unit (my reading). The dots have two shades, black and grey; the book does not explain the shades. The book prints no level above the 3rd floor. | (labels on the drawing) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 139 drawing | My rough count of the dots: basement about 6; ground floor several hundred; mezzanine about 50; 1st floor about 150; 2nd floor about 100; 3rd floor about 70. | (drawing) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; my count of a small print, thus only approximate |
| 139 drawing | My reading of the ground-floor plan. The dots stand in rows along all four edges of the City and along the alleys. The centre has few dots. The plan does not mark the yamen; I think that the empty centre is the yamen. | (drawing) | Suenn Ho drawing, 1992; primary |
| 140 caption | The drawing on p140 shows how different buildings connected. It comes from a Vimeo presentation about the City. | "part of a recent Vimeo presentation about the Walled City" | University of Waterloo (credit, p356) |
| 140 caption | The makers are Lia Tramontini, Haneen Dalla-Ali, Mona Dai and Christopher Glebe: students of architecture and their tutor at the University of Waterloo, Ontario, Canada. | "architectural students and tutor at the University of Waterloo in Ontario, Canada" | not given |
| 140 image | My reading of the Waterloo drawing: a cut-away view from above of about eight rooms in different buildings, with the outlines of the other buildings around them. One large room on the right holds a stair. The rooms have shelves, stools and tables. It is a reconstruction. | (caption above) | University of Waterloo (credit, p356) |
| 140 caption | Adolfo Arranz, senior artist of the *South China Morning Post*, drew the picture on p141. | "Drawn by Adolfo Arranz, senior artist at the South China Morning Post" | not given |
| 140 caption | The newspaper printed the drawing in March 2013, 20 years after the demolition. The drawing won an award. | "published in that paper in March 2013 to mark 20 years since the City's demolition" | not given |
| 140 caption | The editors say that the drawing is not exact as architecture, but that it is true to the life of the City. | "might not be accurate in a strictly architectural sense, but it captures perfectly the reality of life in the Walled City" | the editors' opinion |
| 140 caption | The editors' picture of the City: buildings in a jumble, and a different activity in each room. | "with its buildings jumbled together and containing a riot of different activities in every room" | the editors' opinion |
| 141 image | My reading of the Arranz drawing: a cut-away block of about 12 to 14 storeys. It shows street stalls with awnings, vertical signs, caged balconies with laundry, rooms with bunk beds, workshops and kitchens. The roofs have aerials, plants, an umbrella and children. Use it for ideas only. | (caption on p140) | Adolfo Arranz, *South China Morning Post*, March 2013; a reconstruction |
| 140 essay | For much of its life the City had no real wall. Ideas, unclear law and its bad name were its walls. At the end, the city that grew up around it was also a wall. | "a community walled in by perceptions, by legal ambiguity, by reputation" | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | The City kept the Hong Kong Government out for decades. | "successfully kept undesirable authority, in the form of the Hong Kong Government, out for decades" | not given |
| 140 essay | Many people of Hong Kong saw the City as a place that held unwanted people and activities, out of their view. | "kept undesirable people and activities in" | not given |
| 140 essay | Many people called the City an "anti-city". Saywell says that it was much like Hong Kong. | "Many thought of it as an 'anti-city', yet it mirrored Hong Kong more closely than has often been acknowledged." | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | Saywell calls the Chinese Government the landlord of the City. The City was useful to China in politics, and a trouble to the British in Hong Kong. | "It was a political convenience to its landlord, the Chinese Government" | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | Saywell asks if the City was also useful to the British. It held people and activities that the British otherwise had to deal with. | "Did the Walled City provide a useful container for elements it would otherwise have had to deal with?" | the writer's question |
| 140 essay | Saywell measures the "success" of the City by its growth in its last two decades. He calls this success a copy of the success of Hong Kong. | "As Hong Kong prospered, so too did the Walled City." | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | Saywell's view: the growth of the City did not shame British rule. It gave an indirect proof that British rule worked. | "not so much a blight on British custodianship of its shining Asian outpost as a confirming, if indirect, endorsement of it" | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | Saywell's view: the City was an early form of the Hong Kong block with flats, shops and recreation in one structure. | "the multi-functional super-block, merging residential, commercial and recreational facilities in one structure" | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | Suenn Ho's argument, which Saywell repeats: the City was an ideal form of town, not a failure. | "Kowloon Walled City represented not a failed urban form but an ideal one." | Suenn Ho ("argued years ago"); the essay does not say where |
| 140 essay | Saywell says that Suenn Ho's view has since become an accepted position. He names no one who accepts it. | "(and which has since become an accepted position)" | the writer's claim |
| 140 essay | The first reasons in the essay: the City was socially well joined and used space very efficiently. | "It was highly socially integrated and hyper-efficient with space" | Suenn Ho's argument, in Saywell's words |
| 140 essay | The reasons in the essay: all functions were in one dense area that people crossed on foot, and the City used few resources and materials. | "it consumed a minimum of resources and materials" | Suenn Ho's argument, in Saywell's words |
| 140 essay | More reasons: the residents made the rules and did the repairs. They had almost no oversight, no regulation and no tax. | "democratically self-regulated and self-repairing while enjoying minimal oversight and regulation and taxation" | Suenn Ho's argument, in Saywell's words |
| 140 essay | Saywell's phrase for the City, with a mention of Leon Battista Alberti. | "the City as a House/the House as a City" | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | Its walls had gone decades before. But the old military post still had an almost mythic power to defend itself, in the mind and in its body. | "held near mythic 'defensive' power – both psychological and physical" | the writer's opinion |
| 140 essay | In 1987 the maze made the clearance slow. The clearance team used months to record the premises and to find all the last residents. | "requiring months for the clearance team to record the physical premises and find all its final inhabitants" | not given |
| 140 essay | The site became a public garden. Saywell says that it keeps only very faint signs of the old layout. | "The Walled City would end its physical lifespan as a gentrified public garden" | not given |
| 140 essay | The last sentence of the essay: the City lives on in legend and in memory. It lives in the memories of its residents and also of the people who kept away. | "But it would live on in legend, in the memories of those who lived there, and of those who stayed away." | the writer's opinion |
| 142–143 photo | A colour photo fills the two pages. It has no caption. My reading: tall facades behind a sports ground with a goal and players in sports clothes. Most balconies have grilles or cages, and many have laundry. The roofs have many TV aerials and a few plants. | (no caption) | photo by Ian Lambot (credit, p356); primary |
| 142–143 photo | My reading of the signs. A roof has the sign 牙醫陳國祥. The repo puts this dentist at 108 Lung Chun Road (龍津道). A top storey has the name 東發: the repo names Tung Fat House (東發樓) at 82–84 Lung Chun Road. A sign on p143 is 陳子明牙科 (74 Lung Chun Road in the repo). My inference from these addresses: the photo shows the south side. The book does not name the side. | (signs in the photo) | photo by Ian Lambot; `docs/room_places.md`, `data/room_research.json` |

### Part 5. Jon Resnick, "Popular Culture and the Walled City", and the fold-out by Hitomi Terasawa (寺澤一美) (pp192–217 and the fold-out)

Writer: Jon Resnick (essay, 2014); the editors (captions); Hitomi Terasawa (fold-out, © 2014). Checked on 2026-11-16: 191 rows checked, 22 fixed, 0 deleted, 27 added. 199 rows.

"Rests on" gives the source that the writer names. "fiction" marks a novel, game, manga or film plot: use it only as INFERRED. For the fold-out, "drawing (my reading)" means that I describe what I see; the drawing has no labels.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 192 | Resnick grew up in Milwaukee, Wisconsin. He came to Hong Kong in 1982. He was a cruise ship purser, lawyer, ad man, author and entrepreneur. | "deep-seated wanderlust led him to Hong Kong in 1982" | standfirst (2014) |
| 192 | He wrote seven children's books on endangered species. He lives in Hong Kong with his wife and children. | — | standfirst (2014) |
| 192 | Resnick writes more than 20 years after the demolition. Thus the essay dates from about 2014. | "More than two decades have passed since Kowloon Walled City was razed." | not given |
| 192 | Size of the site: 2.6 hectares. | "its minuscule 2.6 hectare site" | not given |
| 192 | He lists labels that outsiders gave the City in its lifetime, for example "a lawless world within a world", "a cancer in Kowloon" and "a den of iniquity". | "drawn from various sources during its lifetime" | not given (no source for each label) |
| 192 | Resnick says that outsiders made these judgements, often after one visit or none. | "outsiders who may have visited just the once, or never" | his opinion |
| 192 | *Batman Begins* (2005): The Narrows, the slum island of Gotham City, is based on the Walled City, as the director Christopher Nolan says. | "From Hong Kong we took the Walled City of Kowloon [which] is the basis for The Narrows" | Nolan quote; place and date not given |
| 193 | Caption: the early production designs of The Narrows (a model and an illustration) copy the density of the buildings, the rich facades and the gloom and grime of the City. | — | caption |
| 192, 194 | *Call of Duty: Black Ops* (2010, X-Box 360, PlayStation 3, Wii): a CIA team goes into a no-man's-land to extract a British chemist. The game map is the Walled City. | "dilapidated buildings, confusing interiors and interconnected rooftops" | the game (fiction) |
| 194 | Caption: games show the City always at night. *Black Ops* has a long sequence in a setting based on the City. | "invariably portrayed at night" | caption |
| 194 | The game screenshot shows tin-roof huts on flat roofs, wires on a pole, neon signs and a window air-conditioner. This is a game set, not the City. | — | screenshot (fiction) |
| 194 | A San Francisco band called Kowloon Walled City ("sludge metal", "noise rock") played an album-release show at the Hemlock Tavern in January 2013. | — | not given |
| 194 | Co-founder Scott Evans chose the name for its sound, after a friend said it. The band read about the City later. | "just based on the way it sounded" | Evans quote; date not given |
| 194 | Evans: people look the name up when they first hear it. Nearly every review of the band's albums describes the real City. | "nearly every album review I've seen has an introductory section that describes the actual City" | Evans quote; date not given |
| 195 | Caption: Evans chose the name "based solely on the way it sounded". A small picture shows an album cover with a container ship and a line of white capitals, probably "KOWLOON WALLED CITY CONTAINER SHIPS" (blurred). | "only later finding out about the place itself" | caption |
| 195 | Resnick says that the band had never heard of the City and had never been to Hong Kong. | "the band had never heard of the Walled City before, or travelled to Hong Kong" | his reading of the Evans quote |
| 195 | He calls China the "parent company" of the City. The 1898 lease agreement made the City an anomaly inside British Hong Kong. | "an anomaly created by the 1898 lease agreement between the two countries" | not given |
| 195 | He quotes two common labels: "a squalid no-man's-land" and "overrun with every vice and vile expression of humanity". | — | not given |
| 195 | His own words for the alleys. | "its maze of dark, dripping and putrid alleys" | not given (his own visits, p217) |
| 195 | He notes that accounts of normal life in the City exist, but that people prefer the dark image. | "Although there are numerous accounts of normal life in the Walled City, that narrative never sticks" | his opinion |
| 195 | He compares the belief that the City held only evil with two other beliefs: a plot to kill John F Kennedy, and that man never went to the moon. | "For some reason we want to believe there was only evil inside its walls" | his opinion |
| 196 | The Cantonese name "Hak Nam" means "City of Darkness", as the book says. The book prints no Chinese characters for it. | "as its Cantonese name, Hak Nam, translates" | not given |
| 196 | He lists the "brand attributes" of the City: dangerous, murderous, unruly, criminal, congested, claustrophobic, suffocating, putrid, dank, chaotic, evil, isolated, antisocial, scary. | — | his opinion |
| 196 | The City was so close to the end of the runway of Kai Tak (啓德) airport that it "practically had its own airport". | "so close was it to the end of the runway at Hong Kong's old Kai Tak International Airport" | not given |
| 196 | Resnick calls the City a neighbourhood, not a big city. | "it was essentially a neighbourhood after all" | his opinion |
| 196 | Because the City is gone, he puts it beside imaginary places: Shangri-La, Atlantis, Xanadu, Oz and Wonderland. | "has a place in the pantheon of the imaginary" | his opinion |
| 196 | Caption: most Hong Kong people feared the City in its lifetime. In 2014 people see it as heritage, fit for the planned M+ museum. | "Feared as a dark and dangerous place by most Hong Kong residents during its lifetime" | caption |
| 197 | The photo at the top of p197 shows an exhibition hall with a large aerial picture of the City on a wall. I think that the p196 caption (M+) belongs to it. The book does not name the show. | — | photo (layout: my reading) |
| 196-197 | The Hong Kong SAR Government website says that after the war, tenements without permission or proper foundations covered almost the whole site. | "high-rise tenements built without authority and completely lacking proper foundations mushroomed cheek by jowl over almost the whole site" | HK SAR Government website (date not given) |
| 197 | The same website lists drug divans, criminal hide-outs, vice dens and cheap unlicensed dentists. | "a notorious nest of drug divans, criminal hide-outs, vice dens and even cheap, unlicensed dentists" | HK SAR Government website |
| 197 | The Hong Kong Tourism Board website says that fugitives came to the City in the 20th century, and lists drug trafficking, prostitution and unlicensed dentistry. | "everything from drug trafficking and prostitution to unlicensed dentistry flourished" | HKTB website (date not given) |
| 197 | Jackie Pullinger, a British evangelist, came to the City in 1966 to treat drug addicts. Many addicts were tied to the triads. | "many of whom were shackled to the Triads" | not given |
| 197 | Pullinger later founded the St Stephen's Society (聖士提芬會). It continues her work in 2014. | — | not given |
| 197 | Pullinger's two memoirs: *Chasing the Dragon* (1980) and *Crack in the Wall: The Life and Death of Kowloon Walled City* (1989). | — | the books |
| 197 | Pullinger (1980): no building rules held, and the blocks had no sanitation, water or lighting. | "no building regulations can be enforced" | *Chasing the Dragon* (1980) |
| 197 | Pullinger (1980): the tangled wires show that the people took their electricity from the public supply outside the City. | "their electricity is tapped from the public supplies outside the Walled City" | *Chasing the Dragon* (1980) |
| 197 | Pullinger (1980): people emptied excrement into the alleys. | "excrement must be emptied into the stinking alleys below" | *Chasing the Dragon* (1980) |
| 197 | Pullinger (1980): two toilets at street level for all the people, whom she counts as thirty thousand. Each was a hole over a cesspool, one for women and one for men. | "At street level there are two toilets for all thirty thousand people" | *Chasing the Dragon* (1980) |
| 197 | Caption: novels and memoirs that name the City: Pullinger's *Chasing the Dragon*, Martin Booth's *Gweilo*, William Gibson's *Idoru*, Robert Ludlum's *The Bourne Supremacy*. p196 shows the four covers. | — | caption |
| 198 | Caption: films that use the City: *Bloodsport* (1988, Jean-Claude van Damme), *Long Arm of the Law* (1984, Johnny Mak) and *Crime Story* (1993, Jackie Chan). The DVD cover prints *Long Arm of the Law* as 省港旗兵. | — | caption; DVD covers |
| 198 | Caption: *Brothers from the Walled City*. In it and in *Long Arm of the Law*, the dark name of the City is part of the plot. The book gives no year. A DVD cover on the page prints 城寨出來者; the book does not link that cover to this title (my matching). | "the City's dark reputation plays an important role in the plot" | caption; DVD cover |
| 198 | Caption: in *Crime Story* the roofs of the empty City stand for Taiwan in the story. | "the action actually taking place in Taiwan according to the film's storyline" | caption |
| 198 | A film still (film not named) shows a narrow alley at night: a wall with vertical Chinese inscriptions, a door with a cut-out pattern, and a white hanging sign with a red cross and the character 醫. | — | film still (film not named) |
| 199 | The still continues on p199: the same alley, a column with patterned inscriptions and a second hanging sign. Its characters are too blurred to read. | — | film still |
| 199 | A large night still shows sparks falling past a metal window cage, smoke, pink light and barred windows. The book gives no caption for it. | — | film still (film not named) |
| 198 | Martin Booth, *Gweilo: A Memoir of a Hong Kong Childhood* (2004): he went into the City in the 1950s as a boy of eight, against his mother's warnings. | "the streetwise eight-year-old was drawn to it" | *Gweilo* (2004, retrospective) |
| 198 | Booth made friends with a triad member. The man took him round, asked him into an opium den and showed him triad weapons. | — | *Gweilo* (2004, retrospective) |
| 198 | Booth (1950s): new six-storey buildings were going up; some had people in them. Old shanties and two-storey houses leaned. | "A number of new six-storey buildings were being erected" | *Gweilo* (2004, retrospective) |
| 198 | Booth (1950s): the City looked like a squatter area with old permanent buildings in the middle. | "It looked like a squatter area but with permanent structures in the middle in ill repair." | *Gweilo* (2004, retrospective) |
| 198 | Booth (1950s): in an alley he passed a man who pushed a bicycle with a pannier full of cardboard boxes. | "a man pushing a bicycle, the pannier laden with cardboard boxes" | *Gweilo* (2004, retrospective) |
| 198 | Booth (1950s): an alley wound away into the buildings and shacks. The man with the bicycle paid him no attention. | "A hutong [alley] lay before me, winding into the buildings and shacks." | *Gweilo* (2004, retrospective) |
| 198 | A documentary, *Kowloon: The Walled City*, was made in 1989 by an Austrian company. It starts with aerial footage and shows life inside. It is on YouTube. | "made in 1989 by an Austrian production company and can be viewed on YouTube" | the film |
| 199 | The cameraman Hamdani Milas: the crew filmed in high summer and sweated all day. He remembers a busy place of work and survival. | "what a hive of industry and sheer survival it was" | Milas quote (date of quote not given; retrospective) |
| 199 | Milas: cockroaches, rats and open sewers. | "after mingling with the cockroaches and rats and stepping over open sewers" | Milas quote |
| 199 | Milas: something always dripped from above, and the crew had trouble keeping the camera dry. | "keeping the equipment dry was one problem because there was always 'something' dripping down on you." | Milas quote |
| 199 | *Baraka* (1992), by Ron Fricke and Mark Magidson, was shot in 70mm in 24 countries on six continents, with music and no voice-over. It shows the City as urban blight and as a dense mass of people. | "Kowloon Walled City was featured as an example of urban blight" | the film |
| 199 | Resnick says that the documentaries and Milas's memories helped to spread the dark image of the City. | "hard-boiled descriptions of the real Kowloon Walled City" | his opinion |
| 199 | *Long Arm of the Law* (1984), directed by Johnny Mak and produced by Sammo Hung, was the first film shot inside the City. Fugitives hide there; police shoot them. | "some movies were actually filmed inside its walls. The first was the 1984 gangster film Long Arm of the Law" | not given |
| 199 | *Bloodsport* (1988): the City is the setting of a martial-arts tournament to the death. The text does not say clearly that the crew filmed inside. | "the Walled City is the setting for a no-holds-barred martial arts tournament" | not given |
| 199 | *Crime Story* (1993): the crew filmed on the roof of the empty City just before the demolition. They got permission to blow up one building with explosives. | "The filmmakers received permission to use explosives to blow up one of the buildings for the movie's climax." | not given |
| 199 | William Gibson, *Idoru* (1996, Bridge trilogy): a character tells of an outlaw place near an airport that still belonged to China. The people built it higher and higher. | "No rules, just building, just people living." | the novel (fiction) |
| 199 | Gibson's character: drugs, prostitution and gambling, but also factories and restaurants. | "But people living, too. Factories, restaurants." | the novel (fiction) |
| 199 | Gibson's character: the police did not go into the place. | "Police wouldn't go there." | the novel (fiction) |
| 200 | Ludlum: the City has no visible wall, but its edge is clear. The flats look like shacks set on top of one another, near collapse. | "The Walled City of Kowloon has no visible wall around it" | the novel (fiction) |
| 200 | Ludlum: the place is damp and rotten. | "A putrid dampness abounds; all is decay and rot" | the novel (fiction) |
| 200 | Robert Ludlum, *The Bourne Supremacy* (year not given): an open market runs along the street in front of the run-down flats. A short flight of steps leads down inside. | "the congested open market that runs along the street in front of the row of dark run-down flats" | the novel (fiction) |
| 200 | Ludlum: below ground level, cobbled alleys run like tunnels under the buildings. | "cobblestoned alleyways that are in most cases more like tunnels" | the novel (fiction) |
| 200 | Ludlum: beggars, prostitutes and drug sellers in corridors lit by bare bulbs on loose wires. | "naked bulbs that hang from exposed wires along the stone walls" | the novel (fiction) |
| 200 | Resnick says that the film of *The Bourne Supremacy* has no relation to the book. | — | his opinion |
| 200 | Manga *Blood+: Kowloon Nights*: a Hong Kong police officer hunts a killer. Bloodless bodies turn up in the abandoned City; a family sells a drug of immortality. | — | the manga (fiction) |
| 200 | The manga page shows a block of the City, and a bubble calls it deserted and due for demolition. | "WHICH IS DESERTED AND SLATED FOR DEMOLI-TION." | the manga (fiction) |
| 200 | Manga *Crying Freeman*: one character goes to the City to master swordsmanship and to control a cursed sword. | — | the manga (fiction) |
| 200 | Yu Wing Leung writes for The One Comics Publishing Co. His manga *City of Darkness* came out in four parts from 2009: 32 issues of about 40 pages. | "Published in four parts from 2009, the story eventually ran to 32 issues, each of around 40 pages" | not given |
| 200, 202 | At its peak the manga sold 20,000 copies a week. A sequel followed. | "selling 20,000 copies a week" | not given |
| 201 | Caption: the manga became video and board games and a radio drama. Fans dressed as the characters and posed in places like the City's alleys. | "in locations meant to match the Walled City's streets and alleys" | caption |
| 201 | The manga art shows the City skyline with dense TV aerials on the roofs. | — | manga art (fiction) |
| 202 | Caption: Yu Wing Leung ("Yu-Yi") went to school near the City in the early 1980s as a young child. He went into its alleys now and then, but he remembers little. | "I didn't live near [the City], but I used to pass it on my way to school." | Yu quote (retrospective; date not given) |
| 202 | Yu found the Japanese edition of *City of Darkness* in Japan. Its photos led him to write first a novel, then the weekly manga. | "the Japanese edition of City of Darkness, and I was fascinated by the photographs inside" | Yu quote |
| 202 | Caption: the manga copies photos from this book. | "drew heavily on photographs that can be found in this book" | caption |
| 202 | The manga panel shows an alley under a mass of hanging cables. It has a standpipe with a tap and hoses, a jerrycan, a tub and a folding metal shutter. | — | manga art (fiction, drawn from photos) |
| 202 | The panel prints Cantonese dialogue in three bubbles. One says that it is far easier to make money outside than here. One says that he walked most of the day for nothing. The man on the left tells him to take it slowly, he will find it. The tails do not show clearly who says the first two. | "行咗大半日都係食白果" | manga (fiction) |
| 203 | Three manga pages and a large painted panel show a running man in a narrow alley. The alley has rolling shutters, rubbish on the wet ground and a lamp overhead. | — | manga art (fiction) |
| 204 | Yu: his manga taught young people about the City. Some characters became figurines. | "So it turned out that my manga has helped with cultural conservation, which I didn't expect." | Yu quote |
| 204 | Michelle Young, founder of the website Untapped Cities, 2011 article "Behind These Walls": game designers use the City's alleys, exteriors and height. | "The characteristic that most set Kowloon Walled City apart from other slums was its high-rise, skyscraper form." | Untapped Cities, 2011 |
| 204 | Young (2011) also names the small businesses of the inner alleys. | "The cramped businesses in the inner alleys" | Untapped Cities, 2011 |
| 204 | Young (2011): games use the City to give a feeling of repression, confusion or loss, with the mafia and illicit activity as plots. | "used to impart a sense of repression, confusion, or loss" | Untapped Cities, 2011 |
| 204 | *Shenmue II* (2001): a Japanese hero goes to the vertical world of the City. | — | the game (fiction) |
| 204 | *Kowloon's Gate*: set just before the 1997 handover. The City reappears by magic, and a feng shui master restores order. The game came out in Japan in 1997, worldwide in 2010. | — | the game (fiction) |
| 204 | Caption: the City is also the setting of *Shadowrun* (screenshot: a fight on a flat roof with a rail). | — | caption |
| 204 | 『大図解 九龍城』 (*Dai Zukai Kyuuryuu Jou*, "Large-scale Illustrated Kowloon City") came out in Japan in 1997. It was part of a trilogy by the publisher Suzushi Kuwabara on six decades of Japanese apartment blocks. | "examining six decades of Japanese apartment blocks as a habitat" | not given |
| 204 | The Japanese architect Takayuki Suzuki (鈴木隆行) and 10 students spent a week in the empty City. This page says February 1993. | "who had spent a week in the deserted City in February 1993" | not given |
| 204 | Their survey first came out as articles in a Japanese architecture magazine (name not given). | "Initially published as a series of articles in a Japanese architectural magazine" | not given |
| 204 | Hitomi Terasawa drew their cross-section. Later she extended it into a six-metre cross-section of the whole City, the core of Kuwabara's book. | "Based on actual measurements and with meticulous detail" | not given |
| 204 | Douglas Young, co-founder of Goods of Desire (G.O.D.), made Walled City art installations in 2009 and 2010. | — | not given |
| 204 | One installation made visitors squeeze through tiny spaces in a dark room of an empty residential building. Others placed a to-scale copy outdoors. | "forcing visitors to squeeze through tiny spaces in a darkened room inside what was then a deserted residential building" | not given |
| 205 | Caption: Young cast storey-high sections of an imagined facade. He showed them at the 2009 Bi-City Biennale of Urbanism and Architecture and the 2010 Hong Kong Art Fair. | "repeated castings of storey-high sections of a reimagined facade" | caption |
| 205 | The cast facade (photo) shows stacked window cages with bars, about 7-8 storeys high, set against the Hong Kong skyline at night. | — | photo of the artwork |
| 205 | Caption: Japan made at least three books on the City: the Japanese edition of *City of Darkness*, a book by the photographer Ryuji Miyamoto, and 『大図解 九龍城』. | "no fewer than three books" | caption |
| 205 | The cover of 『大図解 九龍城』 (photo) names three credits in small type. They are blurred, but they match 九龍城探検隊, 寺澤一美 and 可児弘明, as `primary-sources/daizukai-kowloon-1997/SOURCE.md` gives them. | — | book cover (blurred) |
| 206 | Caption: Kit Lau's pop-up book *Hong Kong Pop Up* (2009) has a double-page Walled City: a view down into the gaps, with laundry lines across them and aerials on top. | "with strings of laundry spanning the gaps and the facades topped with forests of television aerials" | caption |
| 207 | Douglas Young: in the West a building is fixed when the builders finish. In Hong Kong the residents keep changing it. | "a native Hong Kong building will never be completed because tenants will always be making new adaptations" | Young quote (date not given) |
| 207 | Young: these buildings lack space, light and air; sanitation is poor and the work shoddy, but people survive. | "There is inadequate space, light and ventilation." | Young quote |
| 207 | Young: the same object (his cast facade) made two opposite feelings: the tight, closed room and the open-air copy. | "It's interesting how the same object can create these two opposite extremes" | Young quote |
| 207 | Young: a Hong Kong building changes all its life, like a living thing. | "the building as a living organism never ceases to evolve" | Young quote |
| 207 | Kit Lau's book studies 60 years of public housing. The City pop-up shows TV antennae and laundry that sticks out from the facades. | "buildings topped by TV antennae and with laundry protruding from their facades" | not given |
| 207 | Caption: photos show Lau's paper prototypes and the printed sheets before cutting. | — | caption |
| 208 | Caption: Suzuki heard of the plan to demolish the City. He then led an "expedition" to record part of it. | "he decided to lead an 'expedition' there and produce an accurate record of at least part of the City" | caption |
| 208 | Caption: he got permission to spend one week in a slice of buildings in the middle of the cleared City, with 10 architecture students. | "spend one week in a 'slice' of buildings at the centre of the, by then, cleared City" | caption |
| 208 | Caption: the team took sketches, measurements, drawings and photos back to Tokyo. They drew a large section and added photos of the empty rooms. | "to give an indication of what each space was used for" | caption |
| 208 | Caption: the students drew plans of some flats and sketches of some features. | — | caption |
| 208 | Caption: Suzuki then asked Terasawa to illustrate the section of his team and to fill it with people and activity. Thus the people and the uses in her drawing are her additions, not survey records. | "filling it with people and activity and generally bringing the whole thing to life" | caption |
| 208 | Caption: Terasawa had visited the City on her own before (date not given). She first drew a part section about 8 ft wide and 4 ft high. | "some 8 feet wide and 4 feet high" | caption |
| 208 | Caption: Kuwabara had edited books on one of Japan's first apartment blocks and on Hashima Island (the caption says "see page 214"; Hashima is on p216). He urged Terasawa to finish the drawing. | — | caption |
| 208 | Caption: after a few years, the full drawing was about 18 ft wide. | "now some 18 feet wide" | caption |
| 208 | Caption: Terasawa had no accurate survey drawings for the whole City. Thus the drawing is far from accurate, most of all the gaps between buildings. | "far from accurate, in particular the space between buildings" | caption |
| 208 | The reproduced spread (Daizukai pp24-25; the "24" is blurred) has a header strip that repeats "Grand Panorama of the Kowloon Walled City". It shows a section of about 12-14 floors made of room photos. | — | reproduced page (Daizukai 1997) |

| 208 | The same text: the rooms were lit by an excess of fluorescent lamps, and the walls were pink, blue, green and yellow. The team rebuilt the colours from a photo of each room. The word for the lamps is blurred (its first character can be 蛍 or 螢), thus I give no quote. | — | Daizukai 1997 |
| 208 | A second Japanese note (my reading; blurred): besides the colours, the rooms had small mosaic tiles and decorative wall coverings. | — | Daizukai 1997 |
| 209 | Caption: the spreads show a timeline, a section of room photos and a montage of sketches, plans and photos. The wall finishes hint at the old use of each room. | "the wall finishes giving a clue to their previous use" | caption |
| 209 | Caption: the one-week survey of the empty City was in January 1993, shortly before demolition began. | "one-week survey of the then empty City in January 1993" | caption |
| 209 | The Japanese notes of the spread describe one survey building: a 5-storey building in a gap less than 3 m wide between two thin towers, on Sai Shing Road (西城路), the west passage. The repo has these as M89-M93. | — | Daizukai 1997 (blurred; see `data/room_research.json` M89-M93) |
| 209 | My reading of the same notes: the 2階 (1/F) had a ceiling of about 1.6 m and was probably a store of the shop below. The top-floor toilet probably also served as a shower. | — | Daizukai 1997 (blurred) |
| 209 | My reading of the same notes: the narrow gap beside this building was a passage to the next building and probably the store of the shop in front. Seen from the roof, the gap was a rubbish dump. | — | Daizukai 1997 (blurred; see M92, M93) |
| 209 | A legible Japanese paragraph on the spread: the thin towers stood so close that the gaps between them, the corridors and the stairs were narrow from the start. People used these shared spaces for extensions. Thus only minimal gaps stayed. These gaps let light reach the ground, and they show the life that spilled out of the rooms. | "増築可能な空間として使われてしまい" | Daizukai 1997 (text of the survey book) |

The unit is not printed. | "Kowloon Walled City in 1992 (the densest place on record)" | *NYT* graphic (date not given) |

| 216 | Hoshino's "super aging" paint finish gives a sense of smell and humidity. | "it gives the sense of its smell and humidity by using our super aging techniques" | Hoshino quote |

| 216-217 | Resnick first went into the City in 1983 with an Associated Press reporter. Later he went back several times alone. | "I had first been taken there by an Associated Press reporter and later returned several times on my own." | own visit |
| 217 | His memory of the City: dark alleys, horrible smells, a sense of danger. | "dark alleyways, ghastly smells, a sense of intrigue and foreboding, a quickening heart" | own visit |
| 217 | His memory: drips from above, slime underfoot, the noise of machines, and electric wires everywhere. | "stuff dripping overhead, slimy gunk underfoot, the whine of machines and a riot of electrical wires threading everywhere" | own visit |
| 217 | In 2014 only the yamen remains, with a small display of photos and texts. The park is quiet. | "Today all that physically remains of the Walled City is the yamen, the former magistrate's compound" | own visit (2014) |
| 217 | To a casual visitor the park can be a park anywhere in China. The City is now as absent as it was once overwhelming. | "the Walled City's absence is almost as total as its presence had once been so overwhelming" | own visit (2014) |

### Part 6. Lui Tai Lok (呂大樂), "A Triply Neglected City" (pp220–225)

Writer: Lui Tai Lok (essay, 2014); the editors (two captions). Checked on 2026-11-16: 107 rows checked, 11 fixed, 0 deleted, 12 added. 119 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 220 | The writer is Professor of Sociology at The University of Hong Kong. He also writes regular columns for local newspapers and magazines. | "Lui Tai Lok is Professor of Sociology at The University of Hong Kong." | bio on the page (editors) |
| 220 | The bio names three books of his: two as co-author (2008, 2009) and one in Chinese about the 1970s. The Chinese book got the Hong Kong Book Prize in 2013. Thus the essay dates from about 2013-2014 (the book came out in October 2014, p4). | "His recent publication, The Story of Hong Kong in the 1970s Retold (in Chinese), was awarded the Hong Kong Book Prize in 2013." | bio on the page (editors); the date of the essay is my inference |
| 220 | Some years ago, Hong Kong had several places where people thought it was not proper to go. Everybody knew their names. The City was the best known of them. | "Kowloon Walled City was the most well known, but it was not alone." | not given (the writer's view) |
| 220 | Outsiders put three resettlement estates in the same class as the City: Tsz Wan Shan, Lam Tin and Chai Wan. | "the resettlement estates in Tsz Wan Shan (Temple Hill), Lam Tin and Chai Wan" | not given |
| 220 | The name for such a district was a hongfan (紅番) zone. The book gives the literal sense as a haunt of barbarians. For many Hong Kong people it meant a place with good and bad people together and weak law. | "a place where good and bad elements intermingled, with poor law and order" | not given |
| 220 | People thought that it was best to go to such places as few times as possible. | "it was thought the fewer visits the better" | not given |
| 220 | Hongkongers were able to buy their daily goods without going into these places. The people who went usually had no real tie to the place, and they went because they had no choice. | "They went there out of necessity, not choice." | not given |
| 220 | Chungking Mansions, on Nathan Road in Tsim Sha Tsui (尖沙咀), "in the heart of the city", has the same kind of reputation today (the essay uses the present tense here). The writer says that it is full of people whom many think undesirable, as the City was. | "And like the Walled City, this place teems with people many might consider undesirable." | not given |
| 220 | The writer says that all these places were places of light and of dark at the same time. | "they are simultaneously places of light and dark" | not given |
| 220 | These places were near daily life and easy to get to. But most people did not go in: they went the long way round. | "preferring to take a roundabout route and so avoid setting foot in an unfamiliar world" | not given |
| 220 | People saw them as 'grey' places, with events inside that an outsider did not fully understand. | "most people perceive them as 'grey' places" | not given |
| 220 | The distance was in the mind. People did not know these places and believed that nobody controlled them. | "a kind of psychological distance, usually based on a sense of insecurity" | not given |
| 220 | The writer says that this fear comes from deep inside each person. | "it is a kind of fear that originates in our innermost selves" | not given |
| 220 | Until about the middle or the end of the 1970s, Hong Kong did not have a name for good public order. | "up to the mid-to-late 1970s, say" | not given |
| 220 | In 1973 the colonial government started the 'Fight Violent Crime Campaign'. The writer says that the campaign answered a real and severe problem, and was more than public relations. | "In 1973, for example, the colonial administration launched the 'Fight Violent Crime Campaign'." | not given (he names the campaign, not a document) |
| 220 | In those years, ordinary people in Hong Kong lived with fear of crime each day. | "Ordinary people were going about their everyday lives in a spirit of intimidation." | not given |
| 220 | Some districts had a bad name for crime and street violence. Gangs were active at the local sports grounds and places of entertainment, where they found recruits and frightened other people. | "Gangs were active at neighbourhood sports grounds and entertainment venues" | not given |
| 220 | Gangs made people join, by force or by persuasion. The name of this method was 'kick'em in' (踢人入會, tiren ruhui). The writer says that it was real, not only a film plot. | "was not just plot fodder for Hong Kong Triad movies, but something that really happened" | not given |
| 220 | Robbers worked in the lifts of the housing estates. | "Robberies occurred in elevators in the housing estates" | not given |
| 220 | The slang name of the mugging gangs was gujing dang (箍頸黨). | "gujing dang (箍頸黨, slang for gangs that practised mugging)" | not given |
| 220 | Crime did not happen each day, but it was a real problem in some communities. | "Although rarely a daily occurrence, lawbreaking was a very real problem within certain communities." | not given |
| 220 | In some public housing estates, the residents made their own safety patrols. | "residents came together and organised patrols as a kind of self-organised public safety and security initiative" | not given |
| 220 | In the years when the police force still had corruption, the resettlement areas had groups of drug addicts and illegal gambling dens. | "before corruption had been purged from the police force" | not given |
| 220 | In that period, crime and vice worked almost in the open in many districts of Hong Kong. The phrase for such a district was 'dragons and snakes' entwined: people of high and low status together. | "The underground world of Hong Kong was not hidden away then." | not given |
| 220-221 | A visitor to a district that the visitor did not know stayed on guard and moved carefully. People thought that very crowded districts were less safe, and the City was a main example. | 221 | The name of the City alone caused more fear than a visit to the other districts. The legend said that nobody was able to know the City. | "it made people believe it was unknowable, an enigma" | not given |
| 221 | The three-character term huangdudu (黃賭毒) covers the sex trade, the sale of drugs and illegal gambling. The writer says that the City was not the only place in old Hong Kong that was full of them. | "a three-character term encompassing the sex industry, drug-peddling and illegal gambling" | not given |
| 221 | A person who wanted to see drug addicts in dark alleys found them more easily in Lam Tin at that time. The sight was almost the same as in the City. | "Lam Tin at that time would have been an easier proposition" | not given |
| 221 | Illegal gambling dens and prostitution were in many parts of Kowloon and of Hong Kong Island. | "they could be found in many parts of Kowloon and Hong Kong Island" | not given |
| 221 | Only for a short time in the 1950s did the City have much more vice than other places. The writer gives no year, no number and no source for this. | "Except for a short period in the 1950s, the Walled City wasn't that much more involved in such activities than other places." | not given |
| 221 | Photo with no caption (Greg Girard, p356). It shows a tall face of the City across bare, cleared ground with heaps of rubble, in sunlight. TV antennae stand on the roofs, and trees grow at the foot of the left end. A long row of washing hangs on lines at the foot of the buildings. Two people in dark uniforms are on the open ground. One, in a peaked cap, walks toward the camera. The other is half hidden behind a person in a patterned top. Adults stand and talk, and small children walk farther back. At the right, the ground floors have open fronts between columns, and a small grey hut stands in front of them. | (no words: photo) | photo on the page (primary); my reading; the book gives no caption, date or side |
| 222 | For the sex trade, Temple Street and Jaffe Road were probably more open than the City. | "Temple Street and Jaffe Road were probably more brazen" | not given |
| 222 | The writer's conclusion: in old Hong Kong, illegal trades did not hide, and the City was a 'typical' part of that life. | "probably did not have significantly more illegal goings-on than other places" | not given |
| 222 | The reputation of the City as a legend and a symbol grew very fast ("exponentially"). People with less experience of the City used more hearsay and made more fantasies. | "the more they seem to have relied on hearsay to understand it" | not given |
| 222 | The City made a deep impression on visitors. The main effect was the loss of direction, not the dark. | "It was not so much a matter of being unable to see the light, so to speak; rather, it was just so disorientating." | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | A stranger lost the way very quickly. The stranger did not know the route from one entrance to one exit. | "You did not know how to get from such-and-such an entrance to such-and-such an exit." | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | Very few alleys were straight. | "Very few of the City's alleyways ran in straight lines" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | The alleys did not seem to run north-south or east-west. | "their orientation did not seem to follow the cardinal points of the compass" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | A stranger who looked for an address nearly always took a wrong turn. Then he made the same error again, and came out on Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道). | "you found yourself back on Tung Tau Tsuen Road" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | The stranger went in again and arrived at Lung Chun Road (龍津道), with no idea of the route. | "somehow – you never knew how – you arrived at Lung Chun Road" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | The stranger found the address only after two or three more errors. | "Only after two or three more mistakes did you find what you were looking for." | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | When the visitor looked up and saw the sky, it was fairly easy to see the way. When the visitor had to look down, it was not easy. | "When you could look up and see the sky, you could see where you were going fairly easily" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | Water dripped from above in the alleys, in dry weather and in rain. | "there was usually water dripping down from somewhere" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | The buildings were high-rise, and the visitor went up by the stairs. From the wrong entrance, a visitor who climbed to the "third floor" arrived at the second. INFERRED: the floor count of one building changed with the entrance. | "thinking you were heading for the third floor, only to find you had arrived at the second because you had entered via the wrong entrance" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | An address used a building name or a building number. It was hard to find a place from such an address. | "If you had to find a specific address based on a building name or number, it was a challenge." | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | The writer calls the City a very large labyrinth. He says that his own experience of it was small. | "In my limited experience, the Walled City was one enormous labyrinth." | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | Visitors felt dizzy, lost their sense of direction, and found the place fully strange. The alleys were dim and damp. | "strange environment of murky and damp alleyways" | own visit (undated) |
| 222 | Caption. The photo looks up from an alley through a narrow slot between two buildings of 12 storeys or more. By chance, no later additions filled this slot. Daylight in an alley was rare. | "between two 12-storey and more buildings that by chance had remained free of later additions" | caption (editors); photo by Ian Lambot (p356) |
| 222 | The same photo shows a bright strip of sky at the top. Washing hangs on a pole that sticks out from one wall. A thing like a mop hangs at the end of a second pole. Poles or cables cross the slot, and a corrugated sheet covers part of the upper right side. | (no words: photo) | photo on the page (primary); my reading |
| 223 | In the alleys the visitor stayed alert. The visitor watched for the illegal water pipes, the electric wires and the building work that nobody had approved. | "always conscious of the illegally hooked-up water pipes and electrical wiring and any unauthorised building work" | own visit (undated) |
| 223 | Because the visitor did not understand the place, the visitor felt alienated and made many associations. Then the visitor started to believe all the stories about it. | "You started thinking that everything you had heard about the Walled City was true." | own visit (undated) |
| 223 | The writer sees no direct link between this confusion and crime. He calls the usual picture of the City exaggerated. | "the impression that most people have of the Walled City is rather exaggerated and more of a fantasy" | not given |
| 223 | The Hong Kong film Long Arm of the Law (省港旗兵) showed the City full of drug addicts, with prostitutes in the streets at the entrance. The writer calls the film sensational. | "where drug addicts were everywhere, and as soon as you entered you ran into prostitutes milling around the streets" | the film (named; the essay gives no year) |
| 223 | The writer agrees that Triad members and criminals were still in the City. He says that the picture of a hotbed of crime had no firm base. | "Of course, there were still Triad members, criminals and other malefactors" | not given |
| 223 | But the picture of a hotbed of crime spread widely and went deep into the public mind. The same associations come back each time somebody names the City. | "And yet they spread widely and penetrated deeply into the public consciousness." | not given |
| 223 | The writer asks why people still believe the myths, when the gap between the legend and the reality is so large. | "why do people continue to choose to believe in these overblown and baseless myths?" | not given |
| 223 | The writer calls the City a unique place: a zone of limbo in a great crowd of people. | "The Walled City was a unique place, a zone of limbo amid a mass of humanity." | not given |
| 223 | The Chinese term that people often used for the City was sanbuguan (三不管), 'triply neglected'. | "sanbuguan (三不管) literally means 'triply neglected'" | not given |
| 223 | The three were the colonial government of Hong Kong, the PRC government and the British government. None of them was able to govern the City in the open. | "Neither the colonial administration of Hong Kong nor the PRC and British Governments could intervene or administer it in a conspicuous way." | not given |
| 223 | Many people saw the City as a place with no institutions, where any event was possible. | "this community formed an institutional vacuum, a place where anything could happen" | not given |
| 223 | This political history made the idea of the City as a den of huangdudu easy to believe. | "the notion of the Walled City as a seedy and anarchic den, ridden with huangdudu, became highly persuasive" | not given |
| 223 | Because of this background, the City gave people no limit to their fantasies about it. | "it inspired limitless flights of fancy" | not given |
| 223 | Many people believed that events that were not possible in other places were usual in the City. | "Many thought that things that could not happen under usual circumstances were probably a normal part of life in the Walled City." | not given |
| 223 | Many people thought that the City turned the reality of the rest of Hong Kong upside down. It showed a different social order that outsiders did not understand. | "It had the ability to invert the reality of the rest of Hong Kong society" | not given |
| 223 | People of all classes were in the City, good and bad together. The phrase for it was 'crouching tigers and hidden dragons' (臥虎藏龍): important people were able to hide there with no name. | "a place of 'crouching tigers and hidden dragons' (臥虎藏龍), a place where prominent people could hide themselves in anonymity" | not given |
| 223 | The writer says that the lack of institutions fed the imagination of outsiders. The lack was in government, in law and social norms, and in public order. | "in terms of political governance, judicial and social norms and public order" | not given |
| 223 | People who never went to the City based their view on myth and fantasy. Some visitors saw only the surface. The writer says that what they saw possibly made them believe that nobody controlled the place. | "myth and fantasy form the basis of their appraisal" | not given |
| 223 | On a short visit, an outsider noticed the large number of dentists who worked with no licence. For outsiders this showed that the City did not apply the rules of other districts. | "the large number of dentists who practised there without formal licences" | not given |
| 223 | The disorder of the pipes and the wires gave outsiders the same impression. | "The chaotic plumbing and wiring only reinforced this impression." | not given |
| 223 | Outsiders felt that the City was exotic and far from 'normal' Hong Kong. | "They felt that the Walled City resembled somewhere exotic, far removed from 'normal' Hong Kong society." | not given |
| 223 | The City had a separate identity. But many people knew it a little. For them, its 'limbo' status did not put it outside the control of the Hong Kong Government and its laws. | "the so-called limbo status was not about being beyond the control of the Hong Kong Government and its laws" | not given |
| 223 | Three public services worked in the City. The Hong Kong police enforced the law. The municipal authorities collected the refuse. The Post Office delivered the mail regularly. | "the Hong Kong police did enforce the law there, rubbish was collected by the municipal authorities, and the Post Office operated a regular delivery service" | not given |
| 223 | Thus the 'vacuum' is partly a legend. | "The 'vacuum' of legend is not entirely true." | not given |
| 223 | The writer asks who will take the trouble to find the facts behind the legend. | "But who is going to concern themselves with getting to the bottom of all that?" | not given |
| 223 | The writer says that a legend can be less than fully true. | "Legends are legends; they do not have to be 100 per cent true." | not given |
| 223 | The legend made a wall that nobody saw round the City. This wall made the City look more separate from the life of Hong Kong than it was. | "a kind of intangible wall around the actual settlement" | not given |
| 223 | The residents did not feel that their life was much different from the life of other people. | "the residents who passed their days there did not feel that their life was so different to anybody else's" | not given |
| 223 | People outside the City saw it very differently from the residents. This was true mainly of the people who knew it only at second hand. | "especially those who formed all their impressions on the basis of second-hand information, saw things very differently" | not given |
| 223 | Most people were content with this indirect contact. Their curiosity about the City came later, mostly after it was gone. | "most people were very satisfied with this kind of indirect contact" | not given |
| 223 | Before the demolition, few people had much interest in the City. Nobody wanted to keep its spirit or to make a memorial. | "There was no interest in creating some kind of memorial." | not given |
| 223-224 | At that time most people wanted to stay away from the City. The City was no danger to their daily life. | "just wanted to keep the Walled City at arm's length" | not given |
| 224 | The City was a part of Hong Kong life. But it also seemed to belong to a different world. | "dark, subterranean, unknowable, outside the mainstream and anarchic" | not given |
| 224 | The writer says that the mystery of the City made many people see a fact: their own Hong Kong society had made its own norms and order. | "caused many people to see how the Hong Kong society that they lived in had generated its own social norms and order" | not given |
| 224 | For most of the time, the public did not think about the City. | "for most of the time, the Walled City held no significant place in the minds of the general public at all" | not given |
| 224 | After the demolition, nothing of its physical environment was left. Only then did people start to see the City as a part of Hong Kong, even as representative of Hong Kong. It became a cultural icon. The writer calls this somewhat ironic. | "the Walled City has suddenly evolved into a cultural icon" | not given |
| 224 | Some people now see the City as a symbol of Hong Kong. The writer adds that nobody ever saw life in the City as typical. | "though life inside the community was never seen as typical" | not given |
| 224 | Other people see the City as an icon of "order in chaos", a feature of urban life in the former colony. The writer says that probably only the residents understood this order, and short visitors never did. | "that underlying order was probably only properly understood by the City's residents, never by those who only visited briefly" | not given |
| 224 | Others see the City as a sign of grassroots diversity and vibrancy. The writer says that before the demolition, few people tried to learn how life there was. | "before its demolition, few people tried to find out what life was like there" | not given |
| 224 | Others see an image of tenacity and flexibility in the jungle-like growth of the City's structure. But the writer says that, in the first decades after the war, squatter settlements and illegal facades were in almost every part of Hong Kong. | "squatter settlements, illegal facades and the other unlawful but creative uses of space to cope with high-density living" | not given ("serious observers of Hong Kong", no name) |
| 224 | Some see the City as a sign of bottom-up creativity and innovation. The writer says that this invention from below was a feature of all Hong Kong, not only of the City. | "this was not peculiar to the Walled City, but rather a feature of life in Hong Kong as a whole" | not given |
| 224 | The writer says that the City now carries too many meanings, more than the myths need. | "you could say that it has become overloaded" | not given |
| 224 | People now remember the City for things that were not really part of it. | "the Walled City is remembered today for things that did not really belong to it" | not given |
| 224 | The writer does not call the new interest nostalgia. It has little to do with a wish to find again what was there. | "Is this nostalgia? Probably not" | not given |
| 224 | The new and growing interest in the City mixes many different things. | "The recent and still growing interest in the Walled City is largely characterised by eclecticism." | not given |
| 224 | The people who lived in the City do not lead the new interest in it. | "It is not promoted by people who actually lived there and have an insider's story to tell." | not given |
