| 224 | Instead, other people use the story of the City in new narratives about Hong Kong society. | "the story of the Walled City has been appropriated to newly constructed narratives about Hong Kong society" | not given |
| 224 | The new narratives are not always coherent, and they do not always tell a clear story. | "These narratives are not necessarily coherent" | not given |
| 224 | Some people link the City with grassroots vibrancy, flexibility and tenacity. | "associate the image of the Walled City with grassroots vibrancy" | not given |
| 224 | For people who saw the City while it stood, the new pictures look like the old pictures turned upside down. | "such portraits of the place look like the old depictions turned upside down" | not given |
| 224 | Some people now see the City, with no distinction, as a model of an old working-class district with good neighbours and strong ties. The writer says that such qualities are overstated and misplaced, like the old myths. | "A lot of qualities attributed to the place are overstated and misplaced" | not given |
| 224 | Many people were afraid of the City, often with no good reason. | "What was once a place of which many people were afraid (often without good reason)" | not given |
| 224 | People now casually see the City as a typical Hong Kong community. The writer says that this makes the City normal again, but mostly for the wrong reasons. | "Such reimagining has restored the normality of the Walled City, but mostly for the wrong reasons." | not given |
| 224 | The new pictures try to show the form of the community and its social life. The writer says that the results look abstract and out of context. | "the outcomes look abstract and out of context" | not given |
| 224 | Thus the new narratives and pictures of the City are as unreal as the old myths and hearsay. | "the newly developed narratives and images of the Walled City are equally unreal" | not given |
| 224 | The writer says that this new picture of the City is a wish for something that Hong Kong as a whole is losing. | "a yearning for something that is disappearing from Hong Kong in general" | not given |
| 224 | In the past, the City was the last place that people expected to be a model local community. | "the Walled City seemed the least likely candidate to be seen as an exemplary local community" | not given |
| 224 | A younger generation with an interest in culture now gives the City new meanings. The writer calls these new myths. | "a younger generation interested in culture" | not given |
| 224 | The writer says that, for these new myths, what was really in the City is not important. | "What was really there is of secondary importance." | not given |
| 224 | Because the City no longer exists, people are free to give it new meanings. | "The fact that the Walled City no longer exists opens itself to creative reinterpretation." | not given |
| 224-225 | The new interest goes with a search for things that Hong Kong lost in the change after 1997. | "the political and socio-economic transition that Hong Kong has undergone since 1997" | not given |
| 225 | The writer finds it unexpected that people now put the City on a pedestal. | "this urban anomaly has been placed on a pedestal" | not given |
| 225 | Most people have no first-hand experience of the City. They never went into it and did not try. Very few have true memories or feelings about it. | "have never set foot in it (or even tried)" | not given |
| 225 | When Hong Kong people think about the City today, much of what they believe comes from myth and legend. | "a great deal of what they hold to be true is based on myth and legend" | not given |
| 225 | The present picture of the City is not fully false, but it is much exaggerated. | "Although you cannot call it a total fabrication, this portrayal of the place is certainly grossly exaggerated." | not given |
| 225 | Very few Hong Kong people truly want to find the facts behind the mystery of the City. | "very few Hong Kong people are truly interested in getting to the bottom of the mystery of Kowloon Walled City" | not given |
| 225 | The writer ends with the view that myths and legends are more interesting than the facts, and that they will stay. | "myths and legends are always more interesting – and they will always endure" | not given |
| 225 | Caption. In some places the roofs of the City were connected to each other. The caption compares them with a wild garden that nobody looked after. | "interconnected rooftops resembled a kind of untended and overgrown mutant garden" | caption (editors); photo by Greg Girard (p356) |
| 225 | Caption. The things on the roofs were electric wires, TV antennae, washing lines, rusty metal grilles and trash that collected there. | "electrical wiring, television antennae, clothes lines, rusting metal grills and accumulating trash" | caption (editors); photo by Greg Girard (p356) |
| 225 | The photo shows a roof in low light under a pink sky (dusk or dawn; the book does not say). A small one-storey room of stained plaster stands on the roof, with two concrete steps in front. One leaf of its window is open. Inside are a turquoise wall, a curtain, a round object with a pointed top (perhaps a clock or a mirror) and an orange bucket or tub. Tall frames hold many TV antennae, and many cables cross the view. Paper, rubbish and a round clay pot lie on the roof. White cloths, probably washing, hang behind a grille at the left. Some shapes behind that grille can be people, but the photo is too dark to be sure. | (no words: photo) | photo on the page (primary); my reading |

### Part 7. Ian Lambot, "Myth and Reality", with its captions (pp270–285)

Writer: Ian Lambot (essay, 2014); the editors (captions). Checked on 2026-11-16: 125 rows checked, 27 fixed, 0 deleted, 17 added. 117 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 270 | Lambot's Hong Kong friends, all educated in the West, were afraid for him when he told them that he photographed inside the City. They had never been inside. | "We might never see you again," | own visit (Lambot's memory) |
| 270 | The friends saw the City only from a distance, when they went to the Thai restaurants of Kowloon City (九龍城). | "occasional visits to Thai restaurants in nearby Kowloon City" | own visit |
| 270 | The friends heard about the City's bad name from childhood. They believed that most of the terrible stories were true. | "drummed into them from an early age" | own visit |
| 270 | Nearly everybody in Hong Kong, and all of "polite" society, thought that the City was a bad and dangerous place. People did not go there and seldom spoke of it. | "a bad and dangerous place never to be visited and only rarely mentioned" | Lambot's view |
| 270 | Lambot and Girard first visited the City in the early 1980s. Residents gave them hostile stares and shut their doors. Lambot thinks that they took the two English speakers for officials. | "we both encountered hostile stares and hastily shut doors" | own visit |
| 270 | After the clearance was announced (the page gives no date), most residents accepted the photographers. They let them take photos at work and at home. The residents did not understand the interest in their lives. | "if not cheerful, at least accepting" | own visit |
| 270 | Except for the bad alleys, the City felt like the other poor areas of Hong Kong. Lambot calls it a small model of Hong Kong. | "apart from the terrible state of the alleys, it felt little different to other low-income areas of Hong Kong" | own visit |
| 270 | Lambot says that the City as a town first started just after the Second World War. | "which first appeared in the immediate aftermath of the Second World War" | not given |
| 270 | Up to the early 1930s, squatters lived in the old buildings or in rough houses. Local farmers kept their animals there. | "local farmers used it as a handy place to pen their livestock, mainly chickens and pigs" | not given ("As is explained elsewhere"; INFERRED: probably the Wilkinson essay) |
| 270 | The City was an extension of Kowloon City, an old Chinese town at the head of Kowloon Bay. The Hong Kong authorities made few rules even there. | "the Hong Kong authorities chose to impose few regulations" | not given |
| 270 | The reclamation into Kowloon Bay started in 1923 and later became Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場). It brought the Government's attention to the area. | "started in 1923 and which subsequently became Kai Tak Airport" | not given |
| 270 | In 1935 the Government cleared the City of its residents, against the objections of China. It planned to make the old fort a place for visitors and for the study of old things. Then war came: a few years later Japan occupied Hong Kong. | "a place of popular resort and antiquarian interest" | not given (a quoted phrase with no source) |
| 270 | After 1945 the Government lost confidence and British power in Asia fell. Thus the Government had to act with more care. | "would have to tread a far more cautious path" | not given |
| 270 | The war badly damaged the buildings of Hong Kong. The housing that was left was not enough for the people who came back after the war. | "The territory's building stock had suffered terribly during the war" | not given |
| 271 | Population of Hong Kong: less than half a million in July 1945; near one million at the end of 1946; just over two million by 1950; one million more in the 1950s. | "ending the decade at just over two million" | not given |
| 271 | Many refugees went to the City. It had old, run-down buildings near the Chinese town of Kowloon City. | "its supply of existing if run-down buildings" | not given |
| 271 | Kowloon City suffered in the war, from the Japanese extension of the airfield and from bombing. By 1946 its fast rebuilding was in progress, on a new grid of streets south of the Walled City. These streets still define the area. | "rapidly rebuilt around a grid of new streets to the south of the Walled City" | not given |
| 271 | Housing was too short for the refugees. Temporary buildings went up on any free land. | "temporary buildings began springing up wherever there was available land" | not given |
| 271 | The Government saw the squatter settlements as illegal but let them stay, under strict rules made over the next years. Refugees kept coming into the 1970s. | "it was decided, though under strict guidelines" | not given |
| 271 (caption) | The "Triad Room" of the Hong Kong Police Museum (警隊博物館) holds Triad objects and items taken from opium dens and drug smugglers. The photo shows glass cases with two mannequins in Triad robes (one red with a headband, one black with a red sash and a white cord). The cases also hold swords, red banners with Chinese writing, a red hoop and khaki vests. Low cases hold a long pipe, tins, boxes and a vase. | "full of Triad paraphernalia and items confiscated from opium dens and drug smugglers" | museum photo by Lambot (p356), undated |
| 272 | In January 1948 the Government tried to stop a squatter settlement inside the old City boundary. It started an operation to pull down all the huts and evict the people. The walls were gone. | "in January 1948 it launched an operation to demolish all the illegal huts there and evict the residents" | not given (see McHugh, p330, *China Mail* 13 Jan 1948) |
| 272 | Lambot says that the failure of the 1948 clearance shaped the growth of the City for the next 40 years. | "the failure of that clearance operation was to have ramifications" | Lambot's view |
| 272 | Lambot asks if it was better to let the City grow under the same rules as the other squatter areas. He calls the 1948 decision inexplicable in hindsight. | "might it not have been better to let the area develop under the same guidelines imposed on all other squatter settlements?" | Lambot's view |
| 272 | The 1898 Convention let the Chinese officials stay in the City. They were expelled within a year. An Order in Council of 1899 made the City part of the Colony. China never accepted the Order. | "the Chinese officials now stationed there shall continue to exercise jurisdiction" | 1898 Convention; Order in Council 1899 (quoted) |
| 272 | 1948: talks with the Chinese Government failed. The idea of the International Court came up. The Law Officers thought that Hong Kong's case rested on military security and was uncertain to win. The Government chose to let the matter lie. | "letting the matter lie" | CO 537/4807 (1948), as quoted |
| 272 | Lambot: after 1948, Government authority in the City was very small. | "certainly minimal" | Lambot's view |
| 272 | March 1953: the Commissioner of Police feared the result of a court case for a crime in the City. It can bring a challenge, propaganda, or a Chinese demand for their own court there. | "most unsatisfactory" | CO 1030/394 (March 1953), as quoted |
| 272 (caption) | Opium smoking in Hong Kong in the early 1950s was a task for two men. One man held the bead of opium resin over an oil lamp. The other breathed the smoke through a pipe. A boy or the den owner helped a man who came alone. The photo shows two men at a table with a glass lamp, a cup and scissors on a plate. It does not say that the place is in the City. | "It was a two-man operation" | Getty/Hulton photo, early 1950s |
| 273 | Police patrols went on as normal in the City, except for a few short periods. | "police patrols had continued as normal" | CO 1030/394 (1953), as summarised |
| 273 | The police did not take cases to court. They took the drugs and deported some offenders. Most people that they arrested went free after one or two nights in the cells. | "most of those arrested were released after spending just a night or two in the cells" | CO 1030/394 (1953), as quoted, and Lambot's summary |
| 273 | Only the worst offenders were deported. China took no action against them, thus many came back to Hong Kong. | "many eventually found their way back to Hong Kong" | not given |
| 273 | By the early 1950s illegal businesses of all kinds moved into the City. The police were able to disturb them but not to close them, and most people avoided court. | "In many ways it was a criminal nirvana" | not given |
| 273 | November 1954 police survey: 120 narcotic divans, 20 brothels, five gambling houses (with other things). | "120 narcotic divans, 20 brothels and five gambling houses" | Savingram 11 Mar 1955, CO 1030/934, quoting a police report |
| 273 | 1952 police count: 154 drug divans, seven gambling dens, dog-meat shops, 11 brothels, one theatre for 300 people with two striptease shows a day. The essay prints 15 dog-meat shops; the memo on p275 prints 13. | "15 dog-meat shops" | same report (memo of 1955) |
| 273 | The theatre was the Lung Kung. Full houses. By the middle of February 1953, three more theatres were open, each more daring. | "this theatre, known as the Lung Kung, was then playing to capacity audiences" | same report, as quoted |
| 273 | Fire of 24 February 1953 in the City. Vice operators bought the burnt sites at high prices. By 17 March 1953 eight new theatres stood on the fire site, and four shops were theatres: 16 in all. | "a golden opportunity for vice operators who bought up building sites at very high prices" | same report, as quoted |
| 273 | The Chinese-language newspapers wrote about the theatres and the dog-meat shops. People came from all parts of the Colony. | "the place was thronged with patrons from all parts of the Colony" | same report, as quoted |
| 273 | Many Hong Kong people in the 1950s visited the City for its pleasures. Lambot compares the glamour of crime then to Frank Sinatra in Las Vegas, and to Barbara Windsor and the Kray brothers in the UK. | "many Hong Kong residents were happy to sample the City's delights" | Lambot's view |
| 273 | Luxury cars stood at night along Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道). People said that some belonged to film stars. | "the number of luxury cars that could be found parked at night along Tung Tau Tsuen Road" | "Contemporary records" (not named) and residents interviewed for the 2014 edition |
| 273 (caption) | A simple opium pipe, probably taken in a raid on an opium den. The pipe held no opium. It drew in the smoke from a heated bead of resin. Photo: a long wooden pipe with a pale mouthpiece and a dark bowl, on a clear stand in a museum case. Panels with black triangles hang behind it. | "known in Chinese as 'chasing the dragon'" | museum photo by Lambot (p356), undated |
| 274 (caption) | The 1955 memo went from the Commissioner of Police, through the Governor, to the Secretary of State for the Colonies on 4 March 1955. | "sent to the Secretary of State for the Colonies on 4 March 1955" | the memo (pp274-275) |
| 274 (caption) | The caption says that the memo disproves the myth that the police never entered the City, or entered only in force. Except for a short time in the late 1940s and early 1950s, two-man foot patrols went through the City. | "two-man foot patrols" | the memo, as read by the caption writer |
| 274 (caption) | Thus the authorities knew well what happened in the City, but they were limited in what they were able to do about it. | "allowing the authorities to build up a very accurate picture of what was going on there" | the memo, as read by the caption writer |
| 275 (caption) | The police arrested people and took illegal goods, but did not prosecute, for fear of the Chinese Government. Most people stayed in the cells for a few days. The worst offenders were expelled. A murder in 1958 changed this. | "held in police cells for a few days" | the memo, as read by the caption writer |
| 276 (caption) | A rebuilt Triad altar in the museum. It is made from banners, flags and other items that police took from a Triad meeting hall in a raid in 1978. The caption does not say where the raid was. "(opposite)" points to the Triad ceremony photo on p277. | "assembled using banners, flags and other items seized from just such a meeting hall during a raid in 1978" | museum display; photo by Lambot (p356), undated |
| 276 (caption) | The caption says that "Triad" is a British word. The word probably started in Hong Kong, from the many triangles in all Triad ceremonies. The triangles refer to the "Three Harmonies Society" (earth, heaven and man). | "In Chinese they are more commonly known as 'Dark' or 'Black Societies'." | not given |
| 276 (photo) | The altar holds flags, candlesticks, small red cups, a hoop with red paper teeth, bamboo and pine branches and a coconut. The large cloth carries the words 秉正除奸 (my reading of the photo), with names and dates in ink. | (no words in the text) | photo by Lambot |
| 276 (photo) | Below 秉正除奸, the cloth has columns of names in ink. Each name has a place and a date in the sixty-year cycle, for example 廣東 and 乙卯年九月, and one 上海 (my reading). INFERRED: a list of members. | (no words in the text) | photo by Lambot |
| 276 (photo) | Behind the altar, a red banner lists names in two groups, with the heads 前五祖 and 後五祖 (my reading), for example 蔡德忠 and 林永超. Side banners carry large characters, for example 有忠心方可入 and 切勿拈香 (my reading). The flags carry single characters, for example 金 and 土. A small red banner reads 三軍司命. | (no words in the text) | photo by Lambot |
| 277 | Former residents remember Japanese "sex" tours in the 1950s. Groups of Japanese men went into a striptease theatre and came out later, laughing. | "groups of Japanese men being led into one of the striptease theatres and emerging, giggling and joshing, some time later" | residents interviewed for the 2014 edition |
| 277 | The illegal trades and the ordinary residents lived apart. Neither side interfered with the other. | "neither side interfering with the other" | residents interviewed for the 2014 edition |
| 277 | Residents who were children in the 1950s remember a safe place. They played in the alleys and walked to school without fear, even of the young Triad members. | "where they could play in the alleys and come and go on their way to school without any sense of menace" | residents interviewed for the 2014 edition |
| 277 | Triads were always present. A police report says that the Triad societies of the City lived by extortion from the illegal trades. Police used secret deportation. When police broke up one society, a new one took its place. | "this area has been a breeding ground for Triad activities" | "The same police report quoted above" (1953 or 1955; not clear) |
| 277 | Lambot gives the origin of the Triads in the 17th century: the Hung League against the Qing. | "the Hung League" | not given |
| 277 | A police report of 2000 counts about 50 Triad societies in Hong Kong. Police met 15 of them often: San Yee On, 14K, Wo Shing Wo, Wo Hop To and Wo On Lok are the main ones. | "around 50 known Triad societies in Hong Kong" | police report, 2000 |
| 277 | The same report: only about four per cent of reported crime in Hong Kong was Triad crime. Lambot says that the share was much higher in the 1950s. | "only around four per cent was considered Triad related" | police report, 2000 |
| 277 | Lambot: Triad gangs are local street gangs with Triad ranks and rites, not a central organisation like the Mafia or the Yakuza. They live on extortion, street drug sales, prostitutes, pornography, loan sharking and debt collection, and later pirated goods and false money and credit cards. They fight for their area, even against gangs of the same Triad. | "certainly no criminal masterminds" | not given |
| 277 | For most of the City's life, two or more Triads worked in the City at the same time. | "there were usually two or more Triads operating in the Walled City and gang fights were always a problem" | not given |
| 277 | Lambot thinks that many City people were criminals in the 1950s, but the two sides found a balance. | "the proportion of ne'er-do-wells to honest residents must have been very high" | Lambot's guess |
| 277 (photo) | A black-and-white photo of a Triad ceremony: men sit in rows on each side of a room in front of an altar. One man in white sits beside the altar; one man stands at the other side. The altar wall is faced with stone, and a cloth with large characters hangs on the altar table. Three round mats lie on the floor. Ceiling fans and strip lights hang above. It has no caption of its own and no date. The p276 caption calls the place "a Triad meeting hall (opposite)". | (no caption) | Hong Kong Police Museum photo (p356) |
| 278 | February 1958: a murder in the City. The 1953 police report foretold this case. | "a murder was committed within the Walled City that required a more robust response" | CO 1030/394, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | The killer was Chan Hing, a Chinese man who was not a British subject. He admitted the murder. The Foreign Office told its posts to say nothing. | "On February 27 a murder took place within the Kowloon Walled City." | Foreign Office secret cypher, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | The Foreign Office thought that this was the first murder in the City. It was not: the police had investigated three earlier cases. | "three previous cases investigated by the police" | letter to the Colonial Office, 28 June 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | Case 1, 25 October 1953: a prostitute who lived in the City with a man was found dead in a well about 300 yards outside the City. They had quarrelled. She died of drowning. The Supreme Court found the man not guilty. | "found dead in a well some 300 yards distant from the Walled City" | letter of 28 June 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | The letter says that this case was not strictly a murder in the City, because the well was outside it. But the case was closely connected with the City. | "This was therefore not strictly a case of murder in the Walled City, though it was closely connected with it." | letter of 28 June 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | Cases 2 and 3, both 1955: Triad attacks and fights. The evidence was weak. Police arrested 7 men in the first case and 20 in the second. Eight men were deported for life. | "a total of eight were deported for life" | letter of 28 June 1958, CO 1030/810, as summarised |
| 278 | July 1958: after four months of talks, the Government took Chan Hing to court. China did not object. He was found guilty and executed. | "Chan Hing was found guilty and duly executed" | CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | The Hong Kong Government took a calculated risk. It thought that China was not able to step in easily for such a clear case. | "It was a calculated risk" | not given (Lambot's summary of CO 1030/810) |
| 278 | August 1958: the murder took place "at an address within the Walled City". A second man, Chan Yuen, was also accused of murder in the City. Nobody raised the question of jurisdiction. | "likewise accused of murder within the Walled City" | Governor's letter, August 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 | The Governor proposed to let the police act slowly, case by case, first against drugs, the exploitation of prostitutes, theft and violence. | "to authorise the police to take action gradually on a case to case basis" | Governor's letter, August 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 278 (caption) | Tung Tau Tsuen Road in 1963: low buildings. The bigger ones copy the usual two- or three-storey shophouses of Hong Kong. Nothing shows the activities inside. | "the more substantial units modelled on the typical two- or three-storey shophouses" | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1963 |
| 278 (photo) | The photo shows a corner building with the sign of Hing Fat Bakery (興發餅家; the repo reads it so, `data/room_research.json` D27), a barber's pole, many overhead wires and laundry on the roofs. | (no words in the text) | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1963 |
| 279 | The Governor said that this plan put any defender of the criminals in a poor moral light and made Chinese propaganda harder. | "placing in a poor moral light anyone seeking to defend those prosecuted on purely jurisdictional grounds" | Governor's letter, August 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 279 | The Governor did not want a strict rule that the police act only against these crimes. After the start of law enforcement, a clear division can be impossible. | "not wish to make it a hard and fast rule" | Governor's letter, August 1958, CO 1030/810 |
| 279-280 | 27 December 1958: the Secretary of State agreed. He hoped that the police can act with no new law and with little publicity. | "Conditions in the Walled City are so notorious" | secret telegram, 27 Dec 1958 |
| 279 | The Secretary of State expected no major trouble from China, after the two murder cases. If the first stage passed with no protest, police action will become usual practice. Then China will find it harder to raise the question of jurisdiction. | "it would become progressively less easy for the Chinese to raise the issue of jurisdiction" | secret telegram, 27 Dec 1958 |
| 279 (caption) | Sai Tau Tsuen (西頭村), the squatter settlement beside the City, in 1978. It was an "authorised" settlement and was cleared in 1985. Its ramshackle buildings were four and five storeys high. The caption says that it probably looked like the City of the 1950s, at the height of its "den of iniquity" years. | "An 'authorised' settlement that was only finally cleared in 1985" | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1978 |
| 279 (photo) | The photo looks down on a wide road with cars, a bicycle and people with umbrellas. Beyond it are many low shacks with tin roofs, heaps of sand, a stack of poles and tall blocks. | (no words in the text) | photo by Ko Tim Keung, 1978 |
| 280 | 31 December 1958: Governor Sir Robert Black reported a police raid of 26 November on a "heroin pill manufactory and divan". Police took heroin pipes, lamps, 2184 pills and enough pill mass for 30,000 pills. They arrested 24 people and freed 17 with no charge. He proposed to prosecute five of the other seven: for keeping a heroin divan, for having dangerous drugs and, for two, for Triad membership. | "2184 pills and sufficient pill mass for the manufacture of 30,000 pills" | Governor's message, 31 Dec 1958 |
| 280 | London gave its authority. Lambot says that the "Golden Age" of crime in the City then ended, slowly. | "this "den of iniquity" would be no more" | Sir Robert Black's words, as quoted |
| 280 | In the early 1960s the City started its "second stage". In Hong Kong the population rose threefold in 12 years. The Government started a large public housing programme. | "the threefold rise in population in just 12 years" | not given |
| 280 | Government pay fell behind prices. Police and civil servants took second jobs. Bribes became usual in the police and the civil service. | "corruption was ever present like a speeding bus" | "one senior police officer at the time" (not named) |
| 280 | The Government was able to do little against the corruption, and accepted it. | "perhaps with a feeling that this was the way things were done in this part of the world" | not given |
| 280 | With paid police, criminals moved to richer parts of town. | "Now, they could set up shop wherever they chose" | not given |
| 280-281 | The City stayed a haven for crime, like other squatter areas, because its alleys made arrests hard. A raid needed 30 or 40 officers to close all exits. Lookouts warned the targets before the police arrived. | "the 30 or 40 police officers necessary to cut off all means of escape" | not given |
| 280 (caption) | A police surveillance photo taken from the roof of a tall City building. It looks down on three lookouts in an alley of Sai Tau Tsuen. Police records hardly separate the two areas: crime went on in both. | "looking down on a trio of lookouts posted in one of the alleys in the adjacent Sai Tau Tsuen squatter settlement" | police photo (Nicholas Millman, p356), undated |
| 280 (photo) | The photo looks down on tiled and tin roofs, a striped awning, a stone house with small windows and many loose wires. A person stands in the gap between the roofs. | (no words in the text) | police photo (Nicholas Millman, p356), undated |
| 281 | Lambot thinks that these large raids started the myth that the police entered the City only in large numbers. | "the persistent myth that the police only dared to enter the City in large numbers" | Lambot's view |
| 281 | Police and criminals made a sort of pact. Police let one illegal business go and raided another opium den or brothel, so that arrest numbers stayed up. The criminals left goods to seize and a few addicts or prostitutes who agreed to a few days in jail. | "a pact of sorts was established between the police and the criminal fraternity" | not given |
| 281 | Seized goods often went missing from police stores and came back to the City. An opium pipe, signed as seized in one raid, turned up in a later raid in another place. The arresting officer always marked each seized item. | "such items were always marked at the time of confiscation by the arresting officer" | "One police officer we spoke to" (not named) |
| 281 | In the 1960s crime in the City became less important and much less open. Population: about 10,000 in 1960; near 30,000 by the end of the 1960s. Thus the share of criminals fell. By the end of the 1960s the City was like any working-class area, and many residents found it safer. | "from around 10,000 in 1960 to somewhere closer to 30,000 by the end of the decade" | not given |
| 281 | The main problems for residents in the 1960s: sewage in the alleys, which made them almost impassable from the smell in hot weather, and many very poor drug addicts. | "which often overran with sewage and became nigh on impassable, due to the smell if nothing else, during hot weather" | not given |
| 281 | Addicts came because drugs were cheap and the police did not move them on. Many died in the alleys. Residents walked past the bodies. | "many residents remember walking past the dead bodies" | residents' memories (interviews) |
| 281 | The Kai Fong Residents' Association (the Kai Fong Association, 九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會, in the repo) started in 1962. It or the Urban Services Department (市政總署) took the bodies away. | "Kai Fong Residents' Association (which had been set up in 1962)" | not given |
| 281 | At about the same time (about 1962), the Urban Services Department started to take household refuse from collection points inside the City. | "who had started to remove household refuse from collection points within the City at around the same time" | not given |
| 281 | The usual procedure was to move each body to a collection point by the public lavatories at the edge of the City. Special teams picked them up there. Many USD workers objected to this work. Nobody knows the number of deaths. It happened often in the 1960s and into the 1970s. | "a collection point by the public lavatories on the outskirts of the City" | personal memories of the people interviewed |
| 281 | 1974: the Government set up the Independent Commission Against Corruption (ICAC) (廉政公署). | "in 1974 the Government announced the creation of an Independent Commission Against Corruption (ICAC)" | not given |
| 281 (caption) | Another police surveillance photo, looking down on a heroin "clinic". A line of men wait for their injection. Before a raid, lookouts made all signs go away. The caption does not say if the clinic is in the City or in Sai Tau Tsuen. | "an eager line-up of punters awaiting their injection" | police photo (Nicholas Millman, p356), undated |
| 281 (photo) | The photo looks down into an alley past a corrugated roof, a tiled roof, TV aerials and many wires. A group of men stands close together in the alley below. | (no words in the text) | police photo (Nicholas Millman, p356), undated |
| 282 | After the ICAC started, many police cleaned up, and criminal businesses went into hiding. They did not move in force to the City: in the 1960s they had learnt to work at the edge of the law. Gambling and prostitution were able to stay in the open. Drugs went into hiding. | "heroin which had by now almost completely usurped opium as the drug of choice" | not given |
| 282 | 1978: many police officers rebelled against the ICAC. The Government gave an amnesty for small corruption. Only the worst cases went to court. Many officers resigned or retired early. | "following a rebellion in 1978 by many police officers against the ICAC's tactics" | not given |
| 282 | More arrests after 1974 made drug dealers change their methods. For some, the City was a ready answer. From the end of the 1970s into the early 1980s, drug arrests in the City rose a lot. | "the Walled City saw a significant rise in the number of drug-related arrests" | not given |
| 282 | In the 1950s the City was the main source of illegal drugs and vice, but not the only one. | "if not quite the sole" | not given |
| 282 | By the 1980s the City was one of several drug places. Others were the back streets of Hung Hom market and the squatter area above Tin Hau. The police knew them and let them be, to keep watch. | "the squatter settlement in the hills above Tin Hau" | not given |
| 282 | Hong Kong law at that time: police were not able to arrest an addict for addiction, only for carrying drugs. Those arrests gave police news of the dealers. | "a person could not be arrested if they were a drug addict, only if they were caught carrying drugs about their person" | not given |
| 282 | Dealers, mostly Triad members, opened drug "clinics". Addicts got their injection there and left with nothing on them. In the alleys, the dealers packed up before the police came. | "so-called drug 'clinics'" | not given |
| 282 | The police learnt to close a clinic by disturbing it. The squatter areas for such clinics were going. Sai Tau Tsuen was cleared in 1985, one of the last. | "in 1985, being one of the last" | not given |
| 282 (caption) | The City had prostitution, gambling, drug dealing and drug taking until the end. | "prostitution, gambling and drug dealing (and taking) could be found in the Walled City until the very end" | not given |
| 282 (photo) | A young man with no shirt crouches in a dark, stained corner. He holds a spoon near a lit red candle. A cup and a plastic water bottle stand beside him. INFERRED: he prepares a drug. | (no words in the text) | photo by Greg Girard, undated |
| 283 | Lambot: the true "den of iniquity" lasted only a short time in the 1950s. After that, crime in the City was probably no worse than in many parts of Hong Kong. | "brief period in the 1950s" | "as the record shows" (the files above) |
| 283 | Lambot: the surprise is how little crime the City had for most of its life. He wants to go back and see each period of the City. | "for the majority of its existence there was so little" | Lambot's view |
| 283 | Lambot: the myths last and grow more lurid with time. He thinks that many people secretly like the idea of a place where the normal rules do not apply. | "growing ever more lurid, it seems, as time passes" | Lambot's view |
| 283 (caption) | 1990: a two-man police patrol crosses the roof of the City. The caption says that magazine articles still repeat the myth. The police patrolled the City and Sai Tau Tsuen from the early 1950s; from the middle 1960s, at least once in each eight-hour shift. | "from the mid-'60s at least once during each eight-hour shift" | not given |
| 283 (photo) | The photo shows two officers in uniform and berets on the roof at sunset. A barefoot man stands on a roof box beside a sheet hung to dry. TV aerials, bamboo poles and corrugated sheets cover the roof. Two walls carry painted white numbers: one reads 45, a longer mark ends in 46 (my reading). INFERRED: the numbers can be clearance survey marks. | (no words in the text) | photo by Greg Girard, 1990 |
| 284 (photo, no caption) | A night photo of a street lined with shop and clinic signs. The shutters are down. At the lower left a food stall has folding tables, basins and stools; a railing runs along the kerb. The repo places these signs on Tung Tau Tsuen Road at Nos 17-19 (`data/room_research.json` D30-D38). | (no caption) | photo by Ian Lambot (p356), undated |
| 284 (photo) | Signs that I can read: 楊永光牙科 (Yeung Wing Kwong Dentistry), 許熾昌牙科 (Hui Chi Cheong Dentistry, three signs), 楊泰昌士多 (Yeung Tai Cheong Store), 萬國診所 (with 西醫梅立廉主診, 內兒婦科, X光化驗, 皮膚毒症, 花柳白濁 and 18號二樓; the 號 is blurred), 蕭美美婦科 (18號二樓), 美東醫學化驗中心 (Mei Tung Medical Laboratory Centre), 中華牙科診所, 西醫陳詠蘭, 柔濟診所, 郭衛人醫師 (with 內外婦兒科 and 花柳白濁 皮膚雜症), "...G CHOW SHOES" with ...洲皮鞋, and 松發冰室 under a Coke sign. | (signs in the photo) | photo by Ian Lambot |
| 285 (photo, no caption) | A night view along a road beside the high blocks of the City. Many windows are lit. Street lamps and car light trails show a busy road. Signs include 孫仰先牙科, 莫斯錄牙室, 大眾診所 and 王偉權 (the repo reads the first three in D39-D41, and has 王偉權牙科 at Nos 30-31 in `data/businesses.json`). Other signs that I can read: 西醫, a vertical sign that starts 黃景, and a horizontal sign 城寨平民 (the rest is cut off). A white van with the plate DS 2037 (my reading) stands at the kerb. | (no caption) | photo by Ian Lambot, undated |

### Part 8. Fionnuala McHugh, "The Walled City Clearances", first half (pp328–339)

Writer: Fionnuala McHugh (essay, 2014); the editors (captions). Checked on 2026-11-16: 159 rows checked, 16 fixed, 1 deleted, 11 added. 127 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 328 | Standfirst: McHugh has lived in Hong Kong since 1993. She writes for *The Daily Telegraph*, the *South China Morning Post* and *Modern Weekly*. | "Fionnuala McHugh has lived in Hong Kong since 1993." | standfirst |
| 328 | The authorities tried to clear the City many times. The first clearance was in 1899, less than a year after the Convention of June 1898. | "They had tried to clear the Walled City many times." | not given |
| 328 | The fort was at first a small administrative post on the south coast of the empire. After Britain got Hong Kong Island in 1842, China made the post stronger and built the wall. | "Among other upgrades, they'd built a wall around it." | not given |
| 328 | McHugh compares China in the 1898 lease to a careful landlord. China kept the City as one locked room in the leased house, to show whose house it was. | "keep one room locked within the house, inaccessible to the tenant" | not given (McHugh's image) |
| 328 | The 1898 Convention let the Chinese officials in the City keep their jurisdiction, with one limit. In fact this arrangement lasted 11 months, but its effect lasted for most of the 99 years of the lease. | "except so far as may be inconsistent with the military requirements for the defence of Hong Kong" | Convention of June 1898 (the Second Convention of Peking) |
| 328 | In 1899 the British expelled the first inhabitants. They left in junks and did not fight much. | "sailed off in their junks without putting up much of a fight" | not given |
| 328 | An Order in Council at the end of 1899 made the City a part of the colony. | "part and parcel of His Majesty's Colony of Hong Kong" | Order in Council, end of 1899 (no reference) |
| 328 | Li Hung Chang, Viceroy of Guangdong and Guangxi, passed through Hong Kong in July 1900. The authorities told him the new position. A Colonial Office minute then closed the subject. | "the matter is at an end" | Colonial Office minute, 1900 (no reference) |
| 328 | The second clearance started on 10 June 1933. A Government notice set the resumption (compulsory acquisition) of the land for the end of 1934. | "on 10 June 1933 a second clearance began when the Government issued a notice stipulating resumption of land" | Government notice of 10 June 1933 (no reference) |
| 328 | McHugh gives the area of the City as 6.5 acres. | "the 6.5-acre enclave" | not given |
| 328 | By 1933 the City had a bad name, and McHugh says that it kept this name to its end. | "an image which would endure even after it became dust: it was unsavoury" | not given |
| 328 | In 1933 the City was no longer near the sea, because of reclamation. Squatters lived in it with their livestock. The Government gave hygiene as the reason for the clearance. | "inhabited by squatters who had filtered inside with their livestock" | not given |
| 328 | The City had 436 residents in 1933. | "There were 436 residents, to use that term loosely." | not given |
| 328 | The residents asked China for help. A year later the British offered new houses. China sent an envoy from Canton to stop the residents, but all accepted except one. | "apart from one who held out – the first in a very long line" | not given |
| 328 | China still objected in July 1935. By 1940 one private dwelling was left: the home of a Church of England minister. | "by 1940 the only private dwelling was blamelessly occupied by a Reverend Kwong, a Church of England minister" | not given |
| 328 | The British planned to make the City a place for visitors. The Japanese, who held Hong Kong from 1941, had no interest in the plan. | "a place of popular resort and antiquarian interest" | a British plan that McHugh quotes (no source given) |
| 328 | By 1945 the stones of the wall were in a new sea wall for the larger Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場). | "by 1945, its stones were part of a new sea wall marking the expansion of Kai Tak Airport nearby" | not given |
| 328-329 | By 1947 refugees from the civil war in China moved into the City. It was the one area where China still claimed rights. McHugh calls this an anomaly: people who fled the jurisdiction of China settled where China had a claim. | "refugees from China's civil war were heading for Hong Kong" | not given |
| 328 | Caption: an 1898 photo shows the roofs of old Kowloon City (九龍城), with the walls of the military compound far behind. The photo is from an album made for the signing of the 1898 Convention. | "known even then as the Walled City" | caption; photo credit on p356 (National Archives, UK) |
| 329 | On 27 November 1947 the Government gave the new squatters notice to quit. They refused. The Government got an eviction order from a magistrate. | "On 27 November 1947, these new squatters were given notice to quit." | not given; the "Statement" column of the *China Mail* on p330 seems to tell the same sequence ("Nov. 27", a magistrate's order, "Jan. 5"), but the small print is blurred and I cannot confirm it word for word |
| 329 | The Government gave more warnings and offered free alternative sites. The squatters took no notice. On 5 January 1948 the Government dismantled the huts. | "on 5 January 1948, the 'huts' were dismantled" | not given |
| 329 | The squatters built the huts again at once. On the morning of 12 January 1948 a police party came through a narrow alley for a second attempt. A barrage of stones met the party. | "they were met with a barrage of stones" | not given; the *China Mail* on p330 has a sub-heading "Stones Fly", but its small print is too blurred to confirm the alley |
| 329 | The Government statement said that the police fired warning shots into the ground. It counted five people with minor injuries. A later statement added one person with a wound in the abdomen, possibly from a ricochet. | "for the police to fire warning shots from revolvers into the ground." | two Government statements, January 1948 (no reference) |
| 329 | The *China Mail* told of a pitched battle of 30 minutes. Women and children picked up stones for the men to throw. The police used seven teargas bombs, and six people had gunshot wounds. | "seven teargas bombs were used" | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page (eyewitness account) |
| 329 | After the fight, Lau Chi Leung spoke for the squatters. He was a committee member of the Kowloon Walled City Residents' Association. He said that they will stay until the Chinese Government tells them to go. | "orders to quit" | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page |
| 329 | The same front page had a report from Nanking of an anti-smuggling pact between Hong Kong and China. The pact let Chinese Customs keep inspection centres and staff in Hong Kong. McHugh notes that Customs was a cause of the 1899 trouble. | "shall be at liberty to establish within the territory of Hong Kong inspection centres" | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page |
| 329 | The residents got no order to quit. Instead, they got encouragement to resist more evictions (the book does not say from whom), and Nanking complained officially to London. Students in Shanghai went on strike for two days, and people set fire to the residence of the British consul-general in Canton. | "The students of Shanghai called a two-day strike and the British consul-general's residence in Canton was set on fire." | not given |
| 329 | The colonial authorities stopped the evictions. | "The colonial authorities backed off, brooding." | not given |
| 329 | In late summer 1949 the Hong Kong Government thought again about a clearance and asked for advice. On 27 August 1949 Sir Ralph Stevenson, the British representative in Nanking, sent a telegram to the Foreign Office. | "The continued inability to exercise control of Kowloon City is [a] definite psychological no less than physical disability to the Government of Hong Kong" | telegram of Sir Ralph Stevenson to the Foreign Office, 27 August 1949, "of particular secrecy" (no file number) |
| 329 | Stevenson saw a danger to the security of the colony if the position stayed the same when Canton fell to the Communists. But he feared the results of the "drastic action". He was sure of a hostile reaction, and hoped for no violence like that in Canton in January 1948. He wrote that telegrams from Shanghai showed the British community there to be sensitive to the Hong Kong issue. | "It is difficult not (repeat not) to feel embarrassed on this score" | Stevenson telegram, 27 August 1949 |
| 329 | Caption: a 1933 photo shows the City when the Government announced the second clearance. Many buildings were empty and in bad repair. Only the yamen (衙門), on the left, was in fair order. The photo shows two old cannon barrels on the ground. A person carries two buckets on a shoulder pole, and a child stands at the right edge (my reading of the photo). | "Many of the buildings at this time were deserted and in a serious state of disrepair" | caption; photo credit on p356 (Public Records Office, HK) |
| 330 | Caption: the page reproduces the front page of the *China Mail* of 13 January 1948. It reports the riot of 12 January. That day the police tried to enter the City and demolish the huts that the squatters built again after 5 January. After the demonstrations in China and the fire at the British Consulate in Canton, the Government stopped its work in the City. | "led the Hong Kong Government to cease all operations in the Walled City forthwith" | caption |
| 330 | The *China Mail* front page has a photo of the site, taken shortly after the clash. Its caption says that police stand in the background and that demolished huts lie in the foreground. | "some of the demolished huts" | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, photo caption ("'China Mail' Photo"; small print, read on an enlarged crop) |
| 330 | The anti-smuggling pact of 12 January 1948 was an exchange of notes in Nanking. The Chinese Foreign Minister, Wang Shih-chieh, and the British Ambassador, Sir Ralph Stevenson, signed it. My inference: he is the same Stevenson who sent the telegram of 27 August 1949 (same name and post). | "the British Ambassador, Sir Ralph Stevenson" | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, report from Nanking (bold lead, legible on an enlarged crop) |
| 331 | Stevenson thought that the British were able to ask the Chinese Nationalist Government in Canton to agree. The reason to give: the Communists must not use the City. But he feared that it was difficult to find a person discreet enough not to warn the squatters. | "this convenient site for underground activity" | Stevenson telegram, 27 August 1949 |
| 331 | Stevenson suggested a quarantine of the City on a health pretext, temporary but with no end date. The plan had barbed wire round the perimeter, fair notice to the residents, free alternative housing and transport to it. | "Such an expediency would of course deceive nobody (repeat nobody)" | Stevenson telegram, 27 August 1949 |
| 331 | Five weeks later, on 1 October 1949, Mao Tse-tung declared the People's Republic of China. | "Exactly five weeks later, on 1 October 1949" | not given |
| 331 | On 11 January 1950 a large fire went through the Sai Tau Tsuen (西頭村) squatter settlement beside the City. The fire almost destroyed the City and left 17,000 people with no home. The authorities saved enough of the City for it to continue. | "which left 17,000 homeless" | not given |
| 331 | After the fire the City grew. Thousands of people moved in during the years that followed. | "squeezed into its cracks and crevices" | not given |
| 331 | The 1950 fire was the first of the large squatter fires of the 1950s. Hundreds of thousands of people came from China and built squatter camps. The Shek Kip Mei fire of Christmas Day 1953 left about 53,000 people with no home. It started the public housing and resettlement schemes of the colony. | "left around 53,000 people homeless" | not given |
| 331 | The two-day riots in Kowloon and Tsuen Wan started on 10 October 1956 at a resettlement office. An officer of the Resettlement Department took down a Nationalist flag that flew for the Double Tenth. | "a Nationalist flag, flying in honour of the Double Tenth celebrations and taken down by a Resettlement Department officer" | not given |
| 331 | Governor Alexander Grantham wrote of the refugees in his dispatch to the Secretary of State for the Colonies. | "are people who have fled from their homeland and it is not surprising if their fate has engendered a sense of frustration and bitterness" | Grantham's dispatch on the 1956 riots (no reference) |
| 331 | In the 1956 riots 59 people died. Sham Shui Po and the City were the two centres of the riots. The official report noted that these were also the areas with the most crime. | "Fifty-nine people died, including the wife of the Swiss consul-general who was burnt to death in a taxi." | the official report on the 1956 riots (no title given) |
| 331 | The official report called the City the main source of disorder and an old Triad area. | "the highly congested district immediately west of Kai Tak airport which has long been a haunt of Triad Societies" | the official report on the 1956 riots |
| 331 | After a second night of riots, the police raided the City on the afternoon of 12 October 1956 and arrested 200 people. The total for the riots was 6000 arrests. McHugh adds that most people thought a police entry into the City was rare. | "the police raided it on the afternoon of 12 October, arresting 200 people" | not given |
| 331 | The report told why the police found the work hard in such streets. | "The police are at a disadvantage in a scattered game of tip and run in a maze of streets" | the official report on the 1956 riots |
| 331 | McHugh adds that the same Triad members paid the police well, as people learned years later. | "being handsomely paid off by those same Triad members" | not given |
| 331 | The night curfew over the Kowloon peninsula in October 1956 was the first of its type. | "The night curfew-order across the Kowloon peninsula had been the first of its kind" | not given |
| 331-332 | At the end of his dispatch, Grantham compared about 100,000 refugees from Hungary with the refugees in Hong Kong. | "five to six times that number who fled into Hong Kong in 1949-50" | Grantham's dispatch on the 1956 riots |
| 331 | Caption: a photo shows the burnt site of the Shek Kip Mei fire of Christmas Day 1953. The fire changed Government policy. | "left some 53,000 people homeless" | caption; photo credit on p356: "Internet" |
| 332 | In the 1950s the Government worked most of all to house the refugees. It did little about the new arrivals who moved into the City. | "mostly left to their own devices" | not given |
| 332 | Before dawn on a July morning in 1959, a person stabbed the manager of a heroin divan to death in the City. Three men were charged with the murder (the book does not say who charged them). | "a heroin divan manager was stabbed to death within the City" | not given (court case) |
| 332 | One accused man, Wong Hon, challenged the court. He said that he was a Chinese national and that the Supreme Court had no jurisdiction over the City. His lawyer said that the Order in Council was a one-sided act that China never ratified. | "Wong Hon, also known as Wong Wai" | not given (court case) |
| 332 | Chief Justice Sir Michael Hogan ruled against him. The Convention gave only a 'temporary' jurisdiction to the Chinese officials then in the City. They had gone, thus the laws of the colony applied. The court sentenced Wong Hon to death for murder. | "He was hanged in March 1960." | ruling of Chief Justice Sir Michael Hogan (no reference) |
| 332 | In March 1962 the Government announced a demolition plan under the Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme. The printed sentence has a fault (see Doubts). | "it announced that it had plans to demolish Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme" | not given |
| 332 | Residents formed the Kowloon Walled City Anti-Demolition and Anti-Removal Committee at once. | "to fight the government order to the end" | not given (a quote of the Committee) |
| 332 | The Committee had rightists and leftists. The rightists wrote to Taipei and the leftists to Beijing. The Nationalist Government had no diplomatic relations with Britain, thus only the Communists protested to London. | "the rightists appealed to Taipei, the leftists to Beijing" | not given |
| 332 | On 1 January 1963 the West European Affairs Department of the Chinese Foreign Ministry called in the British chargé d'affaires in Peking. It told him of its deep concern. China then told the leftists of the City that the subject was closed. | "On 1 January 1963, the British chargé d'affaires in Peking was summoned" | not given |
| 332 | The Resettlement Department then served clearance notices in the City. The demolition deadlines were 8, 12, 14, 19 and 20 February 1963. | "(on 8, 12, 14, 19 and 20 February)" | not given (the *Hong Kong Standard* clipping on p334 gives the same dates) |
| 332 | The month before, Nikita Khrushchev spoke of Hong Kong and Macau to the Supreme Soviet in Moscow. McHugh says that China had taunted him in public over Cuba, and that people read the remark as a dare to China: in 1961 the Indian army took Goa after 451 years of Portuguese rule. | "there rises a smell that is no better than the stench that rose from colonial Goa." | Khrushchev's speech to the Supreme Soviet (the book gives no date; "the previous month", thus probably December 1962) |
| 332 | The New China News Agency in Hong Kong attacked the time limit for the demolition and resettlement. | "is a gross violation of China's sovereignty and is intolerable" | statement of the New China News Agency, Hong Kong (the book gives no date; about January 1963) |
| 332 | The story got headlines in many countries. The Government then agreed to suspend its plans until more talks. | "a mass rally and strikes were called; the police patrolled the Walled City; there was a climbdown" | not given |
| 332-334 | A secret Colonial Office briefing of later in 1963 said who opposed the plan. The landlords had no legal title. They feared the loss of their profits if the buildings came down and the tenants got resettlement housing. | "a small group of landlords who had no legal title to their buildings or the land on which they stood" | secret briefing, Colonial Office, London, 1963 (no file number) |
| 332 | Caption: a photo shows the committee members of the Kai Fong Residents' Association at their second annual dinner in 1964. The body was first the Kowloon Walled City Anti-Demolition and Anti-Removal Committee. The caption dates the Kai Fong Association to 1962. | "Government plans to demolish the north-west corner of the City to make way for new public housing" | caption; photo credit on p356 (Frankie Lam) |
| 332 | The banner in the photo gives the full Chinese name of the Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會). It calls the group the standing committee of the second term and gives the date 7 June 1964. The photo shows about 20 women and men, many with rosettes. | "九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會第二屆常務委員合影 一九六四年六月七日" | the banner in the photo (primary) |
| 333 | Caption: a 1962 photo shows the City, Kai Tak Airport and Kowloon Bay. The camera was on the small hill above Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道), at the north edge of the City. | "as seen from the top of the small hill rising above Tung Tau Tsuen Road on the City's northern boundary" | caption; photo credit on p356 (Ko Tim Keung 高添強) |
| 333 | Caption: in 1962 most buildings in the City had three or four storeys. A few buildings along Tung Tau Tsuen Road had one or two storeys more. | "most buildings in the City were three or four storeys high" | caption |
| 333 | Caption: by about 1964 taller buildings, as high as nine storeys, started to appear in all parts of the City. | "within two years taller buildings up to nine storeys high were beginning to appear all over the City" | caption |
| 333 | The 1962 photo shows a dense mass of low roofs in the foreground and a few taller blocks at the right. Beyond them are the Kai Tak terminal and the runway that runs out into Kowloon Bay. A tall pole on a tripod stands on the hill in the foreground (my reading of the photo). | (photo, no text) | photo (primary); credit on p356: Ko Tim Keung |
| 334 | The 1963 briefing said that the landlords had little support. Most workers and tenants accepted the resettlement scheme. The briefing says that the scheme gave them much benefit. | "(76 per cent by 9 January 1963) had accepted the scheme" | secret briefing, Colonial Office, 1963 |
| 334 | The briefing said that the colonial Government still feared for public safety and hygiene. If Britain asked China, it was possible that China asked for a share in the scheme. Britain did not accept a Chinese veto, but it was willing to give China the details early. McHugh notes that this happened only "almost a quarter of a century" later, and calls it the first hint of back-room diplomacy since 1899. | "there might be less objection to providing them with details in advance" | secret briefing, Colonial Office, 1963 |
| 334 | The Cultural Revolution started a few years later. | "There was some Communist agitation within the City" | not given |
| 334 | In December 1972 the Public Works Department stopped the construction and the occupation of two illegal buildings beside the City. China said nothing. | "two unfinished, illegal multi-storey buildings on Crown land right next to it" | not given |
| 334 | In 1975 the Government judged two new buildings in the City too high and too near the flight path into Kai Tak. It made closure orders under the Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) ordinances and had the top floors removed. China did not protest. | "had their tops lopped off" | not given |
| 334 | The 1975 action set the height limit in practice for all other buildings in the City. | "setting the de facto height limit for the rest of the Walled City" | not given |
| 334 | Prime Minister Edward Heath planned his first visit to China for 1974. London asked whether he was to speak of the City with Premier Zhou Enlai. Heath was no longer prime minister when he arrived. The Governor, Sir Murray MacLehose, advised against it. | "better not" | advice of Governor MacLehose to London (no reference) |
| 334 | In 1973 the Government made an official list of its services in the City. The list had electricity, sanitation, the Post Office, the Fire Department, the Labour Department, Social Services and others. | "quite extensive supply of services" | Hong Kong Government list of services, 1973 (no reference) |
| 334 | In the same 1973 list, the Buildings Ordinance Office wrote its policy on the City. | "The Buildings Ordinance Office does not take action against illegal structures or extensions to existing buildings." | Hong Kong Government list of services, 1973 |
| 334 | Caption (for the photo on p335): an aerial photo shows the City in 1973, 11 years after the photo on p333. Many buildings were then 12, 13 and 14 storeys high. | "rising to the maximum height of 12, 13 and 14 storeys" | caption; photo credit on p356 (GIS 政府新聞處) |
| 334 | Caption: the population of the City grew to more than three times its size in that period. | "from around 10,000 in 1960 to well over 30,000 in 1973" | caption; source not given |
| 335 | The 1973 aerial photo shows blocks of many heights packed close together, with narrow gaps between them. A curved road with parked cars runs along one edge, and a large site of bare earth lies beyond the road (my reading of the photo; the photo has no labels). | (photo, no text) | photo (primary); credit on p356: Government Information Services, HK |
| 334 | Caption: the page reproduces a clipping from the *Hong Kong Standard* of 19 January 1963. The caption says that China had announced two things. The City belonged to China alone, and China was not going to tolerate interference by the Hong Kong authorities. | "A clipping from the Hong Kong Standard of 19 January 1963" | caption |
| 336 | Caption: the Buildings Ordinance Office kept out of the City in all cases but one. In 1975 a 14-storey building on Tung Tau Tsuen Road was higher than the limit near the flight path. The Office made a closure order, and the top two storeys came off. | "steps were taken to ensure that the top two storeys were removed"; "a 14-storey building appeared on Tung Tau Tsuen Road in 1975" | caption; photo credit on p356 (GIS) |
| 336 | The photo (the caption implies 1975) shows the tall building in bamboo scaffolding, high above lower and older blocks. Shop signs fill the street level. The large sign of the dental clinic 麗新鑲牙室 (written right to left) is at street level, just right of the scaffolded tower. A sign "新華" is on an upper floor at the right edge. Cars stand at the kerb (my reading of the photo). | (photo, no text) | photo (primary); credit on p356: GIS; the page prints the signs, and the clinic name is also in `primary-sources/city-of-darkness-revisited/SOURCE.md` (D29) |
| 336 | Britain and China signed the Joint Declaration on Hong Kong in Beijing on 19 December 1984. It said nothing about the City. | "It had eight paragraphs, three annexes and two memoranda – and none mentioned the Kowloon Walled City." | Joint Declaration, 19 December 1984 |
| 336 | Gordon Jones was then the District Officer for Kowloon City District. His file on the City had the label 'Secret'. He kept it locked in a safe, with the emergency manual for a plane crash at Kai Tak. | "in a safe labelled 'For DO (KC)'s eyes only'" | Gordon Jones (the essay names no interview or date) |
| 336 | Jones visited the City only a few times, because the zone was sensitive. On one tour in summer the smell almost made him vomit. | "the stench was so bad he'd almost been physically sick" | Gordon Jones |
| 336 | Suenn Ho (何巽), an American architect who later wrote a dissertation on the City, gave her own image of it. | "like being in a dinosaur's mouth" | Suenn Ho (no source given) |
| 336 | On 14 April 1986 Donald Tsang called a meeting with Jones and Richard Margolis. Tsang was Deputy Secretary in the General Duties Branch; many years later he became the second Chief Executive of the Hong Kong Special Administrative Region. Margolis was the Deputy Political Adviser. | "He told the men that the Governor, Sir Edward Youde, wanted to clear the Walled City." | Jones or Margolis (not given) |
| 336 | Tsang gave three reasons. The area now had enough housing to resettle the residents. After the handover, China was able to use the bad conditions as propaganda. The City was a serious fire risk and health risk. | "There was now enough housing stock in the area to resettle the residents" | Jones or Margolis (not given) |
| 336 | The two men had to write a paper on a clearance. After changes in Hong Kong, the Government planned to send it to Beijing for talks. | "Jones would look at the logistics side, Margolis at the political dimension" | Jones or Margolis (not given) |
| 336 | The plan had to stay secret. The Government wanted no press leak before it asked China. It also feared that news of a clearance brings new squatters into the City. | "an influx of squatters into the Walled City hoping to claim rehousing benefits and compensation" | Jones or Margolis (not given) |
| 336 | In the Kowloon City District Office (KCDO), only Jones and his personal secretary knew. They marked the letters on the subject 'Confidential'. A 'Secret' mark was rare for the KCDO, and it pointed to the City. | "classified as 'Confidential' rather than 'Secret'" | Gordon Jones |
| 336 | Jones heard that Deng Xiaoping had a special interest in sanitation. He told a young executive officer to photograph the lavatories of the City. The officer did not know the reason. | "to take photographs of the Walled City's lavatories" | Gordon Jones |
| 337 | Jones and Margolis completed the paper in a few days. The next week, on 21 April 1986, Sir Edward Youde chaired a meeting at Government House. The meeting agreed to go ahead with the clearance. | "on 21 April 1986, a meeting chaired by Sir Edward was held in Government House" | not given |
| 337 | McHugh notes the risk: the operation was sure to cost much, and a violent failure in public was possible. Margolis told her why the officials went on. | "a very powerful desire to leave Hong Kong in good shape" | interview with Richard Margolis, "26 years later" (thus about 2012) |
| 337 | Margolis said that Britain did not want China to share the decisions about the residents. The aim was this: if residents protest to China, China tells them that the subject belongs to the Hong Kong administration. | "Then people would get the message the game was up." | interview with Richard Margolis |
| 337 | Margolis said that no one thought of telling China to deal with the City itself, though China had helped to keep it. | "Here's an eyesore you [China] helped to perpetuate, so why don't you deal with it?" | interview with Richard Margolis |
| 337 | Margolis called the City a small problem that was left after the 1997 settlement. | "We had settled the 1997 issue but there was this tiny, little residual problem" | interview with Richard Margolis |
| 337 | Qiao said that the British official chose the banquet as the place for his proposal partly on purpose. | "In hindsight, making his proposals on such an occasion was partly calculation." | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 337 | After the Sino-British agreement, diplomacy replaced the old reflex of China. The British idea of demolition and relocation seemed reasonable to Qiao. | "British hints at demolition and relocation for urban improvement seemed reasonable" | 2011 Chinese television documentary, as McHugh tells it |
| 337 | China agreed. Qiao Zonghuai told his part in a 2011 Chinese television documentary. He was a former vice-minister of Foreign Affairs and a former deputy-manager of the Hong Kong branch of Xinhua. | "At an informal banquet in 1985, a British Government official had mentioned to him a pre-handover plan for the Walled City." | 2011 Chinese television documentary (no title given) |
| 337 | Qiao was surprised, and at first he wanted a hard line. But he saw that the old automatic answer had changed after the Sino-British agreement. He reported the talk to his superiors in Xinhua and in the Sino-British Joint Liaison Group. | "This is not a matter that you propose off the cuff" | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 337-338 | Two days later, senior Xinhua officials called Qiao to a late meeting at night in Hong Kong. They did not tell him the subject before the meeting. | "Our telephones were tapped" | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 337 | Caption: before the announcement, the Government did not let senior civil servants enter the City at all. After the announcement, many went to see the conditions. The photo shows men in shirts and ties with a paper, beside a water point. An old man fills a kettle at a tap and an old woman washes at a pipe (my reading of the photo). | "senior civil servants began flocking there to experience the conditions for themselves" | caption; photo credit on p356 (*Sing Pao Daily News* 成報) |
| 338 | One Xinhua colleague had known the City from the 1950s. He was against the plan: British control of any work in the City, also demolition, put Chinese sovereignty in doubt. | "he described himself as Boss of the Kowloon Walled City" | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 338 | The same man asked why China did not wait. Qiao answered that a bad result can bring street protests and local riots. | "Why, he asked, couldn't China wait until 1997?" | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 338 | Qiao decided that the British wanted a grand exit from each colony, with no dirty areas left. | "grotty, dirty areas" | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 338 | Qiao wrote in his reports that the British always believed in their right and ability to rule the City. A change was now good for the two sides. | "they'd never had the will to do so because of Chinese opposition" | Qiao's reports, as he told them in 2011 |
| 338 | Two men got the task to talk: Qiao and John Boyd. Boyd was a political advisor to Hong Kong and a member of the Joint Liaison Group. At first the talks were informal: Qiao saw Boyd's garden and met one of his three young daughters. | "Qiao visited Boyd's home on Tregunter Path" | 2011 documentary; Sir John Boyd, spring 2014 |
| 338-339 | Boyd spoke to McHugh in the spring of 2014. He was later Ambassador to Japan and Master of Churchill College, Cambridge. He liked and trusted Qiao. | "The British side wanted to keep the transactions informal." | Sir John Boyd, "in the spring of 2014" |
| 338-339 | Boyd said that Qiao also came to his apartment, and that he wanted to keep in touch with Qiao. Boyd said that he had forgotten these efforts. | "I'd forgotten those efforts to give it a human envelope." | Sir John Boyd, spring 2014 |
| 338 | Caption: the clearance was a full surprise to the residents and caused much alarm. Soon posters in and near the City told how the operation was to go. The photo shows men and a boy who read handwritten Chinese posters on a wall in a lane. | "posters began to appear in and around the Walled City, explaining how the operation would proceed" | caption; photo credit on p356 (*Sing Pao Daily News*) |
| 338 | Caption: the process was long. It took more than four years from the first entry of the authorities in January 1987. | "until the first residents began to move out in the summer of 1991" | caption; source not given (see Doubts) |
| 339 | Qiao found the British too formal. They did not enter his office and used a meeting room in the reception area. They did not sign a memorandum. Boyd answered that he wanted less formality, not more. | "simply ridiculous" | 2011 documentary; Sir John Boyd, spring 2014 |
| 339 | Boyd said that the British side did not use formality to stop the process. The Governor wanted speed. | "Teddy Youde had said: Get on with it." | Sir John Boyd, spring 2014 |
| 339 | Each man wrote a summary for his superiors. Qiao asked Boyd to sign the Chinese report as correct, and Boyd agreed with reluctance. Qiao said that this paper is all that remains of the talks on the City. | "I do not think that was a formal diplomatic document." | 2011 Chinese television documentary |
| 339 | Youde died of a heart attack on a visit to Beijing in December 1986. The secret plans went on fast. | "Youde suddenly died of a heart attack on a visit to Beijing, in December 1986" | not given |
| 339 | Before the announcement, the Housing Department (房屋署) made secret visits to the City. Gregory Chan Tak-ping, an assistant director of housing, led them. The authorities still did not know the City well. | "who went deep-cover by removing his tie and asking his Government driver to drop him off a few streets away" | not given |
| 339 | Early on Wednesday 14 January 1987, a fine and sunny morning, the staff and the police assembled. They believed that it was a drill. | "360 staff from the Clearance and Squatter Control sections of the Housing Department and nearly an equal number of policemen" | not given |
| 339 | The staff learned the destination only at 7.30am. When they went into the City, the police closed all its entrances. | "its 83 entrances were cordoned off" | not given |
| 339 | The staff worked in 60 teams of six (360 persons). A plainclothes policeman went with each team. The teams went from door to door and interviewed the occupants. | "the 60 teams of six, each accompanied by a plainclothes policeman" | not given |
| 339 | At the end of the first day the teams had a count. My arithmetic gives about 3.8 persons for each family. | "19,606 people from 5116 families had been registered" | not given |
| 339 | On the first day 22 families refused to give the staff any information about themselves. The teams found no illegal immigrants. | "22 families had refused to reveal anything about themselves to the department staff, and no illegal immigrants had been found" | not given |
| 339 | At the end of the second day (15 January 1987) the operation was complete. The total included the people who worked in the City but lived in other places. | "of just over 33,000" | not given |
| 339 | Caption: before the clearance few outsiders went into the City. In the last phase of the clearance it drew tourists. The colour photo shows a group with round badges on a street, beside a small café with a round table and stools (my reading of the photo). | "even attracting coach parties of Japanese tourists" | caption (secondary); photo credit on p356: Greg Girard ("339(tr)"), thus a primary photo; the caption puts it in "the final phase of the clearance" (no year given) |
| 339 | Caption: an owner had to show proof of ownership to get compensation. Thus owners brought many handwritten contracts, like the one on p116. The officials had to check and approve each one. The photo shows officials with name badges who hold a long handwritten Chinese paper, while a man films them (my reading of the photo). | "resulting in a flood of handwritten contracts" | caption; photo credit on p356 (*Sing Pao Daily News*) |

### Part 9. Fionnuala McHugh, "The Walled City Clearances", second half; the park today; the caption and key of The Map; Acknowledgements; Credits (pp340–356)

Writer: Fionnuala McHugh (essay, 2014); the editors (captions, the map caption); the publisher (pp355–356). Checked on 2026-11-16: 175 rows checked, 57 fixed, 0 deleted, 7 added. 160 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 340 | By then "the whole territory knew" of the clearance (the registration began on 14 January 1987, p 339). At the same time, a park on the site was announced, "for the greater enjoyment of the public". The park was to surround the yamen (衙門), the old magistrate's office. | "a garden of sunlight and air encircling the yamen" | not given |
| 340 | The next day (thus about 15 January 1987, INFERRED from pp 339-340), the Housing Authority set up a Special Committee. It advised on the clearance, the rehousing and the compensation. | "to advise on the clearance, rehousing and the compensation arrangements" | not given |
| 340 | The Special Committee dealt with 28,200 occupants in 8800 apartments in the end. This gives about 3.2 occupants for each apartment (my arithmetic, INFERRED). | "28,200 occupants residing in 8800 separate apartments" | not given |
| 340 | The residents at once lamented the news (the writer: "A sound of lamentation and wailing"). A shopkeeper, Cheung Mak Ching, at first expected China to act. He said that they had been through many clearance plans. | "We'll see what China says to this. We have been through many clearance plans." | not given |
| 340 | The Chinese Foreign Ministry accepted the plan to clear the City and make a park. The statement prints "Hong Kong [sic]". | "Like other parts of Hong Kong, the Kowloon Walled City is a question left over from history." | statement of the Chinese Foreign Ministry, date not given |
| 340 | In February 1991, the Hong Kong High Court ruled on a Walled City case. An adjudicator of the Small Claims Tribunal had refused to hear a Walled City dispute because, he said, China controlled the City. The Court said that he was wrong. Thus a Walled City dispute reached the Hong Kong courts by 1991 (the essay does not say what the dispute was about). | "It ruled that that error was his." | High Court ruling, February 1991 |
| 340 | The Government used its normal policy for public rental housing to decide who was eligible. | "it applied its then current policy to determine eligibility for public rental housing" | not given |
| 340 | Caption: the Government used rules from about 30 years of squatter clearances. Tenants got a public housing unit or cash to buy a flat. Building owners got compensation. In both cases, the size of the flat or building set the amount. | "Following protocols that had been refined over 30 years of clearing other 'authorised' squatter settlements" | caption |
| 340 | Caption: to measure each claim, the Government made a sketch survey of every flat on every floor of every building: about 300 buildings, up to 15 storeys. | "With some 300 individual buildings, each rising up to a possible 15 storeys, it was a mammoth task." | caption |
| 340 | The reproduced sketch-survey plan (sheet 1) shows one 4/F flat at 6 Sai Shing Road (西城路). It divides the flat into a living room, two cubicles, a kitchen, a toilet and two bed spaces.
