A team of three measured it; the handwriting gives C. S. Wong, K. W. Man and P. K. Mo. The printed scale is "1:50", with "Approx." added by hand. | "Measurement Team:" | the plan (Government sketch-survey, 9 Nov 1987; see Quoted records) |
| 340 | Sheet 1 writes figures in brackets inside the rooms: cubicle (C) "(3.54)", cubicle (B) "(3.54)", kitchen (D) "(2.00)", toilet (E) "(1.70)", and "(4.65)" or "(9.65)" (the first digit is unclear) near the living room (A). Other figures give the sides: the west wall is 2.39 and 2.94 (about 5.3 m, my arithmetic); bed space G is 1.76 long, bed space F 1.72. INFERRED: the bracketed figures are floor areas in m², the others lengths in metres. The two bed spaces stand inside the living room. | — | the plan (sheet 1) |
| 340 | Sheet 2 draws five 5/F flats around two staircases. Three are at 3 Cheung On Lane (Blk C; Blk A Rear; Blk B Rear). Two are at 31B "T.T.T. Rd", Shing Hing Bldg, Rear Blk 3 (Flats A and B). INFERRED: "T.T.T. Rd" is Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道), and flats with addresses on two streets met at shared stairs. The handwritten names are hard to read. | "B24/115" | the plan (sheet 2), undated |
| 341 | Caption: a private security company guarded and patrolled the City during the clearance. It stopped break-ins and kept out squatters and vandals. | "to deter squatters and vandalism" | caption |
| 341 | Caption: the residents of phase 1 handed in their keys. The guards hung the keys in rows, for quick access in a fire. | "Keys handed in by the residents of phase 1 were hung in neat rows" | caption |
| 341 | Photo (Girard): the key room. Hundreds of keys with round tags hang in rows on boards on three walls. Four or five long black batons (INFERRED from their shape) hang in studded holders from hooks. A uniformed guard (white short-sleeved shirt, shoulder badge, grey trousers, wristwatch) reaches up to a board. A typewriter stands on a desk; a black chair with a chrome frame is in front. | — | photo, Girard (p 356: 341(t)), undated |
| 341 | Photo (Girard): a bank's wall calendar shows months 9 and 10. September 1 is in the first column and October 1 is in the third column. If the first column is Sunday, this layout fits September-October 1991 (and also 1985) (INFERRED from the weekdays; the printed year is not legible). The clearance years give 1991. Phase 1 ended on 8 January 1992 (p 345). | — | photo, Girard |
| 341 | Photo (Girard): a sign at the top of the wall ends "...有限公司" (company limited). A handwritten board below it is not legible. | — | photo, Girard |
| 341 | Caption: the clearance team made its own numbers for buildings, floors and flats. When a flat was empty, the team marked its outer door and put a new padlock in place of the resident's lock. | "replacing the resident's lock with a new padlock" | caption |
| 341 | Photo (Lambot): a dark green steel door with a chalk mark like "C3?/14/1-4", a typed notice in Chinese and English taped to it, a brass padlock on a hasp, and an empty lock hole. The form of the mark is like the "H.D. Structure Ref." on the p 340 plan, "A 19/57 (1-4)" (INFERRED: the same numbering). | — | photo, Lambot (p 356: 341(bl)) |
| 342 | Caption: most of the 33,000 residents accepted the rehousing and compensation. Many left before their deadline. A few hundred said the clearance was illegal and immoral. | "a small minority of just a few hundred vociferously rejected the clearance as illegal and immoral" | caption |
| 342 | Caption: officials forcibly evicted a few dozen objectors. Some continued with a sit-in on the pavement of Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道). | "a sit-in on the pavement along Tung Tau Tsuen Road" | caption |
| 342 | Photo (Girard): a cloth banner in English across shuttered shop fronts: "PROTEST AGAINST SPECIAL DUTTIES OFFICE" (spelled so), then "... UNFAIR TREATMENT TO SUPPRESS THE RESIDENTS OF KOWLOON WALLED CITY". | "PROTEST AGAINST SPECIAL DUTTIES OFFICE" | photo, Girard (p 356: 342) |
| 342 | Photo (Girard): a street plate "西城路" with "WEST CITY R..." on a corner pillar, and a framed notice board below it. Four police officers in dark uniforms stand at the shop fronts: two in berets, two in peaked caps. The steel shutters and folding gates carry handwritten notices. One shutter carries a chalk mark "B22" over "81" (INFERRED: a clearance number, as on pp 341 and 354). At the left, a black banner carries white Chinese brush characters (not legible to me). | — | photo, Girard |
| 342 | Photo (Girard): the signboard of a decorator, "欣記". It offers carpentry (做木), plastering (泥水), painting (油漆), wallpaper (牆紙), ceilings (天花) and floors (地板). It gives three numbers: factory (工廠) 7165977, home (住宅) 3279680, pager (傳呼機) 1128999, call 9328. The sign also has "裝飾" (decoration). Faint older digits show under the numbers: the numbers were painted over (INFERRED). | "7165977", "3279680" | photo, Girard |
| 342-343 | Photo (Girard): an old man kneels on the pavement with a small placard in Chinese. Four police officers stand by shutters and a tiled step behind him. | — | photo, Girard (p 356: 342/343) |
| 340-343 | Rehousing rule (the Government's "then current policy" for public rental housing): most members of the household must have seven years of continuous residence in Hong Kong. | "members must have been in continuous residence in Hong Kong for seven years" | Government policy (papers not cited) |
| 343 | Rehousing rule: "Genuine residents" also qualified if they lived in the City on and since 14 January 1987 (the registration day). | "if they'd been living in the Walled City on, and since, 14 January 1987" | not given |
| 343 | This rule is for households eligible for public rental housing, with at least two relatives. They can buy a Home Ownership Scheme flat with "priority green status". Or they can get a Home Purchase Loan Scheme loan for a private flat. The others got temporary housing. | "could opt to buy a Home Ownership Scheme apartment by using the priority green status" | not given |
| 343 | All owners, resident or absentee, got the market value of their property and a Home Purchase Allowance to buy elsewhere. | "preferably in the same area" | not given |
| 343 | The City had no lease conditions. Thus the Special Committee did not classify property by use: shops, factories and homes got the same treatment. | "owners of shops, factories or homes were to be treated alike for compensation purposes" | Special Committee |
| 343 | Legal occupiers of commercial and industrial property got statutory compensation. The Committee added ex-gratia allowances, because the statutory process was slow. The allowances depended on removal costs, decoration costs, fees and a sliding scale by location. | "resistance to clearance arising from the necessary lead time" | Special Committee |
| 343 | A proviso: the ex gratia allowance to legal tenants must not be less than the allowance to squatters. | "should not be less than the ex gratia allowance payable to squatters" | Special Committee |
| 343 | The unlicensed doctors and dentists got a special lump sum of HK$342,000: three years' pay of a medical and dental technician at HK$9500 a month. | "medical and dental proprietors/practitioners" | Special Committee |
| 343 | Prostitutes got rehousing but no extra package. The essay counts them among "the Walled City's professions". | "prostitutes were rehoused without any additional packages" | not given |
| 343 | In May 1987, the Buildings and Lands Department surveyed a random sample of premises. The usual size was 23.44 m². This figure became the average. Later, 26 m² was found more accurate, and the Government paid HK$119 million more. | "usually 23.44 square metres" | B&L survey, May 1987 |
| 343-344 | In 1993, the Director of Buildings and Lands told ExCo that his department did not expect its survey data to set the compensation. | "under no impression" | ExCo, 1993 |
| 343 | Caption: small factory owners suffered most. Their compensation needed proof of turnover and profit, and most kept no accurate books. | "a nigh on impossible task for those who had never bothered with accurate book-keeping" | caption |
| 343 | Photo (Girard): against a dark background, lit by flash, a man wears a board of snapshots with a handwritten heading on his chest. A child in a yellow jumper stands beside him. A black banner behind shows the characters "官地" (government land) in white; the other characters are cut off. | — | photo, Girard (p 356: 343) |
| 344 | The Director of Audit found that the Government overspent by HK$210 million. | "overspent by HK$210 million" | Director of Audit, by May 1993 |
| 344 | In May 1993, Miss Leung Wai Tung, a member of the Special Committee, told ExCo that the package was generous. | "From day one, we knew our compensation was on the generous side." | ExCo, May 1993 |
| 344 | The Norwegian Lutheran Mission bought a City property in 1966 for HK$6300. In 1985 it gave the property to the Hongkong and Macau Lutheran Church. Later, a Lee Yin Ping said that he had owned part of it since 1978. He showed a deed: a Lee Chi On had sold it to him. The church went to court in 1991 and won in the end. | "purchased it in 1966 for HK$6300" | court case, 1991 |
| 344 | The writer: "Canny landlords made a killing." One landlord got HK$34.6 million for about 100 flats. The SCMP found him: he was Chang Kat Kong. His Chiu Chow (潮州) friends pooled money to build his first 12-storey block. He said that his own share was only HK$5 million. | "One individual was paid HK$34.6 million as compensation for about 100 apartments." | SCMP, 1993 |
| 344 | Chang Kat Kong had an air-conditioner-repair shop in the City. In 1993 he said that he preferred that shop and "a happy and stable life" to the money. | "I'd rather still be running my air-conditioner-repair business at my Walled City shop" | SCMP, 1993 |
| 344 | Businesses made large claims that the Revenue Department did not believe. | "You would have thought they were all doing more business than Jardine Matheson." | SCMP column, Kevin Sinclair, 1992 |
| 344 | The last businesses to refuse were the fishball makers, the herbalists and the noodle makers. | "the fishball merchants, the herbalists, the noodle makers" | not given |
| 344 | By the beginning of 1991, "the year the actual clearances were due to start", 96% of residents but only 51% of businesses had accepted the compensation. | "96 per cent of residents but only 51 per cent of businesses" | not given |
| 344 | Residents who moved out came back to their empty flats to make tea and play mahjong with friends. | "had a tendency to nip back to their vacated flats to make tea and play mahjong with their friends" | not given |
| 344 | After the announcement, the Government worked with the Kowloon Walled City Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會). Before that, the District Office had almost no contact with it. | "We'd had almost no contact at all with the Kai Fong" | Duncan Pescod (District Officer at the time), words of 2013 (the essay does not say to whom) |
| 344 | Pescod added that the old relation was not good administration, on either side. | "It wasn't exactly a sparkling example of good administration on either side." | Pescod, 2013 |
| 344 | The writer: in the City, "actual book-keeping was almost non-existent", thus claims and counter-claims flourished. She sets this in the tense time before the handover and, after June 1989, after Tiananmen. | "actual book-keeping was almost non-existent" | writer's view |
| 344 | The first formal meeting was nervous; then there was frequent contact. The writer says that this was the first and last time a British administration was present in the City. | "For the first, and last, time, a British administration made its presence felt within the Walled City." | writer's view |
| 344 | Caption: the clearance took 12 months, July 1991 to August 1992, in three phases: the western end, then the centre, then the eastern end. The caption calls it "A disorientating time for children". | "from July 1991 to August 1992 in three phases" | caption |
| 344 | Caption: the empty City had no fixtures, fittings or lights, and was frightening at night. | "Stripped of fixtures, fittings and light" | caption |
| 344 | Photo (Girard): a small child in red holds a bundle against a flowered spring mattress that leans on a shop front. A signboard above reads "統治男..." and "精醫花..." (the rest is hidden). INFERRED: a doctor's sign. Steel shutters, a folding gate and tiled steps are beside it. | — | photo, Girard (p 356: 344) |
| 344-345 | The contact with the Kai Fong Association let the Government deal with possible concerns. Among them: the rats of the City moving at once to nearby housing estates, and the collapse ("implosion") of the whole structure when the dismantling began. | "the rat population instantly relocating to nearby housing estates" | not given |
| 345 | The Agriculture and Fisheries Department collected unwanted pets: it got one cat and four dogs. The RSPCA got nothing in 10 weeks. | "Not even a piece of hair was handed in by residents," | RSPCA superintendent Dennis Jones, date not given |
| 345 | Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場) often called the District Office about flocks of pigeons from the roof lofts of the City. Before the clearance, the Office had no power to act. | "people had pigeon lofts on their roofs, we'd regularly get calls from Kai Tak Airport" | Pescod (words of 2013, p 344) |
| 345 | Pescod saw a clear difference between the informal rule inside the City and the formal Government rule of the clearance years. | "The difference between the informal regulation of the City and formal regulation" | Pescod (words of 2013, p 344) |
| 345 | Pescod: the clearance years gave the Government a direct channel into the City. He said that the departments were not aggressive: they tried to solve a problem, not to make a new one. | "Suddenly, you had a direct channel to deal with things," | Pescod (words of 2013, p 344) |
| 345 | The protests about compensation were sporadic and separate (the book prints "discrete"). Philip Chok Kin Fun, Deputy Secretary for Home Affairs (Special Duties) City and New Territories Division, was "the Government's man on the front line". He said this "rather testily". | "We're talking about 30,000 people, I can't have a dialogue with every 10 residents who call themselves an association." | not given |
| 345 | Before the end of phase 1, protesters burned an effigy of Chok outside LegCo. | "Chok's effigy was burnt outside LegCo" | not given |
| 345 | An unlicensed dentist, Ma Chong Chung (馬創忠), led the Kai Fong Association in this period. He wanted HK$1.5 million and refused HK$1.2 million. The Association resisted the British, as it did in 1948. | "led by an unlicensed dentist called Ma Chong Chung" | not given |
| 345 | 28 November 1991, the start of the end of phase 1: at 6.45am, 150 police, with Tactical Unit officers, blocked the main entrances of the western part. | "150 policemen and women, including Tactical Unit officers, arrived at 6.45am" | not given |
| 345 | At 8am, 50 Special Duties Unit clearance workers went in with hammers and welding equipment. In 11 hours they took 49 buildings (the book: "they took over 49 buildings"). They carried one man out, and talked another man out of a jump from the roof. | "carrying hammers and welding equipment" | not given |
| 345 | By 8pm, about 30 protesters camped outside Government House. Later they moved to the New China News Agency in Happy Valley, the Star Ferry and the Government Secretariat in Central. They also wrote to the British Labour Party, which did not help. | "By 8pm that night, about 30 protesters had set up camp outside Government House." | not given |
| 345 | Ma compared the residents with the Vietnamese boat people. He said that the boat people were illegal immigrants, but the Government used no force to send them back. | "Even the Vietnamese boat people are better treated than us," | not given |
| 345 | Then the protesters put up tents and an illegal camp on the pavement of Tung Tau Tsuen Road, next to the City. | "pitched tents and established a camp, illegally, on the pavement" | not given |
| 345 | In a legal challenge that caused delay, some householders said that the Crown Land Ordinance (the legal tool for the evictions) did not apply. They said that they were the owners, because they had openly occupied the land for over 60 years. ExCo approved the Land Acquisition (Possessory Title) Ordinance instead; it needed one month's notice. | "having openly occupied the land for more than 60 years" | not given |
| 345 | Phase 1 was complete at nightfall on 8 January 1992. That day, the work stopped for a time. A Japanese consular official came and persuaded a Japanese television crew and six local photographers to come out of a shop where they hid. | "by nightfall on 8 January 1992, the first phase of clearance was complete" | not given |
| 345-346 | 3 March 1992, the last day of phase 2: an explosion brought bomb experts. The cause was four large home-made firecrackers in an empty flat. | "four large, home-made firecrackers" | not given |
| 345 | Photo (Lambot): an emptied tall building (more than ten storeys) against a pink sky, with dark open window holes. Faded painted signs on its balconies are not legible. A row of white hoarding panels, each with a round emblem, stands at street level. | — | photo, Lambot (p 356: 345) |
| 346 | On 3 March 1992, protesters burned a home-made Union Jack and beat the police with a home-made Chinese flag. The police made no arrests. | "a Chinese flag (ditto) was used to beat the police" | not given |
| 346 | 7 April (1992), completion of the third and last phase: a resident with a chopper wounded a clearance official, his assistant and two policemen. These were the only serious injuries of the whole clearance. | "a resident wielding a chopper bloodied a clearance official, his assistant and two policemen" | not given |
| 346 | 1 July 1992, 6am: 200 police, with Tactical Unit officers in helmets and with riot shields, closed off the last uncleared area. | "barricaded the last uncleared area"; "At 6am on 1 July 1992, 200 police" | not given |
| 346 | Statistics of 1 July 1992: 158 empty flats repossessed; 10 households left freely; officials persuaded 14 to leave; 6 were removed by force. The total of evictions became 16. | "158 vacant flats were repossessed" | "That day's statistics", source not given |
| 346 | At 4.30pm on 1 July 1992, a middle-aged couple gave up their flat and walked out (INFERRED from "finally": the last residents to leave). | "At 4.30pm, a middle-aged couple finally surrendered their flat and walked out." | not given |
| 346 | The protest camp on Tung Tau Tsuen Road became a small shanty town. One hut had six bunk-beds, a black-and-white television, three folding tables and a portrait of Mao. | "six bunk-beds, a black-and-white television set, three folding tables and a portrait of Mao" | not given |
| 346 | The authorities did not want "one slum replaced by another". They dismantled the camp on 2 July 1992 and made the whole area a restricted zone. From 14 January 1987 to 2 July 1992 is 1997 days, counting both days. | "a spookily resonant 1997 days"; "from 14 January 1987 until 2 July 1992" | not given |
| 346 | Ten of the evicted people moved into a park in Tung Tau Estate (東頭邨). In September 1992, they tried to give a petition to Governor Christopher Patten during his surprise visit to the site. | "with a hastily-assembled petition" | not given |
| 346 | For a time, a subgroup occupied a corridor on the 31st floor of the City and New Territories Administration head office in Wanchai. Michael Suen Ming Yeung, Secretary for Home Affairs, and his staff used a back stair from the 30th floor. | "obliged to use a back staircase from the 30th floor" | not given |
| 346 | January 1993: JS Corrigall, Assistant Director (Policy) of the Building and Lands Department, said that only 10 people camped, in turns, with seven beds at night. The SCMP had said more than 20. | "only seven beds have been counted at night time" | letter to a newspaper, January 1993 |
| 346 | Of the 10 campers: 4 had not lived in the City; 1 already rented public housing; 1 had used his right to buy a Home Ownership Scheme flat; 1 had accepted the offer; 1 had accepted but wanted new terms; 2 had not yet chosen a rehousing option. | "four were ineligible because they hadn't been living in the Walled City" | letter, Corrigall, January 1993 |
| 346 | The deadline for appeals was 10 October 1992. The Appeals Tribunal got 77 cases (ownership disputes, rehousing eligibility, business-loss compensation, late offers, floor area). | "Seventy-seven cases were referred to the Appeals Tribunal" | not given |
| 346-347 | The Assessment Review Board (ARB) handled 112 cases about the fairness of the offers. 70% of these got more than the first offer. | "70 per cent were eventually awarded compensation in excess of the original offer" | not given |
| 346 | Photo (Sing Pao, top): aerial view of the empty City. The high blocks still stand; along one side, the low edge buildings are gone and a strip of rubble lies between the blocks and a hoarding on the road. The photo shows a red crawler crane with a tall lattice jib and a yellow excavator. A lorry and cars are on the road, and a jet airliner is in the sky. Low sheds stand on the cleared ground at the left. INFERRED: early in the demolition (1993). | — | photo, Sing Pao Daily News (p 356: 346), undated |
| 346 | Photo (Sing Pao, bottom): an operator in a white hard hat sits in a red crane cab under its lattice boom, beside the City. A yellow excavator arm shows below. The walls behind have large broken holes; window cages still hang on them. A shop sign at the bottom is not legible. | — | photo, Sing Pao Daily News |
| 347 | By June 1993, "when demolition of the Walled City was already well advanced", the Tung Tau group had shanties numbered one to 10 at Tung Tau Estate (the park of p 346). Local residents complained. The Legal Department later found the site outside the estate boundary (next row). | "shanties carefully labelled one to 10" | not given |
| 347 | An assistant housing manager spoke against the shanties. Estate staff who tried to clear them met threats, with knives waved. | "No one wants to see a mini-squatter area inside the estate," | not given |
| 347 | The Housing Department asked for a court order. The Legal Department found that the site was outside the estate, thus the Department had no right to manage it. | "not within the boundary of the estate" | Legal Department, 1993 |
| 347 | Caption: in 1994 the site was cleared; only the old yamen buildings stood. As much of the original yamen as possible was kept. Later much of it was rebuilt or remodelled to look like a Suzhou magistrate's house, not the military buildings it was. | "in an attempt to turn military buildings into a Suzhou magistrate's house" | caption |
| 347 | Caption (INFERRED: for the p 346 photos; the page does not say): the demolition was slow. Nobody knew what held each building up, and an unexpected collapse was a real fear. The workers used a swinging ball on one building at a time, from a safe distance. | "an old-fashioned swinging ball" | caption |
| 347 | Caption: the demolition took over a year. | "from January 1993 to March 1994"; "the whole demolition taking over a year from January 1993 to March 1994" | caption |
| 347 | Photo (Sing Pao), black and white: the cleared site, flat bare earth. The low yamen buildings with broken roofs stand near a tree. Two excavators work at one side. A four-engine jet airliner flies low overhead. A fence and a road run along the right edge. | — | photo, Sing Pao Daily News (p 356: 347), 1994 per caption |
| 347-348 | The Government gave the former residents a date to leave, then extended it. | "issued the former Walled City residents with a date to leave; this was extended" | not given |
| 348 | At an eviction on 18 January 1994, an elderly woman tried to put her head under the wheel of a removal truck. | "attempted to wedge her head under the wheel of a removal truck" | not given |
| 348 | The last protesters held a sit-in at the Tung Tau Estate office. The next day, 50 housing workers and six police removed them. | "cleared out by 50 housing workers and six policemen" | not given |
| 348 | Raymond Hu Fat Kwong was chairman of the Walled City Clearance Assessment Review Board. For a month, the protesters lived outside his 10th-floor office in Manulife Tower, North Point. | "the 10th-floor office of Raymond Hu Fat Kwong" | not given |
| 348 | Then they went back to the old City area and put up a nylon-covered shelter outside Mei Tung Estate (美東邨). The Government evicted them at last on 15 December 1995. | "They were finally evicted on 15 December 1995" | newspapers (not named) |
| 348 | The writer ends: the newspapers gave the last eviction only short reports, in a city that prepared for its new future. Like the Chinese officials of almost a century before, the former residents "disappeared into the pages of history". | "had disappeared into the pages of history" | writer's view |
| 348 | Caption: in the park today, trees hide the outline of the old fort. The yamen entrance claims to be original, but it was much rebuilt (see p 298). The park shows almost nothing of the City. | "the exact outline of the old magistrate's fort lost beneath a blanket of trees" | caption, 2014 |

### Part 10. The short features with no speaker (pp32–33, 54–55, 80–81, 104–111, 240–241, 246–249, 262–263, 310–317, 322–327)

Writer: the editors (captions and commentary, 1993 or 2014); the photos are by Girard and Lambot (primary). Checked on 2026-11-16: 173 rows checked, 34 fixed, 0 deleted, 6 added. 170 rows.

"photo" in "Rests on" means a photo by Girard or Lambot, made about 1987-1992 (the book gives no date for these photos). A photo row describes what the photo shows; the photo is primary, the description is mine.

The Credits page (p356) names the photographer of each page. Greg Girard: pp32, 54, 55, 80, 81, 108, 109, 110/111, 240/241, 241, 246, 247, 248/249, 249, 262, 263, 310/311, 311, 314, 314/315, 316/317, 322, 323, 324, 324/325. Ian Lambot: pp33, 106, 313, 326/327. The binding cuts the ends of two lines of Lambot's list. They probably hold pp104/105 and p312.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 32 | **Fuk Tak Shrine (福德古廟).** Folklore says that a Sung Dynasty emperor built the Fuk Tak Temple that first stood on Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道). The emperor brought a statue of the god from the capital of China. | "erected by a Sung Dynasty emperor who brought a statue of the god from the capital of China" | folklore ("According to folklore") |
| 32 | When that temple was demolished, the statue went to this much simpler shrine on the east side of the City. | "the statue was moved to this much simpler shrine" | not given |
| 32 | The shrine stood squeezed in a row of 'temporary' structures that held dai pai dongs (street restaurants) and dentists. | "squeezed among the row of 'temporary' structures housing dai pai dongs" | not given |
| 32 | The shrine was especially popular with the local Chiu Chow (潮州) people, inside and outside the City. | "especially popular with local Chiu Chow residents" | not given |
| 32 | Fuk Tak is a minor god, common throughout Hong Kong (香港). People believe that he protects the residents of a place and brings them good fortune. | "A minor deity common throughout Hong Kong" | not given |
| 32 | Most visitors were women. The writer guesses that they prayed for simple favours or for luck in daily life. | "Women comprised the majority of visitors" | not given (the prayers are a guess: "perhaps") |
| 32 | The row of low structures was technically a no-man's-land. People usually took the City boundary to be the taller buildings behind the row. These were on the east side of Lung Shing Road (龍城路), a north-south street inside the City. | "technically a no-man's-land" | not given |
| 32 | In practice the row counted as part of the City. Its residents and businesses got the same compensation and rehousing as everyone else in the clearance. | "received the same compensation and rehousing" | not given |
| 32 | Photo (two views inside the shrine): women light joss sticks and red candles at square stone incense urns. Big coils of incense hang from the ceiling. Plates of fruit (oranges, apples, a pomelo) and red paper offerings lie on the altar. Carved wooden boards carry characters (福, 古 and 廟 are readable; the rest is not clear). | (photo) | photo |
| 33 | Photo: the east face of the City. Tall blocks of 10 or more storeys, with window cages and laundry, rise behind the low row of stalls under corrugated roofs. | (photo) | photo |
| 33 | Photo: the shop signs in the row. Sang Kee Restaurant (生記飯店) lists meal times and Chiu Chow (潮州) dishes in small characters (partly unreadable). The next sign is Tang Chi Shing Dentistry (鄧志成牙科). A low hut to the right carries 華冠牙科 (Wah Koon dentistry). | "生記飯店", "鄧志成牙科", "華冠牙科" (signs in the photo) | photo |
| 33 | Photo: the shrine is an open front under an awning. A red carved altar table stands inside. Coil incense and a round red lantern hang from the roof. Two people sit inside. A red vertical signboard with characters stands at one side (not read). | (photo) | photo |
| 33 | Photo: a blue hatchback with the number plate "DF 8613" and a white van stand in front of the row. | "DF 8613" (plate in the photo) | photo |
| 54 | **Mrs Law.** In many lower-income families of the City, both parents worked full-time. | "it was common for both Walled City parents to work full-time" | not given |
| 54 | The extended family was vital. A grandparent often minded the children during the day or after school. | "common for a grandparent to care for the children during the day or after school" | not given |
| 54 | Mrs Law fetched her two grandchildren from school every day. She took them to her small flat for the afternoon. | "Mrs Law (above) collected her two grandchildren from school every day" | not given (probably the authors' visit) |
| 54 | The children often did their homework on her bed while they knelt on the floor. | "they often did their homework on her bed while kneeling on the floor" | not given (the p54 photo shows it) |
| 54 | The parents stopped at her flat on their way home from work and took the children home. | "Their parents would stop by and pick them up on their way home from work" | not given |
| 54 | The community was close. In one building, a grandmother or a housewife often minded several neighbours' children as well as her own. | "a grandmother or housewife might take care of several of the neighbours' children" | not given |
| 54 | Stairwells and landings were often full of children at play. In later years, some parents became less willing to let younger children out of their sight, unless someone went with them. | "Stairwells and landings were often alive with children playing" | not given |
| 54 | Photo: Mrs Law, an old woman in black, reaches up to a black wall telephone high on the door frame (the handset and its cord show). Her kitchen corner has shelves of pots, a kettle, a rice cooker, an orange plastic can and a gas ring on a tiled counter. The floor is mosaic tile. An iron bed stands behind. | (photo) | photo |
| 54 | Photo: a girl writes her homework on the bed, which a patterned mat covers. Another child lies on the bed and reads. The bed has an iron frame. | (photo) | photo |
| 55 | Photo: three children (a boy in denim dungarees, a girl, and a toddler in red on her lap) sit at the narrow door of a tiny room. Behind them are a bunk with a curtain and a shelf of bottles. Plastic bags hang on nails on the green walls. There are a round metal object high on the wall (a mirror or a lid; not clear), a mosaic floor, a tiled wall and orange plastic basins. The text speaks of two grandchildren; the book does not say who these three children are. | (photo) | photo |
| 80 | **Law Yu Yi (羅婉儀).** Aged 90, she lived in a small, windowless and very humid flat on the third floor, off Lung Chun First Alley. | "a small, windowless and exceptionally humid third-floor apartment off Lung Chun First Alley" | not given |
| 80 | She lived with her daughter-in-law, aged 68. The writer sees in this the old Chinese duty of a son's wife to serve his parents. | "the traditional Chinese obligation for a son's wife to serve his parents and family" | the writer's view ("almost certainly") |
| 80 | Both women were widows. The writer says that this often made women of two generations close companions who depended on each other. | "Both their spouses had passed away" | not given |
| 80 | She was formerly a housewife and came from Guangdong. She had lived in the City for 28 years. | "had lived in the City for 28 years" | not given |
| 80 | A son of hers was still alive. He lived on a lower floor of the same building. | "a surviving son lived on a lower floor in the same building" | not given |
| 80 | Her daily round: she went to the adjoining park, where she groomed her face with great care in the traditional Chinese way. She also went to the Old People's Centre (老人中心) for tea and friends. | "groom her face meticulously in the traditional Chinese way" | not given |
| 80 | Photo: a pink wall with two wall clocks (one marked "DIAMOND"), a calendar print of a god with a company name and telephone numbers, three frames of family photos and electric wire run on the wall. | (photo) | photo |
| 80 | Photo: a tear-off day calendar shows "31", "FRIDAY 星期五" and the lunar day "廿四日" (24th). The month line is torn; it can read "MAR". INFERRED (my own calendar check, not in the book): of the Friday-the-31st dates from 1984 to 1993, only 31 March 1989 falls on lunar day 24. Thus the photo was probably made on or soon after 31 March 1989. | "31", "FRIDAY", "廿四日" (on the calendar) | photo; the date is INFERRED |
| 81 | Photo: two old women (probably Law Yu Yi and her daughter-in-law; the photo has no caption) eat at a red-topped table with rice bowls and chopsticks. The room holds an iron bed with pillows, and a shelf bunk above it with a blue checked quilt. On the walls are a wall fan, plastic bags on hooks (one from Wellcome), hats and a small red household altar. A small TV set stands on a cabinet with a red-and-white carton on top (its design looks like a Marlboro carton). There are also bowls and pots on a shelf, newspapers, bottles under the bed and a folding chair. A door leads to a tiled room. | "WELLCOME" (on a bag in the photo) | photo |
| 104 | **Refuse Collection.** The writer calls it a misconception that the City had no Government controls or services. But the political sensitivity of the City meant that the usual Hong Kong rules were seldom enforced there. | "more often merely suggested than enforced" | not given |
| 104 | The refuse collectors of the Urban Services Department (市政總署) (USD) gave one essential service. | "One essential service, however, was provided by the Urban Services Department (USD) refuse collectors." | not given |
| 104 | About two tons of rubbish built up in the City each day. The Government gave its removal priority: for hygiene, to control the large rat population, and to remove a fire hazard. | "the two tons of rubbish that accumulated each day" | not given |
| 104 | Orange USD buckets were everywhere. Many refuse collection points organised the work. | "Orange USD buckets were commonplace" | not given |
| 104 | Ten sweepers patrolled the City. | "the 10 sweepers who patrolled the City" | not given |
| 104 | Residents were not model citizens about rubbish. Sometimes they joined communal clean-ups of the roofs, the alleys and the overhead pipes and electricity cables. The Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會) organised these clean-ups every year. | "organised every year by the Kai Fong Association" | not given |
| 104 | Photo: a narrow passage with boards on the floor. A door carries the white mark "W33", a down arrow and other chalk marks (not readable). The wall beside the door carries "114". A wooden cart or bin holds sacks of rubbish and brooms. Electric boxes hang on the wall. | "W33", "114" (on the door and the wall) | photo |
| 105 | Photo: an alley of closed steel shutters and lattice gates under a dense mass of pipes and cables, with fluorescent tubes. A stair goes up at the end. A stack of round orange USD bins stands by the stair. Litter covers the floor. Chalk marks and a paper notice on a shutter are not readable. | (photo) | photo |
| 106 | **The Water Supply.** Many flats had piped water from privately drilled artesian wells. Safe drinking water came only from eight Government stand-pipes. | "safe drinking water was available from just eight Government stand-pipes" | not given |
| 106 | Only one stand-pipe stood inside the City: the one on Tai Chang Street (大井街). | "of which only this one, on Tai Chang Street, was within the City" | not given |
| 107 | The writer says that the water supply of the City showed much inventiveness and enterprise, but not all of it was healthy. | "considerable inventiveness and entrepreneurial skill, not all of it healthy" | the writer's view |
| 107 | Over the years, the only concession of the authorities was a few public fresh-water stand-pipes. The writer says that they were barely enough. | "The only concession the authorities made over the years was to install a few public fresh-water stand-pipes" | not given |
| 107 | The Government long refused to connect the buildings, flats and factories to the mains. Thus people paid private suppliers, who pumped water from wells under the City. Or they paid local Triad groups for water tapped illegally from nearby mains. | "local Triad groups for water tapped illegally from nearby mains supplies" | not given |
| 107 | By 1987, eight stand-pipes served up to 35,000 residents and hundreds of factories. One stood inside the City; the others stood outside its edge. | "By 1987, just eight stand-pipes were in place to supply up to 35,000 residents and hundreds of factories"; "Only one of these was located within the City" | not given |
| 107 | The Government supplied mains water inside the City to some recognised charities. This was an exception. | "Mains water was supplied inside the City to various recognised charities" | not given |
| 107 | The first stand-pipe came in 1963. A Government statement of the time called it free and unrestricted. | "access to a free, unrestricted water supply"; "The first stand-pipe was installed in 1963" | Government statement, 1963 |
| 107 | By 1963 the City had over 10,000 residents. | "Residents, by then numbering over 10,000" | not given |
| 107 | In 1964 the newly formed Kai Fong Association went to the Secretariat for Chinese Affairs. Its representatives complained of an acute water shortage, and pointed to China's East River scheme. | "the Chinese Government is working selflessly on the East River scheme" | Kai Fong complaint, 1964 |
| 107 | The writer calls the 1964 complaints of the Kai Fong well grounded, though dressed in rhetoric. | "Their complaints were well grounded, if dressed in rhetoric." | the writer's view |
| 107 | After the drought of 1964 ended, four emergency hydrants were removed. Five stand-pipes were left. | "Four emergency hydrants had been removed following the end of the drought that year, leaving just five stand-pipes." | not given (probably the same 1964 record) |
| 107 | In 1964, residents paid labourers $12-15 a month to carry six buckets of water a day from the stand-pipes to their flats. The buildings then had four to nine storeys. | "Residents were paying $12-15 a month for labourers to carry six buckets of water each day" | not given |
| 107 | In the building boom of the late 1960s and early 1970s, water-carrying got harder. New factories needed much water, and carriers had to climb six storeys or more. Thus entrepreneurs dug for new sources. | "trudging six or more storeys to individual apartments" | not given |
| 107 | The new well diggers were mostly property owners who drilled on their own land. People said that some were former water-carriers. | "some were reputedly former water-carriers themselves" | hearsay ("reputedly") |
| 107 | There were wells in the City in its earlier days. A former resident remembers that people took water from a couple of wells at the northern and eastern gates, at the turn of the century (INFERRED: about 1900; the book does not give a year). | "a couple of wells at the northern and eastern gates at the turn of the century" | a former resident's memory (not named) |
| 107 | In 1987, Government surveyors who assessed compensation claims found 67 working wells. About 40 suppliers owned them. | "identified 67 working wells owned by some 40 suppliers" | Government surveyors, 1987 |
| 107 | People said that up to 300 wells had been sunk over the years. Residents called them 'scientific wells'. Many ran dry from overuse. | "up to 300 'scientific wells'" | hearsay ("were said to have been sunk") |
| 107 | Recent wells went as deep as 100 metres, because the shallow water had run out or become too polluted. Private drilling firms did the work. | "as far as 100 metres below the surface" | not given |
| 107 | Pumps lifted the water to crude storage tanks on the roofs. From there, a tangle of pipes ran down and branched into the blocks and flats. | "a twisted congestion of pipes ran downward again" | not given |
| 107 | A well-water connection cost up to several thousand dollars. The price depended on the height above the well and the distance from it. | "could cost as much as several thousand dollars" | not given |
| 107 | By the late 1980s, the monthly charge was $50-$70 for each household. | "monthly charges were anywhere between $50 and $70 per household" | not given |
| 107 | Reports said that many people did not have the money for the steep connection fee. But most residents appear to have had well water. | "the majority of City residents appear to have been linked up to receive well-water" | unnamed "reports" |
| 107 | Pressure and pump problems meant that many pumps ran only at set times, usually noon and midnight, to refill the tanks. This gave only a few hours of water. Residents still stored water in baths and buckets. | "usually noon and midnight" | not given |
| 107 | About 20 people worked the supply valves at points in the City, to the well-owner's timetable. | "About 20 people were employed to control the supply at various locations" | not given |
| 108 | Much of the well water was not drinkable: it smelled, it was murky, and it held seepage or industrial waste. People used it only to wash things and floors. | "it was not even fit to boil" | not given |
| 108 | Drinking and cooking water still came by hand from the stand-pipes. A small workforce carried water until the end. | "a small workforce was engaged in this activity till the end" | not given |
| 108 | Before the 1970s, raw sewage left the City in open drains along the tiny streets. Much of it soaked into the ground. The shallow wells were thus contaminated. | "like a giant septic tank" | not given |
| 108 | In the 1970s the Government and the Kai Fong put in a mains sewer. | "Government got together with the Kai Fong to install a mains sewage line in the 1970s" | not given |
| 108 | The sewer, basic policing, lighting, social services and rubbish removal were the exceptions to the Government's rule of non-intervention. | "one of the several exceptions which broke the rule of non-intervention" | not given |
| 108 | Sewage and waste removal were a matter of public hygiene. They also affected the health of people outside the City. | "had implications for the health of people beyond the City" | the writer's view |
| 108 | The Government let private people run the water supply with no regulation. | "extra-legally but not illegally" | not given |
| 108 | The Government gave technical reasons for no household mains: the dense buildings had no proper provision for water or waste, and mains in the narrow streets meant much disruption. The writer thinks that the real reason was a wish not to encourage permanent settlement. | "a reluctance to further encourage permanent settlement in the City" | Government argument (no record named); the writer's view |
| 108 | The wells were not enough. People tapped mains water illegally outside the City, mostly from the adjacent Sai Tau Tsuen (西頭村) and Mei Tung estates. At first the Triads held a monopoly of this trade. | "notably from the adjacent Sai Tau Tsuen and Mei Tung estates" | not given |
| 108 | The Triads also built much of the City. Thus the newer buildings had at least crude water and waste provision, or the Triads pressed the buyers to get connected. | "responsible for much of the construction in the City" | not given |
| 108 | Caption: the Tai Chang Street stand-pipe served homes and businesses. Ordinary residents and paid water 'suppliers' came all day to fill plastic containers and buckets. They carried them home, often up many flights of stairs. | "be carried home, often up many flights of" | the photos; the authors' visits |
| 108 | Caption: people also came to the stand-pipe to wash clothes or to bathe. | "But people also came here with their washing" | the photos |
| 108 | Caption (photo on p109): a man shampoos his hair in a shaft of sunlight. Beside him, water from a tap thaws a basket of frozen eels. A City food factory will make fishballs from them. | "water pours from a tap to thaw a basket" | photo |
| 110 | Caption: a great cluster of hosepipes ran from the stand-pipe to the many food factories nearby. From time to time a worker fixed his firm's hosepipe to a tap to refill a storage tank in the factory. | "a great cluster of hosepipes that led off to the many food factories" | the photos |
| 110 | Several water suppliers sometimes competed in one building, mostly where the building had more than one owner. | "particularly where the building was not owned by a single proprietor" | not given |
| 110 | A connected household kept clear rules. Residents paid on time, because they feared that the supplier cut off their water or damaged their pipes. | "for fear their water would be cut off or their pipes damaged" | not given |
| 110 | Fee collectors told customers that the dues paid for the upkeep of the system and for bribes to officials. | "partly for the upkeep of the system and partly for bribes to officials" | not given |
| 110 | In 1980, a Triad leader announced a price rise. He threatened to chop up, in public, any person who led the opposition. The writer gives this as a case where the supply was "more than a simple business relationship". | "we'll chop him up in front of the crowd." | not given (see Quoted records) |
| 110 | In time the Triads sold most of their water 'business' in the City. Illegal mains taps stayed an important source of drinking water until the end. | "the illegal tapping of mains water remained an important source of drinking water until the end" | not given |
| 110 | The clearance staff did not like to admit how large the illegal tapping was. The writer argues that 67 wells cannot have supplied 33,000 people and 700 businesses. | "33,000 people and 700 businesses" | the writer's argument |
| 110 | The authorities chose to ignore the illegal taps, because to close them meant hardship and resentment. To clear the City solved the problem for good. | "it was better to turn a blind eye" | not given |
| 106 | Photo: the stand-pipe corner on Tai Chang Street. Dozens of hosepipes run up the wall and overhead. Brass taps sit on a galvanised pipe frame. A round concrete cylinder carries one more tap. A blue drum stands near it. A long narrow alley goes off to the left. A large painted character 發 and a vertical signboard are on the wall. The signboard reads "和興" and "公司" (a firm; its second line looks like 傢俬, furniture, but is not clear). A red notice painted on the wall to the right reads "此井水不適宜飲用" and "市政總署", as on p111. | "發", "和興", "公司", "此井水", "市政總署" (in the photo) | photo |
| 108 | Photo: a person holds a white plastic can and reaches for a tap among the pipes. The 和興 signboard of p106 shows at the top right. | (photo) | photo |
| 108 | Photo: a man in a yellow shirt and shorts carries a red bucket along a dark alley, past a lit doorway. The doorway has a white-tiled wall, a mosaic floor, a box above the door (perhaps an air-conditioner) and one vertical sign with red characters (not read). A barber's pole (a red, white and blue spiral in a red case) hangs by the door. | (photo) | photo |
| 109 | Photo: the man washes his hair under a tap. Water from another tap pours onto a wicker basket. A bottle stands on the round concrete cylinder. The floor is wet mosaic tile. Part of the red notice of p111 shows on the wall ("此井", "不適", "飲用" and part of "市政總署"). | (photo) | photo |
| 110-111 | Photo: a man bends to wash his hair at the stand-pipe, beside a blue bucket, a mosaic-tiled plinth and a mass of hosepipes. A red notice painted on the wall says that the water of this well is not fit to drink, and names the Urban Services Department. INFERRED: a well, or well water, was also at this corner. | "此井水不適宜飲用", "市政總署" (painted on the wall) | photo |
| 241 | **Tin Hau Temple (天后廟).** The temple was built in 1951 on an alley off Lo Yan Street (老人街). | "Built in 1951 on an alley off Lo Yan Street" | not given |
| 241 | It replaced a temple in a nearby coastal village. That temple was torn down during the Japanese occupation to extend the airport. Local community leaders kept the main shrine safe. | "torn down during the Japanese occupation, to make way for the extension of the airport" | not given |
| 241 | Tin Hau is the patron goddess of fishermen, and her temples usually stand near the sea. The City suited her because its large Chiu Chow community had once been fishermen. | "once fishermen themselves" | not given |
| 241 | It was a simple working temple. A bad-tempered dog guarded it. | "guarded by an irascible dog" | not given |
| 241 | A metal mesh grill above the temple caught the refuse that people threw from the flats round it. The grill was cleared only once a year. | "which was cleared just once a year" | not given (the photos show it) |
| 240 | Photo: a view down onto the temple roof from below the metal grill (the caption says so). The underside of the grill, with refuse on it, shows along the top. The roof has green glazed tiles with curled, moulded ridge ends, under leaves and litter. Behind it rises a plain wall with windows, a lit stairwell seen through a wall opening, and laundry. | (photo) | photo |
| 241 | Photo: the grill from above. The steel grating carries a heap of plastic bags, boards, a red plastic basin, a wire rack, paper and a broken electrical set (a radio or a heater). Tall walls rise round it (four or more storeys show). They have barred windows, laundry on poles, pot plants on ledges, lit fluorescent tubes in open stair windows and a red reel on a wall bracket. | "the metal grill protecting it from all manner of refuse dispatched from the surrounding apartments" | photo |
| 246 | **Life in the Alleys.** The alleys were the main streets of the City. Business and household foot traffic shared the same narrow ways. | "commercial and residential pedestrian traffic shared the same narrow passageways" | not given |
| 246 | Hong Kong summers are hot and humid, and the City made the heat worse. Sweating workmen with no shirts were a common sight. | "Sweating workmen stripped to the waist were a common sight" | not given |
| 246 | The writer says that prejudice against the residents lasted to the end, with no real cause. | "prejudices towards the City's residents persisted until the end, without any real justification" | the writer's view |
| 246 | A rare survey of 1975 found that most residents did not fit the public image of the City. They were like the tenants of public housing estates: they worked hard, and they worried about jobs, bad housing and their children's schools. | "more akin than one would expect to residents of public housing estates elsewhere" | a survey of 1975 (maker not named) |
| 249 | At ground level, about 35,000 residents and factory workers and some 900 factories, restaurants and shops depended on about 30 main streets and alleys. They used them to move about and for all deliveries. | "around 30 main streets and alleys"; "35,000 or so residents and factory workers"; "some 900 factories, restaurants and shops" | not given |
| 249 | Some alleys were no wider than three feet. | "some no more than three feet across" | not given |
| 249 | The alleys were often fetid and damp. Water dripped all the time from the makeshift pipes above. | "water permanently dripping from the makeshift pipes overhead" | not given |
| 249 | The alleys were an obstacle course of delivery trolleys, broken concrete and wet rubbish. | "an obstacle course of delivery trolleys, broken concrete and sodden rubbish" | not given |
| 249 | Crude plastic and metal sheets gave little cover from the water that ran down. On some routes, people carried an umbrella or wore a waterproof hat. | "an umbrella or at the very least a waterproof hat" | not given |
| 249 | Men who carried sacks of flour were an everyday sight. So were families who carried drinking water from one of the eight stand-pipes up to high floors. | "men carrying sacks of flour"; "one of the eight Government stand-pipes" | not given |
| 246 | Photo (top): a man carries a huge blue-and-white striped woven plastic bag, printed with characters, along a narrow passage past folding steel lattice gates. A board leans on the wall. | (photo) | photo |
| 246 | Photo (bottom): a man carries two big white sacks on his shoulder, with a white cloth over his head and shoulders as a pad. Paper notices are stuck on the wall of the dark alley. | (photo) | photo |
| 247 | Photo: a shirtless man carries two white cloth-wrapped bundles out of a dark doorway with a fluorescent tube. He has a cigarette in his mouth, a towel on his shoulder, a round pendant, black shorts and sneakers. Next door, a woman works in a workshop among stacks of round aluminium trays or tins, plastic bags and a wicker basket (the trade is not clear; perhaps food). The floor is wet. | (photo) | photo |
| 248 | Photo: a man in a blue shirt carries two white sacks. White powder lies spilt on the wet alley floor (perhaps flour: the p249 text tells of men who carry sacks of flour). Paper notices in Chinese cover the wall, and cables and pipes run overhead. | (photo) | photo |
| 248 | Photo (left part): behind a glass window is a room with a large beige hood lamp, a chrome stand with a cup and a tray of small bottles. INFERRED: probably a dental surgery; the book does not say. | (photo) | photo |
| 249 | Photo: a girl in a red T-shirt carries two plastic water bottles up a stair. An adult behind her carries more. Laundry hangs along the wall of the stair. | (photo) | photo |
| 249 | Photo: a dark alley with dim lamps and litter on the floor. | (photo) | photo |
| 263 | **Jackie Pullinger (潘靈卓).** In 1973 she set up 'The Well', a religious meeting place. | "'The Well', the religious meeting place established by Jackie Pullinger in 1973" | not given |
| 263 | Daily prayer there had healing hands, hymns and open confession to God. | "Healing hands, hymns and open confession to God" | not given |
| 263 | The Well was an evangelical mission of the St Stephen's Society (聖士提芬會). Most of the congregation were drug addicts who tried to stop. | "mainly made up of drug addicts" | not given |
| 263 | The voluntary programme tried to fill the gap of heroin withdrawal with God. It claimed much success, compared with the Government drug programmes in other parts of Hong Kong. | "claimed some considerable success" | the mission's own claim |
| 263 | Several foreign missionaries worked in the City. She was the best known and the most accepted. | "the one who received the widest acceptance" | not given |
| 263 | She needed some years to win the trust of the addicts and of the Triads. In the early years her relation with the Triads was tense. Some of her first recruits were Triad members who wanted to stop drugs. A few of them left their gangs; the writer calls this a courageous act. | "a number of her earliest recruits were Triad members looking to kick their own habit" | not given |
| 263 | Local and expatriate volunteers helped, many for several years at a time. | "many staying for several years at a time" | not given |
| 263 | Foreign women started missionary work in the City in the late 1950s and early 1960s. Maureen Clarke set up the Child Care Centre in 1959. | "Maureen Clarke, who set up the Child Care Centre in 1959" | not given |
| 263 | Mrs Donnithorne, the wife of Hong Kong's archdeacon, first brought Jackie Pullinger into the City and to her life's work. | "the person who first introduced Jackie Pullinger to the Walled City" | not given |
| 263 | Three nuns, one Korean and two Italian, stayed in the City until the end. They lived on Lung Chun Road (龍津道) and cared for many old people of the area. | "living on Lung Chun Road and caring for many of the elderly" | not given |
| 263 | Shortly after the clearance announcement, she moved to Mongkok and started a similar drug programme there. | "Shortly after the clearance was announced, Jackie Pullinger moved to Mongkok" | not given |
| 263 | In 1997 the Hong Kong Government gave her land and funds in the hills above Shatin. She made there a refuge for drug addicts, the poor and the homeless. The book says that she runs it "to this day" (the 2014 text), for 200-300 addicts and others at a time. | "between 200 and 300 addicts and others at any one time" | not given (2014) |
| 262 | Photo (top): men pray with raised hands and closed eyes; one man holds a hand on his chest. A tinsel banner of Latin letters hangs on the wall below a wall fan. Only "CHRIS" can be read; it is probably a Christmas greeting. | (photo) | photo |
| 262 | Photo (bottom): men sit on folding chairs of wood and steel. One man lays his hands on another man's shoulders. A whiteboard carries handwritten Chinese lines (not readable). There are a wall fan, a dark heavy doorway and shoes left on the floor by the door. | (photo) | photo |
| 263 | Photo: the congregation sings with palms up and eyes closed. Two Western women stand among the men: one with grey hair and a spiral-bound songbook, one in a striped apron. Paper and tinsel garlands hang from the ceiling. There are two wall fans and a wall hanging with an orange cross. The book names nobody in the photos. | (photo) | photo |
| 311 | **Yu Hing Wan (余慶雲).** At 86 he still worked. He was the last muslin maker of the City. Muslin was once a major industry there, before synthetic cloth came. | "Still working at 86, Yu Hing Wan was the last of the City's muslin makers" | not given |
| 311 | His factory was on Lung Chun Road. He chose the place for the cheap rent, because he needed room for large machines and for stored cloth. | "chose his location on Lung Chun Road for its cheap rent" | not given |
| 311 | Most other factory owners also came to the City for the cheap rent. | "Like most other factory owners in the City" | not given |
| 311 | He felt that outside the City the business was not viable, and that he never had a chance to own a factory there. | "he would never have been able to own his own factory" | his own view, as the writer reports it |
| 311 | He had lived in the City for 18 years. He was a Hakka from Guangdong province, thus a minority among the mainly Chiu Chow population. | "A long-term resident of 18 years" | not given |
| 311 | His factory was neat and clean. After long years of hard work, he suffered most from the noise of the old machines and from the total lack of ventilation. | "the factory's complete lack of ventilation" | not given |
| 310 | Photo: an old man in a brown jumper, grey trousers and sandals works a large old machine (no caption names him; the feature is about Yu). Thin cloth runs over rollers. A chain on a toothed wheel, under a curved guard, drives the machine. A bench holds scissors, tins and bottles, and a mirror hangs above it. The wall is rough brick and peeling plaster; a high window has bars. Bundles of white cloth lie on the floor. | (photo) | photo |
| 311 | Photo: Yu rolls a bolt of white muslin on a table beside stacked rolls. Towels hang on the wall. | (photo) | photo |
| 311 | Photo: Yu folds a length of muslin. A standing electric fan, a rice cooker on a stand, a fluorescent tube and a pillar are near him; machines stand behind. | (photo) | photo |
| 311 | Photo (left, dark): a machine and a poster of three standing figures with characters (not read). | (photo) | photo |
| 312 | **Life on the Roof.** Many illegal structures stood on top of the buildings and set the tone of the roofscape. | "Numerous illegal structures added to the top of buildings set the tone" | not given |
| 312 | The roofs held small private gardens, tangles of TV aerials and cables, washing lines, makeshift water tanks, and many cracks and gaps between the buildings. | "small private gardens, tangles of television aerials and cabling, washing lines, makeshift water tanks" | not given |
| 312 | Rubbish and neglect were everywhere. Weeds grew thick on the few roofs that nobody reached. | "weeds grew profusely on the few roofs that were inaccessible" | not given |
| 312 | Corrugated asbestos sheets were very common on the roofs and in the rest of the City. In the final clearance, teams of workers in masks and suits stripped them off to make the demolition less hazardous. | "teams of masked and suited workers were seen stripping the corrugated asbestos sheeting" | an unnamed witness ("were seen") |
| 312 | Photo: a building with tall grass and bushes growing over its roof. Three storeys show above a lower corrugated roof. It has a window air-conditioner, a balcony ledge with pot plants, an orange-painted balcony with an iron railing and a corrugated awning over each balcony. Aerials stand on the roofs beyond; laundry hangs on lines on a lower roof. | (photo) | photo |
| 313 | Photo: a wide roofscape. Rooftop shacks have corrugated roofs; there are a striped awning, a wooden hut, steel and bamboo frames, a lattice tower and tall pot plants. A person pegs laundry on lines hung with blankets. Aerials stand along the skyline. | (photo) | photo |
| 314 | Jet noise often drowned rooftop talk. The aircraft made their final turn a few hundred feet above the City, near its south-west corner. Then they descended to the runway of Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場), a quarter of a mile to the south. | "Rooftop conversations were frequently drowned out by the scream of jet engines" | not given |
| 314 | Because of the flight path, a height limit held the City to 150 feet, or 15 storeys at most, along its south edge. It was the only building rule that the authorities enforced strictly. | "the only building regulation to be strictly enforced" | not given (the rule is not named) |
| 314 | Photo: a man adjusts a TV aerial in a forest of aerials. A large jet flies low behind him. A tarpaulin lies on the roof. | (photo) | photo |
| 314 | Photo (right strip): aerials and a rooftop wall with a barred window. It is probably the left edge of the p315 photo, across the binding (p356 credits "314/315" to Girard). | (photo) | photo |
| 315 | Photo: a four-engined Cathay Pacific jet, with its wheels down, passes just above the roofs. The roofs carry dense aerials and laundry; a person stands among the laundry, and a folding chair stands on the roof. | "CATHAY PACIFIC" (on the plane) | photo |
| 316 | At nightfall, the shrieks of children at play gave way to the low murmur of adults. After dinner they came up for a quiet chat and the cool night air, then went back to hot, stuffy flats for the night. | "who emerged after dinner for a quiet chat and to enjoy the cool night air" | not given |
| 316 | Photo and caption: the night view looks south from the south-west corner of the City, over Kowloon City (九龍城), to the lights of the Kowloon peninsula. Behind the TV aerials is the light trail of a plane that lands at Kai Tak. A line of lights crosses the middle of the view (perhaps the airport; the caption does not say). | "The view from the south-west corner of the Walled City at night" | photo |
| 316 | Kai Tak was one of Asia's last major inner-city airports, famous for its low approach and its sharp final turn over Kowloon City. It closed in 1998. | "Kai Tak was finally closed in 1998" | not given (a later fact) |
| 317 | Photo: night. Aerials on poles stand in front. Light trails of aircraft cross the sky over the lit city. A block of about seven storeys and a road with car light trails lie below. | (photo) | photo |
| 322 | In the early evening, whole families came up to the roof. A grandmother watched her grandchildren and her neighbours' children while her daughter cooked supper in a flat below. | "Early evening brought whole families up to the roof" | not given |
| 322 | The roof was an exciting playground, among the junk of unwanted fittings and furniture. Out of school hours it was full of children. Some played noisily; some sat quietly at one side and did their homework. | "the detritus of people's unwanted fittings and furniture" | not given |
| 322 | In a few places there was room to ride a bike. | "In a few places there was even enough room to ride a bike." | the photo |
| 322 | Photo (top): children play on a roof among a forest of aerials. Two boys stand on top of a rooftop shed with sticks. Two children climb over debris (planks, a round table top, bags). Two schoolchildren in white shirts and dark skirt or shorts, one with a satchel strap, go into the dark doorway of the shed. The doorway has a decorative iron grille. Hills and high-rise blocks lie far off. | (photo) | photo |
| 322 | Photo (bottom): a boy rides a small bicycle along a narrow rooftop path between parapets, pipes and cables. | (photo) | photo |
| 323 | Photo: a girl does her homework in a folding chair, with a board across its arms as a desk, on a bare concrete roof. Tangled aerials stand round her. A jet airliner flies low behind (its striped tail looks like the Cathay Pacific tail of p315, but no name can be read). A Marlboro billboard stands far off. There are laundry, pot plants and a puddle. | (photo) | photo |
| 324 | TV antennae stood at the eye level of a six-year-old. | "television antennae precariously positioned at the eye level of a six year old" | the photo |
| 324 | The writer says that the City was a relatively safe place for most residents, mostly for children, even in the 1950s. This was true, the writer says, despite its obvious dangers and its fearsome image. | "a relatively safe place for most of its residents, particularly children" | the writer's view; a resident's memory |
| 324 | A resident remembers the 1950s: the children played marbles in the streets, and sometimes on the roofs. They liked to move from one roof to another and watch things far away. | "We used to play marbles in the streets" | an unnamed resident's memory (date of the talk not given) |
| 324 | The same resident remembers the drug addicts, and opium dens all over the City. For fun the children ran in to look at the people who lay there and smoked. There was never any trouble. | "There were opium dens all over the City in those days" | an unnamed resident's memory |
| 324 | Photo: children on a roof. A boy in a yellow vest holds his palm up to the camera. A boy in a white vest sits on the frame of a folding lounger with striped cloth. A boy in a striped vest stands behind. A girl in red sits at the left, with her back to the camera. | (photo) | photo |
| 324 | Photo (right): aerials with a row of small birds on one boom; high-rise blocks and hills lie beyond (the book does not name the place). | (photo) | photo |
| 325 | Photo: a barefoot girl in a blue dress holds a black aerial pole. Boys in vests play behind her. White canvas shoes stand on the roof edge. A cushion and a heap of orange rubber bands (perhaps a skipping rope) lie on the roof. There are a folding chair, dense aerials, hills and a far-off Marlboro sign. | (photo) | photo |
| 326 | Photo: a wide roofscape by day (the scene of p313, with another crop). It shows shacks, corrugated roofs, a striped awning, plants, water tanks, steel frames and a lattice tower. | (photo) | photo |
| 327 | Photo: a roof strewn with broken wood, a ladder and a ball. A black animal (probably a dog) lies on the roof. Dense aerials and cables, barred windows and window air-conditioners surround it. Quilts air on a parapet or tank in front. | (photo) | photo |

### Part 11. Standfirsts, captions and glosses on the interview pages, part 1 (pp20–31, 34–53, 56–61, 76–79, 82–85, 86–103, 112–115)

Writer: the editors (1993 or 2014). Checked on 2026-11-16: 120 rows checked, 25 fixed, 0 deleted, 16 added. 132 rows. Then 5 rows for pp84–85 were added on 2026-11-16 (not checked by a second agent): 137 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 22 | The photo looks down Lo Yan Street (老人街) from Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道). The book translates the name as "Old People's" Street. | "An entrance to the labyrinth, looking down Lo Yan (Old People's) Street from Tung Tau Tsuen Road" | own photo |
| 22 | Tung Tau Tsuen Road made the north edge of the City. | "the road which defined the Walled City's northern perimeter" | not given |
| 22 | A large vertical sign at this entrance named the Kowloon Walled City Landlords' Association (九龍城寨業主聯誼會). | "The large vertical sign advertises the presence of the Kowloon Walled City Landlords' Association" | own photo |
| 22 | A horizontal sign further down Lo Yan Street marked a doctor's clinic. | "the horizontal sign further down the alley is for a doctor's clinic" | own photo |
| 22 | The City stood on a slope. The ground level fell four storeys from Tung Tau Tsuen Road to Lung Chun Road (龍津道) on the south edge. | "the City dropped four storeys at ground level" | not given |
| 22 | Lo Yan Street was one of the four main alleys that ran north-south across the City. | "One of the four main alleys that ran north-south across the City" | not given |
| 22 | Lo Yan Street opened to the sky where it passed the central courtyard of the old yamen (衙門). Thus it was the only street with some daylight. | "the only street that had some degree of natural lighting along its course" | not given |
| 23 | Yau Lap Cheong came from Chiu Yeung, China, in 1928, at age 20. He lived near and in the City until he moved out in 1990. | "until the day he moved out in 1990, during the first phase of the clearance" | the interview |
| 24 | His one-room flat probably had an area of 120 sq ft or less. This area included a tiny kitchen and a smaller washroom with a lavatory. | "no more than 120 square feet, including the tiny kitchen and an even smaller washroom/lavatory" | own photo (p25) |
| 24 | The room had no windows and no air-conditioning. He spent his days there and rarely went out; a radio and a television were his only pastime. | "Without windows or air-conditioning" | own photo (p25) |
| 25 | Photo detail (the p24 caption is for this photo): Yau sits in a wicker armchair beside a bed with folded quilts. Jackets and a striped shirt hang on hangers on the walls, below wall cupboards. An electric fan stands at the left. A doorway at the right opens to the tiny kitchen, with pots and a stove. The floor has small tiles. | (no words) | own photo |
| 27 | The photos show details of the east and the west elevations. | "Detail views of the City's east (opposite) and west (right) elevations" | own photos |
| 27 | The Government's Public Works Department had no dealings with the City, apart from a strict height limit. The limit was there because Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場) was near. | "With the exception of a strict height limit due to the proximity of Kai Tak Airport" | not given |
| 27 | No rule made the City's contractors submit plans or drawings. | "the contractors there were not obliged to submit plans or drawings" | not given |
| 27 | Builders worked from experience and simple sketches, and thus saved professional fees. | "most blocks were built from experience using simple sketches, thereby saving on professional fees" | not given |
| 27 | Builders usually built "by sight alone". Thus the storeys of one building often had very different floor areas. This made the compensation assessment hard. | "a serious inconvenience when it came to assessing compensation" | not given |
| 27 | Many buildings stood on very narrow sites. Each building made a separate vertical slice, with no horizontal line in common with its neighbours. | "a complete disregard for any sort of horizontal uniformity with their neighbours" | own photos |
| 28 | Ho Chi Kam (何智錦) lived in the City long before 1985. He took over the barber's shop at 10 Tai Chang Street (大井街) in 1985. | "only took over the running of the barber's shop at 10 Tai Chang Street in 1985" | the interview |
| 28 | Ho was unhappy about the clearance. He gave the interviewers only a short interview. | "He was unhappy about the clearance and would only give a short interview." | the interviewers' own account |
| 29 | The barber's door faced the dark Tai Chang Street, near the only Government stand-pipe inside the City. | "not far from the sole Government stand-pipe within the confines of the Walled City" | not given |
| 29 | The shop was small but prosperous. Almost all its customers were local residents. | "Customers were almost entirely local residents" | not given |
| 29 | Cuts and shampoos were cheap. Thus customers sometimes queued. | "low prices for cuts and shampoos meant the occasional queue for service" | not given |
| 29 | The shop closed in 1991, shortly before phase two of the clearance started. | "The shop eventually closed in 1991, shortly before the start of phase two of the clearance." | not given |
| 31 | The photo (p30) shows Ho giving a customer a perm while his wife watches. | "Ho Chi Kam tends to a customer's perm as his wife looks on." | own photo |
| 30 | Photo detail: two towels hang on wire hangers on the wall. A white box with pipes is fixed high on the wall (INFERRED: a water heater). The customer has curlers in her hair, and a round metal tray of curlers stands on a pole beside her. The lower wall has white tiles. | (no words) | own photo |
| 31 | Small business owners were perhaps hardest hit by the uncertainty after the clearance was announced. | "Small business owners in the City were perhaps hardest hit by the uncertainties" | not given |
| 31 | The Government's compensation formula used the cheap rents inside the City. Thus many sole proprietors were unable to pay for new premises outside, and they closed. | "calculated according to a formula based on the cheaper rents within the Walled City" | not given |
| 34 | Many City dental practitioners, Wong Yu Ming (黃如明) among them, worked mainly on dentures, often under contract to registered dentists outside the City. | "shaping and refining dentures, often under contract to registered dentists outside the City" | not given |
| 34 | A certificate on his clinic wall said that he was also a trained herbal doctor. | "A certificate of accreditation displayed in his clinic" | own visit |
| 34 | Photo detail: a tear-off wall calendar beside Wong shows 新曆 七月 21 and 星期五 (Friday), with the lunar date 舊曆 6月19. These fit Friday 21 July 1989 (INFERRED; see Doubts). Glass cases above the calendar hold rows of small objects that look like false teeth (INFERRED). | "七月", "21", "星期五" (on the calendar in the photo) | own photo |
| 35 | Wong Yu Ming was born in 1930. The standfirst says that he fled to Hong Kong with his wife and children in the late 1950s. He opened his dental clinic on Lung Shing Road (龍城路) within a few months. | "opening his dental clinic on Lung Shing Road within a few months of his arrival" | the interview (but see Doubts) |
| 36 | His wife, Cheung Mei, was his dental assistant. She learnt the trade from him. | "Dental assistant Cheung Mei learnt her trade from her husband, Wong Yu Ming" | not given |
| 36 | In the City's heyday, people thought that more than 150 illegal dentists worked in the City. By 1987 the number was 86. | "more than 150 illegal dentists were thought to be practising in the City"; "the figure had dropped to 86 by 1987" | not given |
| 36 | Standards were usually poor. Most dentists kept to simple work. | "simple dentistry such as teeth cleaning, small fillings and denture work" | not given |
| 36 | For serious work, for example pulling teeth, many dentists sent the patient to a qualified practitioner elsewhere, because they feared complications. | "many would make a referral to qualified practitioners elsewhere" | not given |
| 36 | A few dentists went too far. Stories of patients in hospital with heavily bleeding gums were not rare. | "stories of patients being admitted to hospital with heavily bleeding gums were not uncommon" | not given (stories) |
| 37 | Photo detail (the p36 caption is for this photo): Cheung Mei stands in the clinic beside a dental chair with a lamp on an arm. A wall clock with a pendulum hangs behind her, and a glass cabinet stands at the door. In the dark alley outside, a man in shorts walks past. White marks on the alley wall read "D44" over "125"; more marks below are not legible. They are probably a clearance reference number of the kind that the p53 caption tells of (INFERRED). | "D44", "125" (on the wall in the photo) | own photo |
| 39 | The Sai Tau Tsuen (西頭村) squatter settlement was demolished in 1985. | "after the Sai Tau Tsuen squatter settlement had been demolished (in 1985)" | not given |
| 39 | After 1985, and before Carpenter Road Park was finished, the City stood alone in open ground for its last years. | "the Walled City stood in magnificent isolation" | own photos |
| 39 | The south elevation faced the new park. It had over 30 separate buildings of different heights and widths along 240 yards. Some were one room wide. | "with over 30 individual buildings of different heights and widths – some just one room wide – jammed together along its 240-yard length" | not given |
| 39 | The aerial photo of pp38-39 was taken in 1989. Kai Tak Airport shows in the lower right corner. | "Photographed in 1989" | own photo |
| 39 | Later event: Kai Tak closed in 1998, and new building then changed the whole area. | "triggered an explosion of new building that has totally transformed the entire area" | not given |
| 40 | Hui Kwong (許光) was 75 in 1991. He still played in the Chiu Chow Music Club. | "Though 75 at the time of the interview (in 1991), Hui Kwong was still an active member of the Chiu Chow Music Club." | the interview |
| 40 | Hui Kwong lived in the City from 1961. For many years he was financial secretary of the Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會). | "had lived in the City since 1961"; "financial secretary of the Kai Fong Association there for many years" | the interview |
| 40 | Editors' gloss: in the interview, Hui Kwong says that the Kai Fong Association was formed when the Government tried to pull the City down. The editors date that attempt to 1962. | "[in 1962]" | not given |
| 40 | Editors' gloss: Hui Kwong tells of overseas Chinese who went back to China in large numbers. The editors add the reason. | "[to help with the revolutionary effort]" | not given |
| 40 | Editors' gloss: Liu Shaoqi became President of the People's Republic in 1959. | "[Liu became President of the People's Republic in 1959.]" | not given |
