| 41 | Editors' gloss: the 'reporter' who sometimes visited the Kai Fong was an employee of the New China News Agency. | "[an employee of the New China News Agency]" | not given |
| 41 | Editors' gloss: in 1984 the Sino-British Joint Declaration was signed. | "[when the Sino-British Joint Declaration was signed]" | not given |
| 41 | The photo shows a member of the music club playing the erhu, a two-stringed instrument. | "the two-stringed instrument that gives traditional Chinese music its distinctive sorrowful tone" | own photo |
| 43 | Lung Chun Back Road (龍津後街) was the only alley that ran east-west across the City for a long distance. It was one of the main thoroughfares. | "As the only alley that ran east-west across the City for any significant distance" | not given |
| 43 | Like all City alleys, Lung Chun Back Road was hardly ever crowded. For most of the day it was very quiet. | "hardly ever crowded and for most of the day, as here, it was very quiet indeed" | own photo |
| 43 | The photo (p42) looks west. On the right is the door of the Salvation Army (救世軍) kindergarten. Opposite it, closed shutters hide a fishball factory. | "opposite closed shutters that conceal a fishball factory" | own photo |
| 43 | The entrance to the yamen and the central courtyard was further west along the alley, on the right (thus on the north side: INFERRED). | "the entrance to the yamen and the City's central courtyard (page 296) was further down the alley on the right" | own photo |
| 44 | Jetly Chau Sau Yee (周秀儀) was born in Chiu Chow (潮州). He came to Hong Kong at age six. In 1968, at 14, he left school for the family bakery. | "In 1968, at the age of 14, he left school to help his father in the family bakery business." | the interview |
| 44 | After the 1987 announcement, guided tours of the City became popular. | "After the clearance was announced, in 1987, guided tours of the City became increasingly popular" | not given |
| 44 | His Chiu Chow cake shop stood at an entrance with space for tour buses to stop. It became a regular stop for foreign tour groups in the last years. | "a regular stopping point for foreign tour groups in the final years" | own visits |
| 44 | A sign in English described his cakes for visitors. In the photo (p45), a board reads "VERY NICE TO EAT" / "AND GOOD LOOK.". A paper label below it reads "FOR CHINESE WORSHIP IDOL" and one more letter, probably "S". A Chinese label under that reads 傳統中國拜神祭品. Another board reads "BEAN SOUP" / "50 ¢". | "English introduced his wares to non-Chinese speaking visitors" | own photo |
| 47 | The south elevation had ad hoc caged balconies. They were technically illegal and typical of the City's outer edge. | "the ad-hoc, and technically illegal, caged balcony extensions" | own photos |
| 47 | Such cages were also common in older, low-income housing in Hong Kong at that time. They were later called a safety hazard and are now gone. | "They were deemed a safety hazard in the late 1990s" | not given |
| 47 | The cages gave small homes extra outside space. | "adding valuable external space to limited living quarters" | not given |
| 47 | Television cables covered the whole facade. They often ran up 10 or more storeys to many separate aerials on the roof. | "often running up 10 or more storeys to a riot of individual aerials on the roof" | own photos |
| 48 | Lau Yeung Yin (劉陽賢) was one of 12 children. In 1982 he took over the family weaving business, in a small factory on Sai Shing (West City) Road (西城路). | "One of 12 children" | the interview |
| 49 | The photo shows Lau among the large spindles of his weaving and threading factory, with his baby son. | "Lau Yeung Yin sits among the giant spindles of his weaving and threading factory with his baby son." | own photo |
| 49 | Most of his clients were outside the City. He did regular business with the City muslin maker Yu Hing Wan (余慶雲). | "Lau maintained regular business contact with resident muslin-maker, Yu Hing Wan" | not given |
| 49 | Photo detail: Lau sits on a large white roll. Machines with more large white rolls fill the left of the room. A fluorescent tube lights the room, a wrench hangs on the wall, and a metal grille gate closes the right side. | (no words) | own photo |
| 51 | Photo detail (no caption): a man puts a white spool on a tall rack full of white thread spools. More spools lie in boxes on the floor. The page stands in Lau Yeung Yin's pages; thus the place is probably his factory (INFERRED). | (no words) | own photo |
| 53 | Many alleys and ground-level ways were untidy and neglected, and near squalid in places. | "verging on the squalid in more than a few places" | not given |
| 53 | The public stairs inside the buildings were usually free of rubbish and fairly clean. Their faults were old paint and, sometimes, a leaking pipe with damp walls and mould. | "public stairways inside the buildings were usually rubbish free and relatively clean" | own visits |
| 53 | Hand-painted signs on the stairs told where a business was, or the way to it. In the photo, the characters 運通 and an arrow are painted on the stair walls. | "Hand-painted signs advertised the presence of or directions to a business" | own photo |
| 53 | White reference numbers on a security gate (the left photo, p52) showed that the residents had left in the clearance. | "white reference numbers on a security gate (left) indicated the residents had moved out as part of the clearance" | own photo |
| 56 | The photo shows the cashier's desk of the Chung Fat café. A ladder in the corner goes up to a small cockloft. Ng Kam Mui's (吳錦妹) mother sometimes slept there. | "the ladder in the corner leading to a small cockloft where Ng Kam Mui's mother occasionally spent the night" | own photo |
| 56 | The Chung Fat café was a typical cha chaan teng. It sold Chinese and Western drinks, snacks and quick meals at low prices, from early morning until late at night. | "usually open from early morning until late at night" | not given (general knowledge of the writer) |
| 56 | Typical cha chaan teng food: yuan-yang tea (half coffee, half tea), thick buttered toast with condensed milk, and instant noodles with fried egg and luncheon meat. | "thick buttered toast with condensed milk drizzled on top" | not given (general) |
| 56 | Photo detail: on and behind the cashier's desk are a black dial telephone, shelves of cigarette cartons, Coca-Cola bottles, a wall clock and a panel of electric switches and sockets. | (no words) | own photo |
| 56 | Photo detail: a tear-off calendar on the wall shows Friday 10 March 1989 (三月, lunar 初三日). Thus the photo was probably taken on that day (INFERRED). | "MARCH 1989" (on the calendar in the photo) | own photo |
| 57 | Ng Kam Mui lived or worked at the Chung Fat café on Tung Tau Tsuen Road for nearly 30 years. The café closed in 1991, in the first phase. | "from the day it opened until its closure in 1991 during the first phase of the clearance" | the interview |
| 58 | The Chung Fat café was popular with taxi drivers because it was on Tung Tau Tsuen Road. It was one of the City's smarter eating places. | "A popular haunt for taxi drivers due to its location on Tung Tau Tsuen Road" | not given |
| 58 | The photos (pp58-59) show a more basic Chiu Chow restaurant on the south side, just off Lung Chun Road. It was as popular, and its customers were almost all men. | "this Chiu Chow restaurant on the south side of the City, just off Lung Chun Road"; "solidly male clientele" | own photo |
| 59 | Photo detail: in the Chiu Chow restaurant, men eat at small tables and sit on round stools and folding chairs. A fan is fixed high on the wall, framed pictures hang above the tables, and the floor has small tiles. | (no words) | own photo |
| 60-61 | Photo detail (no caption): a facade with caged balconies, windows, aircons and a TV aerial. Signs for doctors show on it: 西醫陳詠蘭 (p60), and 國際診所 on a large sign and a small one (p61). | 西醫陳詠蘭; 國際診所 (signs in the photos) | own photo |
| 76 | Lee Yu Chun (李玉珍) worked in the family candy business from its start in 1964, when she was under 10. | "from its beginning in 1964, when she was not yet 10 years old" | the interview |
| 76 | The factory started in Sham Shui Po. It moved into the City a few years later, and to its last site at 12 Sai Shing Road in the early 1970s. | "final location at 12 Sai Shing (West City) Road in the early 1970s" | the interview |
| 77 | The candy 'factory' was a small, cramped room. The work spread out into the alley, Sai Shing Road. | "in reality a small cramped room" | own photo |
| 77 | A machine inside the room made the candy. Thus the room was always hot and sticky. | "always hot and sticky" | own visit |
| 77 | All wrapping and packing took place at tables along the alley. Her children, and sometimes their friends, often helped in the evening after school. | "all the wrapping and packaging took place on tables set out along the alley" | own photo |
| 79 | The Lees employed a number of women, all City residents, to wrap the candies in cellophane. | "all Walled City residents, to wrap the candies in their cellophane wrappers" | own photo |
| 79 | Health and safety had a low priority. | "Health and safety precautions were not high on the agenda" | own visit |
| 79 | Lee Yu Chun was very unhappy with the compensation offer. The Government said that it paid businesses by their profit, if they had records to prove it. | "the authorities said they would compensate businesses in proportion to their profitability as long as verifiable records could be produced" | not given (a Government statement) |
| 79 | Photo details: in the top photo, an old woman in an apron and a boy with a tray of red candies stand by a machine. A man works at the back. In the lower photo, women wrap candies at a table in the alley. An electric fan and a radio-cassette player stand on a shelf. A fruit carton reads "WASHINGTON APPLES". Telephone numbers are painted on the wall after 電話, beside large red characters 樓 and 上. | "WASHINGTON APPLES" (on the carton in the photo) | own photo |
| 82 | Chan Pui Yin (陳沛然) came to Hong Kong from Chiu Chow county in 1947. Eight years later, at 29, he opened his own store on Lung Chun Road. | "Eight years later, at the age of 29, he opened his own store on Lung Chun Road." | the interview |
| 82 | Like many traditional herbalists, his shop had two sides: Chinese medicines on one side and Western toiletries on the other. | "Chinese medicines on one side and Western toiletries" | own photo |
| 83 | Photo detail (the p82 caption is for this photo): the toiletries side has glass-fronted shelves, toilet rolls stacked high, detergent, shampoo, toothpaste and a glass case of cigarettes (Kent, Marlboro). | (no words; the labels "KENT" and "SABA" show in the photo) | own photo |
| 84 | Chan Pui Yin mixed his Chinese medicines at the counter. They were usually infusions or poultices. | "usually infusions or poultices" | own photo |
| 84 | He took the herbs from glass jars or from the large array of drawers, and he weighed them out on traditional Chinese hand-scales. | "weighing them out with traditional Chinese hand-scales" | own photo |
| 84 | His drawers had no labels: his years of experience made labels unnecessary. | "Years of experience meant labels were unnecessary." | own visit |
| 84 | The caption says that the next spread (pp86–87) shows the west elevation of the City. | "a typical Walled City composition with buildings of different sizes and heights" | not given |
| 84–85 | Photo detail (no caption on p85): the herbal side of the shop (p84) has a wall of pale green drawers with brass handles, shelves of glass jars of dried herbs and a glass counter with boxed medicines. The p85 photos show the other side, with a wall clock, a ceiling fan, cartons, stacked toilet rolls and bottles, and Chan Pui Yin at his counter in front of the drawers. | (description) | own photo |
| 88 | Chan Kwong came to Hong Kong at 26. He worked as a hawker and in a plastics factory. In 1970 he opened a golf-ball factory in Kowloon City (九龍城). | "opening his own golf-ball factory in Kowloon City, in 1970" | the interview |
| 88 | In 1972 Chan Kwong moved the factory to Tai Chang Street in the City. | "Two years later, he moved to Tai Chang Street in the Walled City." | the interview |
| 89 | The photo shows a noodle maker's premises. Flour dust covered every surface. | "the dusting of flour that covered every surface" | own photo |
| 89 | Like all City businesses, the noodle maker had no health and safety inspections. But a China Light and Power (中華電力) bill showed that some things were checked. | "an electricity bill from China Light and Power provides proof that not all facets of the business escaped scrutiny" | own photo |
| 89 | Photo detail: the CLP envelope is addressed to "FLT 2 G/F", 31 "LO YAN ST", "SAI TAU VIL". A tear-off calendar shows Saturday 27, 十月, lunar 初十日. | "PRINTED PAPERS" (on the envelope) | own photo |
| 91 | The store in the photo (p90) stood at the corner of Lung Chun Back Road and Tai Chang Street. It was a few yards from the City's one Government stand-pipe. | "just yards away from the one Government stand-pipe located within the City" | own photo |
| 91 | It was one of the City's busiest stores. Customers came all day: some for household goods, others only for a chat. | "some stopping to buy household necessities of one sort or another, while others just called in for a chat" | own visits |
| 91 | Like many City shops, it was usually open from 6am to midnight, seven days a week. | "usually open from 6am to midnight, seven days a week" | not given |
| 91 | A barber's 'pole' further down Tai Chang Street marks Ho Chi Kam's salon. Further down again, a person waits at the stand-pipe. | "someone can be seen waiting at the stand-pipe" | own photo |
| 90 | Photo detail (the p91 caption is for this photo): a lit, glass-fronted drinks cabinet holds bottles and cans. Bags of snacks hang from a rack above the counter. Cardboard cartons stand in front, and a folding stool stands beside the cabinet. | (no words) | own photo |
| 92 | Lee Pui Yuen's (李培元) shop on Tai Chang Street was modest, but it gave a good living. | "Lee Pui Yuen's shop on Tai Chang Street was a modest affair, but he made a good living there" | not given |
| 92 | The shop sold basics (rice, tinned fish, condensed milk, toilet paper) and more profitable goods (cigarettes, soft drinks, ice cream, Tic-Tac sweets). | "rice, tinned fish, condensed milk and toilet paper"; "a little unexpectedly, Tic-Tac sweets" | own photo |
| 92 | Photo detail: Lee sits behind the counter in a vest, with a boy behind him. A glass cabinet of cigarettes carries "KENT" signs, and a chest freezer carries a "PEPSI" sign. Toilet rolls fill the top shelves, and tins fill the lower shelves. A black dial telephone stands at the left. | "KENT"; "PEPSI" (signs in the photo) | own photo |
| 93 | Lee Pui Yuen lived in the City for 15 years. In 1979 he opened his own store at 12 Tai Chang Street. | "before opening his own store there at 12 Tai Chang Street, in 1979" | the interview |
| 93 | He lived and worked in the store with his wife and young son for 12 years. They left in the second phase of the clearance, in 1991. | "They moved out as part of the second phase of the clearance in 1991." | the interview |
| 93 | Editors' gloss: Lee's load of 40 catties of rice equals 60 pounds. | "[60 pounds]" | not given |
| 94 | A simple partition divided the store from a small living area at the back. Lee, his wife and his son slept there. | "Just a simple partition separated Lee Pui Yuen's store from a small living area at the back of the shop" | own photo |
| 94 | The family lived in the store. Thus the store stayed open until Lee and his wife turned off the television, shuttered the shop and went to bed, after a 16-hour day. | "at the end of a 16-hour day" | own visits |
| 95 | Photo detail (the p94 caption is for this photo): behind the partition, a boy sits on a folding chair. A bed with folded quilts stands at the right, and a fan is fixed high on the wall. Shop shelves show at the left. | (no words) | own photo |
| 97 | Most City buildings were torn down and rebuilt often, but not all. | "Not all of the buildings had been regularly torn down and rebuilt like so many in the City." | not given |
| 97 | At the east end of the south elevation stood two older brick buildings among 13-, 14- and 15-storey concrete ones. They probably dated from the early 1960s. | "a pair of much older brick buildings, probably dating back to the early 1960s" | not given (estimate) |
| 97 | Each was little more than 12 ft wide. One had five storeys, a sure sign of age; its neighbour had eight. | "Both were little more than 12 feet wide" | not given |
| 97 | A rusty sign on them advertised a dental practice. | "Inevitably, the rusty sign advertised a dental practice." | own photo |
| 98 | Section heading: "The Electricity Supply". The meters looked chaotic, but they had an order. | "The haphazard appearance of the" (the rest of the line is cut at the binding) | own photo |
| 98 | Meter readers followed careful notes. They carried torches and dog repellent. They read 400 to 5[00?] meters a day. | "who were armed with torches"; "dog repellent – could cover 400 to" | not given |
| 98 | Mok Chung Yuk worked for China Light and Power from the mid-1960s. He was one of the engineers for the regulated supply in the City in the late 1970s and early 1980s. | "responsible for the regulated installation of electricity within the Walled City during the late '70s and early '80s" | the interview |
| 98 | Mok Chung Yuk refused to be photographed. | "He declined to be photographed." | the interviewers' own account |
| 98 | Editors' gloss: "China Electric" is the old name of China Light and Power. | "China Electric [now China Light and Power]" | not given |
| 100 | The photo (on p101: INFERRED) shows a tangle of wires, crusted with cobwebs and dirt. It runs from a bank of meters on the ground floor to the flats above. The wires are fixed directly to the walls. | "the wires tacked directly to the walls" | own photo |
| 103 | Daylight reached the street only at narrow gaps between buildings. Plastic bags and rubbish often blocked these gaps. | "obstructed by plastic bags and assorted rubbish" | own photos |
| 103 | Where there was space, people hung washing out to dry in these gaps. | "Where space permitted, washing would be hung out to dry." | own photos |
| 103 | Many smaller alleys in the middle of the City had lights only in places. | "many of the smaller alleys in the middle of the City were only intermittently lit" | own visits |
| 112 | Chan Shing was one of about 70 private water suppliers in the City. | "One of the City's 70 or so private water suppliers" | not given |
| 112 | He collected the monthly charges from his customers in person. Sometimes he let old people off. The caption gives his reason in his own words. | "we are all poor people here and we should help each other" | Chan Shing's words, as the caption reports them ("as he said") |
| 112 | His pump was usually hidden behind a floor panel. He switched it on at different times to fill a large storage tank on the roof. | "Usually concealed behind a floor panel, the pump would be switched on at various times to replenish a large storage tank on the roof." | own photo |
| 112 | Photo detail: Chan Shing crouches beside an open hatch in the mosaic floor of a tiled room. In the hatch are a pipe and a valve with a red wheel. A small altar with a red light stands low in a cabinet, and kettles stand on the floor. | (no words) | own photo |
| 113 | Chu Yiu Shan was born in 1951 and grew up in the City. For many years he was a property developer and estate agent there. | "He operated as a property developer and estate agent for many years" | the interview |
| 113 | Chu Yiu Shan moved to Junk Bay only when the clearance was announced. | "only moving to Junk Bay when the clearance was announced" | the interview |
| 23, 29, 57, 93 | The standfirsts and captions use phases of the clearance: Yau moved out in 1990 and the Chung Fat café closed in 1991, in the first phase. Lee left in 1991, in the second phase. | "during the first phase of the clearance" (p23, p57); "the second phase of the clearance in 1991" (p93) | not given |

### Part 12. Standfirsts, captions and glosses on the interview pages, part 2 (pp144–191)

Writer: the editors (1993 or 2014); a quotation of Jan Morris (1990). Checked on 2026-11-16: 120 rows checked, 25 fixed, 4 deleted, 16 added. 109 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 144 | The photo shows postman Lui Man Sang. He comes out of a building staircase into an alley on his round. | "enters one of the Walled City's alleys while making his rounds" | own photo |
| 144 | The editors say that few people knew the layout of the City as completely as Mr Lui. | "Few people had as complete an understanding of the City's layout" | not given |
| 144 | Probably no one knew better how the buildings, alleys, staircases, hallways and rooftops linked together. | "probably no one better understood the interconnectedness of its buildings, alleys, staircases, hallways and rooftops than Mr Lui" | not given |
| 144 | In the building boom of the late 1970s, the street patterns and the addresses changed all the time. | "especially during the building boom of the late 1970s when street patterns and addresses were constantly in flux" | not given |
| 145 | The photos of Mr Lui first appeared, with others, in the original volume (*City of Darkness*, 1993). They recorded one day in his life. | "a record of a day in the life of Mr Lui, the postman" | own photos |
| 145 | Mr Lui was a willing subject for photos. But at the time he told the photographers little about his work. | "Although obliging as a photographic subject, he volunteered little of his experiences at the time." | own visit |
| 145 | For the 2014 edition, the editors spoke with Lam Po Chun, a former colleague of Mr Lui. He tells of delivering mail in the City with Mr Lui (pp145-149, retrospective testimony). | "while researching this new edition, we were able to speak Mr Lui's former colleague, Lam Po Chun" | interview (2014) |
| 146 | Editors' gloss in square brackets: the 'three vices' (huangdudu) are pornography, illegal gambling and drug-peddling. | "[huangdudu: pornography, illegal gambling and drug-peddling]" | not given |
| 146 | Editors' gloss in square brackets: the City was 'triply neglected' by Great Britain, Hong Kong and China. | "[by Great Britain, Hong Kong and China]" | not given |
| 146 | Mr Lui started his daily round at a store on Lo Yan Street (老人街). | "Mr Lui's daily round began at a store on Lo Yan Street" | own photos |
| 146 | From the store he made several separate trips. Some trips were simple drops at collective mail-banks. | "several separate sorties which took him to simple deliveries at collective mail-banks" | own photos |
| 146 | Other trips took him up intricate climbs into the dark stairwells of older blocks, to find obscure drop-off points. | "intricate climbs into the dark stairwells of older blocks in search of obscure drop-offs" | own photos |
| 146 | At a skylight he went out onto the roofs. He crossed from building to building, then went down into the dark again. | "On reaching a skylight, he would cross the rooftops, hopping from building to building before disappearing down into the darkness again." | own photos |
| 149 | Most postmen wanted to avoid the City round at all costs, because of its bad reputation. | "the City was considered a round to be avoided at all costs such was its poor reputation" | not given |
| 149 | The working conditions were also notorious. A hat was standard issue against the constant dripping in the alleys. The photos on pp144, 147 and 148 show Mr Lui in a blue cap, with a brown bag on a shoulder strap. | "a hat was standard issue against the constant dripping in the alleyways" | not given; own photos |
| 149 | On a normal Hong Kong route, a trainee learned a new route in a day or so. In the City it took about three weeks. | "in the subterranean maze of the City it was more like three weeks" | not given |
| 150 | The caged balconies were nearly always painted dark green or orange, with rust-preventing paint. | "Invariably painted in the characteristic dark green or orange rust-preventing paint" | own photos (pp150-151) |
| 150 | Each balcony showed the character of the people behind it, and the editors found them a constant fascination. Some were untended and a very few totally unused. Others were jam-packed and untidy. Most were in good order. | "most were kept in good order" | own photos |
| 150 | The contents of a balcony showed the owners' interests, for example songbirds in bamboo cages, or pot-plants. | "whether songbirds in bamboo cages or pot-plants" | own photos |
| 150 | The washing on a balcony helped to show the make-up of a family, or their age. | "Even the washing helped identify the make-up of a family or their age." | own photos |
| 150 | Each balcony frame followed a standard pattern. Each maker added his own flourishes. | "based around a standard pattern but made distinctive via the various flourishes of its maker" | own photos |
| 153 | Lam Shu Chuen (林樹全) lived in the City for more than 50 years. | "Lam Shu Chuen lived in the City for more than 50 years." | the interview |
| 153 | He was a senior member of the Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會). For many years he was chairman of the Industrial Commercial Liaison Committee. | "he served as chairman of the Industrial Commercial Liaison Committee for many years" | not given |
| 153 | He owned a family factory. It made components for radio-controlled toys and other devices. | "a family-run factory making components for radio-controlled toys and other devices" | not given |
| 153 | After the clearance he moved the factory to Tai Po. It was still there in 2014. | "which he relocated to Tai Po after the clearance where it remains to this day" | not given |
| 153 | The photo on p152 shows the Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道) side of the City at dusk. | "Dusk on the Tung Tau Tsuen Road side of the City." | own photo |
| 153 | The editors call the City a slum only in a limited sense. In their view it was a working community that its people made, with no overbearing authority but with its own rules. | "a fully functioning community fashioned by those who lived there" | not given |
| 154 | As all over Hong Kong, a small family shrine protected many City flats. Families regularly gave it fresh food and incense, for good fortune and against evil spirits. The p154 photo shows a tiled stair landing with red posters and small shrines at the doors. | "regularly blessed with fresh offerings of food and incense to bring good fortune and steer away evil spirits" | own photos (pp154-155) |
| 154 | Families put up printed posters at the start of Chinese New Year. The posters were to give a harmonious and safe household for the next 12 months. | "to ensure a harmonious and safe household over the coming 12 months" | own photos |
| 154 | The flats in the buildings on Tung Tau Tsuen Road were the most desirable and the best in the City. | "were considered the most desirable and were undoubtedly the best in the City" | not given |
| 154 | These flats were relatively spacious and had natural light. People entered them straight from the main road, and thus avoided the City proper. | "accessed straight off the main road, avoiding the problem of having to enter the City proper" | not given |
| 154 | Their stairways were often cleaner than other stairways in the City. Their residents watched more closely for fire. | "Stairways were often noticeably cleaner than elsewhere in the City" | own photos |
| 157 | Chung Lo Ying moved into the City in 1958. She lived in several buildings. Then she bought a flat on the fourth floor of 82 Lung Chun Road (龍津道), over the park. | "buying an apartment on the fourth floor of 82 Lung Chun Road, overlooking the park" | not given (the printed interview gives "more than 30 years" and a fourth-floor flat, but no year or address) |
| 157 | She had 10 children. One or two of them often came for dinner, among them Chung Kwai Mao and Chung Yuk Yee. | "She was regularly joined for dinner by one or two of her 10 children" | own visit |
| 157 | In the dinner photo (p156), Chung Kwai Mao wears glasses, and Chung Lo Ying sits with her back to the wall. | "Lo Ying is sitting with her back to the wall." | own photo |
| 158 | Her flat was on the south side of the City. It was small but tidy, with fresh air and views of the park. | "small but tidy apartment opened onto fresh air and views of the park" | own visit |
| 158 | The editors judge the flat attractive and relatively spacious, even beside similar low-income housing elsewhere in Hong Kong. | "even by the standards of similar low-income housing elsewhere in Hong Kong" | own visit |
| 158 | Her daughter Chung Yuk Yee often came for dinner and helped to cook. | "often joined her mother for dinner and helped with the cooking" | own visit |
| 159 | Chung Yuk Yee's flat had no windows and lay deep inside the City. | "Chung Yuk Yee's windowless flat was in the bowels of the City" | own visit |
| 159 | Her flat was 230 sq ft. A thin wooden partition divided it into two rooms. | "Just 230 square feet in area, it was divided into two rooms by a thin wooden partition." | own visit |
| 159 | She, her husband and their two children slept in one room. | "the bedroom where she, her husband and two children slept" | own visit |
| 159 | In the other room, the living room, family and friends sometimes played mahjong. The photo shows a game. | "family and friends gathered occasionally to play mahjong" | own photo |
| 160 | Her two small children watched television in the small bedroom that they shared with their parents. | "Chung Yuk Yee's two small children watch television in the small bedroom they share with their parents" | own photo |
| 160 | Electric fans were the only relief from the summer heat. | "with electric fans the only relief against the summer heat" | own photo |
| 160 | She ran the household. First she cooked at her mother's flat; later she cooked in her own flat. | "first with the cooking at her mother's place and then, later, in her own apartment" | own visit |
| 160 | She bathed her son and daughter in murky water. A pipe brought the water from a rooftop storage tank, and ground wells under the City filled the tank. The photo on p161 shows a child in a red basin. | "the murky water piped from a rooftop storage tank fed by ground wells beneath the City" | not given; own photo |
| 161 | Chung Yuk Yee was born in the City in 1961. She moved out in 1991, during part of the clearance. | "lived in the Walled City from her birth, in 1961, until moving out during part of the clearance in 1991" | not given (the printed interview gives neither year) |
| 161 | She was married, with a son and a daughter. Her flat was in the same building as her mother's, but it faced north, with no fresh air and no view. INFERRED: thus it was at the back of the building at 82 Lung Chun Road. | "in an apartment in the same building as her mother, but facing north without fresh air or a view" | own visit |
| 163 | The City had many plastics factories, especially ones that pressed simple moulds or made cheap toys. The photos (pp162-163) show moulding machines, big sacks (one is marked "GPPS"), a red drum and fluorescent tubes. | "Plastics factories were abundant in the City, particularly those pressing simple moulds or tooling cheap toys." | own photos |
| 163 | They stored and used highly flammable chemicals, a serious fire risk. Sometimes neighbouring residents and businesses joined to object to a workshop that they thought dangerous. | "neighbouring residents and businesses would occasionally unite to voice their concerns" | not given |
| 163 | The Fire Department inspected the workshops and gave advice. It had the power to close a workshop at a serious risk to life. | "they also had the power to close a workshop down if they thought there was a serious risk to life" | not given |
| 164 | Only two of the City's "350 or so" buildings had lifts. | "With lifts in just two of the City's 350 or so buildings" | not given |
| 164 | Thus people reached the upper floors of the 10- to 14-storey blocks by stairs. People near the top had long climbs. | "access to the upper floors of the 10- to 14-storey apartment blocks was nearly always by stairs" | not given |
| 164 | A system of linked stairways, bridges and corridors at different levels partly eased the climbs. It grew in the building boom of the 1960s and early 1970s. | "which took shape – somewhat organically – during the construction boom of the 1960s and early '70s" | not given |
| 164 | It was possible to cross the City from north to south at about the third or fourth storey, and never come down to street level. | "travel across the City from north to south – at about the third or fourth storey – without once coming down to street level" | not given |
| 164 | On such a route, one staircase can link two, three or even four buildings. | "a single staircase might link two, three or even four buildings" | not given |
| 164 | Even the most experienced residents rarely mastered the network. They kept to routes that they knew. Hand-painted notices gave directions to businesses on the way. | "with hand-painted notices offering directions to various businesses along the way" | own photos (pp164-166) |
| 167 | At ground level, large and small alleys ran between the separate buildings. Most buildings had their own entrance. | "most of which had their own entrances" | own photos |
| 167 | Higher up, the lines between buildings dissolved. Simple bridges linked one building to the next. | "Simple bridges linked one building to its neighbour" | own photos |
| 167 | Developers made more space to rent. Where possible, they put corridors and stairways into the gap between their building and the next. | "maximise rentable space" | not given |
| 167 | Where such a stairway already stood, the developer next door built with no stairs at all. | "a neighbouring developer could construct their building without any stairs at all" | not given |
| 167 | In many parts, above the first or second floor, the gaps between buildings were gone. Only the old, now useless windows showed where the gaps had been. | "the only sign that they had once been there the now redundant windows" | own photos |
| 169 | Cheng San (鄭山) set up his small factory on Tai Chang Street (大井街) in 1968. He made wooden rulers there for 22 years. | "He made wooden rulers there for the next 22 years" | not given (the printed interview says "20 years", but gives no year or street) |
| 169 | He moved out in 1990, during the first phase of the clearance. The photo on p168 shows his lit workshop, with stacks of wood, open to a dark alley. | "moving out in 1990 during the first phase of the clearance" | not given; own photo |
| 170 | The noodle factory of Hui Tung Choy (許東財) was tiny, barely 200 sq ft. | "his tiny noodle-making factory, barely 200 square feet in area" | own visit |
| 170 | It had no windows. The only air came through the narrow door onto Kwong Ming Street (光明街). | "Without windows, the only ventilation came from the narrow doorway where it opened on to Kwong Ming Street" | own visit |
| 170 | Kwong Ming Street was one of the larger alleys, but it also had little fresh air. | "one of the City's larger alleys but itself lacking in an overabundance of fresh air" | not given |
| 170 | As in most City factories, summer working conditions were often gruelling. | "like most factories in the City, working conditions during the summer months could be gruelling" | not given |
| 170 | The family owned a flat on a higher floor of the same building. His two daughters often joined him and his wife in the factory after school. | "his two daughters would often join Hui and his wife in the factory after school" | own visit |
| 172 | Hui Tung Choy opened his noodle factory at the back of 2 Kwong Ming Street in 1965. | "opened his noodle factory at the back of 2 Kwong Ming Street in 1965" | not given (the printed interview says "the past 25 years", but gives no year or address) |
| 172 | In 1992 he was married, with two young daughters. INFERRED: the interview dates from about 1992. | "In 1992, he was married with two young daughters." | not given |
| 173 | By day the factory was a workplace. At night it became the family's living room, when his wife and two daughters joined him. His working hours often ran late into the evening. | "A workplace during the day became a living room at night" | own visit |
| 173 | The children played and did homework on the flour-encrusted work bench. By day he made and packed his noodles on it. The photo shows two girls on the bench. | "The children's play and homework space was the flour-encrusted work bench" | own photo |
| 175 | Long routes through the City on the upper storeys were rare. They were possible if you knew which staircase to take, and on which floor a new corridor started. | "Extended routes through the City on the upper storeys were rare" | not given |
| 175 | Short corridors and bridges often linked clusters of next-door buildings. They twisted and turned to use the space between the buildings. | "clusters of adjacent buildings were often linked by short corridors and bridges, often twisting and turning" | own photos |
| 175 | Where there was a bit more space, residents opened small convenience stores on different floors. Thus people on the upper floors bought goods without the trek down to street level. | "enterprising residents opened small convenience stores at different floor levels" | not given |
| 175 | These stores were also meeting places. People stopped to chat, or rested on the climb to their 12th-floor flat. | "take a break on the climb up to their 12th floor apartment" | not given |
| 175 | Signs painted on the walls and beams gave directions to other buildings and nearby businesses. | "Signs painted directly on to the walls and beams offered directions to other buildings and nearby businesses." | own photos |
| 176 | The City had no zoning. Factories and family flats stood side by side everywhere. Both spilled out into the shared alleys. | "side by side throughout the City" | own photos |
| 176 | In one corridor, people packed the goods of a plastics factory for delivery. In another corridor, a family's washing hung to dry. The photo shows two people among cardboard boxes in a corridor under rows of electricity meters. | "the products of a local plastics factory being packaged for delivery" | own photo |
| 176 | In the same photo, some of the cardboard boxes are printed fruit cartons. One carton reads "MANSON APPLES". | none (photo) | own photo |
| 176 | The editors say that the corridors held surprises at every turn. A long corridor always tempted the adventurous. | "always the lure of a long corridor leading who knows where" | not given |
| 176 | A lost person usually found a stairway nearby that led down to a better-known alley at ground level. | "there was always a stairway nearby, leading down, with luck, to a more familar alley at ground level" | not given |
| 176 | A sign painted on the corridor wall (photo p177) offered extra tuition and homework help for schoolchildren. The red characters in the photo read 補習社. | "offers extra tuition for schoolchildren and help with their homework" | own photo |
| 178 | Chan Hip Ping (陳協平) no longer lived in the City. He still spoke for the community as Vice-President of the Kai Fong Association, a post he held from 1966. | "Though no longer a resident of the Walled City"; "a position he had held since 1966" | not given (the printed interview says neither) |
| 180 | The Kai Fong offices were on the third floor of 16 Tung Tau Tsuen Road. The photo (p181) looks down a stairwell towards them. | "Looking down into the Kai Fong's offices on the third floor of 16 Tung Tau Tsuen Road." | own photo |
| 180 | The offices were open every weekday, in normal working hours. Two full-time secretaries helped residents with their problems. | "These were open every weekday, during normal working hours, with two full-time secretaries" | not given |
| 180 | A large room off the office held many official functions, among them an annual Christmas party for elderly residents. | "including an annual Christmas party for elderly residents" | not given |
| 183 | Baker Law Kim Kwong (羅儉光) worked through the night to finish steamed sweet cakes for a Chiu Chow (潮州) festival. The photos (pp182-183) show rows of cakes in small bowls, tall steamers, sacks of flour, a lit stove and a cat. | "had worked overnight to finish these steamed sweet cakes for a Chiu Chow festival" | own photos |
| 182 | In the p182 photo of the bakery, clothes hang on hangers at the back of the room. The left photo on p183 also shows a hanging shirt. | none (photo) | own photos |
| 183 | He had lived in the City for 20 years. He came from the Chiu Chow area of eastern Guangdong. | "Resident in the City for 20 years" | not given |
| 183 | The Chiu Chow people had their own dialect, different from Cantonese, and their own culture. | "Different in dialect from the Cantonese, and with their own distinct culture" | not given |
| 183 | Chiu Chows made up more than 70 per cent of the City's people. | "Chiu Chows comprised more than 70 per cent of the City's population" | not given |
| 183 | Chiu Chows led the social and economic order of the City: in the Kai Fong, as developers, as water suppliers, and in the Triads. | "in the Kai Fong, as developers or water suppliers, or in the Triads" | not given |
| 183 | Chiu Chow settlers squatted in and around the City in the late 1940s. Their refugee kin came in the early 1950s, to a place with a familiar language and culture. | "Chiu Chow settlers squatted in and around the Walled City in the late 1940s" | not given |
| 184 | Tsin Mu Lam opened his clinic on the second floor of 10 Tung Tau Tsuen Road in 1973. | "opened his clinic on the second floor of 10 Tung Tau Tsuen Road in 1973" | the interview (the year); not given (the address) |
| 184 | He worked and lived there with his wife and son. The clinic closed in 1992, during the third phase of the clearance. | "worked and lived there with his wife and son until it was closed in 1992, during the third phase of the clearance" | not given |
| 184 | The p184 photo shows Tsin as he crouches beside a seated patient. The patient's shirt is pulled up at the lower back. A calendar hangs on the door. The caption does not describe this scene. | none (photo) | own photo |
| 185 | The photo shows an acupuncture chart and an X-ray in his clinic. Like all China-trained doctors, he used both traditional Chinese and Western medicine. | "like all China-trained doctors, Tsin Mu Lam combined traditional Chinese and Western medical practices" | own photo |
| 185 | A pink-framed photo above the X-ray shows Tsin as he shakes hands with the then Governor, Sir David Wilson. | "Tsin shakes hands with then-Governor, Sir David Wilson" | own photo |
| 185 | The same photo shows a blood-pressure gauge, flowers in a vase, an electric fan and piles of newspapers on the desk. | none (photo) | own photo |
| 185 | That photo marks his appointment to a Government sub-committee. The sub-committee looked at the prospects of the City's doctors and dentists after the clearance. | "the Government sub-committee tasked with examining prospects for the City's doctors and dentists after the clearance" | not given |
| 187 | Many City doctors and dentists displayed medical certificates, invoices and letters of recommendation. These papers gave an air of 'professionalism'. The photo (p186) shows such papers in a frame. | "Medical certificates, invoices and letters of recommendation were all enlisted in service of the 'professionalism'" | own photo |
| 187 | Their patients were mostly working class. The papers were to reassure them. | "to reassure their mostly working-class clientele" | not given |
| 188 | Postboxes were usually at street level, in the main entrances of the high-rise buildings. | "Postboxes were usually found at street level in the main entrances of the high-rise buildings" | own photos |
| 188 | Some postboxes stood in ordered ranks (photo p188). At 94 Lung Chun Road (photo p189), single boxes hung on the wall at random. The photo shows 九十四號 ("No. 94") painted large on that wall. | "as at 94 Lung Chun Road (opposite) – as individual boxes tacked haphazardly to the wall" | own photos |
| 188 | The set of boxes was incomplete and out of sequence. Thus postmen often jammed mail into any free crevice. The editors call this one of the many problems of the City's postmen. | "mail often had to be jammed into any available crevice" | own photos |
| 188 | Small white posters on the same wall advertised a removal company. It charged $100 to move the contents of a small room, $200 for a small family and $300 for a large family. | "charging $100 to move the contents of a small room, $200 for a small family and $300 for a large one" | own photo (the posters) |
| 191 | The editors print a passage from Jan Morris (1990) beside a photo of a dark, wet alley. See "Quoted records". | "Jan Morris, Hong Kong: Epilogue to an Empire, Penguin, 1990" | Jan Morris (1990) |

### Part 13. Standfirsts, captions and glosses on the interview pages, part 3 (pp226–239, 242–245, 250–261, 286–309, 318–321)

Writer: the editors (1993 or 2014); quotations of Lui Tai Lok and of Martin Booth (2004). Checked on 2026-11-16: 136 rows checked, 19 fixed, 0 deleted, 6 added. 139 rows.

| Page | Detail | Words in the book | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 226 | Caption. The photo shows Chan Wai Shui (陳偉瑞) in the centre, with his co-workers Mr Shum on the left and Kwan Tsin On on the right. | "Chan Wai Shui (centre) with his co-workers, Mr Shum (left) and Kwan Tsin On (right)." | photo |
| 226 | Standfirst. Chan Wai Shui came from China in 1947, at age 18 (INFERRED: thus born about 1929). | "An immigrant from China in 1947, when he was 18 years old" | not given (interview) |
| 226 | Standfirst. He ran his own noodle factory on Lo Yan Street (老人街) for more than 10 years. | "operated his own noodle factory on Lo Yan Street for more than 10 years" | not given (interview) |
| 228 | Caption. Mr Shum wears a mask because flour fills the air of the factory. | "Masked against the flour that filled the air" | photo |
| 228 | Caption. Mr Shum rolls up the fresh pastry sheet. The workers cut it into small squares, the wrappers for won tun. The caption then quotes Mr Shum (primary, not recorded). | "ready for cutting into the small squares that will become the outer wrapping for won tun" | photo; interview |
| 228 | Caption gloss. Won tun is a meat dumpling for soup. | "the popular meat dumpling put in soups" | not given |
| 229 | Caption. Flour covers the whole factory and the desk that is Chan Wai Shui's office. | "a liberal dusting of flour coated the desk that served as Chan Wai Shui's office" | photo |
| 229 | Caption. Health and hygiene officers did visit the City's factories, but they only gave advice. | "health and hygiene officers did visit the City's factories, but could do little more than offer advice" | not given |
| 230-231 | Caption. Small family-run factories, for noodles (photo p230) or plastic parts (photo p231), were all over the City. | "Small family-run factories" | photos |
| 231 | Caption. These factories were usually, but not always, on the lower floors. | "usually" ... "on the lower floors" | not given |
| 231 | Caption. Conditions varied: some factories were well kept; others were a health hazard, most of all with food or animal matter. | "others clearly a health hazard, especially if food or animal matter was involved" | not given |
| 231 | Caption. The worst factories drew rats: large sewer rats at street level, smaller and more nimble rats on the upper floors. | "the large sewer variety at street level, smaller more nimble species on the upper floors" | not given |
| 231 | Caption. During the clearance, the Urban Services Department (市政總署) baited the rats with success. It thus stopped a feared mass exodus of rats at the final demolition. | "a successful rat-baiting campaign" | not given |
| 233 | Standfirst. Wong Hoi Ming (王海明) was born in 1920 in the Chiu Chow (潮州) region of Guangdong. The page spells the province "Guandong". | "Born in the Chiu Chow region of Guandong Province in 1920" | not given (interview) |
| 233 | Standfirst. He spent much of his youth in Thailand. In 1938 he was expelled back to China because he campaigned against the Japanese occupation of Manchuria. | "expelled back to China in 1938, for campaigning against the Japanese occupation of Manchuria" | not given (interview) |
| 233 | Standfirst. He left his wife and family in China to protect the family land. He fled to Hong Kong alone in 1949. | "Leaving his wife and family to protect the family land in China, he fled to Hong Kong alone in 1949." | not given (interview) |
| 234 | Caption. A photo of Sai Shing (West City) Road (西城路) in late afternoon sun. | "Late afternoon sunlight filters into Sai Shing (West City) Road." | photo |
| 234 | Quoted essay. Lui Tai Lok (呂大樂) gives the Chinese term sanbuguan, "triply neglected". Neither the colonial administration nor the PRC or British Governments was able to intervene in the City or administer it "in a conspicuous way". | "sanbuguan, literally means 'triply neglected'" | Lui Tai Lok, "A Triply Neglected City" (this book, 2014) |
| 234 | Quoted essay. Lui calls the City "a zone of limbo amid a mass of people". He says that, because of its history, the City "inspired limitless flights of fancy". | "a zone of limbo amid a mass of people" | Lui Tai Lok essay (2014) |
| 234 | Quoted essay. Many people saw the City as an "institutional vacuum" where anything can happen; thus the idea of a den of vice became persuasive. | "a seedy, anarchic den ridden with huangdudu vice" | Lui Tai Lok essay (2014) |
| 236 | Caption. The photo shows a factory on Lo Yan Street. The caption calls Lo Yan Street the main north-south street of the City. | "the City's main north-south artery" | photo |
| 236 | Caption. To outsiders the City stayed forbidding, but life inside was like life in other working-class parts of Hong Kong. | "impenetrable and forbidding place" | not given |
| 236 | Caption. When the clearance was announced in 1987, many residents thought the City safer than other Kowloon neighbourhoods. | "the City was considered safer" | not given (the residents) |
| 237 | Standfirst. To Gui Bon (杜古邦) made rubber plungers and other goods for 25 years in a small factory on Lung Chun Back Road (龍津後街). | "For 25 years, To Gui Bon (below right) produced rubber plungers and other products" | not given (interview) |
| 237 | Standfirst. He was born in 1937. He was in the same trade before he moved to the City. | "Born in 1937" | not given (interview) |
| 238 | Caption. His part-time assistant works a heated press. The press stamps out each plunger from a square of uncured rubber. | "the heated press that stamps out the individual plungers from squares of uncured rubber" | photo |
| 238 | Caption. The workers cut the squares with scissors from flat rubber sheets on the next table. | "cut with scissors from the flat sheets on the adjacent table" | photo |
| 238 | Caption. In summer, only an old electric fan moves the air. The heat and the smell of hot rubber can overpower a person. | "with only an old electric fan to circulate the air" | photo; own visit (INFERRED: the page does not say) |
| 238 | Caption. An unused staircase at the back of the factory, closed at ceiling level long ago, gives extra storage. | "A redundant staircase at the back of the factory, long since closed off at ceiling level, provides extra storage." | photo |
| 242 | Standfirst. Cheng Koon Yiu (鄭冠耀) came to Hong Kong with his family in 1948, at age five (INFERRED: thus born about 1943). | "came to Hong Kong with his family in 1948, when he was five years old" | not given (interview) |
| 242 | Standfirst. His father ran a small business. The family lived near the Walled City. | "His father ran a small business and they lived near the Walled City." | not given (interview) |
| 242 | Caption. The photo shows a clinic on Lung Chun Road (龍津道) that is unusually clean. It is not Cheng Koon Yiu's clinic. | "An unusually clean clinic (not Cheng Koon Yiu's) on Lung Chun Road" | photo |
| 242 | Caption. Dentists and doctors put their clinics on Lung Chun Road and Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道). Thus customers avoided the trouble of a walk into the City proper. | "so that customers could avoid the inconvenience of having to enter the City proper" | not given |
| 244 | Caption. Dentists usually made false teeth to measure. Poorer patients sometimes bought a used set that fitted well enough. | "it was not unusual for poorer patients to buy a used set if they could find a good enough fit" | not given |
| 244 | Caption. Hygiene was basic. Dentists often used equipment again without proper sterilisation. | "equipment was often reused without proper sterilisation" | not given |
| 244 | Caption. Medical care was also lax: practitioners gave vitamin tablets and steroids for many symptoms. | "vitamin tablets and steroids dispensed as a general panacea" | not given |
| 244 | Caption. The steroids worried the medical authorities outside the City. Steroids reduced inflammation and pain but did not treat the disease. The caption says that this can lead to severe complications later. | "of serious concern to the medical authorities outside the City" | not given |
| 251 | Caption. An ordinary patrol of two policemen, in light summer uniforms, stops at one of the City's factories. They make a call back to the police station. | "An ordinary two-man police patrol, in their lightweight summer uniforms" | photo |
| 251 | Caption. Deep in the City, police radios worked only over a short range. Thus large raids were hard to co-ordinate. | "their radios would only work over a short range" | not given |
| 251 | Caption. By the time of the clearance announcement, raids on heroin dens and the like had mostly stopped. | "were largely a thing of the past" | not given |
| 251 | Caption. Most police officers said that by the mid-1980s crime in the City was like crime in other areas of Hong Kong. | "by the mid-1980s, crime in the City was little different to other areas of Hong Kong" | police officers (not named) |
| 251 | Standfirst. The speaker is a young police officer. He asked to stay anonymous because he spoke without permission from his department. | "as he spoke to us without department authorisation" | own interview |
| 251 | Standfirst. The photographers made the police photos (p250 and the pages after it) when they met police officers by chance in the City. | "police officers were encountered by chance inside the City" | own visits |
| 252 | Caption. The photo shows members of a police task force on regular patrol. | "Members of a police task force on regular patrol." | photo |
| 252 | Caption. In the City's last decade, serious crime was lower than in many other parts of Hong Kong. | "serious crime was lower than in many other parts of Hong Kong" | not given |
| 252 | Caption. The most common offences were burglary and possession of drugs. | "the most common offences were burglary and the possession of drugs" | not given |
| 252 | Caption. Against popular belief, police patrolled the City almost from the start. | "police patrolled the City almost from the very beginning" | not given |
| 252 | Caption. The City stopped being a "den of iniquity" by the early 1960s. Then the big Triad and criminal operations moved to more profitable areas of Kowloon and Hong Kong Island. | "it had ceased to be the 'den of iniquity' that everyone believed it to be by the early 1960s" | not given |
| 253 | Editorial gloss in the interview: ICAC is the Independent Commission Against Corruption. | "ICAC [Independent Commission Against Corruption]" | editors |
| 254 | Commentary box. Hong Kong set up the ICAC in 1974 because of widespread corruption. | "In 1974, in response to pervasive corruption in Hong Kong, the Independent Commission Against Corruption (ICAC) was established." | not given |
| 254 | Commentary box. The ICAC made Triad work harder in the colony. Thus, for a time, Triad activity in the Walled City grew again. | "for a time their activities in the Walled City grew once again" | not given |
| 254 | Commentary box. The unclear legal status of the City gave some protection, but not from the most serious crimes. | "the uncertain legal status of the Walled City offered some protection, though not from the most serious crimes" | not given |
| 254 | Commentary box. Crime paid more elsewhere in Hong Kong. Thus the City slowly became a backwater for most illegal activities. | "the Walled City gradually became something of a backwater" | not given |
| 256 | Caption. The City had about 700 industrial premises. Metal-working factories were a large share of them. | "the City's 700 or so industrial premises" | not given |
| 256 | Caption. Many industrial premises were between the ground floor and the fifth floor. A few were higher, as access allowed. | "between the ground and fifth floors, though with a few even higher depending on access requirements" | not given |
| 256 | Caption. Machine repairer Yu Kwok Fai kept a bird in his small workshop. Many workers did the same. | "one of the many workers who kept a bird in his small workshop" | photo; own visit (INFERRED: the page does not say) |
| 257 | Caption. Metal workshops with large machines were almost always on the ground floor: access was easy, and the noise and vibration disturbed the fewest people. | "almost invariably located on the ground floor" | not given |
| 257 | Caption. Smaller workshops were almost anywhere, often next door to flats and quieter businesses. | "as often as not next door to residential apartments" | not given |
| 257 | Caption. Life in the City needed tolerance and compromise. | "A high degree of tolerance and an ability to compromise" | not given |
| 258 | Standfirst. Wong Wai Chung had a love-hate relationship with the City. In his late teens he thought it as beautiful as Lan Kwai Fong. | "thought it as beautiful as Lan Kwai Fong" | interview |
| 258 | Standfirst. He liked the bustle. He spent his time with his lifelong friends Wilson and Ah Xian. | "his lifelong companions Wilson and Ah Xian" | interview |
| 258 | Standfirst. All three left school early and were poor. Their fathers were gamblers or drug addicts. | "their fathers were gamblers or drug addicts" | interview |
| 258 | Editorial gloss: "white powder" means pure heroin. | "[pure heroin]" | editors |
| 258 | Gloss in the interview: the page prints the Chinese name and the pinyin of the gang League of Heroes, 聯英社 (lianyingshe). Wong names the gang (interview, not recorded here). | "League of Heroes (聯英社, lianyingshe)" | editors or translator (INFERRED: the page does not mark it) |
| 258 | Editorial gloss on Wong's phrase "crossing the Chu River" (his words for leaving the City): the Chu River was a historical border of the Han lands. | "[The Chu River was a historical border of the Han lands.]" | editors |
| 259 | Editorial gloss: Shuiyuan, the name of Jackie Pullinger's (潘靈卓) youth centre, means "water origin". The move of the centre to Tai Chang Street (大井街) and the new name are Wong's words (interview, not recorded here). | "Shuiyuan ['water origin']" | editors |
| 259 | Caption. In the 1960s, heroin addicts gathered on Kwong Ming Street (光明街), on the east side of the City. | "During the 1960s, heroin addicts invariably congregated on Kwong Ming Street" | not given |
| 259 | Caption. People then called it "Electric Station" Street, after the local word for the wooden stalls where addicts inhaled the smoke. | "better known as 'Electric Station' Street" | not given |
| 259 | Caption. The younger addicts of the late 1980s injected the drug. | "preferred to inject the drug" | not given |
| 260 | Caption box. Some heroin addicts stayed in the City until the end. By the 1980s dangerous drugs were no more common there than in similar parts of Hong Kong. | "A few heroin addicts remained in the City until the end" | not given |
| 260 | Caption box. The era of drugs, prostitution and gambling was mainly the 1950s and early 1960s. | "The era of drugs, prostitution and gambling" | not given |
| 260 | Caption box. Its last line, cut at the binding, says that the residents of that era lived among extreme depravity. | "residents undeniably lived among the most extraordinary" | not given |
| 286 | Caption. The photo shows pig carcasses made ready for barbecue. Several such businesses were in the City. | "There were a number of similar businesses in the City" | photo |
| 286 | Caption. The animals were slaughtered outside the City and brought in for processing. | "with animals slaughtered outside being brought inside for processing" | not given |
| 286 | Caption. Yim Kwok Yuen (嚴國源) did not mind the dirty conditions, because his home village was worse (his own words follow, primary). | "Yim dismissed them" | interview |
| 286 | Standfirst. Yim opened his cooked-meat factory on Lo Yan Street in 1981, a few years after he came to Hong Kong. | "opened his cooked meat factory on Lo Yan Street in 1981" | not given (interview) |
| 287 | Caption. Huge cylindrical ovens were the standard for the commercial roasting of chickens, geese and pigs. | "Huge cylindrical ovens were standard for the commercial roasting of chicken, geese and pigs." | photo |
| 287 | Caption. The ovens raised the summer heat by several degrees. Their exhaust covered the walls and surfaces with grease and grime. | "a permanent layer of grease and grime" | photo; own visit (INFERRED: the page does not say) |
| 289 | Caption. The City made many cooked foods in factories that had no health inspections, and sold them to restaurants outside, which did have inspections. | "in factories where there were no health inspections, but sold to restaurants on the outside where there were" | not given |
| 290 | Caption. Employee Kwok Tsan Ming ladles fried fishballs into a basket in a small factory off Kwong Ming Street. | "Employee Kwok Tsan Ming (below) ladles a batch of fried fishballs into a basket" | photo |
| 290 | Caption. Most of the City's fishball makers were off Kwong Ming Street. | "where most of the City's fishball operators were located" | not given |
| 290 | Caption. People ate fishballs with soup or noodles, or from wooden skewers at hawker stalls. | "chewed from wooden skewers at hawker stalls" | not given |
| 290 | Caption. People said that more than 80 per cent of Hong Kong's fishballs came from the City. | "it was said that more than 80 per cent of the territory's fishballs originated in the Walled City" | hearsay ("it was said") |
| 291 | Caption. Conditions in the food factories shocked first-time visitors. | "likely to shock first-time visitors" | own visits (INFERRED: the page does not say) |
| 291 | Caption. Male workers took off their clothes as they wished and smoked at work. They used no hairnets, gloves or other safety equipment. | "Male workers often stripped themselves as they saw fit, smoked on the job" | photos |
| 291 | Caption. The conditions rarely kept customers away. | "the conditions rarely seemed to deter customers" | not given |
| 291 | Caption. The top-left photo shows the brother of Ng Kam Mui (吳錦妹) at work in his bakery behind the Chung Fat café (松發冰室). | "Ng Kam Mui's brother at work in his bakery behind the Chung Fat café (see page 56)" | photo |
| 292 | Standfirst. Lam Leung Po (林良寶) became a partner in the minced-fish factory in 1983. | "became a partner in his minced-fish factory in 1983" | not given (interview) |
| 292 | Standfirst. The factory was in an alley off Tai Chang Street. | "Located in an alley off Tai Chang Street" | not given |
| 292 | Standfirst. His factory was one of many food-processing factories in an area conveniently close to the City's only Government stand-pipe. The page does not say that the stand-pipe was the reason. | "the one Government stand-pipe to be found in the City" | not given |
| 293 | Caption. Female part-time workers make minced-fish dumplings. They wear warm clothes against the winter cold and the air-conditioning of the workshop. | "Bundled up against the chill of a Hong Kong winter and the workshop's air-conditioning" | photo |
| 293 | Caption. They work at a low table, "presumably" to reach the materials stacked on the floor. | "presumably for easy access to stacked materials on the floor" | photo (editors' guess) |
| 293 | Caption. This factory was one of the cleaner and better-equipped food factories. It did a brisk trade with shops and restaurants near and far. | "one of City's cleaner and better equipped food processing establishments" | own visit (INFERRED: the page does not say) |
| 294 | Caption. A busy hardware store stood among low, temporary-looking structures on the unclear no-man's-land off Tung Tsing Road (東正道). | "the ambiguous no-man's-land off Tung Tsing Road" | photo |
| 294 | Caption. The authorities treated that area, in name, as inside the eastern boundary of the City. | "an area the authorities ostensibly treated as within the City's eastern boundary" | not given |
| 294 | Caption. The p295 photo shows a dai pai dong (streetside restaurant) further down Tung Tsing Road. It was typical of the many cafés and restaurants that the whole Kowloon City (九龍城) area liked. | "A dai pai dong (streetside restaurant) further down Tung Tsing Road" | photo |
| 294 | Caption. Taxi drivers were regular customers because they parked their cabs at the kerb. | "Taxi drivers, in particular, were regular patrons" | not given |
| 294 | Caption. This was in the days of the dog-meat restaurants. People said that luxury cars brought rich and famous people to Tung Tau Tsuen Road for illicit meals and perhaps more. | "luxury cars would discharge wealthy and well-known personalities on Tung Tau Tsuen Road for illicit meals" | hearsay ("it was said") |
| 296 | Standfirst. Lam Tseng Yat (林俊逸) was born in China in 1916. He came to Hong Kong 10 years later with his father and four brothers. | "Born in China in 1916" | not given (interview) |
| 296 | Standfirst. In 1947 the family bought a small property at 25 Tai Chang Street and opened their first store there. | "they bought a small property at 25 Tai Chang Street" | not given |
| 296 | Standfirst. The store became one of the largest grocery businesses in the City. | "one of the largest grocery businesses in the City" | not given (interview) |
| 296 | Glosses in parentheses inside the interview give the meanings of four street names: Tai Chang Street (大井街) "Big Well", Lo Yan Street (老人街) "Old People's", Kwong Ming Street (光明街) "Bright", Lung Shing Road (龍城路) "Dragon City". | "Tai Chang (Big Well) Street" | translator or editors (INFERRED: the page does not mark them) |
| 299 | Caption. The oldest part of the City was the yamen (衙門), the inner compound of the resident military official. It was built around 1845 because the British had come to Hong Kong Island. | "built around 1845 in response to the arrival of the British" | not given |
| 299 | Caption. The compound was much altered and rebuilt. Only the original entrance building (photo left) was kept after the clearance. | "only the original entrance building (left) was preserved after the clearance" | not given |
| 299 | Caption. The CNEC (中華傳道會) Living Word Church took over the whole compound in the early 1970s. | "taken over by the CNEC Living Word Church in the early 1970s" | not given |
| 299 | Caption. Reverend Isaac Liu (劉知三), the CNEC representative in the City, kept the compound from redevelopment. He believed that one part of the City must have fresh air and natural light. | "at least one part of the City should be able to enjoy fresh air and natural light" | not given |
| 299 | Caption. People threw refuse from the flats around the courtyard. A tree near the entrance, always hung with refuse, had the nickname "Christmas tree". | "the tree near the entrance was known somewhat mordantly as the 'Christmas tree'" | not given |
| 299 | Caption. On sunny days the courtyard was a popular meeting place. Residents brought chairs from their flats to sit and talk, prepare vegetables for the evening meal, or air their bedding. | "residents bringing chairs from their apartments to sit and chat" | own visits (INFERRED: the page does not say) |
| 300 | Standfirst. The Salvation Army (救世軍) was one of several missionary societies in the City. | "Just one of several missionary societies which used to operate in the City" | not given |
| 300 | Standfirst. It ran a well-attended kindergarten at its premises on Lung Chun Back Road for many years. | "a well-attended kindergarten at its premises on Lung Chun Back Road" | not given |
| 300 | Standfirst. Simon Wong (王國清), the Salvation Army officer responsible for the Walled City, gave the interview. | "an officer in the Salvation Army responsible for the Walled City" | own interview |
| 301 | Editorial gloss: the "big brothers" are senior Triad members. | "[senior Triad members]" | editors |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. Martin Booth walked down a hutong (alley) into the buildings and shacks. A man pushed a bicycle whose pannier carried cardboard boxes. | "a man pushing a bicycle, the pannier laden with cardboard boxes" | Martin Booth, *Gweilo* (2004); date of the walk not given |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. Through open doors he saw "industrial domesticity": a kang or metal-framed bed with folded bedding on one side, and people at a table on the other side. | "To one side would be a kang or metal-framed bed, piled with neatly folded bedding" | Booth (2004) |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. The people at the tables sewed, assembled torches, put coloured pencils in boxes or painted lacquer boxes. | "sewing, assembling torches, placing coloured pencils in boxes or painting lacquer boxes" | Booth (2004) |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. A baker put trays of buns in a wood-fired oven. | "a baker was placing trays of buns in a wood-fired oven" | Booth (2004) |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. Two men made noodles: they swung sheets of thin dough around a wooden rolling pin. Flour dust made their shack white. | "the interior of their shack ghost-white under a layer of flour dust" | Booth (2004) |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. The air smelled of wood smoke, joss-sticks, boiling rice and human excrement. | "the air was redolent with smells of wood smoke, joss-sticks, boiling rice and human excrement" | Booth (2004) |
| 303 | Quoted memoir. Sewage ran in open gullies beside the alleys into holes in the ground lined with stone slabs. | "flowed down open gullies at the side of the hutongs" | Booth (2004) |
| 304 | Standfirst. Isaac Liu became a minister in the CNEC Living Word Church in the late 1950s. He ran a school, a clinic and a small chapel. | "Running a school, a clinic and a small chapel" | not given (interview) |
| 304 | Standfirst. In 1971 he took over the old magistrate's buildings at the centre of the City. He kept them from redevelopment for 20 years, until the clearance forced him out in 1991. | "protecting them from redevelopment over the next 20 years until forced to vacate the premises as part of the clearance in 1991" | not given |
| 304 | Caption. He served the City's drug addicts, children and old people for more than 30 years. | "ministered to the City's drug addicts, children and elderly for more than 30 years" | not given |
| 306 | Caption. The photos (pp306-307) are portraits of regular visitors to Liu's Old People's Centre (老人中心). | "Portraits of some of the regular visitors to Reverend Isaac Liu's Old People's Centre" | photos |
| 306 | Caption. The Centre worked as an informal welfare service: if a member was absent for a day or more, friends and staff quietly checked on that member at home. | "anyone absent for a day or more would arouse concern among friends and staff" | not given |
| 306 | Caption. The Centre did not require Christian faith: it "was not a precondition of joining". Some were Christians, and not a few admitted that they hedged their bets. | "not a few admitted to hedging their bets" | members (not named) |
| 306 | Caption. The Centre gave secular classes, for example calligraphy for the many old women who had never learnt to write. | "calligraphy classes for the many elderly women who had never been taught to write" | not given |
| 306 | Caption. The Centre gave company and support, a daily afternoon tea, and television, a luxury beyond the means of many members. | "a daily afternoon tea, and television" | not given |
| 308 | Standfirst. Kwok Lau Hing (郭柳馨) never lived in the City itself. She was a regular visitor to the Old People's Centre. | "Though never a resident of the City itself" | not given |
| 308 | Standfirst. She kept coming even after she moved to distant Junk Bay. | "continuing to make the journey even after moving to distant Junk Bay" | not given |
| 308 | Standfirst. She was 73 at the interview in 1989 (INFERRED: thus born about 1916). | "At the time of the interview (in 1989), she was 73 years old." | own interview |
| 309 | Caption. The photo shows Kwok Lau Hing in the centre, at the Old People's Centre. | "Kwok Lau Hing (centre) at the Old People's Centre." | photo |
| 309 | Caption. Many of the regulars were old women. Many lived alone or in small shared flats. | "many of whom lived alone or in small shared apartments" | not given |
| 309 | Caption. Many had been amahs for middle-class Chinese families. Their small savings bought a cheap space in the City. | "Quite a number had worked as amahs in service to middle-class Chinese families" | not given |
| 309 | Caption. Other regulars were retired factory workers, and a few were housewives. | "Others were retired factory workers and there were a few housewives." | not given |
| 309 | Caption. The end of the City was "a bitter pill" for them. The clearance scattered them across the new housing estates. Some never got used to the loss of the City and of their friends. | "was a disruption some never got used to" | not given |
| 318 | Standfirst. Chan Kwan Leung (陳昆亮) moved into the City in 1981, when he worked for Hongkong Telecom. | "moved into the Walled City in 1981, while working for Hongkong Telecom" | not given (interview) |
| 318 | Standfirst. Soon after, he left his job and bred racing pigeons on the roof of his building. | "began breeding racing pigeons on the roof of his apartment building" | not given (interview) |
| 321 | Caption. In good weather he exercised the pigeons every day, usually in mid-afternoon, when fewer planes landed at Kai Tak Airport (啓德機場). | "usually in mid-afternoon when there was a lull in the number of landings at Kai Tak Airport" | not given |
| 321 | Caption. The authorities quietly tolerated the City's racing pigeons, although a law banned the breeding of animals in urban Hong Kong. | "a law prohibiting the breeding of animals within urban Hong Kong" | not given |

## Quoted records

The records, documents, signs and quotations that the secondary parts quote or show. They are
in page order; "Part" gives the part of "Facts and details". A quoted record is a lead: find the
record itself before you use it as a primary fact. The documents that the book reproduces in
full (for example the police memo of 1955, pp274–275, and the plans of p340) are primary: the
twin file gives them.

| Page | Record | What it says | Words in the book | Part |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| p10 | Royal Order in Council, Queen Victoria, 1899 (no date or reference here; Wilkinson, p71, gives "signed on 27 December 1899") | Britain claimed jurisdiction over the City. | "asserted jurisdiction over the Walled City by a Royal Order in Council" | 1 |
| p10 | Governor of Hong Kong, secret memo to the Secretary of State, London (no date, no reference) | For domestic purposes the British claim to the City was beyond challenge. | "for domestic purposes ... unchallengeable" | 1 |
| p10 | Attorney General (of Hong Kong), opinion, "five decades later" (about 1950: my inference), no reference | Abroad the claim was hard to support. A trial of a City offence in a Hong Kong court invited a challenge from Chinese Communist interests. | "would be hard to support ... Any open claim, eg, by trying an offence committed in the area in the Hong Kong courts" | 1 |
| p10 | Confidential memo, maker not named, "a few years after" the failed post-war attempt (no date, no reference) | Big improvements (the example is the electricity) implied that Britain accepted the situation for ever. | "to make substantial improvements" ; "that we accepted the present situation for ever" | 1 |
| p11 | *Hong Kong Standard*, report, no date (early 1960s) | Most residents, 20,000 to 30,000, agreed to resettlement on the Government's terms. | "The majority of the 20,000 to 30,000 residents are willing to be resettled on government's terms" | 1 |
| p11 | Hong Kong Government, eviction notices with deadlines, early 1960s (not quoted) | Notices to leave by fixed dates. McHugh (p332) gives deadlines of 8, 12, 14, 19 and 20 February. | "issuing time-bound eviction notices" | 1 |
| p11 | New China News Agency, Hong Kong, articles, no date (early 1960s) | The demolition plans broke China's sovereignty. McHugh (p332) quotes more of the same statement. | "a gross violation of China's sovereignty" | 1 |
| p12 | Hong Kong police, survey of 1952 (no reference) | 154 narcotic divans (opium dens), 11 brothels, 7 gambling houses, 13 dog-meat shops. The Commissioner of Police's memorandum, sent to the Secretary of State on 4 March 1955 (caption p274), gives the same four figures for "late 1952" (p275, para 9). Lambot's text (p273) prints 15 dog-meat shops (see Doubts). | "In 1952 a survey by Hong Kong police recorded that the City had 154 'narcotic divans' (opium dens)" | 1 |
| p12 | Hong Kong police report, about March 1953 (no reference) | Popham: 16 strip-tease theatres by March 1953, "each more daring than the other" and full. The memo on p275 is more exact. One theatre (the Lung Kung) was open in late 1952; three more opened by mid-February 1953 (para 10). After a fire of 24.2.53, the total was 16 on 17.3.53 (paras 11-12). By 19 March 1953 the theatres were gone, and the dog-meat stalls were closed too (para 14). Lambot (p273) says that a Savingram of 11 March 1955 from Governor Sir Robert Black (file CO 1030/934) quotes this report. | "each more daring than the other" ; "and all playing to capacity crowds" | 1 |
| p12 | Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) "Act", used in 1975 | The legal basis to order the top floors of new City blocks demolished. McHugh (p334) says closure orders under the "ordinances". | "citing the Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) Act" | 1 |
| p12 | Hong Kong Government, statement of 1973 (no reference) | The Resettlement Department did no clearance in the City. | "The Resettlement Department does not conduct clearance activities within the Walled City." | 1 |
| p16 | Peter Popham, Introduction to *City of Darkness* (1993) | His verdict on the City as an 'organic megastructure' that met every need. | "the city as 'organic megastructure', not set rigidly for a lifetime but continually responsive to the changing requirements of its users" | 1 |
