| p19 | Suenn Ho, Fulbright research study of the City (no title or date here; p16 puts the work of the two architects "in the months before it was torn down") | How the City grew: building on terraces, roofs and gaps without the neighbours' permission; the strength of communal living. | "Construction in the Walled City" ... "takes advantage of every opportunity" | 1 |
| p19 | Aaron Tan, writing on the City, before the demolition (no title or date) | The City as a 'rhizome': ill-defined structures gave the whole its stability. | "The built structures are rhizomatic in nature" | 1 |
| 27 | Hong Kong Government height limit for buildings near Kai Tak Airport; no date or reference given | Apart from a strict height limit near the airport, the Public Works Department had no dealings with the City. | "a strict height limit due to the proximity of Kai Tak Airport" | 11 |
| 31 | Hong Kong Government compensation formula for the clearance (1987-1992); no reference given | The formula used the cheap rents inside the City. Thus sole proprietors were unable to pay for premises outside. | "a formula based on the cheaper rents within the Walled City" | 11 |
| 62 | J.H. Stewart Lockhart, Colonial Secretary of Hong Kong, survey of the New Territories, 1898 (no reference) | The size and build of the wall; six towers, four gates, 119 embrasures. | "The wall is built of granite ashlar facing, is 15 feet [cut] width at the top and averages in height 13 feet." | 2 |
| 62 | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, article, 1904 | Old broken cannons lie all over the City. | "Kowloon might ... be styled the City of Cannons," | 2 |
| 65 | Ordinance of 1850 (Hong Kong; no number) | Hong Kong hands Chinese fugitives to the Kowloon officials. | "required that Chinese fugitives in Hong Kong were handed over to Kowloon officials" | 2 |
| 65 | The Walled City mandarin, warning to the British, 1884 | A Triad rising is possible. | "warned British authorities of a possible Triad uprising" | 2 |
| 65 | Reverend Krone, writing about San On district, 1858 | Little to say about the fort. | "low walls and miserable forts of Kowloong [sic]" | 2 |
| 65 | *Hongkong Guide*, 1893 | A picnic walk ends at the dirty little City; the walk round the walls is five minutes; gambling is the chief trade. | "The chief trade of the place is from the several gambling houses erected near the beach there." | 2 |
| 65 | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, article, 1904 | Good houses and wide streets inside the walls. | "of a very superior class"; "wide and commodious" | 2 |
| 65 | *The Hongkong Telegraph*, report, 1890 | The suburb is a mass of poor huts. | "wretched agglomeration of Chinese hovels" | 2 |
| 65 | "the Hong Kong press" (no title, no date) | The suburb is a centre of vice and of British gambling. | "favourite [gambling] rendezvous of a considerable number of the British and foreign community" | 2 |
| 65 | Hong Kong General Chamber of Commerce, 1898 | The place adds to crime in Hong Kong; take it into the Colony. | "It is not too much to say that this place, with its encouragements to vice and dishonesty, adds enormously to the task of preserving order" | 2 |
| 65 | Sir Claude MacDonald, British Minister in Peking, report to the Foreign Office, early 1898 | The officials do not want to leave Kowloon City. | "Great difficulty is Kowloon City" | 2 |
| 66 | Arthur Balfour, Foreign Office, telegram to MacDonald, 1898 | Offers money, or Chinese officials under British authority. | "If money compensation is required we could offer reasonable terms" | 2 |
| 66 | MacDonald, reply, 1898 | The issue is face. | "saving 'face', a very important consideration with Chinese" | 2 |
| 66 | MacDonald, summary after the final draft of the New Territories lease, 1898 | China fought hardest for jurisdiction in the City; British rule will come soon. | "no harm can result from allowing it do to so for a few years longer" | 2 |
| 66-68 | Convention for the Extension of Hong Kong, signed at Peking, 9 June 1898; in force 1 July 1898 | Chinese officials keep jurisdiction in the City, except against the defence of Hong Kong; the landing-place stays for Chinese ships. | "within the city of Kowloon, the Chinese officials now stationed there shall continue to exercise jurisdiction" | 2 |
| 68 | Hong Kong General Chamber of Commerce, after the Convention, 1898 | Chinese rule keeps a moral danger and hurts British prestige. | "would assuredly have a damaging effect on British prestige in South China" | 2 |
| 68 | "A British journal" (no title, no date) | The officials must leave for order and sanitation. | "We will never be able to maintain good order nor enforce sanitary measures as long as Chinese officials are allowed to remain in Kowloon." | 2 |
| 69 | Governor Sir Henry Blake, request to the Viceroy of Canton, 1899 | Asks for protection against the resistance. | "ample protection" | 2 |
| 69 | *The Daily Press*, 1899 (the book gives no month; the text is about the flag-raising of 16 April) | British residents missed the ceremony. | "a pleasant little outing and an opportunity for indulging in an outburst of patriotic fervour" | 2 |
| 70 | The Governor, request of 1899, after the Viceroy sent 600 more troops (the book does not name the addressee) | All Chinese troops and officials must leave the leased land. | "removal of all Chinese troops and officials from leased territory, except Customs officials" | 2 |
| 70 | Colonial Office proposal; British Government decision of 14 May 1899 | Blockade the garrison; then seize the City. | "proposing that the garrison should be blockaded and starved out" | 2 |
| 71 | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, 1899, after the occupation of May 1899 (the book gives no date) | The troops enjoyed the occupation. | "The trip", "was immensely enjoyed by all who took part in it." | 2 |
| 71 | Lord Salisbury, British Prime Minister, to the Chinese Minister in London, 1899 | Chinese authority will not come back to the City. | "it is impossible for Her Majesty's Government to allow the resumption of Chinese authority within the walls of that City" | 2 |
| 71-72 | Order in Council, 27 December 1899 | The City becomes part of the Colony. | "part and parcel of Her Majesty's Colony of Hong Kong" | 2 |
| 72 | *Hongkong Weekly Press*, article, 1904 | The City is empty. | "nothing but desolation" | 2 |
| 72 | Hong Kong Government, plan announced in 1933 | Pull down the houses, pay 436 squatter-residents, make a park. | "a place of popular resort and antiquarian interest" | 2 |
| 72 | George Kennedy-Skipton, District Officer South, memory recorded in 1970 | The compensation made people move, against China's minister. | "despite resistance from the Nationalist Chinese Minister for Foreign Affairs who made a trip from Canton to dissuade them from moving" | 2 |
| 79 | Hong Kong Government ("the authorities"), statement on business compensation; no date or reference given | A business got compensation in proportion to its profit, if it had records to prove the profit. | "compensate businesses in proportion to their profitability as long as verifiable records could be produced" | 11 |
| 89 | China Light & Power Company, Limited, an electricity bill in its envelope, photographed in a noodle maker's premises (calendar: Saturday 27, 十月) | The caption cites the bill as proof that CLP billed the business. The envelope is marked "PRINTED PAPERS" and addressed to Flat 2, G/F, 31 Lo Yan Street, Sai Tau Village. | "an electricity bill from China Light and Power provides proof" | 11 |
| 107 | Government statement, 1963, on the first stand-pipe (no reference given) | The first stand-pipe gave the City free, unrestricted water. | "access to a free, unrestricted water supply" | 10 |
| 107 | Representatives of the Kai Fong Association, call on the Secretariat for Chinese Affairs, 1964 (no reference given) | They complained of an acute water shortage, and pointed to China's East River scheme for Hong Kong. | "the Chinese Government is working selflessly on the East River scheme to provide ample water for Hong Kong residents" | 10 |
| 107 | Government surveyors, assessment of compensation claims, 1987 (no reference given) | 67 working wells, owned by about 40 suppliers. | "identified 67 working wells owned by some 40 suppliers" | 10 |
| 107 | Unnamed "reports" (no date) | Many people did not have the money for the steep connection fee for well water. | "reports of numerous people being unable to afford the steep connection fees" | 10 |
| 108 | The Government's stated reasons for refusing household mains water (no date, no reference) | The buildings were too dense and had no provision for water or waste; the streets were too narrow for mains. | "the high concentration of buildings, none of which had adequate provision for water or waste" | 10 |
| 110 | Words of a Triad leader on a water price rise, 1980 (source not given) | A threat against any person who led opposition to the rise. | "Whoever dares to take the lead in opposing the hike" ... "we'll chop him up in front of the crowd." | 10 |
| 116 | Sale contract, handwritten in Chinese, 14 December 1960. Credit (p356, "116(br)"): Kowloon Walled City Residents' Association. The book reproduces the full sheet. No reference number. | Caption: Wong Shek Yin sold two buildings to Law Wun Cheung for $2200. The repo's reading (fact C51): two stone houses that the seller built, front and back, at No. 11 Lo Yan Street (老人街). The next words of the contract add "及龍津路" and a house number that ends "拾五號": thus the address also names a number on Lung Chun Road (龍津道; the contract writes 路). My reading of the handwriting adds these points. The seller sells because of an urgent need for money. A friend introduced the buyer. The sale covers the house from the ground floor to the roof. The contract gives the same price, 2200 Hong Kong dollars. The money and the deed changed hands on the same day. The seller answers for any doubt about the origin of the house. If an accident occurs, or if the government pulls the house down, each party accepts its fate. At the end, the contract names the seller, a middleman (中人) and a witness. An X mark stands near the middleman's line. The date at the left edge is in Chinese numerals: 1960, month 12, day 14. | "for an agreed price of $2200" (caption); "自建石屋前後兩間坐落土名九龍城內老人街門牌拾壹號" (contract, as fact C51 reads it) | 3 |
| 116, 120, 121 | Mak Fung, three photos, 1950 (reproduced): waste land with boys at play; a ground well in old Kowloon City; a Government stand-pipe. | See the Details rows for each photo. | "From 1950 onwards, when this photograph was taken" (p121) | 3 |
| 117, 122–123, 129 | Ko Tim Keung collection, three photos (reproduced): low buildings, 1962; the corner of Tung Tsing Road and Tung Tau Tsuen Road, 1968; the land south-east of the City, 1962. | See the Details rows for each photo. | "in 1968 (right)" (p122) | 3 |
| 118, 119, 122 | Government Information Services, three photos (reproduced): new towers, 1972; aerial view, 1976; a fire, 1970. | See the Details rows for each photo. | "the aerial view of 1976 (opposite)" (p118) | 3 |
| 124–125 | Survey and Mapping Office, aerial photo, 1961 or 1962 (reproduced; no photo number). | The City lies between the Sai Tau Tsuen huts (south and west), the Kowloon City street grid (south) and the slab blocks of the Tung Tau estate (north-east). | — (caption blurred) | 3 |
| 125 | Plan to extend the Tung Tau estate over the north-east corner of the City, and China's objection of 1963 (cited, not shown; no reference). | The Government dropped the plan in 1963. | — (caption blurred) | 3 |
| 126–127 | Survey and Mapping Office, Hong Kong: survey map of the City and the land round it, "prepared in 1968". The book reproduces a part of it. The caption gives no sheet number. | In the City: lot numbers, street names, weaving factories, schools, a child care centre, "Kowloon City Alms House", "Tin Hau Temple" (see facts C78–C97 in the primary file). Round the City, my reading: north of Tung Tau Tsuen Road, "TUNG TAU COTTAGE RESETTLEMENT AREA (PUI MAN TSUEN)" with a chapel, schools, "Staff Quarters", "Government Quarters", playgrounds and a latrine. East of "TUNG TSING ROAD": estate "Block 1" and "Block 2", and a playground with "POND", "SWINGS", "SEESAW", "MERRY-GO-ROUND" and "SLIDE"; at the top edge, "TUNG WOR R…", cut at the edge of the copy. South-east: lanes with a "Noodle Factory", a "Preserved Food Factory", a "Weaving Factory" (a second label of the same shape is too faint to read) and a "Drying Ground", on streets labelled "SAI TAU TSUN CENTRE ST", "MIDDLE ST" and "HOK LO MAIN STREET". Street labels inside the City that I can read: "TAI CHANG STREET", "LO YAN ST", "ALMS HOUSE BACK STREET", "SZ KUNG ST", "ON KUI LANE", "LUNG CHUN ROAD". | "This map was prepared in 1968" | 3 |
| 127 | Survey and Mapping Office surveys of the City "from as early as 1961" (cited, not shown). | The Office made regular and detailed surveys. | "undertook regular and detailed surveys of the City from as early as 1961" | 3 |
| 127 | Agreement on electrical work in the City, "by 1962" (cited, not shown; no parties named except China Light and Power; no reference). | China Light and Power can do and check all electrical installations in the City. | "by 1962 an agreement had been reached" | 3 |
| 127 | Police patrols and arrests in the City from the early 1950s (cited, not shown; no reference). | Few cases went to court. | "The police had been patrolling the City from the early 1950s" | 3 |
| 128 | Survey and Mapping Office, aerial photo, 1949 (reproduced; no photo number). | The same area with far fewer buildings. | "An aerial view of the Walled City in 1949" | 3 |
| 129 | "a Government survey" of 1954. The essay gives no maker and no reference. | About 800 structures: 648 of stone and 152 wooden huts. | 129 | Government attempt to clear the Walled City area, 1947 (cited, not shown; no reference). | The attempt failed. | "without success, in 1947" | 3 |
| 130 | *Hong Kong Standard*, 11 January 1963, news report (clipping reproduced on the page; credit on p356: Public Records Office, HK (歷史檔案館); no file number) | The two headlines of the report. | "Walled City gets a face-lift" and "BIG BUILDING BOOM IS ON" | 4 |
| 130 | the same, photo caption in the clipping | New buildings of up to nine storeys rise fast in the City. They are taller than the one-storey houses behind them. | "NEW buildings up to nine storeys high are mushrooming in the Kowloon Walled City." | 4 |
| 130 | the same, first paragraph | Private developers pull down old one-storey buildings. Most of the new buildings have nine storeys. The book's caption quotes these words with small changes. | Caption: "private developers are busily pulling down old single-storey buildings and putting up new ones – most of them nine storeys high" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The report calls the City a rectangular piece of land of six acres. Building there is in a boom, while building in the rest of Hong Kong is slow. | "the six-acre rectangular piece of land" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | Trade sources: at least twelve sites are in redevelopment. A few small private contractors, who know the City well, do the work. | "no less than a dozen of sites are now being re-developed by a handful of small-scale private contractors who know the place well" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The new flats are not cheap. A flat of about 200 square feet inside sells for about $10,000. | "about $10,000 per flat with an internal area of around 200 square feet" | 4 |
| 130 | the same, part "High prices" | One constructor gives the unit price: about $50 for each square foot of floor. He says that this is too high even for luxury flats on the Peak. | "about $50 per square foot of floor" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The flats are almost all sold when the building is near completion. Many buyers pay a deposit to reserve a floor. | "flats are almost sold out when they are near completion" | 4 |
| 130 | the same, part "Down payment" | The terms to secure a "lau fa": $4,000 at the start, $3,000 while the reserved floor is under construction, and the balance at occupancy. The clipping explains "lau fa" only as "flat flower". From the context, it is a flat that a buyer reserves before it is built (my inference). | "'lau fa' (flat flower)" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The constructor says that few sites are free for redevelopment. | "the number of sites available for re-developments are limited" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The constructor must get the permission of the landlord for a "joint venture". The landlord gives the land. The constructor pays for the demolition and the construction. | "the landlord provides the land and the constructor contributes the funds for demolition and construction" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The split of the new building: the landlord gets the lower three floors. The constructor gets the floors from the third floor up to the eighth or ninth floor. | "The landlord will get the lower three floors of the new building" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The City has a name for dirty streets. But the new buildings have water closets and running water. | "modern facilities like water closets and running water" | 4 |
| 130 | the same, part "Telephone" | Electricity and telephone services are available in the new buildings. | "Electrical and telephone services are also available." | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The builders keep the costs as low as possible. They do not use consulting engineers or architects. | "The services of consulting engineers and architects are not being engaged." | 4 |
| 130 | the same | One skilled worker, the "master-mind", is in charge of all the work on a building. His pay is $600 each month. | "He is paid $600 a month and is in charge of all operations." | 4 |
| 130 | the same | One "master-mind" says that the buildings are safe, because all the pillars are of reinforced concrete. | "These buildings are safe, as all pillars are built with re-inforced concrete" | 4 |
| 130 | the same, part "Thick floors" | The floors are four inches of reinforced concrete. He says that this is the standard for new tenements in all of Hong Kong. | "The floors are of four inches thick of re-inforced concrete" | 4 |
| 130 | the same | The report asks why people pay such a high price. Its answer: a buyer pays no property tax and no rates in the City. | "You don't have to pay property tax and rates there." | 4 |
| 130 | the same | A second reason: the sons and grandsons of the buyer can inherit the flat, because no law limits the time of occupancy of the land. | "there is no law to regulate the length of occupancy of your land" | 4 |
| 130, 131 | Photo of Tung Tau Tsuen Road, 1963, by Ko Tim Keung (credit, p356) | The caption on p130 uses the photo as evidence against the newspaper. In 1963 the street had buildings of four or five storeys, not of one storey. | "as the 1963 photograph of Tung Tau Tsuen Road (right) shows" | 4 |
| 132–133 | Photo of the north side, 1972, by Ko Tim Keung (credit, p356) | The caption on p132 uses the photo as evidence. In 1972 the buildings of nine storeys filled almost all of the City, and some had 12 storeys and more. | "by 1972, when this photograph was taken" | 4 |

November 1954: 648 stone buildings, 152 wooden huts; 120 narcotic divans, 20 brothels, 5 gambling houses, 18 eating houses, 9 weaving factories, 9 dentists, 94 shops of general goods, 4 schools (3 right wing, 1 left wing). | "the whole area is intensely congested" | 7 |
| 274 | same memo, para 5 (end) | No sanitation; filth; refuse piles up for years; many beggars with diseases. | "The place is swarming with mendicants suffering from all sorts of diseases." | 7 |
| 274 | same memo, para 6 | Police action is only: arrest, fingerprint, hold 48 hours, release with a warning; deport the worst (harder and harder); destroy drugs and drug kit; treat money seized in gambling raids as unclaimed property. | "detaining for 48 hours and then releasing with a warning" | 7 |
| 274 | same memo, para 7 | At least 2 Inspectors and 8 constables work in the area at all times. | "At least 2 Inspectors and 8 P.Cs. are continously in operation in the area" | 7 |
| 274-275 | same memo, para 7, seizures 1953 / 1954 | Opium pipes 1,133 / 1,296; heroin pipes 1,057 / 1,210; pots of opium 5,912 / 4,899; jars of opium 43 / 51; raw opium 15 taels / 40 taels; heroin pills 63,968 / 108,164; heroin powder 532 / 4,200 packets; morphine powder 467 / 12,922 packets and 5 large tins. | "Heroin Pills" | 7 |
| 275 | same memo, para 7, arrests 1953 / 1954 | Chinese males 2,403 / 8,134; Chinese females 377 / 821; banishees 250 / 402. | "Banishees" | 7 |
| 275 | same memo, para 8 | End of 1953: about 60 drug divans left; police felt in control. Then large squatter fires in Kowloon made the City population grow fast, and drug traders moved their headquarters into it. | "following a series of large scale squatter fires in Kowloon the population of the Walled City increased rapidly" | 7 |
| 275 | same memo, para 9 | 1950/52: "K.C. Division" (INFERRED: Kowloon City Division) was busy at Kai Tak and was not able to patrol the City regularly. In late 1952 it had more men and looked at the City again. Then it found 154 drug divans, 7 gambling dens, 13 dog meat shops, 11 brothels, 1 theatre (formerly Cantonese plays) with 2 strip-tease shows a day, 300 seats, no fire precautions. | "The accommodation of this theatre was 300 and there were no fire precautions." | 7 |
| 275 | same memo, paras 10-13 | The Lung Kung theatre; three more theatres by mid-February 1953; fire of 24.2.53 destroyed some 390 buildings; 8 new theatres and 4 converted shops by 17.3.53, 16 in all; press publicity. | "destroying some 390 buildings" | 7 |
| 275 | same memo, para 14 | The Commissioner of Police met the theatre owners. By 19 March 1953 the theatres were gone. The dog-meat stall owners were also seen, and the stalls closed. | "Dog meat stall owners were also interviewed and were also closed." | 7 |
| 275 | same memo, para 15 | A team of 1 detective and 7 constables under 2 Inspectors works only on the City, and still did in 1955. | "a team consisting of 1 detective, 7 P.Cs. under the leadership of 2 Inspectors" | 7 |
| 277 | "The same police report quoted above" (the 1953 report or the 1955 memo; the essay does not say which) | The City is a breeding ground for Triads. They live by extortion. Police use secret deportation; new societies replace broken ones. | "As soon as one society has been broken up a new one has been formed to take its place." | 7 |
| 277 | Police report, 2000 (Hong Kong Police; no reference) | About 50 Triad societies in Hong Kong; 15 often seen; about four per cent of reported crime is Triad crime. | "A 2000 police report noted" | 7 |
| 278 | Foreign Office secret cypher to British posts, 1958, CO 1030/810 | Murder of 27 February in the City; Chan Hing arrested and admitted it; say nothing; do not argue the law. | "We do not want to attract attention to this case" | 7 |
| 278 | Letter to the Colonial Office, 28 June 1958, CO 1030/810 | Three earlier cases: the well death of 25 October 1953 (not guilty at the Supreme Court); two Triad cases of 1955 (7 and 20 arrested, 8 deported for life). | "asphyxia by drowning as a result of being placed in the well" | 7 |
| 278-279 | Governor to the Secretary of State for the Colonies, August 1958, CO 1030/810 | Chan Hing executed; no jurisdiction challenge; Chan Yuen also accused. Proposes slow, case-by-case police action against internationally recognised offences. | "Chan Hing has now been executed for a murder committed at an address within the Walled City" | 7 |
| 279-280 | Secret telegram, Secretary of State to Governor Sir Robert Black, 27 December 1958 | Agrees. Expects no major Chinese trouble. Asks for no new law and little publicity. | "efforts could be made to diminish or restrain publicity" | 7 |
| 280 | Message of Sir Robert Black to the Secretary of State, 31 December 1958 | Raid of 26 November 1958 on a heroin pill factory and divan; seizures; 24 arrested, 17 freed, 5 to be charged; asks for authority. | "This is an important narcotics case" | 7 |
| 303 | Martin Booth, *Gweilo: A Memoir of a Hong Kong Childhood*, Doubleday, 2004. A memoir written long after the walk; it is not a record of the time, and the book gives no date for the walk. | A boy's walk down an alley of the City: beds and bedding, home industries at tables, a baker's wood-fired oven, noodle makers, smells, open sewage gullies. | "Through the open doors I spied scenes of industrial domesticity." | 13 |
| 314 | The airport height restriction (law and date not given) | 150 feet, or 15 storeys at most, along the south edge of the City; the only building rule that was strictly enforced. | "limited the upward thrust of the City to 150 feet or a maximum of 15 storeys along the southern boundary" | 10 |
| 321 | A Hong Kong law (maker, date and name not given) | It banned the breeding of animals in urban Hong Kong. The authorities did not enforce it against the City's racing pigeons. | "a law prohibiting the breeding of animals within urban Hong Kong" | 13 |
| 324 | Memory of one resident about the 1950s (name and date of the talk not given) | Children played marbles in the streets and on the roofs. For fun they ran into the opium dens to look at the smokers, with no trouble. | "We used to play marbles in the streets" ... "but there was never any trouble." | 10 |
| 328 | Convention between Great Britain and China Respecting an Extension of Hong Kong Territory, June 1898 (the Second Convention of Peking) | It leased the New Territories to Britain. | 328 | Order in Council, end of 1899 (no reference) | It declared the City a part of the colony. | "part and parcel of His Majesty's Colony of Hong Kong" | 8 |
| 328 | Colonial Office minute, after July 1900 (no reference) | The officials told Li Hung Chang the new position, and the Colonial Office saw the subject as closed. | "the matter is at an end" | 8 |
| 328 | Government notice, 10 June 1933 (no reference) | It set the resumption of the land in the City for the end of 1934. | "a notice stipulating resumption of land (ie, compulsory acquisition) by the end of 1934" | 8 |
| 328 | A British plan for the City, before 1941 (maker and date not given) | The City was to become a place for visitors and for people with an interest in old things. | "a place of popular resort and antiquarian interest" | 8 |
| 329 | Notice to quit, 27 November 1947, and a magistrate's order for eviction (no reference) | The squatters got notice to quit and refused. The Government then got the order. | "a magistrate's order for eviction was obtained" | 8 |
| 329 | Government statement on the events of 12 January 1948 (no reference) | The police fired warning shots from revolvers into the ground. Five people had minor injuries. | "for the police to fire warning shots from revolvers into the ground." | 8 |
| 329 | Later Government statement, January 1948 (no reference) | One more injured person came for treatment. | "a further casualty has since reported for treatment suffering from an injury in the abdomen provisionally diagnosed as from a ricochet bullet" | 8 |
| 329, 330 | *China Mail*, Hong Kong, Tuesday 13 January 1948, front page (No. 33869 on the masthead), reproduced on p330 | Main headings: "KOWLOON HUT CITY INCIDENT", "Defiant Squatters Battle With Police" and "Teargas Finally Ends Rioting". The sub-headings of the report are "Statement", "Eyewitness", "Stones Fly", "Teargas" and "Defiance". | "Violence flared up yesterday in Kowloon City when squatters, resisting Police action to remove re-erected structures, hurled stone and bricks at the Police." | 8 |
| 329 | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page: the eyewitness account, as McHugh quotes it | A pitched battle of 30 minutes. Women and children picked up stones for the men. Seven teargas bombs. Six people with gunshot wounds. | "the police were forced to open fire with revolvers" | 8 |
| 329 | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page: the statement of Lau Chi Leung, committee member of the Kowloon Walled City Residents' Association | The squatters will stay on the site until the Chinese Government tells them to leave. | "orders to quit" | 8 |
| 329, 330 | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page: report from Nanking, dated 12 January, under the heading "Hong Kong-China Anti-Smuggling Pact Signed" | Hong Kong and China signed a pact against smuggling, by an exchange of notes in Nanking between the Chinese Foreign Minister, Wang Shih-chieh, and the British Ambassador, Sir Ralph Stevenson (bold lead on p330). | 329, 331 | Telegram of Sir Ralph Stevenson, British representative in Nanking, to the Foreign Office, 27 August 1949 (no file number) | The lack of control over Kowloon City hurt the Hong Kong Government. | 330 | *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page: the second paragraph of the report | Six people went to hospital, with bullet wounds and the effects of tear smoke. | "Six villagers — four men and two women — have been admitted to hospital." | 8 |

| 331 | The official report on the Kowloon and Tsuen Wan riots of October 1956 (no title given) | Sham Shui Po and the City were the centres of the riots and the areas with the most crime. | 332 | Ruling of Chief Justice Sir Michael Hogan in the case of Wong Hon (also Wong Wai), for a murder of July 1959 (no reference) | The Convention gave only a 'temporary' jurisdiction to the Chinese officials then in the City. | 332 | Statement of the New China News Agency, Hong Kong (the book gives no date; after the clearance notices, thus probably January 1963) | China called the time limit for the demolition and resettlement a violation of its sovereignty. | "in spite of the warning of the Chinese government, is a gross violation of China's sovereignty and is intolerable" | 8 |
| 332 | Clearance notices of the Resettlement Department, January 1963 | The notices gave demolition deadlines of 8, 12, 14, 19 and 20 February 1963. | "began to serve clearance notices, complete with deadlines" | 8 |
| 332-334 | Secret briefing of the Colonial Office, London, later in 1963 (no file number) | A small group of landlords with no legal title opposed the scheme, for their profits. | 334 | *Hong Kong Standard*, 19 January 1963: the scheme | The City had an area of "about 70 mow". The plan was to clear it under Stage II of the Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme. | 334 | *Hong Kong Standard*, 19 January 1963: the leftists | The leftists sent at least two delegations to Canton and Peking for support. They kept Peking informed of the progress of the resettlement scheme. | "raised more than $10,000 last summer" | 8 |

## Leads to primary sources

One merged list of the leads of the 13 note files. The page is the page of the book that names
the lead. The book gives no file number unless the line gives one. Some leads are also in
`../../primary-sources/README.md`.

**The National Archives, Kew (UK)**
- CO 537/4807: Colonial Office papers of 1948 on the jurisdiction question and the failed talks
  (p272).
- CO 1030/394: the report of the Commissioner of Police, March 1953, on the strip-tease theatres,
  the dog-meat shops and the divans (pp12, 272–273). The text can be a covering letter of the
  Governor.
- CO 1030/934: the Savingram of 11 March 1955 from the Governor to the Secretary of State, with
  the police report (pp12, 273). The numbers 394 and 934 have the same digits: check both.
- CO 1030/810: papers of 1958: a Foreign Office cypher, a letter of 28 June 1958, the
  Governor's letter of August 1958, and probably the telegram of 27 December 1958 and the
  message of 31 December 1958 (pp278–280).
- The secret memo of a Governor to the Secretary of State on jurisdiction over the City (p10). No
  date. Probably in the CO files (INFERRED).
- The Attorney General's opinion on the City, "five decades" after 1899 (p10).
- The confidential memo on improvements to the City's electricity, a few years after the failed
  post-war attempt (p10).
- Foreign Office papers of 1898: MacDonald's reports from Peking, Balfour's telegram, and
  MacDonald's summary after the final draft of the lease (pp65–66).
- Papers of 1899: Governor Sir Henry Blake and the Viceroy of Canton; the Governor's request for
  the removal of the Chinese troops; the Colonial Office proposals; the decision of 14 May 1899;
  Lord Salisbury to the Chinese Minister in London (pp69–71).
- The Colonial Office minute after the visit of Li Hung Chang in July 1900 ("the matter is at an
  end") (p328).
- The telegram of Sir Ralph Stevenson, Nanking, to the Foreign Office, 27 August 1949, marked
  "of particular secrecy" (pp329–331).
- Governor Grantham's dispatch on the riots of October 1956, and the official report on the riots
  (p331).
- The secret Colonial Office briefing of 1963: 76 per cent acceptance "by 9 January 1963"
  (p334).
- Governor MacLehose's advice to London before the visit of Edward Heath in 1974 (p334).

**Treaties and laws**
- The Peking Convention for the Extension of Hong Kong, 9 June 1898, and its Kowloon City clause
  (pp66, 328).
- The Order in Council of 27 December 1899 (pp10, 71, 328).
- The Hong Kong ordinance of 1850 on Chinese fugitives (p65). No number given.
- The Hong Kong Airport (Control of Obstructions) Ordinance: the closure orders of 1975 for the
  tall buildings on Tung Tau Tsuen Road (東頭村道) (pp12, 334, 336). The height rule of 150 feet,
  or 15 storeys, on the south edge (p314). The height limit near Kai Tak, with no law named
  (p27).
- The Land Acquisition (Possessory Title) Ordinance, and the minutes and papers of the Executive
  Council of 1991–1993: the Director of Buildings and Lands (1993) and Miss Leung Wai Tung (May
  1993) (pp343–345).
- The law that banned the breeding of animals in urban Hong Kong (p321). No name or number.
- The founding of the ICAC in 1974 (p254).

**Hong Kong Government records before the clearance**
- J.H. Stewart Lockhart, report on the New Territories, 1898: the wall's size, towers, gates and
  embrasures (p62).
- The plan of 1933 to clear the City: the notice of 10 June 1933 on the resumption of the land,
  and the papers on the 436 residents and their new houses, to 1940 (pp72, 328–329).
- The notice to quit of 27 November 1947, the magistrate's eviction order, and the two
  Government statements of January 1948 (pp328–329). Saywell dates this clearance to 1947
  (p129).
- The police survey of 1952 and the police report of about March 1953 (p12). The memo of 4 March
  1955 is in the book (pp274–275), but only two of its pages. The other pages, with the Triad
  passage if it is in this memo, are not in the book (p277).
- Hong Kong Police records of patrols and arrests in the City from the early 1950s (p127).
- The ruling of Chief Justice Sir Michael Hogan on jurisdiction, in the trial of Wong Hon
  (1959–1960) (p332). The repo has a *China Mail* report of 3 December 1959 on this trial (see
  `docs/people.md`).
- The 1963 plan to demolish the north-east corner of the City for the Tung Tau estate, and
  China's objection (pp125, 132): the clearance notices of the Resettlement Department of
  January 1963, the papers of Stage II of the Tung Tau Resettlement Scheme, and the time-bound
  eviction notices of the early 1960s (pp11, 332–334). The Government attempt "[in 1962]" (p40)
  belongs here.
- The petitions of the Anti-Demolition Committee to the Secretary for Chinese Affairs, with the
  petition of 29 December 1962 on public utilities (p334). The committee of March 1962 and its
  delegations to Beijing and Taipei (p11). Its papers, if any, are not named.
- The Government statement of 1963 on the first stand-pipe (p107), probably a press release.
- The call of the Kai Fong Association on the Secretariat for Chinese Affairs in 1964, on the
  water shortage. The same record probably gives the four emergency hydrants removed after the
  drought, the five stand-pipes left, and the $12–15 a month paid to water-carriers (p107).
- China Light and Power: the agreement of "by 1962" on the electrical installations (p127).
- The Survey and Mapping Office: the full sheets of the 1968 map (pp126–127), the surveys "from
  as early as 1961" (p127), and the aerial photos of 1949 (p128) and of 1961 or 1962
  (pp124–125).
- The Public Works Department (工務司署) action of December 1972 on two illegal buildings beside
  the City (p334).
- The Government statement of 1973 on the Resettlement Department (p12), and the 1973 list of
  services in the City with the comment of the Buildings Ordinance Office (p334).
- The 'Fight Violent Crime Campaign' of 1973, and the resident patrols in the public housing
  estates in the 1970s (p220). These are about all of Hong Kong.
- The survey of the residents of 1975 (p246). Maker and title not given.
- The Sai Tau Tsuen (西頭村) squatter area, cleared in 1985, and the making of Carpenter Road Park
  (p39).
- Urban Services Department (市政總署) records: the refuse collection (two tons a day, ten
  sweepers, the collection points, p104), and the rat-baiting campaign of the clearance (p231).
- The visits of "health and hygiene officers" to the factories (p229). No department named.
- Fire Department inspections, advice and closures of the plastics workshops (p163).
- The records of the three services that Lui names with no reference (p223): the police in the
  City, the refuse collection and the mail. The book tells of each: p104 (refuse), pp144–149
  (postman), pp250–257 (policeman).
- Post Office records of the City round: the training time and the hat (p149). Lam Po Chun names
  the Lung Kong Road (龍崗道) Post Office (p146).

**Clearance records, 1986–1995**
- The Kowloon City District Office file on the City; the paper of Gordon Jones and Richard
  Margolis of April 1986; the record of the meeting at Government House of 21 April 1986
  (pp336–337). The photos of the City's lavatories that a young executive officer took for Jones
  in 1986 (p336).
- The Housing Department (房屋署) registration of 14–15 January 1987: 19,606 people from 5116
  families on the first day, 22 refusals, 83 entrances, just over 33,000 in all (p339).
- The Special Committee of the Housing Authority, from January 1987: papers and minutes, the
  rehousing rules, the ex-gratia scales, the HK$342,000 medical and dental package (pp340–344).
- The Government's compensation formula and rules for businesses (pp31, 79).
- The clearance sketch-survey plans of 1987 (p340): "BLD/SDU/COMP/SS 642(A)" and "H.D. Structure
  Ref.: A 19/57 (1-4)" for 6 Sai Shing Road (西城路), 4/F, Tai Hing Bldg, measured 9 November
  1987; the sheet 2 references B24/76, B24/77, B24/114, B24/115 and B24/116. The full set covers
  "every apartment on every floor in every building" (p340). The book's copies come from Suenn
  Ho (p356). The records of the clearance team of 1987 listed the premises and the residents
  (p140).
- The clearance phases that the standfirsts name: Cheng San (鄭山) left in 1990, in "the first
  phase" (p169); Chung Yuk Yee left in 1991 (p161); Tsin Mu Lam's clinic closed in 1992, in "the
  third phase" (p184). The clearance files can give the dates and the premises.
- The painted and chalk marks in the photos look like the numbers of the clearance team: "D44" /
  "125" (p37); "B22/128" (p144); "B22/120" (p148); "045/8/1-5up" or "D45/8/1-5up" (p164); "?23/8"
  (p168); "C3?/14/1-4" (p341); "B22" / "81" (p342); "B3?/73 (1-4)" (p354); perhaps "W5168"
  (p10). The clearance files can match them to buildings, floors and flats. The repo's GRS
  (政府檔案處) catalogue lists HKRS1255-3 (Lands Department (地政總署) case files of business
  claimants) and HKRS1078-1 (City and New Territories Administration (政務總署), Special Duties)
  (see `../../primary-sources/grs-catalogue-kwc/`).
- The Government survey of 1987 for compensation claims: 67 working wells and about 40 suppliers
  (p107). HKRS1255-3 can hold the claims of the well owners.
- The Buildings and Lands Department random survey of premises, May 1987 (23.44 m²) (p343).
- The statement of the Chinese Foreign Ministry on the clearance, January 1987 (p340).
- The Government sub-committee on the future of the City's doctors and dentists, on which Tsin Mu
  Lam (錢茂林) served (pp185, 187). HKRS1078-1-17 to -22, a working group on unregistered medical
  and dental practitioners (1987–1992), can be the same body (INFERRED).
- The Director of Audit's report on the clearance (overspend of HK$210 million), about 1993
  (p344).
- The judgment of the High Court of February 1991 on the adjudicator of the Small Claims Tribunal
  (p340).
- The court case of 1991 of the Hongkong and Macau Lutheran Church and Lee Yin Ping, with the
  deeds of 1966 (Norwegian Lutheran Mission, HK$6300), 1978 (Lee Chi On to Lee Yin Ping) and
  1985 (transfer to the church) (p344).
- The files of the Appeals Tribunal and of the Walled City Clearance Assessment Review Board
  (chairman Raymond Hu Fat Kwong), 1992–1994 (pp346–348).
- The Housing Department application for a court order on the shanties of the Tung Tau Estate
  (東頭邨), and the opinion of the Legal Department, 1993 (p347).
- Agriculture and Fisheries Department and RSPCA records of the pet collection: one cat and four
  dogs (p345).
- The records of the Kai Fong Association (九龍城砦街坊福利事業促進委員會): the yearly clean-ups of
  roofs, alleys, pipes and cables (p104); the mains sewer that it put in with the Government in
  the 1970s (p108); the office at 3/F, 16 Tung Tau Tsuen Road, with office hours, two full-time
  secretaries and the yearly Christmas party for old residents (p180); the clearance years under
  Ma Chong Chung (馬創忠) (p345). The photo of its standing committee of 7 June 1964 (Frankie Lam)
  (p332).
- The property records with the stamp of the Kai Fong Association (p135). The essay gives no
  example.
- The Kowloon Walled City Residents' Association: the handwritten sale contract of 14 December
  1960 (p116). The caption calls it "typical", thus the Association can hold more contracts.

**Organizations in the City**
- The Industrial Commercial Liaison Committee, chaired for many years by Lam Shu Chuen (林樹全)
  (p153). His family's factory of parts for radio-controlled toys, moved to Tai Po after the
  clearance and still there in 2014 (p153). The book does not name the firm.
- The Salvation Army (救世軍): its kindergarten and chapel on Lung Chun Back Road (龍津後街)
  (p300).
- The CNEC (中華傳道會) Living Word Church: the yamen (衙門) compound, from the early 1970s to
  1991, and the Old People's Centre (老人中心) (pp299, 304, 306, 309). The lease of the yamen to a
  missionary society in 1949 (p135; no source, no name).
- The Tin Hau Temple: built in 1951 on an alley off Lo Yan Street (老人街); the earlier temple in
  a coastal village, torn down in the Japanese occupation for the airport (p241).
- The Child Care Centre, set up by Maureen Clarke in 1959, and 'The Well' of the St Stephen's
  Society, set up in 1973 (p263).
- The repo's GRS file HKRS1255-3-261, Fuk Tak Temple (福德土地廟), G/F, 5 Tai Chang Street (大井街),
  5th Lane. Compare it with the shrine of pp32–33.
- The Hong Kong Police Museum: the Triad Room, the altar items of a Triad hall raided in 1978,
  the opium pipes and the undated photo of a Triad ceremony (pp276–277).

**Courts and police, 1953–2000**
- The trial for the death of 25 October 1953: a prostitute found in a well about 300 yards from
  the City; the man was found not guilty (p278).
- The deportation records of two Triad cases of 1955: 7 and 20 men arrested, 8 deported for life
  (pp272–278).
- The trials of Chan Hing (murder of 27 February 1958 in the City; executed in 1958) and of Chan
  Yuen (accused of a murder in the City, 1958) (pp278–279).
- The police raid of 26 November 1958 on a heroin pill factory and divan: 24 arrested (p280).
- The Hong Kong Police report of 2000 on Triad societies (p277).
- The "Contemporary records" of the 1950s on luxury cars parked on Tung Tau Tsuen Road at night
  (p273). Not named.

**Newspapers and broadcasts**
- The *Hongkong Weekly Press*: 1899, probably May, on the British occupation (p71); 1904, on
  Kowloon City ("City of Cannons", "very superior class", "nothing but desolation") (pp65, 72).
- *The Hongkong Telegraph*, 1890, on the Kowloon City suburb (p65). *The Daily Press*, 1899, on
  the flag-raising of 16 April 1899 (p69). The *Hongkong Guide*, 1893, on the walk to Kowloon
  City (p65). Reverend Krone's paper on San On district, 1858 (p65). The Hong Kong General
  Chamber of Commerce, statements of 1898 (pp65, 68).
- The Chinese-language newspapers of February–March 1953 on the strip-tease theatres and the
  dog-meat shops (p273). The 1955 memo names no title.
- The *China Mail*, 13 January 1948, front page, "Kowloon Hut City Incident" (No. 33869) (p330).
  The copy in the book is too small to read. A clear copy can confirm the "Eyewitness" column
  (see "Doubts", part 8).
- The *Hong Kong Standard*: 11 January 1963, "Walled City gets a face-lift / Big building boom is
  on" (p130); 19 January 1963, "Walled City: 'cause for disquiet'" (p334). The report that "The
  majority of the 20,000 to 30,000 residents" agreed to resettlement (p11) can be one of these.
  A full-size copy will settle the blurred digits.
- The New China News Agency statements against the demolition plan, about 1962–1963, and of
  January 1963 (pp11, 332).
- *Ta Kung Pao* (大公報) of about 18 January 1963, which two women pasted on Tung Tau Tsuen Road
  (p334).
- The *South China Morning Post*: the column of Kevin Sinclair (1992); the report on the landlord
  Chang Kat Kong (1993); the report of "more than 20 former residents" camping, and J.S.
  Corrigall's reply (January 1993); reports of 28 November 1991, 8 January 1992, 3 March 1992,
  7 April 1992, 1–2 July 1992, September 1992 (Patten), June 1993, 18–19 January 1994 and
  15 December 1995 (pp344–348).
- The Associated Press reporter who took Resnick into the City in 1983: AP reports of about 1983
  (p217).
- The Chinese television documentary of 2011 with Qiao Zonghuai (p337). No title and no
  broadcaster. Qiao's summary of the talks, which John Boyd signed (pp337–338).
- *99% Invisible* (2012) and *The New York Times* of 1 March 2012, with its density graphic
  (pp213–215). Both are secondary, but they can name their sources for the 33,000 and 603
  figures.

**Testimony**
- McHugh's own interviews: Richard Margolis (about 2012), Sir John Boyd (spring 2014) and
  probably Gordon Jones (pp336–339). The words of Duncan Pescod "in 2013" (pp344–345).
- Lambot's interviews with former residents and with one police officer (pp273, 277, 281). The
  book does not name them.
- The unnamed "reports" that many people did not have the money for the connection fee for well
  water (p107). The source of the Triad threat of 1980 about the rise of the water price (p110).
- The sources of the counts in the captions: "more than 150" dentists at the peak and "86 by
  1987" (p36); "70 or so private water suppliers" (p112; compare the 67 wells of some 40
  suppliers, p107).
- The claim that more than 80 per cent of Hong Kong's fishballs came from the City (p290). It
  rests on hearsay. A trade or Government survey of the time can test it.

**Photos, drawings and films**
- The images of Wilkinson's essay (credits p356): the 1845 survey map (The National Archives,
  UK) (p64); the 1898 view from Lung Chun Pier (龍津石橋) and the undated second view (The
  National Archives, UK) (pp63, 68–69); the 1865 photo from the hill (Arthur Hacker) (pp66–67);
  the paper-burning pavilion, the cannon of the early 1920s and the alley of about 1910 (Hong
  Kong Museum of History (香港歷史博物館)) (pp68, 70); the 1923 draft map (Survey and Mapping
  Office) (p71); the 1924 photo of the paddy fields (Public Records Office, HK) (pp72–73). The
  original 1847 plan of the Viceroy of Canton: the book prints a modern drawing (p62).
- Mak Fung, photos of 1950 (pp116, 120, 121). Ko Tim Keung: street photos of 1962 and 1968
  (pp117, 122–123, 129), Tung Tau Tsuen Road in 1963 (pp131, 278), the north side in 1972
  (pp132–133), Sai Tau Tsuen in 1978 (p279). The book gives no collection or number.
- The Government Information Services photo library: the fire of 1970 (p122), the new towers of
  1972 (p118), the aerial views of 1973 and 1976 (pp119, 335), the building of 1975 (p336).
  Newspaper reports of the fire of 1970 (p122).
- William McElhinney, the footprint plans of 1962 and 1987 (p134), and the base maps or aerial
  photos under them.
- Nicholas Millman, the police surveillance photos of the City and of Sai Tau Tsuen (pp280–281;
  undated).
- The *Sing Pao Daily News* photo archive, 1987–1994, and the posters in its photos (pp337–339,
  346–347).
- Ian Lambot, the photo of the facades behind the sports ground (pp142–143): no date, no side
  named.
- The original photos of Girard and Lambot, with their dates. Three photos show a calendar (pp34,
  56, 89).
- The "day in the life" photos of the postman Lui Man Sang in the 1993 edition (p145).
- Signs in the photos that a sharper copy can read: the removal posters at 94 Lung Chun Road
  (龍津道), 美景 (p189); the "NO TRESPASSING" notice (p144); the advert for new flats in Luen Hing
  Building (聯興樓), linked to 24 Tung Tau Tsuen Road (p165); the store 廣泰號 (p174), which the
  repo links to Kwong Tai Hoo Store (HKRS1255-3-334A, -334B); the CLP envelope for Flat 2, G/F,
  31 Lo Yan Street, Sai Tau Village (p89).
- Suenn Ho's Fulbright study of 1991–1992: the report of 32 pages, the full set of level plans
  above the 3rd floor, and her dissertation (pp19, 136–140, 336). The repo has her M+ archive
  (`../../primary-sources/mplus-suenn-ho-ca17/`).
- Fiona Hawthorne's sketches of 1986: "dozens"; p210 shows two (pp210–211).
- Takayuki Suzuki's team: the articles in a Japanese architecture magazine (about 1993; not
  named) and 『大図解 九龍城』 (1997) (pp204–209). See `../../primary-sources/daizukai-kowloon-1997/`.
- Ryuji Miyamoto's photo book of the City (title not given) and the Japanese edition of *City of
  Darkness* (p205).
- Films of the time: *Kowloon: The Walled City* (1989), an Austrian documentary, cameraman Hamdani
  Milas, on YouTube (pp198–199); *Baraka* (1992), 70mm (p199).
- Feature films shot in the City: *Long Arm of the Law* (1984, Johnny Mak; "As early as 1982" on
  p15), the first film shot inside (pp15, 198–199, 223); *Crime Story* (1993), shot on the roof
  of the empty City (pp198–199); *Bloodsport* (1988): check if the crew filmed in the City
  (pp198–199); *Brothers from the Walled City* (probably the DVD cover 城寨出來者; year not given)
  (p198); the unnamed film of the stills on pp198–199. The real lanes and roofs are evidence;
  the plots are not (see `../README.md`, rule 5).

**Books and memoirs**
- Jackie Pullinger, *Chasing the Dragon* (1980) and *Crack in the Wall: The Life and Death of
  Kowloon Walled City* (1989): first-hand accounts from 1966 (pp15, 197).
- Martin Booth, *Gweilo: A Memoir of a Hong Kong Childhood* (Doubleday, 2004): memories of the
  City in the 1950s (pp197–198, 303).
- Jan Morris, *Hong Kong: Epilogue to an Empire* (Penguin, 1990): the passage on the City
  (p191). No page and no date of her visit.
- Peter Popham's Introduction to *City of Darkness* (1993) (p16).
- George Kennedy-Skipton, District Officer South: his account of 1970 (p72). Form not given.
- Aaron Tan's writing on the City as a 'rhizome' (p19). No title or date.
- Lui Tai Lok, *The Story of Hong Kong in the 1970s Retold* (in Chinese; Hong Kong Book Prize
  2013) (p220). Secondary, but it can lead to records of crime and order in the 1970s.

**Later works (secondary, for ideas only)**
- The bridge studies of Urbanus (p136); the Vimeo presentation of the University of Waterloo by
  Lia Tramontini, Haneen Dalla-Ali, Mona Dai and Christopher Glebe (pp121, 140); the drawing of
  Adolfo Arranz in the *South China Morning Post*, March 2013 (p141). Their source lists can name
  records (for example on the wells).
- The estimate of "seven square feet per person" (p135). No source.
- The map key (p353) places the people and places of the features: 12 Fuk Tak Temple, 18 Jackie
  Pullinger, 23 Law Yu Yi, 27 Old People's Centre, 30 Standpipe, 31 Tin Hau Temple, 34 Well,
  40 Yu Hing Wan. Compare the pins with the texts (see "Doubts", parts 9 and 10).

intro, a feature or an essay. The page after each id is its `cite`. The cluster `map` has no

file.

## Check (2026-11-16)

A second agent checked this file and the twin file as an adversary. The scripts are in
`research/sources-2026-11/codr/tmp/check16/`.

**1. No lost row.** The script `rows.py` reads each table row of "Details" and "Quoted records"
of the 13 note files (2067 rows). It found each row in this file: 1834 rows exactly, and 233
rows with the added "Part" cell or with the added words of the twin. Lost rows: 0. After the
changes below, 44 rows have a new wording of the "Detail" cell. Their page, words and source
are the same.

**2. No lost line of the twin.** I compared the twin with its last commit. Its changes are only
the `ocr/` row, the short "Secondary" section, the pointer in place of the captions table, and
the pointer in the room-facts table. Each quote of the removed lines is in this file. The 13
rows of the old table "Captions and essays (secondary: use as leads)" are in parts 3, 4, 8, 9,
10 and 11. Their facts, quotes and pages are there. One detail was missing: Wilkinson's essay
tells the history to 1940. I added it to the table "What is secondary in this book".

**3. A second check of the content.** I took 45 rows of "Facts and details" and "Quoted
records" with a fixed step (every 46th row from row 8), from all 13 parts and the quoted
records. I checked each row against the page image. For text, I used the OCR text of the page
image. For photos, signs, the map and small Chinese print, I looked at the image or at a crop.
Wrong rows: 0 of 45. Thus I did not check a second set.

**4. The sort.** No row gives the words of a speaker of the 32 interviews as a secondary fact.
The rows of parts 11–13 quote only the standfirsts, captions and glosses. The essays also quote
other witnesses (Margolis, Boyd, Jones, Qiao, Pescod, Lambot's residents, Chan Shing, Yu Wing
Leung). I added a paragraph to "What is secondary in this book": their words are testimony, not
secondary facts. Chinese characters: 318 strings. 61 of them are in no other file of the repo.
These are the book's own glosses (for example 紅番, 踢人入會 and 臥虎藏龍, which I saw on the page)
and readings of signs in the photos. 呂大樂 and 都市實踐 came from the twin's old text.

**5. The form.** The sections "What it rests on", "Limits" and "Leads to primary sources" are
there. Each relative link goes to a file that exists. No quote has more than 25 words. I made
these changes to the text outside the quotes:
- One path in "Doubts" had no `../../primary-sources/` prefix. I added it.
- Five sentences had "could" or "would" (pp331, 334, 336, 338). I wrote them again.
- 47 sentences had more than 25 words. I divided them. Some lists of names, pages and objects
  stay longer than 25 words.

## More items: the source search of 2026-11 (catalogued, not read)

- **Status:** catalogued on 2026-11-17 by the source search of 2026-11. Nobody has read these items for the repo yet.
  "What it gives" is the short note of the agent that found the item. It is not a checked fact.
  Before you use a fact in the model, read it on the source and write it in this file with its
  page and a short exact quote.

1 items. Other editions of the book of Girard and Lambot.

### repo-audit-37: 黑暗之城──九龍城寨的日與夜 (book page and 目錄)

- **Creator:** Greg Girard and Ian Lambot; 中華書局(香港)Chung Hwa Book Co. (有限公司)
- **Date:** 2015-07-07
- **Kind:** catalogue
- **Class:** secondary (A publisher's catalogue page of the Chinese edition; the repo uses it only for the Chinese names.)
- **Value:** medium
- **Language:** zh
- **Access:** open
- **Licence:** © Chung Hwa Book Co.
- **Link:** https://www.chunghwabook.com.hk/book/detail/id/1239925267914383362/
- **Other links:** https://www.chunghwabook.com.hk/upload/files/2024/05_08/f7c27235b9e14a40c8c2bc25b4a1f46d.pdf
- **Proof that it exists:** 中華書局 黑暗之城──九龍城寨的日與夜; ISBN 9789888340897, 464 pages, 2015-07-07
- **What it gives (finder's note, not checked):** The Chinese names and trades of the interviewees of City of Darkness Revisited, for example 黃如明 (Wong Yu Ming), dentist, and 林寶鎮 (Lam Po Chun), the rooftop postman, and the Chinese titles of the essays.
- **Notes:** data/people.json: 32 entries (one for each interviewee); data/mysteries.json 2; docs/people.md 32 lines. The repo holds the English book (city-of-darkness-revisited/) but no folder cites this Chinese edition. The page links a 試讀 (sample) PDF of the Chinese edition (alt URL): not read, and not used by the repo. The table of contents spells one name 'Chan Wai Chui' where the English book has Chan Wai Shui.
- **Same item in the finder files:** books-13
